Chapter 10 Substance-Related, Addictive and Neurocognitive Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 10.1 Drugs, Dependence and Addiction: The Vocabulary and the Manuals: define the core terms of substance use and classify substance and addictive disorders in ICD-10, DSM-5-TR and ICD-11, including gambling and gaming disorder.
  2. 10.2 Why People Become Addicted: Reward, Learning, Genes and Personality: explain the reward circuit and the learning, genetic, psychodynamic and social theories of addiction, and why some drugs capture more users than others.
  3. 10.3 Alcohol: The Drug, Intoxication, Withdrawal and the Damage Done: describe how alcohol acts on the brain, the stages of intoxication and withdrawal, delirium tremens, alcoholic hallucinosis and the harm heavy drinking does.
  4. 10.4 Treating Alcohol Use Disorder: Detoxification, Drugs and Staying Sober: outline safe detoxification and the drugs that help people stop and stay stopped, and where the psychological treatments fit.
  5. 10.5 Opioids: From Opium to Heroin, and Their Treatment: tell the natural, semi-synthetic and synthetic opioids apart, recognise intoxication, overdose and withdrawal, and compare the treatments.
  6. 10.6 Stimulants, Tobacco and Caffeine: describe cocaine, the amphetamines, MDMA, nicotine and caffeine: how they act, what intoxication and withdrawal look like, and how dependence is treated.
  7. 10.7 Cannabis, Hallucinogens, Inhalants and the Sedative-Hypnotics: describe the effects and dangers of cannabis, the hallucinogens, inhalants, benzodiazepines and barbiturates, including cross-tolerance and dangerous withdrawal.
  8. 10.8 From Organic Brain Syndrome to Neurocognitive Disorder: The Cognitive Domains and Delirium: use the DSM-5-TR cognitive domains, recognise delirium, its types, signs and causes, and tell it apart from dementia.
  9. 10.9 Alzheimer's Disease and Mild Neurocognitive Disorder: describe the course, pathology, genetics and treatment of Alzheimer's disease and the idea of mild cognitive impairment.
  10. 10.10 The Other Neurocognitive Disorders: distinguish vascular, frontotemporal, Lewy body, Parkinson's, Huntington's, prion, HIV and traumatic brain injury neurocognitive disorders, and cortical from subcortical dementia.
  11. 10.11 Wernicke-Korsakoff Syndrome, Pseudodementia and Mental Disorders Due to Medical Illness: explain the alcohol-related amnestic syndrome, depressive pseudodementia and the psychiatric syndromes caused by endocrine, infectious and other medical illness.

Weightage. 36 of the 411 questions mapped in this book (8.8%) test this chapter, 23 of them from real past papers. 2 of its 11 sections are High yield.

Early in 1975 a new resident arrived at a home for the elderly near New York City. His transfer note was short and cryptic: "Helpless, demented, confused and disoriented." The neurologist who examined him, Oliver Sacks (1933 to 2015), found nothing of the kind on the surface. Jimmie G. was a handsome, healthy-looking man of forty-nine with a curly bush of grey hair, cheerful, friendly and warm. He talked with pleasure about his schooldays, his special fondness for mathematics and science, and above all the navy. He had been seventeen, just out of high school, when he was drafted in 1943, and he had served as an assistant radio operator on a submarine. He told these stories in vivid detail, and when he reached his navy days he slipped into the present tense, as if he were still living them. Then Sacks asked him what year it was. "Forty-five, man. What do you mean?" And how old was he? "Why, I guess I'm nineteen, Doc. I'll be twenty next birthday."

Sacks then tried an experiment. He handed Jimmie a mirror and asked whether that was a nineteen-year-old looking out of it. Jimmie turned ashen and gripped the sides of his chair. "Jesus Christ," he whispered. "Christ, what's going on? What's happened to me? Is this a nightmare? Am I crazy? Is this a joke?" Sacks soothed him, led him to the window to watch some children playing baseball on a lovely spring day, and slipped out of the room, taking the mirror with him. Two minutes later he came back. Jimmie was still at the window, smiling at the game, and he greeted Sacks cheerfully as a doctor he had never met. When Sacks laid his watch, his tie and his glasses on the desk, covered them, and after a minute's chat asked what was under the cover, Jimmie remembered none of them, nor even that he had been asked to remember. On intelligence testing he did excellently, and he could become keenly involved in a game; what he could not do was keep anything new for more than a minute or so. A diary was tried. He kept it dutifully but could not recognise his own earlier entries, though he knew the handwriting was his.

The navy records showed that Jimmie had stayed in the service until 1965 and had been perfectly competent then. The trouble began after he left. His drinking grew heavier in 1970, and around Christmas of that year, his brother understood, he had suddenly "blown his top", becoming deliriously excited and confused, and had been taken to Bellevue Hospital. Sacks's impression was Korsakoff's syndrome, the outcome of years of heavy drinking. Unsure what could be done, he wrote to the great Russian neuropsychologist Alexander Romanovich Luria (1902 to 1977). "There are no prescriptions," Luria replied, "in a case like this. Do whatever your ingenuity and your heart suggest. There is little or no hope of any recovery in his memory. But a man does not consist of memory alone. He has feeling, will, sensibilities, moral being", matters, Luria went on, of which neuropsychology cannot speak. When Sacks asked the Sisters at the home whether they thought Jimmie had a soul, they told him: "Watch Jimmie in chapel, and judge for yourself." In chapel Jimmie was wholly held and absorbed, and as he took Holy Communion he seemed to have a continuity that his memory could not give him. He found something similar in the garden, where he took over some of the work and almost never lost his way. Sacks published the story as "The Lost Mariner" in The New York Review of Books in February 1984, by which time he had known Jimmie for nine years and his memory had not changed at all, and it became a chapter of The Man Who Mistook His Wife for a Hat (1985).

Think about what happened to Jimmie. One drug, taken for years, and a vitamin that the drinking starved his brain of, left a clever, competent man stranded in 1945, unable to carry a single new experience forward into the next minute. That journey is the subject of this chapter. It begins with what drugs are, how the manuals classify the trouble they cause, and why people come to depend on them; it moves through alcohol, the opioids, the stimulants and the other drugs, with their intoxications, their withdrawals and their treatments; and it ends with what happens when the brain that holds a life together breaks down, in delirium, in the dementias, and in disorders like Jimmie's. Remember Jimmie G.; you will return to him at the end of the chapter.

10.1 Drugs, Dependence and Addiction: The Vocabulary and the ManualsMedium yield · 4 Qs

Few areas of psychiatry are as crowded with loaded words as this one. Drunkard, addict, junkie, abuser, charsi, bewda: every one of them carries a verdict about the person as well as a description of the behaviour. For most of history the heavy drinker was simply a sinner or a weakling, and the words reflected that. Science needs something different. A researcher comparing heroin users in Amritsar with heroin users in Glasgow, or a clinician deciding whether the man in front of her needs a brief word of advice or three weeks in hospital, needs terms that mean the same thing to everyone who uses them, and rules that say exactly when a pattern of use becomes a disorder. Much of the history of this field is the history of trying to find those words and those rules.

This section builds the vocabulary from the ground up, and then shows how the three classifications that you will meet in practice and in examinations, ICD-10, DSM-5-TR and ICD-11, turn it into diagnoses. It ends with the behavioural addictions (gambling and gaming) and with the numbers: how many people in the world and in India use which drugs. The theories of why people become dependent are the business of Section 10.2; the individual drugs fill Sections 10.3 to 10.7.

What counts as a drug, and the words for using one

Start with the word drug. In pharmacology a drug is any chemical agent that alters the biochemical or physiological processes of the body: aspirin, insulin and an antibiotic are all drugs. In everyday Indian and English speech the word has narrowed to mean an illegal substance ("he got into drugs"). Psychology and psychiatry are interested in a particular group of drugs, those that act on the brain and so change how a person feels, thinks and behaves. A psychoactive substance is any substance that, once taken into the body, alters mood, cognition, perception, consciousness or behaviour by acting on the central nervous system. The biopsychologists John P. J. Pinel and Steven J. Barnes put it in one line: psychoactive drugs are drugs that influence subjective experience and behaviour by acting on the nervous system. To do that a substance must reach the brain, which in practice means it must cross the blood-brain barrier, the tightly packed lining of the brain's blood vessels that keeps most bloodborne chemicals out.

Notice what the definition does not mention: legality, danger or medical use. Alcohol, nicotine and caffeine are psychoactive and legal; morphine, diazepam and methylphenidate are psychoactive and prescribed; heroin and cocaine are psychoactive and illegal. Pinel tells of the time he was invited to lecture a convention of high-school teachers on the dangers of drugs: the organisers first invited him to join them in a round of single-malt whisky, and he then gave his talk through a haze of cigarette smoke. The point of the story is that legal status says very little about safety: most drug laws were written in the early twentieth century, long before there was any research on the drugs they controlled. The word psychotropic overlaps with psychoactive, but in clinical use it usually refers to medicines prescribed to treat mental disorders, such as antidepressants and antipsychotics. These are psychoactive, yet most of them produce little pleasure and are rarely taken for their own sake, which is why they seldom appear among the drugs this chapter is about. When the manuals speak of a "substance", they mean three kinds of thing together: drugs of abuse, medications, and toxins such as solvents or insecticides.

Psychoactive substances can be grouped by their main effect. Depressants slow the central nervous system: alcohol, the sedative-hypnotics (benzodiazepines and barbiturates) and most inhalants. Stimulants speed it up: cocaine, the amphetamines, and in milder form nicotine and caffeine. Opioids (opium, morphine, heroin and their relatives) relieve pain and produce a dreamy euphoria. Hallucinogens (LSD, psilocybin, mescaline) distort perception. Cannabis fits awkwardly, with sedative, euphoriant and mildly hallucinogenic effects. This simple scheme is a good first map, and many Indian textbooks use it, but it is crude: nicotine both calms and arouses, alcohol is a stimulant at low doses for many people, and ketamine and phencyclidine (PCP) do not fit any box. That is why the manuals list drug classes by name rather than by effect, as you will see in Section 10.1.5.

Now the words for using. Substance use is simply taking a substance, whether a single experimental puff, a social drink at a wedding or a daily habit; use by itself is not a disorder. Misuse means use for a purpose or in a way that does not fit medical or legal guidelines, as when someone takes a relative's sleeping tablets, or swallows a whole bottle of codeine cough syrup for the high. Hazardous use (also called risky use) is a pattern that appreciably raises the risk of harm to the user or to others even though no harm has yet occurred: the man who drives home after five pegs every Saturday, or the student who drinks to the point of vomiting at every hostel party. Harmful use is use that has actually damaged physical or mental health, such as hepatitis from shared needles or a depressive episode brought on by heavy drinking. Finally, substance abuse is the American term that DSM-III (1980) and DSM-IV (1994) used for a maladaptive pattern of use causing problems but falling short of dependence. The World Health Organization (WHO) has long avoided the word "abuse", because it means different things to different people and carries a moral judgement, and DSM-5 dropped it as a diagnosis in 2013. Keep these four steps in mind (use, hazardous use, harmful use, dependence); the manuals are largely arguments about where to draw the lines between them.

The word that everyone uses, addiction, has had the most turbulent career of all. It comes from the Latin addictus, a person bound over or handed over, originally a debtor surrendered to his creditor, and the image of a person enslaved to a habit has clung to it ever since. In the 1950s the WHO tried to separate "drug addiction" (compulsion, escalating doses, physical dependence, harm to the individual and society) from a milder "drug habituation" (desire without compulsion and without physical dependence). Nobody could apply the distinction consistently, and in 1964 the WHO Expert Committee on Addiction-Producing Drugs recommended that both terms be replaced by a single one, drug dependence. "Dependence" duly became the official word in the ICD and in DSM-III and DSM-IV. Addiction did not go away, however. It survived in everyday speech, in the names of hospitals, journals and professional bodies, and in neuroscience, and in 2013 DSM-5 brought it back into the title of its chapter, "Substance-Related and Addictive Disorders", partly so that the chapter could hold gambling disorder. Even so, DSM-5-TR says plainly that "drug addiction" is not used as a diagnostic term, because its definition is uncertain and its connotations are potentially negative; the official, more neutral diagnosis is substance use disorder.

Exam trap

"Psychoactive" means a drug that alters mood, cognition, perception and/or behaviour by acting on the brain. The distractors are word games. "A drug-induced hallucination" describes one possible effect of some psychoactive drugs (the hallucinogens), not the meaning of the word. "A particularly active psychopath" plays on psychopathy, a personality construct (Chapter 11). "The psychology of physical activity" is exercise and sport psychology. Also remember the year the WHO replaced "addiction" and "habituation" with "dependence": 1964.

Intoxication, withdrawal and tolerance

Three things can happen when a psychoactive substance meets a brain repeatedly: the brain is changed for a few hours (intoxication), it adapts so that the drug does less (tolerance), and once it has adapted it protests when the drug is taken away (withdrawal). These three ideas are the building blocks of every diagnosis that follows, so it is worth taking them slowly.

Intoxication is a reversible, substance-specific syndrome caused by the recent intake of a substance. ICD-10 describes it as a transient condition following the intake of alcohol or another psychoactive substance, with disturbances of consciousness, cognition, perception, affect or behaviour. DSM-5-TR adds that for intoxication to count as a diagnosis there must be clinically significant problematic changes, such as belligerence, mood lability or impaired judgement, accompanied by the signs typical of that drug (slurred speech and an unsteady gait with alcohol, pinpoint pupils with opioids, dilated pupils and a racing heart with cocaine). The physiological sense of the word is wider than the diagnosis: a person whose heart races after three cups of strong filter coffee is, physiologically, intoxicated by caffeine, but if nothing else is wrong there is no disorder to diagnose. Intoxication is shaped by much more than the dose. The route matters (smoked or injected drugs reach the brain in seconds, swallowed ones in tens of minutes), so do tolerance, the person's expectations and the setting, and the same blood alcohol level can make one person merry and another aggressive. Intoxication usually ends when the drug is cleared, although some effects outlast it. DSM-5-TR does not recognise tobacco intoxication at all: nicotine at the doses smokers take does not produce a problematic syndrome of this kind.

Withdrawal is the substance-specific syndrome that appears when a person who has used a substance heavily and for a long time stops or cuts down, so that its level in the blood and tissues falls. Its most useful property is that it is, roughly, the mirror image of the drug's acute effects. As Pinel points out, withdrawal from anticonvulsant drugs tends to cause convulsions and withdrawal from sleeping pills causes insomnia. A sedative such as alcohol leaves behind an over-excited brain (tremor, sweating, anxiety, insomnia, in severe cases seizures and delirium); a stimulant such as cocaine leaves behind an exhausted, slowed and miserable one; an opioid, which constricts the pupils, dries the secretions and constipates, leaves behind dilated pupils, running eyes and nose, and diarrhoea. How bad withdrawal is depends on the drug, on the size and duration of the doses, and on how fast the drug leaves the body. DSM-5-TR spells out the rule of half-life: within a drug class, short-acting substances are more likely to produce withdrawal, and withdrawal from them starts sooner and is more intense; long-acting substances produce withdrawal that starts later, lasts longer and is milder. That is why heroin withdrawal begins within hours and methadone withdrawal only after a day or more, and why a long-acting drug is used to ease people off a short-acting one. The danger also varies enormously. Withdrawal from alcohol, barbiturates and benzodiazepines can kill through seizures and delirium (Sections 10.3 and 10.7); withdrawal from opioids is wretched but rarely dangerous in a healthy adult (Section 10.5).

Tolerance is a state of decreased sensitivity to a drug that develops as a result of exposure to it. It can be shown in two ways, and the manuals define it in both: a markedly increased amount of the substance is needed to achieve intoxication or the desired effect, or the same amount has a markedly diminished effect with continued use. Pinel's neat way of seeing this is as a shift of the dose-response curve, the graph of the size of a drug's effect at each dose, to the right (Figure 10.1). The young man whose first two pegs of whisky made him giddy now drinks half a bottle before he feels anything; the codeine syrup user who began with one bottle a week now needs three a day.

Tolerance has more than one mechanism. In metabolic tolerance (also called dispositional or pharmacokinetic tolerance) less of the drug reaches its site of action, because the body breaks it down faster: chronic drinking and barbiturate use, for example, induce the liver enzymes that metabolise these drugs. In functional tolerance (pharmacodynamic tolerance) the same amount of drug reaches the brain, but the brain responds less, because receptors have become fewer or less sensitive or the cells' responses to them have been damped down. Pinel's verdict is that tolerance to psychoactive drugs is largely functional. A third, more surprising kind is behavioural tolerance, tolerance that depends on learning. In studies of contingent tolerance, rats given alcohol before each of a series of convulsive stimulations became almost completely tolerant to alcohol's anticonvulsant effect, while rats given exactly the same injections after each stimulation developed no tolerance at all: tolerance developed only to effects the animals actually experienced. In conditioned tolerance, tolerance is fully expressed only in the setting where the drug is usually taken, which turns out to explain some otherwise baffling overdose deaths; that story, and the conditioned compensatory responses behind it, belong to the learning theories in Section 10.2.

Three further facts about tolerance matter clinically. First, tolerance can begin within a single session. Intoxication is greater while the blood alcohol level is rising than at the same level on the way down, so that a drinker can feel more sober two hours into an evening than his blood level warrants; this acute tolerance is often called the Mellanby effect. Second, tolerance develops to different effects of the same drug at different rates, a point DSM-5-TR makes with the example of tolerance to respiratory depression and to sedation developing at different speeds. Heroin users become tolerant to the euphoria faster than to the slowing of breathing, so chasing the old high pushes them steadily closer to a fatal dose. Third, tolerance is lost with abstinence, with deadly consequences for the person who returns to his old dose (see the Clinical link below). Do not confuse tolerance with ordinary differences in sensitivity: DSM-5-TR notes that some first-time drinkers show very little intoxication after three or four drinks while others of similar weight slur their speech. That inborn low sensitivity is not tolerance, although, as Section 10.2 shows, it predicts later alcohol problems.

Tolerance does not stop at the drug that produced it. Cross-tolerance is tolerance to one drug that carries over to other drugs, usually drugs that act by the same mechanism. Alcohol, the barbiturates and the benzodiazepines all enhance the action of the inhibitory transmitter GABA at the GABA-A receptor, so a heavy drinker is tolerant to diazepam and phenobarbitone as well, and anaesthetists know that alcohol-dependent patients may need larger doses of sedatives and anaesthetics. Cross-tolerance also links the opioids with one another, and the classic hallucinogens LSD, psilocybin and mescaline with one another. It has an important practical consequence. Because a benzodiazepine can stand in for alcohol, a long-acting benzodiazepine such as chlordiazepoxide or diazepam is used to carry a person safely through alcohol withdrawal and then tapered off, the standard detoxification method described in Section 10.4. It also has a dangerous one: cross-tolerance does not stop the effects of two depressants from adding together, and alcohol or benzodiazepines taken with opioids is one of the commonest recipes for a fatal overdose (Section 10.7).

The partner idea is cross-dependence: the ability of one drug to suppress the withdrawal syndrome produced by another and to maintain the physical dependence. Chlordiazepoxide prevents alcohol withdrawal; methadone and buprenorphine prevent heroin withdrawal; nicotine patches blunt the withdrawal of stopping cigarettes. Cross-tolerance and cross-dependence usually go together, and both rest on a shared mechanism of action. They are the pharmacological basis of detoxification and of substitution or maintenance treatment.

Repeated doses do not always weaken a drug's effect. Sensitisation, also called reverse tolerance, is an increase in the effect of a drug with repeated, usually intermittent, use: the dose-response curve moves to the left. Pinel describes the experiment by Stephan Anagnostaras and Terry E. Robinson (1996), in which ten injections of amphetamine, one every three or four days, greatly increased amphetamine's power to activate the movement of rats, but only when the rats were injected and tested in the same environment as before. In people, repeated cocaine or amphetamine use can produce paranoia, psychosis or seizures at doses that once caused only euphoria, and a leading theory of addiction holds that drugs sensitise the brain's "wanting" system (incentive sensitisation, Section 10.2). Be aware that "reverse tolerance" is also used, loosely, for something quite different: the late-stage drinker whose liver is so damaged that it can no longer metabolise alcohol, so that a man who once drank a bottle now becomes drunk on two pegs. That is a loss of metabolic tolerance, not sensitisation of the brain. Table 10.1 sets the confusable terms side by side.

Table 10.1 Five terms that examiners like to confuse.
TermWhat happensExample
ToleranceMore drug needed for the same effect, or less effect from the same doseA drinker needs half a bottle to feel what two pegs once gave him
Cross-toleranceTolerance to one drug extends to another, usually with the same mechanismAn alcohol-dependent man is tolerant to diazepam and to anaesthetics
Cross-dependenceOne drug suppresses the withdrawal of another and maintains the dependenceChlordiazepoxide for alcohol withdrawal; methadone for heroin
Sensitisation (reverse tolerance)Repeated, intermittent use increases a drug's effectParanoia or seizures with smaller and smaller doses of cocaine
WithdrawalA drug-specific syndrome, roughly opposite to its acute effects, on stopping after heavy useDilated pupils, yawning and diarrhoea after stopping heroin
Clinical link

Lost tolerance kills. The weeks after release from prison and after discharge from a detoxification unit are the most dangerous time of all for a person with opioid dependence, because the brain's tolerance has faded while the memory of the old dose has not. Good discharge planning therefore includes warning the patient (and the family) about this, and, where available, continuing substitution treatment and supplying the overdose antidote naloxone (Section 10.5).

Exam trap

Cross-tolerance means that exposure to one drug produces tolerance to another (usually related) drug, so it reduces the person's sensitivity to that second drug. The keyed option in one paper, "exposure to one drug reduces/changes tolerance to other drugs", is loosely worded (strictly, exposure to one drug produces tolerance to the other), but it is the only option that describes cross-tolerance, so choose it. "No addiction to any drug" is a meaningless distractor, and "both" therefore fails too. Do not mix it up with cross-dependence (one drug relieves the other's withdrawal) or with sensitisation (the effect grows, the opposite of tolerance). Metabolic tolerance is the liver's doing, functional tolerance the brain's, and Pinel says tolerance to psychoactive drugs is largely functional.

Dependence, addiction, craving and relapse

Tolerance and withdrawal are two faces of the same adaptation. Pinel suggests that exposure to a drug produces compensatory changes in the nervous system that offset its effects, and those changes are the tolerance; when the drug is suddenly removed, the compensatory changes are left with nothing to offset, and they show themselves as withdrawal effects opposite to the drug's own. A person who suffers a withdrawal syndrome on stopping a drug is said to be physically dependent on it. Physical dependence (physiological dependence) is therefore the adapted state of the body that reveals itself in withdrawal. Psychological dependence is the older name for a compelling need to keep using a drug, for its pleasure or to escape discomfort, whether or not there is any physical withdrawal. For decades textbooks used this pair to rank drugs, saying for example that cocaine produced "only psychological dependence" because its withdrawal lacked the dramatic bodily signs of heroin or alcohol withdrawal. The distinction has worn badly. Every dependence is ultimately a change in the brain, cocaine does have a withdrawal syndrome (Section 10.6), and the drugs with the mildest bodily withdrawal include some of the hardest to give up. DSM-IV still let clinicians specify whether dependence was "with physiological dependence", but the modern manuals put far more weight on the behaviour than on the withdrawal.

The most important point in this subsection is that physical dependence is not the same thing as addiction. A woman with cancer who has taken morphine for months will have withdrawal symptoms if it is stopped suddenly; she is physically dependent, but she is not addicted, because she does not crave the drug, seek it compulsively or use it in harmful ways, and once her pain is gone she can be tapered off it without difficulty. A baby born to a mother maintained on methadone may need treatment for withdrawal in the first days of life, yet nobody would call the baby an addict. Conversely, a person can be thoroughly addicted with little physical withdrawal at all, as in gambling or binge cocaine use. Pinel defines addiction by the behaviour: addicted people are habitual drug users who continue to use a drug despite its adverse effects on their health and social life, and despite repeated efforts to stop. The heart of it is compulsion and loss of control, not withdrawal. Pinel adds the decisive piece of evidence. If addiction were simply a matter of avoiding withdrawal, it could be cured by keeping people in hospital for a week or two until the withdrawal had passed; in fact most addicted people return to the drug even after months of enforced abstinence.

What drives that return? Part of the answer is craving, an intense desire or urge to use the drug. Craving can strike at any time, but it is most likely in the places, company and moods in which the drug used to be taken: the bar on the way home, the friend who always had a supply, the evening argument with the spouse. DSM-5-TR notes that craving involves classical conditioning and activates specific reward structures in the brain, and that clinicians often track it during treatment because a surge of craving can signal an impending relapse. A lapse is a single slip, one drink or one smoke after a period of abstinence; a relapse is a return to the old pattern of use. Whether a lapse becomes a relapse depends a great deal on how the person interprets it, which is the subject of relapse prevention (Book 2, Section 9.9.6). Because relapse is so common, many authorities describe substance dependence as a chronic, relapsing condition, more like diabetes or asthma than like a broken leg, to be managed over years rather than cured in a single admission.

Real patients rarely confine themselves to one drug. Polysubstance use, the use of more than one substance either at the same time or in sequence, is the rule rather than the exception: most heavy drinkers also smoke or chew tobacco, many heroin users also take benzodiazepines, and some mix heroin and cocaine. DSM-IV once had a diagnosis of "polysubstance dependence" for people who met dependence criteria for a group of drugs without meeting them for any single one; DSM-5 abolished it, and DSM-5-TR asks clinicians to diagnose each substance use disorder separately, naming the specific drug ("severe heroin use disorder; moderate cocaine use disorder"). ICD-10 keeps a code (F19) for multiple drug use when it is impossible to say which drug contributes most, and ICD-11 has categories for multiple specified and for unknown substances.

Behind all the modern definitions of dependence stands one short paper. The British psychiatrist Griffith Edwards (1928 to 2012) was born in India, trained at the Maudsley Hospital in London and in 1967 founded the Addiction Research Unit at its Institute of Psychiatry. While assessing patients for a series of alcohol research studies, he noticed that certain features kept turning up together.

Key study: Edwards and Gross describe the alcohol dependence syndrome (1976)

On 1 May 1976 Edwards and the American researcher Milton M. Gross published "Alcohol dependence: provisional description of a clinical syndrome" in the British Medical Journal. Instead of asking whether someone "was an alcoholic", they described a dependence syndrome, a cluster of related features that can be present to a greater or lesser degree, so that dependence has a range of severity. Its seven elements are: (1) narrowing of the drinking repertoire, so that the person drinks the same drink, in the same way, at the same times, whatever the day or the occasion; (2) salience of drink-seeking behaviour, as drinking takes priority over family, work, health and money; (3) increased tolerance to alcohol; (4) repeated withdrawal symptoms; (5) repeated relief or avoidance of withdrawal by further drinking, as with the morning "eye-opener"; (6) subjective awareness of a compulsion to drink, felt as loss of control or craving; and (7) reinstatement of the syndrome after abstinence, so that a dependent drinker who starts again returns to the full old pattern within days rather than months. Edwards and his colleagues also insisted that dependence should be kept separate from the physical, psychological and social damage that drinking causes (the alcohol-related problems or disabilities): a person can have serious problems without dependence, and dependence without many problems yet. That two-axis idea is why ICD-10 has separate categories for harmful use and for dependence. The syndrome was taken into ICD-9, DSM-III-R, ICD-10 and DSM-IV, and its elements are still recognisable in the DSM-5-TR criteria and in ICD-11's three features of dependence. The criticism is that the syndrome rested on clinical observation rather than measurement, and that how much of each element counts was left to clinical judgement; the American psychiatrist Marc Schuckit, reviewing its influence in 1995, noted that five of the seven elements (salience, tolerance, withdrawal, relief drinking and the sense of compulsion) are fairly easy to apply, but that the other two, narrowing of the repertoire and rapid reinstatement, are harder to define objectively.

Here is what the syndrome looks like in a real life. Lalit is a 41-year-old medical representative in Lucknow. He began drinking beer with friends at weekends at 22. By his early thirties he was drinking whisky every evening, and over the next few years his drinking became fixed: the same brand, three or four large pegs, starting at the same hour whether it was a working day or a holiday (narrowing of the repertoire). Evenings with his children, his morning walks and much of his salary gave way to drinking (salience). Four pegs now do what one did in his twenties (tolerance). He wakes shaky, sweating and nauseous (withdrawal), and for a year he has kept a quarter bottle in his bag for a "small one" before his first doctor's visit of the day (relief drinking). He knows he cannot stop at two once he has started (compulsion). Last year, frightened by a liver test, he stopped completely for two months, then had "just one" at a cousin's wedding, and within a week he was back to his full evening quota and his morning drink (reinstatement). Lalit has every element of the dependence syndrome. You will meet him again in the next two subsections, because the clearest way to understand the manuals is to push one patient through all of them.

Exam trap

The dependence syndrome was described by Griffith Edwards and Milton Gross in 1976 (British Medical Journal), for alcohol; it is not Jellinek's disease concept (1960) or Kandel's gateway stages (1975), both of which are in Section 10.2. Physical dependence (a withdrawal syndrome on stopping) is not addiction: the cancer patient on morphine and the newborn of a mother on methadone are physically dependent but not addicted. A lapse is one slip; a relapse is a return to the old pattern.

ICD-10: the F1 block and its clinical patterns

The tenth revision of the WHO's International Classification of Diseases (ICD-10; its chapter on mental and behavioural disorders appeared with clinical descriptions in 1992) is still the system used in many Indian hospitals and in most Indian examination papers, and its way of handling substances is elegant. All the disorders sit in one block, F10 to F19, "Mental and behavioural disorders due to psychoactive substance use" (the place of the block in the ICD as a whole is described in Chapter 1, Section 1.9). Every code is built from two pieces of information. The third character names the substance (Table 10.2), and the fourth character, after the decimal point, names the clinical condition (Table 10.3). So F10.2 is alcohol dependence syndrome, F11.3 is opioid withdrawal state, and F12.5 is a psychotic disorder due to cannabis. Learn the two lists separately and you can build any code.

Table 10.2 The ten substance codes of ICD-10 (third character).
CodeDisorders due to use ofCodeDisorders due to use of
F10AlcoholF15Other stimulants, including caffeine
F11OpioidsF16Hallucinogens
F12CannabinoidsF17Tobacco
F13Sedatives or hypnoticsF18Volatile solvents
F14CocaineF19Multiple drug use and use of other psychoactive substances
Table 10.3 The ICD-10 clinical conditions (fourth character), with examples from this chapter.
CodeClinical conditionExample
.0Acute intoxicationDrunkenness; pathological intoxication is F10.07 (Section 10.3)
.1Harmful useRepeated binges causing gastritis, without dependence
.2Dependence syndromeLalit's drinking
.3Withdrawal stateTremor and sweating on the first dry morning; .31 with convulsions
.4Withdrawal state with deliriumDelirium tremens (Section 10.3)
.5Psychotic disorderAlcoholic hallucinosis; amphetamine psychosis
.6Amnesic syndromeKorsakoff's syndrome (Section 10.11)
.7Residual and late-onset psychotic disorderHallucinogen flashbacks; lasting personality change; alcohol-related dementia
.8Other mental and behavioural disordersDisorders not covered above
.9Unspecified mental and behavioural disorderNot enough information to say more

Three of these conditions need more than a line. Harmful use (F1x.1) is a pattern of use that is actually causing damage to health, physical (as in hepatitis from injecting) or mental (as in a depressive episode secondary to heavy drinking). ICD-10 attaches three careful rules to it. The damage must be real and clinically recognisable, not merely a risk. The fact that a pattern of use is disapproved of by the family or the culture, or has led to socially negative consequences such as an arrest or marital quarrels, is not by itself evidence of harmful use. And harmful use is not diagnosed if a dependence syndrome is present: dependence takes precedence. Notice what these rules exclude. A college student who has twice been arrested after drunken brawls, but whose health is unharmed, has no ICD-10 diagnosis of harmful use, although, as you will see, his recurrent legal problems would have qualified him for substance abuse in DSM-IV.

The dependence syndrome (F1x.2) is ICD-10's version of Edwards and Gross. ICD-10 describes it as a cluster of physiological, behavioural and cognitive phenomena in which the use of a substance takes on a much higher priority for the person than other behaviours that once had greater value, and its central descriptive feature is the desire, often strong and sometimes overpowering, to take the drug. A definite diagnosis requires three or more of six features, experienced or shown at some time during the previous year:

  1. A strong desire or sense of compulsion to take the substance.
  2. Difficulty in controlling substance-taking behaviour: its onset, its termination, or the amount taken.
  3. A physiological withdrawal state when use is stopped or reduced, or use of the same or a closely related substance to relieve or avoid withdrawal.
  4. Evidence of tolerance.
  5. Progressive neglect of alternative pleasures or interests, with more and more time spent obtaining, taking or recovering from the substance.
  6. Persisting with use despite clear evidence of overtly harmful consequences, such as liver damage, depressed mood after heavy use, or drug-related impairment of thinking, when the person is, or could be expected to be, aware of the harm.

ICD-10 also names the narrowing of the personal repertoire of use as a characteristic feature, a direct inheritance from Edwards and Gross. A fifth character then describes the person's current state: currently abstinent (F1x.20), abstinent but in a protected environment such as a hospital or prison (.21), on a clinically supervised maintenance or replacement regime such as methadone (.22), abstinent but taking aversive or blocking drugs such as disulfiram or naltrexone (.23), currently using (.24), with continuous use (.25), or with episodic use (.26, the old "dipsomania" of periodic binges). Lalit, drinking every evening and showing all six features, would be coded F10.24 (currently using) or, more precisely, F10.25 (continuous use). If he were admitted shaking after a seizure on his second dry day, the withdrawal would be coded as well (F10.31, withdrawal state with convulsions).

The withdrawal state (F1x.3) and the withdrawal state with delirium (F1x.4) follow, and delirium tremens is the classic F10.4 (Section 10.3). The psychotic disorder (F1x.5) covers psychotic symptoms that arise during or soon after use and are not simply part of intoxication or withdrawal, such as alcoholic hallucinosis or amphetamine psychosis (Chapter 4, Section 4.5.6). The amnesic syndrome (F1x.6) is Korsakoff's syndrome, Jimmie G.'s disorder, taught in Section 10.11. The residual and late-onset category (F1x.7) collects lasting effects that persist beyond the period in which the drug could be acting directly: flashbacks after hallucinogens, lasting personality change, and dementia related to alcohol or other substances.

Two codes outside the F1 block complete the ICD-10 picture. F55, "abuse of non-dependence-producing substances", covers the repeated, unjustified use of substances such as laxatives, analgesics, antacids, vitamins, steroids and herbal or folk remedies, which do not produce dependence of the F1 kind; it is the one place in ICD-10's chapter on mental and behavioural disorders where the word "abuse" appears in a diagnosis title. And pathological gambling is F63.0, not in the substance block at all but among the habit and impulse disorders (Section 10.1.7). One more source of confusion: the United States uses its own clinical modification, ICD-10-CM, in which the F1x.1 codes are relabelled "abuse" (F10.10 is "alcohol abuse, uncomplicated"), and DSM-5-TR codes a mild substance use disorder there. That is an American coding convenience. In the WHO's ICD-10, which Indian textbooks follow, F1x.1 is harmful use.

Exam trap

Asked which is NOT one of the ICD-10 patterns of disorder due to substance use, choose "drug abuse". Acute intoxication (.0), harmful use (.1), dependence syndrome (.2), withdrawal state (.3) and withdrawal with delirium (.4) are ICD-10 categories; "abuse" was the DSM-III and DSM-IV category, and ICD-10's nearest term is harmful use. The dependence syndrome needs 3 of 6 features at some time in the previous year. Social disapproval, an arrest or a marital quarrel is not by itself evidence of harmful use.

DSM: from abuse and dependence to substance use disorder

The American manual took a long road to its present position. DSM-I (1952) listed alcoholism and drug addiction under "sociopathic personality disturbance", treating them as signs of a defective character, and DSM-II (1968) still placed them among the personality disorders and other non-psychotic conditions. DSM-III (1980) gave the substance disorders a class of their own and introduced the pair of diagnoses that ruled for the next three decades: substance abuse and substance dependence. DSM-III-R (1987) rebuilt dependence around the Edwards-Gross syndrome, and DSM-IV (1994) gave the pair its final shape.

In DSM-IV, substance abuse required one or more of four problems within a 12-month period: recurrent use resulting in failure to fulfil major obligations at work, school or home; recurrent use in physically hazardous situations, such as driving while intoxicated; recurrent substance-related legal problems, such as arrests for disorderly conduct; and continued use despite persistent social or interpersonal problems caused or made worse by the substance. Abuse could be diagnosed only if the person had never met the criteria for dependence on that class of substance. Substance dependence required three or more of seven criteria in the same 12-month period: tolerance; withdrawal; taking the substance in larger amounts or for longer than intended; a persistent desire or unsuccessful efforts to cut down; a great deal of time spent obtaining, using or recovering; important activities given up or reduced; and continued use despite knowing that it was causing or worsening a physical or psychological problem. The clinician could add "with physiological dependence" if tolerance or withdrawal was present. The logic was that abuse was a milder, earlier stage and dependence the full disorder, with dependence trumping abuse in a hierarchy.

Research gradually took that logic apart. The DSM-5 Substance-Related Disorders Work Group, whose analysis was published by Deborah S. Hasin, Charles P. O'Brien and colleagues in 2013, drew on studies of more than 200,000 participants and found several problems. Abuse was not reliably a stage on the way to dependence: many people developed dependence without ever meeting the abuse criteria. The abuse diagnosis was much less reliable than the dependence diagnosis. The one-criterion threshold for abuse meant that a single criterion, very often repeated drinking before driving, was enough for a diagnosis. At the same time it created diagnostic orphans, people who met two of the dependence criteria but none of the abuse criteria and therefore received no diagnosis at all, although they were often in more trouble than people diagnosed with abuse. Most tellingly, statistical analyses of how the criteria hang together showed that the abuse and dependence criteria were intermixed along a single dimension of severity rather than forming two separate disorders. The work group therefore recommended combining them into one disorder, dropping the legal problems criterion, adding craving, adding withdrawal syndromes for cannabis and caffeine, bringing the tobacco criteria into line with those for other substances, and moving gambling into the chapter.

The result, in DSM-5 (2013) and unchanged in substance in DSM-5-TR (2022), is a single diagnosis. Substance use disorder is a problematic pattern of use of a substance leading to clinically significant impairment or distress, shown by at least two of eleven criteria occurring within a 12-month period. The manual itself groups the eleven into four families (Figure 10.2), and learning them as families is the easiest way to remember them.

  • Impaired control (criteria 1 to 4). The person takes the substance in larger amounts or over a longer period than intended ("I only went for one"); has a persistent desire to cut down, or makes repeated unsuccessful efforts; spends a great deal of time obtaining the substance, using it or recovering from it, so that in severe cases the whole day revolves around it; and feels craving, an intense desire or urge to use.
  • Social impairment (criteria 5 to 7). Recurrent use leads to failure to fulfil major obligations at work, school or home; use continues despite persistent social or interpersonal problems caused or worsened by it, such as quarrels with the spouse about money or behaviour; and important social, occupational or recreational activities are given up or reduced.
  • Risky use (criteria 8 and 9). The person uses repeatedly in situations where it is physically hazardous, such as riding a motorcycle or operating machinery while intoxicated, and continues despite knowing that use has caused or worsened a persistent physical or psychological problem. DSM-5-TR stresses that the key to criterion 9 is not the existence of the problem but the failure to abstain in spite of it: the drinker with a damaged liver who keeps drinking, the cocaine user who keeps using despite panic attacks.
  • Pharmacological criteria (criteria 10 and 11): tolerance and withdrawal, defined as in Section 10.1.2. Withdrawal counts either as the characteristic withdrawal syndrome or as taking the substance (or a closely related one) to relieve or avoid withdrawal. Neither is necessary for the diagnosis. Withdrawal is not listed at all for phencyclidine, the other hallucinogens and inhalants, because significant withdrawal has not been documented for them, although DSM-5-TR notes that a past history of withdrawal generally goes with a more severe course.

Table 10.4 shows where each criterion came from. Three of the four DSM-IV abuse criteria survived; legal problems were dropped because few people endorsed them, they added almost no information once the other criteria were known, and they depend heavily on local law (in a state with prohibition, buying a single bottle can bring an arrest). Craving was added because the evidence for it was strong and because ICD-10 already counted a strong desire or compulsion to use.

Table 10.4 Where the DSM-5-TR criteria came from.
DSM-5-TR criterionIn DSM-IV it was part ofDSM-5-TR group
1 Larger amounts or longer than intendedDependenceImpaired control
2 Desire or failed efforts to cut downDependenceImpaired control
3 Much time spentDependenceImpaired control
4 CravingNot in DSM-IV (new)Impaired control
5 Failure to fulfil major rolesAbuseSocial impairment
6 Continued use despite social problemsAbuseSocial impairment

Severity is graded by the number of criteria: mild with two or three, moderate with four or five, and severe with six or more. The threshold of two was chosen partly because it kept the overall prevalence close to that of DSM-IV abuse and dependence combined. Change over time can also be tracked through the frequency and amount of use (days of use a month, standard drinks a day). Two course specifiers describe recovery. Early remission means that, after the full criteria were once met, none of them has been met for at least 3 months but less than 12 months; sustained remission means that none has been met for 12 months or longer. In both, craving is the one criterion allowed to persist, a realistic concession, since craving can outlast years of abstinence. Two further specifiers describe circumstances. "In a controlled environment" is added when the person is in a setting where access to the substance is restricted, such as a closely supervised jail, a therapeutic community or a locked ward, because abstinence there says less about recovery. "On maintenance therapy" applies to opioid use disorder (a person stable on methadone or buprenorphine, or on the antagonist naltrexone) and to tobacco use disorder (a person on long-term nicotine replacement), as long as no criteria are met apart from tolerance to, or withdrawal from, the maintenance medicine.

Return to Lalit and count. He drinks more than he means to once he starts (1); he has tried and failed to stop (2); his evenings and mornings are organised around drinking (3); he craves a drink at the end of a working day (4); he has been warned at work for missing appointments after heavy nights (5); his wife has threatened to leave over his drinking, and he still drinks (6); he has given up his walks and his children's school functions (7); he rides his scooter home after drinking (8); he continues despite an abnormal liver test (9); he is tolerant (10); and he has withdrawal and drinks to relieve it (11). Even if you are cautious and accept only seven of these, he has a severe alcohol use disorder, because six or more is severe. Under DSM-IV he would have had alcohol dependence with physiological dependence; under ICD-10, alcohol dependence syndrome (F10.2).

One rule protects patients from a serious mistake. Symptoms of tolerance and withdrawal that occur during appropriate medical treatment with prescribed medicines, such as opioid painkillers, sedatives or stimulants, are specifically not counted towards a substance use disorder. DSM-5-TR explains why: the normal, expected tolerance and withdrawal of medical treatment have led to erroneous diagnoses of "addiction" when these were the only symptoms present. The rule does not mean that prescribed drugs can never be the object of a disorder. When compulsive, drug-seeking behaviour appears, the diagnosis can be made.

Clinical link

Deepa, a 52-year-old schoolteacher in Nagpur, was prescribed alprazolam by her family doctor after her husband died, and has taken 0.5 mg at night for six years. The tablet no longer helps her sleep as it once did, and when she ran out during a holiday she had two nights of rebound insomnia and jitteriness. On these facts alone, tolerance and withdrawal from a medicine taken as prescribed, she does not have a sedative use disorder, and labelling her an "addict" would be both wrong and harmful. The picture changes if she begins taking three tablets a night, obtains extra prescriptions from a second doctor without telling the first, and keeps taking them after a fall caused by drowsiness. Then she shows impaired control and risky use as well, and a diagnosis (recorded by the specific drug, as alprazolam use disorder) is justified. Her case is also a reminder that long-term benzodiazepine prescription is one of the commonest routes into dependence (Section 10.7).

Alongside the use disorders, DSM-5-TR recognises the substance-induced disorders, a group that contains three things: substance intoxication, substance withdrawal, and the substance/medication-induced mental disorders. The last are syndromes that look like other mental disorders but are caused by the physiological effects of a substance on the brain, whether a drug of abuse, a medicine (corticosteroids are a well-known example) or a toxin such as an organophosphate insecticide. There are substance-induced psychotic, bipolar, depressive, anxiety, obsessive-compulsive, sleep, sexual, delirium and neurocognitive disorders, each described in its own chapter. They share a set of common features: the symptoms developed during or soon after intoxication, withdrawal or exposure to a medicine; the substance involved is capable of producing them; and they are not better explained by an independent mental disorder. Evidence of an independent disorder would be that the symptoms began before the substance use, or persisted for a substantial period, about a month, after acute withdrawal or severe intoxication ended. In general, sedating drugs (alcohol and the sedative-hypnotics) produce depressive syndromes during intoxication and anxiety during withdrawal, while stimulants produce psychotic and anxiety syndromes during intoxication and depression during withdrawal. This is why an alcohol-dependent patient who is depressed on admission is usually reassessed after a few weeks of abstinence before an independent depressive disorder is diagnosed (Section 10.3).

Finally, DSM-5-TR organises all of this by drug, in ten classes. Its reason for ten is practical rather than pharmacological, and it admits that the classes "are not fully distinct". What the classes share, the manual says, is that all drugs taken in excess directly activate the brain's reward system, the circuit of Section 10.2. Table 10.5 shows which diagnoses exist for which class, and its gaps are favourite examination material.

Table 10.5 The ten DSM-5-TR substance classes and the diagnoses each can receive.
ClassUse disorderIntoxicationWithdrawalNotes
AlcoholYesYesYesWithdrawal can be fatal (Section 10.3)

A few recording conventions complete the picture. The clinician records the name of the specific drug rather than the class ("moderate alprazolam use disorder", not "moderate sedative use disorder"). If criteria are met for more than one substance, each disorder is diagnosed. Substances that fit no class, such as anabolic steroids, go under "other (or unknown)", and so does a substance that cannot be identified. Caffeine use disorder, internet gaming disorder and a few others sit in Section III of DSM-5-TR, "Conditions for Further Study", which means they are research proposals, not official diagnoses.

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR substance use disorder: 2 or more of 11 criteria within 12 months; mild 2 to 3, moderate 4 to 5, severe 6 or more. Craving was added and legal problems were dropped; abuse and dependence were merged. Tolerance and withdrawal from medicines taken as prescribed are not counted. Caffeine is the one class with no use disorder in the main text; tobacco has no intoxication diagnosis; phencyclidine, other hallucinogens and inhalants have no withdrawal diagnosis. "Addiction" appears in the chapter title, "Substance-Related and Addictive Disorders", but is not a diagnosis.

ICD-11: disorders due to substance use or addictive behaviours

The eleventh revision of the ICD was approved by the World Health Assembly in May 2019 and came into effect on 1 January 2022. Its mental disorders chapter (Chapter 6) contains a block called "Disorders due to substance use or addictive behaviours", with two parts: disorders due to substance use, and disorders due to addictive behaviours (gambling and gaming, Section 10.1.7). The substance part keeps the ICD-10 idea of a substance code plus a clinical condition, but it updates the list of substances and redraws the boundary between harm and dependence.

The list of substances grows from the nine specific classes of ICD-10 to fourteen (Table 10.6). The newcomers reflect what people around the world now actually take: synthetic cannabinoids (the laboratory-made "spice" products), synthetic cathinones (the "bath salts" related to the khat leaf chewed in East Africa and Yemen), MDMA and related drugs such as MDA, and dissociative drugs including ketamine and phencyclidine. Caffeine, which ICD-10 lumped with the other stimulants, gets its own code, and ICD-11 speaks of nicotine rather than tobacco and of volatile inhalants rather than volatile solvents. There are also categories for other specified substances (including medicines), for multiple specified substances, for unknown substances, and for non-psychoactive substances such as laxatives, growth hormone and erythropoietin, which can be harmful but produce no intoxication, dependence or withdrawal; this last category covers much of the ground of ICD-10's F55.

Table 10.6 The substance classes of ICD-11 (Chapter 6).
CodeDisorders due to use ofCodeDisorders due to use of
6C40Alcohol6C49Hallucinogens
6C41Cannabis6C4ANicotine
6C42Synthetic cannabinoids6C4BVolatile inhalants
6C43Opioids6C4CMDMA or related drugs, including MDA
6C44Sedatives, hypnotics or anxiolytics6C4DDissociative drugs including ketamine and phencyclidine
6C45Cocaine6C4EOther specified psychoactive substances, including medications
6C46Stimulants including amphetamines, methamphetamine or methcathinone6C4FMultiple specified psychoactive substances
6C47Synthetic cathinones6C4GUnknown or unspecified psychoactive substances
6C48Caffeine6C4HNon-psychoactive substances

For each substance ICD-11 offers a parallel ladder of diagnoses. Alcohol shows the pattern: 6C40.0 episode of harmful use, 6C40.1 harmful pattern of use, 6C40.2 dependence, 6C40.3 intoxication, 6C40.4 withdrawal, and then the alcohol-induced mental disorders (6C40.5 delirium, 6C40.6 psychotic disorder, and others such as mood and anxiety disorders). Three steps in this ladder are new or changed.

The first is the episode of harmful use (often called a single episode of harmful use), a category ICD-10 did not have. It is a single episode of use that has damaged a person's physical or mental health, or has led to behaviour that harmed the health of others, in a person who has no known pattern of harmful use or dependence. The young man who, on his first big night of drinking, crashes his motorcycle and injures his pillion rider qualifies. The category exists so that emergency departments can record and respond to such episodes, which are an opportunity for brief intervention, without attaching a label of disorder to a first event. The second is the harmful pattern of use, the successor of ICD-10's harmful use: a pattern of use that has caused damage to the person's physical or mental health, or has resulted in behaviour leading to harm to the health of others. The pattern must be evident over at least 12 months if use is episodic, or at least one month if use is continuous (daily or almost daily), and it can be specified as episodic or continuous. Notice the new phrase in both definitions, harm to the health of others. ICD-10 counted only damage to the user; ICD-11 recognises that the drunk driver's victims, and the family of a violent drinker, are harmed by his use as well.

The third is dependence itself. ICD-11 describes it as a disorder of regulation of substance use arising from repeated or continuous use, whose characteristic feature is a strong internal drive to use. ICD-10's six features are folded into three pairs, and a diagnosis needs at least two of the three:

  1. Impaired control over use, in its onset, level, circumstances or termination, often but not necessarily with a subjective sense of urge or craving.
  2. Increasing precedence of use over other aspects of life, so that use takes priority over other interests, daily activities, responsibilities, and health or personal care, and continues despite problems.
  3. Physiological features showing neuroadaptation: tolerance, withdrawal after stopping or reducing use, or repeated use to prevent or relieve withdrawal. The withdrawal must be the characteristic syndrome for that substance, not merely a hangover.

The features are usually evident over at least 12 months, but dependence can be diagnosed sooner if use has been continuous (daily or almost daily) for at least three months. As in ICD-10, there is a hierarchy: dependence takes precedence over a harmful pattern of use, which takes precedence over a single episode. Lalit, with impaired control, drink taking precedence over his family and health, and tolerance and withdrawal, has all three features: ICD-11 alcohol dependence, 6C40.2.

ICD-11 also reaches one step further down the ladder than any earlier manual. Hazardous use of a substance, a pattern that appreciably increases the risk of harmful physical or mental consequences for the user or others, but has not yet caused harm, is coded in Chapter 24, "Factors influencing health status or contact with health services", as hazardous alcohol use (QE10), hazardous drug use (QE11) and hazardous nicotine use (QE12). These are not mental disorders. They are risk factors, coded so that a primary-care doctor can record, and act on, the patient who drinks too much but has not yet come to harm. The same chapter has codes for hazardous gambling or betting (QE21) and hazardous gaming (QE22). Figure 10.3 puts the four classifications side by side.

Remember

Push one patient through all the manuals and the differences become clear. Lalit is ICD-10 alcohol dependence syndrome (F10.2: 3 of 6 in the past year), DSM-IV alcohol dependence (3 of 7), DSM-5-TR severe alcohol use disorder (6 or more of 11) and ICD-11 alcohol dependence (6C40.2: 2 of 3). The student twice arrested after drunken brawls, with no other problem, had DSM-IV alcohol abuse (recurrent legal problems) but has no ICD-10 diagnosis. In DSM-5-TR the legal criterion no longer exists, so he needs two other criteria before he has any disorder. In ICD-11, if his brawls injured other people, the harm to others could count towards an episode of harmful use, or a harmful pattern of use if they recur over a year.

Exam trap

ICD-11 dependence needs 2 of 3 features (impaired control, increasing precedence, physiological features), usually over 12 months, or 3 months if use is continuous. ICD-10 dependence needs 3 of 6. ICD-11's new categories are the single episode of harmful use and the harmful pattern of use (12 months if episodic, 1 month if continuous), both of which count harm to others. Hazardous use is not a mental disorder in ICD-11: it is a Chapter 24 risk factor (QE10 to QE12). The new substance classes are synthetic cannabinoids, synthetic cathinones, MDMA, dissociative drugs and caffeine on its own.

Behavioural addictions: gambling disorder and gaming disorder

If addiction is defined by compulsion and loss of control rather than by a chemical, then there is no reason in principle why a behaviour should not be addictive. The phrase behavioural addiction is used for repetitive, rewarding behaviours that show the same core pattern as substance dependence: impaired control, increasing priority over the rest of life, continuation despite harm, and often something like tolerance (needing more of the behaviour for the same thrill) and withdrawal (restlessness and irritability when it is stopped). The obvious danger is that the idea can be stretched to anything people enjoy a great deal, from chocolate to cricket. The manuals have therefore admitted behavioural addictions one at a time, and only when the evidence was strong.

The first to be admitted was gambling. Pathological gambling entered DSM-III in 1980 as an impulse-control disorder, and ICD-10 placed it among the habit and impulse disorders (F63.0), beside kleptomania and pyromania. DSM-5 renamed it gambling disorder and moved it into the addictions chapter, reflecting evidence that gambling activates reward systems similar to those activated by drugs and produces behavioural symptoms comparable to those of the substance use disorders. At the same time the threshold was lowered from five of ten criteria to four, and one criterion, committing illegal acts to finance gambling, was removed. Gambling disorder is the only behavioural addiction in the main text of DSM-5-TR. Its diagnosis requires persistent and recurrent problematic gambling leading to clinically significant impairment or distress, with four or more of nine features in a 12-month period:

  1. Needs to gamble with increasing amounts of money to achieve the desired excitement (the gambling equivalent of tolerance).
  2. Is restless or irritable when trying to cut down or stop (the equivalent of withdrawal).
  3. Has made repeated unsuccessful efforts to control, cut back or stop.
  4. Is often preoccupied with gambling: reliving past wins, planning the next bet, thinking of ways to get money for it.
  5. Often gambles when feeling distressed (helpless, guilty, anxious, depressed).
  6. After losing money, often returns another day to get even, which is called chasing losses.
  7. Lies to conceal the extent of the gambling.
  8. Has jeopardised or lost a significant relationship, job, or educational or career opportunity.
  9. Relies on others to provide money to relieve desperate financial situations caused by gambling (a "bailout").

The gambling must not be better explained by a manic episode, which can produce reckless betting of its own. The course can be specified as episodic or persistent, remission as early or sustained (using the same 3-month and 12-month rules as for substances), and severity as mild (4 to 5 criteria), moderate (6 to 7) or severe (8 to 9). Chasing is the hallmark: many gamblers chase a loss for an evening, but the person with gambling disorder chases frequently and for years, placing larger bets to win everything back at once. Preoccupation and chasing are the criteria most often endorsed in mild cases, and jeopardised relationships and bailouts mostly in severe ones. Distorted thinking is typical: superstitions, a sense of control over chance events ("I can feel when the card is coming"), and the belief that money is both the cause of and the cure for the person's problems. DSM-5-TR puts the past-year prevalence at about 0.2 to 0.3 percent of the general population of the United States, and notes one striking cause: gambling disorder can appear as a direct effect of the dopamine-boosting medicines used to treat Parkinson's disease, and such cases are still diagnosed as gambling disorder.

Gambling in India has changed shape in the smartphone era. Satta, lotteries, card games at Diwali and betting on cricket matches have long existed, but online rummy, poker and real-money fantasy sports reached millions of young people within a few years, with the round-the-clock access, rapid repeat play and instant digital payments that make any gambling more dangerous. In August 2025 Parliament passed the Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Act, which bans online money games (games played for money stakes, whether of skill, of chance or of both), their advertising, and the processing of payments for them by banks and payment systems, while promoting e-sports and online social games. The Act and its rules came into force on 1 May 2026.

Gaming without money stakes is the second behavioural addiction, and here the two manuals part company. Consider Tenzin, a 20-year-old engineering student in Bengaluru who grew up in the Tibetan settlement at Bylakuppe. For the past fourteen months he has played an online multiplayer battle game for nine or ten hours a day, often until four in the morning. He misses most morning classes, has failed two semester papers, has stopped playing football, which he once loved, and lies to his parents about how long he plays. When his roommates switched off the router to make him study, he became so irritable and restless that he moved to a cyber cafe. He has tried several times to limit himself to two hours a day and has never managed more than three days.

In DSM-5-TR, Tenzin's problem has a name but no official diagnosis. Internet gaming disorder sits in Section III, "Conditions for Further Study": its proposed criteria require five or more of nine features in 12 months (preoccupation, withdrawal symptoms such as irritability, anxiety or sadness when gaming is taken away, tolerance in the form of more and more time spent, unsuccessful attempts to control it, loss of other interests, continued excessive gaming despite knowing the problems, deceiving others about the amount, gaming to escape a negative mood, and jeopardising a relationship, job or education). The proposal covers only non-gambling internet games; internet gambling belongs to gambling disorder, and ordinary social media use, work on the computer and sexual websites are excluded. The DSM-5 work group reviewed more than 240 articles and found real similarities to gambling and substance disorders, but judged that the lack of a standard definition and of good natural-history data made it premature to make the condition official.

ICD-11 took the step that DSM-5 did not. Gaming disorder (6C51) is a full diagnosis in the block of disorders due to addictive behaviours, alongside gambling disorder (6C50). It is a persistent or recurrent pattern of gaming, online or offline, with three features: impaired control over gaming (its onset, frequency, intensity, duration, termination and context); increasing priority given to gaming, to the extent that it takes precedence over other interests and daily activities; and continuation or escalation of gaming despite negative consequences. The pattern must cause marked distress or significant impairment in personal, family, social, educational, occupational or other important areas, and it is normally evident over at least 12 months, although the period can be shortened if all the requirements are met and the symptoms are severe. It can be specified as predominantly online (6C51.0) or predominantly offline (6C51.1), and gambling disorder can be specified in the same way. Tenzin meets all of this: ICD-11 gaming disorder, predominantly online. The WHO stresses that only a small minority of people who play video games develop the disorder, and that, as with alcohol, hazardous gaming (QE22) can be recorded before it becomes a disorder. In India, the first dedicated clinic for problems with technology use, the Service for Healthy Use of Technology (SHUT) clinic at NIMHANS in Bengaluru, opened in 2014, and gaming is its commonest reason for referral.

What about the other "addictions" of everyday speech: shopping, sex, exercise, work, food, the internet in general? DSM-5-TR says plainly that these are not included, because there is insufficient peer-reviewed evidence to establish diagnostic criteria and course descriptions. ICD-11 does recognise a compulsive sexual behaviour disorder (6C72), a persistent failure to control intense, repetitive sexual impulses or urges, but it deliberately places it among the impulse control disorders, with pyromania, kleptomania and intermittent explosive disorder, not among the addictive behaviours (Chapter 11). Table 10.7 summarises where gambling and gaming have lived in each manual.

Table 10.7 Gambling and gaming across the classifications.
ManualGamblingGaming
ICD-10 (1992)Pathological gambling (F63.0), among habit and impulse disordersNot listed
DSM-IV (1994)Pathological gambling, an impulse-control disorder; 5 of 10 criteriaNot listed
DSM-5-TR (2022)Gambling disorder, in "Substance-Related and Addictive Disorders"; 4 of 9 in 12 monthsInternet gaming disorder, Section III only; 5 of 9 in 12 months proposed
ICD-11 (2022)Gambling disorder (6C50), disorders due to addictive behavioursGaming disorder (6C51), same block; usually 12 months; online or offline
Exam trap

ICD-11 places gaming disorder (6C51), with gambling disorder (6C50), in "disorders due to substance use or addictive behaviours". It is not under the disorders specifically associated with stress (post-traumatic stress disorder, complex PTSD, prolonged grief disorder, adjustment disorder), not under the mood disorders, and not under the impulse control disorders. That last option tempts because pathological gambling was an impulse disorder in ICD-10 (F63.0) and DSM-IV, and because ICD-11 does put compulsive sexual behaviour disorder there. In DSM-5-TR, gambling disorder is the only behavioural addiction; internet gaming disorder is a condition for further study, not an official diagnosis.

How common: the world and India

How many people use drugs? The United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC) publishes the best global estimates each year in its World Drug Report, and they deliberately leave out alcohol and tobacco. The 2026 report estimated that 331 million people used a drug at least once in 2024, about 6.2 percent of the world's population aged 15 to 64, up from 5.2 percent a decade earlier. Cannabis was by far the most widely used drug, with about 256 million users, followed by the opioids (63 million), the amphetamines (32 million), cocaine (25 million) and ecstasy (21 million). Treatment reaches only a small minority: about one in nine men and one in 23 women with a drug use disorder were in treatment. Alcohol and tobacco, which are legal almost everywhere, are used by far more people than all the illegal drugs together, and they do far more damage to health; their figures are given in Sections 10.3 and 10.6.

For India, the key source is the report Magnitude of Substance Use in India, commissioned by the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment and carried out between December 2017 and October 2018 by the National Drug Dependence Treatment Centre (NDDTC) of the All India Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS), New Delhi, in a team led by Atul Ambekar; it was released in February 2019. The previous national survey, conducted with the UNODC in 2000 to 2001 and published in 2004, had interviewed only men and had given no state-level estimates. The 2018 survey combined two methods. A household survey covered the general population aged 10 to 75 in all 36 states and union territories: 200,111 households in 186 districts were visited and 473,569 people interviewed. Because household surveys miss most people who are dependent on illegal drugs, a second survey used respondent-driven sampling (each person interviewed recruits others from the same hidden population) to reach 72,642 people dependent on illicit drugs in 135 districts. The survey studied alcohol, cannabis, opioids, cocaine, amphetamine-type stimulants, sedatives, inhalants and hallucinogens; it did not study tobacco. Its main national findings are in Table 10.8.

Table 10.8 Current use of psychoactive substances in India among people aged 10 to 75 (Magnitude of Substance Use in India, 2019).
SubstanceCurrent useKey points
Alcohol14.6% (about 16 crore people)17 times more common in men; 5.2% (over 5.7 crore) have harmful or dependent use
Cannabis2.8% in the past year (3.1 crore)Bhang 2%, ganja or charas 1.2%; about 0.66% (72 lakh) need help
Opioids2.06% (about 2.26 crore)Heroin 1.14%, pharmaceutical opioids 0.96%, opium 0.52%; about 0.7% (77 lakh) with opioid use disorders
Sedatives (non-medical)1.08% (about 1.18 crore)Highest prevalence in Sikkim, Nagaland, Manipur and Mizoram
Inhalants1.17% of children and adolescents; 0.58% of adultsThe only class used more by children than adults
Cocaine, amphetamine-type stimulants, hallucinogens0.10%, 0.18%, 0.12%The least used categories

Several of these numbers deserve a second look. Alcohol was by a long way the most used substance (tobacco aside), then cannabis, then the opioids. Among the alcohol users, country liquor ("desi") and Indian Made Foreign Liquor (IMFL) were each the main drink of about 30 percent, and roughly one in every three drinkers needed help for alcohol-related problems. Among the opioids, heroin had overtaken the traditional opium, and more people were dependent on heroin than on opium or pharmaceutical opioids. Inhalants, the glue, petrol and correction fluid that cost a few rupees, were the only substances more often used by children than by adults, and an estimated 4.6 lakh children needed help for inhalant use. Nationally there were about 8.5 lakh people who inject drugs, mostly injecting heroin or pharmaceutical opioids, and many reported risky injecting practices. Finally, the treatment gap was enormous: only about one in 38 people with alcohol dependence had received any treatment, and only about one in 180 had ever been admitted to hospital for it.

The picture also varies enormously from one region to another. The states with the highest prevalence of alcohol use were Chhattisgarh, Tripura, Punjab, Arunachal Pradesh and Goa, and alcohol use disorders exceeded 10 percent of the population in Tripura, Andhra Pradesh, Punjab, Chhattisgarh and Arunachal Pradesh. Cannabis use was above the national level in Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, Sikkim, Chhattisgarh and Delhi. More than half of all the people with opioid use disorders lived in a handful of states (Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, Haryana, Maharashtra, Madhya Pradesh, Delhi and Andhra Pradesh), but as a proportion of the population the worst affected were the north-eastern states of Mizoram, Nagaland, Arunachal Pradesh, Sikkim and Manipur, together with Punjab, Haryana and Delhi. Punjab, on the heroin route from across the north-western border, and the north-east, close to the opium-producing region where Myanmar, Laos and Thailand meet and long troubled by injecting drug use and the HIV infection that spreads with shared needles, are the two regions most often discussed, but no part of the country is untouched.

The National Mental Health Survey of India (2015 to 2016), carried out by NIMHANS among adults in twelve states, gives the psychiatric view. Mental and behavioural problems due to psychoactive substance use, including tobacco, affected about 22.4 percent of adults, making them the largest group of disorders in the survey. Tobacco use disorder (moderate and high dependence) alone affected about 20.9 percent, alcohol use disorder about 4.6 percent (9.1 percent of men and 0.5 percent of women) and illicit drug use disorders about 0.6 percent. Two lessons follow. Tobacco, which the 2019 survey did not measure, is the commonest substance problem of all in India (Section 10.6).
