And alcohol problems in India are concentrated overwhelmingly among men, which means that the burden falls, very often, on their wives and children.

Indian context

India's main drug law is the Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances (NDPS) Act, 1985, which brought India's law into line with the United Nations drug conventions. It controls narcotic drugs (opium, poppy straw, coca, cannabis and their products) and listed psychotropic substances, and punishes their cultivation, production, possession, sale and transport, with penalties graded by quantity; consumption itself is an offence under Section 27. Section 64A offers immunity from prosecution to a person charged with consumption or possession of a small quantity who volunteers for de-addiction treatment, a recognition that dependence is a health problem as well as a crime. One quirk matters for exam questions about cannabis: the Act defines cannabis as charas (the resin, including hashish oil) and ganja (the flowering or fruiting tops, excluding the seeds and leaves when not accompanied by the tops). Bhang, made from the leaves, therefore falls outside the Act's definition, and its sale is regulated instead by state excise laws, which is why licensed bhang shops can operate in some states.

Exam trap

In the 2019 national survey (people aged 10 to 75, tobacco not studied) the order of current use was alcohol (14.6%), cannabis (2.8%), opioids (2.06%); among opioids, heroin led, then pharmaceutical opioids, then opium. Inhalants were the only class used more by children than adults. The survey was done by the NDDTC, AIIMS, New Delhi, for the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, not by NIMHANS, which ran the National Mental Health Survey (2015 to 2016). Bhang is outside the NDPS Act's definition of cannabis; ganja and charas are inside it.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. A drug that alters mood, cognition, perception or behaviour by acting on the brain is called a substance.
  2. Tolerance that results from faster breakdown of a drug by the liver is tolerance; tolerance that results from a reduced response of the brain is functional tolerance.
  3. A heavy drinker who is also tolerant to diazepam shows .
  4. The ability of methadone to suppress heroin withdrawal is an example of .
  5. The alcohol dependence syndrome was described in the British Medical Journal in 1976 by Griffith Edwards and .
  6. In ICD-10, the fourth character .1 (as in F10.1) stands for , and .2 for dependence syndrome.
  7. A DSM-5-TR substance use disorder with four or five criteria in 12 months is graded .
  8. The one DSM-IV abuse criterion dropped from DSM-5 was recurrent problems; the new criterion added was craving.
  9. ICD-11 dependence requires at least of its three central features.
  10. ICD-11 places gaming disorder (6C51) in the block "disorders due to substance use or ".

Answers: (1) psychoactive, (2) metabolic, (3) cross-tolerance, (4) cross-dependence, (5) Milton Gross, (6) harmful use, (7) moderate, (8) legal, (9) two, (10) addictive behaviours.

10.2 Why People Become Addicted: Reward, Learning, Genes and PersonalityMedium yield · 4 Qs

Stand outside a liquor shop in any Indian town at seven in the evening and you are looking at the puzzle this section tries to solve. Most of the men in the queue will drink tonight, go to work tomorrow and never meet the criteria for a substance use disorder that Section 10.1 laid out. A few will. Some of those few began drinking at the same age, with the same friends and at the same weddings as the men who stayed out of trouble. Exposure to a drug is necessary for addiction, but it is nowhere near sufficient. So why do some users lose control while most do not? Why do they go on using when the drug no longer gives them much pleasure and is wrecking their lives? Why do they return to it after months of abstinence? And why do some drugs, tobacco above all, capture so many of the people who try them?

Kuldeep is 36 and runs a tractor-repair workshop on the outskirts of Jalandhar. His father drank heavily, was jailed after a brawl when Kuldeep was ten and died of liver disease at 52. Kuldeep had his first drink at 14, from his uncles' bottle at a cousin's wedding, and liked it at once: "For the first time in my life I was not restless." By 18 he was drinking most evenings with the young men who gathered at the workshop after closing time, and by 22 he had been in two fights and had one police case against him. Today he drinks most of a bottle of country liquor every day. He has stopped many times, once for four months after a frightening talk with his doctor, but on the first evening he sat with his old friends at the workshop he "only had one", and within a week he was back to a bottle a day. "Doctor sahib," he says, "the first glass does nothing for me now. I drink so that my hands stop shaking and my mind stops troubling me."

Every theory in this section claims a piece of Kuldeep. The disease model calls him an alcoholic who has lost control over drinking; the brain researcher points to a reward circuit that now wants alcohol more than it enjoys it; the learning theorist points to the workshop at closing time, a place that has become a signal for drink; the geneticist points to his father; the personality researcher points to the restless, novelty-seeking boy; the psychoanalyst points to his mouth; and the sociologist points to the cheap liquor, the friends and the culture of drinking around him. The section takes these explanations in roughly the order they appeared historically, and ends by fitting them together.

From sin to sickness: the moral model and Jellinek's disease concept

For most of history, heavy drinking was treated as a moral failing. In the moral model of addiction, the drunkard chooses to drink and could choose not to; drunkenness is a sin or a weakness of character, and the proper responses are blame, punishment and exhortation. The model still shapes how families, police and courts treat people with addictions, and it is one reason so many hide their problem until it is far advanced. In the nineteenth century the moral view drove the temperance movement, which began in Britain and the United States as a campaign for moderation and hardened into demands for total abstinence and then for prohibition by law. Its greatest victory was national Prohibition in the United States from 1920 to 1933, a period that cut drinking but also handed the liquor trade to organised crime. India had its own temperance tradition. Gandhi saw liquor as a scourge of the poor; picketing liquor shops was part of the Civil Disobedience movement of 1930, and the Constitution of India, in Article 47 of the Directive Principles of State Policy, directs the State to endeavour to bring about the prohibition of intoxicating drinks and harmful drugs except for medicinal purposes. Which states have tried prohibition, and with what results, is taken up in Section 10.3.

Alongside the moralists, a few physicians had begun to call habitual drunkenness a disease. The American physician Benjamin Rush (1746 to 1813), a signer of the Declaration of Independence, described the habitual drinking of ardent spirits (distilled liquor) in 1784 as a progressive disease, and argued that the only cure was to give spirits up entirely. The Scottish naval physician Thomas Trotter treated drunkenness as a disease in his Essay on Drunkenness of 1804, and in 1849 the Swedish physician Magnus Huss coined the word "alcoholism" (alcoholismus chronicus) for the bodily damage done by long drinking. But the idea that alcoholism is a disease only became the dominant view in the twentieth century, and the man most responsible for it was a biostatistician, not a physician.

Elvin Morton Jellinek (1890 to 1963), known to friends as "Bunky", worked at the Yale Center of Alcohol Studies and later for the World Health Organization. His first contribution was a description of the stages through which drinking problems were thought to progress. In 1945 the magazine of Alcoholics Anonymous (AA), the self-help fellowship of recovering alcoholics, circulated a questionnaire to members asking when each drinking problem had first appeared. Jellinek analysed just 98 replies, chosen from the small number returned, and published "Phases in the drinking history of alcoholics" in 1946. In 1952 he refined it into four phases of alcohol addiction: a prealcoholic phase of social drinking that increasingly serves to relieve tension; a prodromal phase announced by blackouts (which he called alcoholic palimpsests), secret drinking, gulping and guilt; a crucial phase marked by loss of control, in which any drink sets off a chain of drinking the person cannot stop; and a chronic phase of prolonged binges, moral and physical deterioration, impaired thinking, tremor and finally the admission of defeat. The U-shaped chart that still hangs on the walls of many de-addiction centres, a descent through these phases on the left and a climb back through recovery on the right, is called the Jellinek curve, but the recovery arm was added by the British psychiatrist Max Glatt in the 1950s, and Jellinek later distanced himself from the chart.

Jellinek's great book, The Disease Concept of Alcoholism (1960), went further. Drawing on clinical reports from France, Britain, Germany and the United States, he argued that there was not one alcoholism but several, which he named with Greek letters and called "species". Table 10.9 sets them out. The key idea is that only two of them, gamma and delta, involve the bodily changes of true addiction (increased tissue tolerance, adaptation of cell metabolism and withdrawal symptoms), and Jellinek reserved the word disease for those two. They differ in the way control fails. The gamma alcoholic shows loss of control: he can stay dry for days or weeks, but once he starts drinking he cannot stop until he is drunk or ill. This was the type Jellinek found among AA members and in the spirit-drinking English-speaking countries, and it became the model of "the alcoholic" in the popular mind. The delta alcoholic shows the opposite failure, inability to abstain: he rarely gets drunk and can control how much he drinks on any one occasion, but he cannot go without alcohol for even a day or two without withdrawal. Jellinek's example was the French wine drinker who takes two or three litres of wine through the day, is seldom visibly intoxicated and has never had a sober day in years. Gamma alcoholism, in his account, grew mainly out of psychological vulnerability; delta out of social and economic custom.

Table 10.9 Jellinek's species of alcoholism (1960). Only gamma and delta were, in his view, diseases.
SpeciesCore featureDependenceJellinek's notes
AlphaDrinking to relieve emotional or bodily pain; breaks social rules but no loss of controlPsychological only"Problem drinking"; can develop into gamma
BetaPhysical damage (cirrhosis, neuritis, gastritis) from heavy drinkingNoneCustom and poor nutrition, not addiction
GammaTolerance, withdrawal and loss of control once drinking startsPsychological, then physicalThe AA type; common in English-speaking, spirit-drinking countries
DeltaTolerance, withdrawal and inability to abstain, with little drunkennessPhysical, then psychologicalThe steady wine drinker of France
EpsilonPeriodic bouts of very heavy drinking (dipsomania) between sober spellsNot described in detailToo little known, he said, to describe in detail

Why did this idea matter so much? Because it moved the alcoholic from the jail to the hospital. If alcoholism is a disease, the alcoholic is a patient, not a sinner; he deserves treatment, hospitals can admit him and insurers can pay for him. The disease concept underpinned AA's teaching that the alcoholic is powerless over alcohol and must never drink again, and it helped persuade the medical profession to take addiction seriously.

It also drew heavy fire. Jellinek's phases rested on 98 hand-picked questionnaires from one self-help group, filled in from memory; when Marc Schuckit and his colleagues studied 636 alcohol-dependent men in treatment in 1993, they did find a typical sequence (heavy drinking escalating in the late twenties, interference with work and family in the early thirties, then a sense of loss of control, then worsening social, job and physical problems in the mid to late thirties), but it was a general pattern reconstructed from the ages at which problems first appeared, not a fixed series of phases through which every drinker must pass. Loss of control, the heart of the gamma type, turned out to be less absolute than AA believed. In 1962 the British psychiatrist D. L. Davies reported that 7 of 93 men treated for alcohol addiction at the Maudsley Hospital had been drinking normally for years afterwards, and the experiments on expectancy described in Section 10.2.4 showed that alcoholics who were given alcohol without knowing it did not lose control at all. In 1988 the philosopher Herbert Fingarette attacked the whole idea in Heavy Drinking: The Myth of Alcoholism as a Disease, arguing that heavy drinking is a way of life, shaped by choices and circumstances, rather than a disease that strikes. The modern successor of Jellinek's view is the brain disease model, stated most famously by Alan Leshner, then director of the US National Institute on Drug Abuse, in a 1997 article in Science titled "Addiction is a brain disease, and it matters". Its evidence is the brain research described next. Its critics point out that most people with a substance use disorder recover, often without any treatment and often in their twenties as adult responsibilities arrive, which is not how most chronic brain diseases behave. Today's manuals sidestep the argument: DSM-5-TR speaks of substance use disorders graded by severity (Section 10.1), not of a disease with species.

Exam trap

Gamma means loss of control (cannot stop once started; the AA, Anglo-American type). Delta means inability to abstain (cannot go a day without, rarely drunk; the French wine type). Do not swap them. Alpha is psychological reliance without loss of control, beta is physical damage without dependence, and epsilon is periodic binge drinking (dipsomania). Some revision notes call epsilon "the most advanced" form; the species are types, not stages, and Jellinek thought too little was known about epsilon to describe it in detail. His phases (prealcoholic, prodromal, crucial, chronic; 1946 and 1952) are a different scheme from his species (1960). The disease concept book is 1960; the U-shaped "Jellinek curve" was completed by Max Glatt.

The brain's reward circuit

The first scientific theories of addiction were built on withdrawal. According to the physical-dependence theory of addiction, a person who has used a drug long enough to become physically dependent is trapped in a vicious circle: every attempt to cut down brings withdrawal, and withdrawal drives the person back to the drug. If that were the whole story, the cure would be simple. Take the person into hospital, withdraw the drug gradually until the withdrawal syndrome has passed, and send home someone who no longer needs it. Early treatment programmes did exactly that, and they failed. Almost every detoxified user returned to the drug soon after discharge. The theory had two further problems. Some of the most addictive drugs, cocaine and the amphetamines, produce only mild physical withdrawal; and many users live in a cycle of binges and enforced detoxification (a weekend binge, a dry week when the money runs out), which shows that clearing the body of the drug does not clear the mind of the wish for it. As John Pinel tells the story in his textbook of biopsychology, these failures pushed researchers in the 1970s and 1980s towards positive-incentive theories of addiction: people take drugs not mainly to escape withdrawal but to obtain the drug's anticipated pleasure, its positive-incentive value. If that is so, the place to look is the brain's machinery for pleasure, and that machinery had been found, by accident, twenty years earlier.

In the early 1950s James Olds (1922 to 1976), a young American psychologist trained at Harvard, arrived as a postdoctoral fellow in the laboratory of Donald Olding Hebb (1904 to 1985) at McGill University in Montreal, where a graduate student, Peter Milner, was assigned to teach him how to implant electrodes in a rat's brain. Olds was aiming for the reticular formation of the midbrain, which was then thought to control arousal. He was new to the technique, and in his first rat the electrode ended up far forward of its target, near the septal area. When Olds switched on a weak current while the rat explored a table top, he noticed something odd: if the rat was stimulated when it happened to be in one corner, it kept coming back to that corner, and if he moved the stimulation to another spot, the rat moved its preference too. The stimulation was acting as a reward. Olds and Milner then put rats in a Skinner box in which pressing a lever delivered a brief pulse of current to the brain, and in 1954 they reported that rats would press, and press, for the stimulation alone. The phenomenon, intracranial self-stimulation (ICSS), turned out to be spectacular at some sites: rats would press a lever thousands of times an hour, stopping only when they were exhausted. The sites soon became known as "pleasure centres". Olds and Milner argued that they were the circuits that normally carry the pleasure of natural rewards such as food, water and sex, and mapping them became a research programme of its own.

The map that emerged is centred on dopamine. The mesotelencephalic dopamine system is a set of dopamine-releasing neurons whose cell bodies lie in two small nuclei of the midbrain (mesencephalon) and whose axons project forward to the telencephalon (Figure 10.4). Neurons from the substantia nigra project mainly to the dorsal striatum (the caudate and putamen); this nigrostriatal pathway is the one that degenerates in Parkinson's disease (Section 10.10) and it is concerned with movement and with habits. Neurons from the ventral tegmental area (VTA) project to the prefrontal cortex, the amygdala, the hippocampus, the septum and, above all, the nucleus accumbens, the main part of the ventral striatum; this is the mesocorticolimbic pathway. Its two limbs are usually named separately: the mesolimbic dopamine pathway from the VTA to the nucleus accumbens and other limbic structures, and the mesocortical pathway from the VTA to the prefrontal cortex. (The same pathways reappear in the dopamine hypothesis of schizophrenia in Chapter 4, Section 4.8.) Self-stimulation rises when dopamine agonists are given and falls with dopamine antagonists, lesions of the mesocorticolimbic pathway disrupt it, and it is accompanied by dopamine release along the pathway. The pleasure centres were, for the most part, a dopamine circuit.

How do you show that a drug acts on this circuit? Laboratory work on addiction relies on two methods. In the drug self-administration paradigm, an animal presses a lever to inject a drug into its own bloodstream, or directly into one part of its brain, through an implanted tube; animals readily learn to self-administer almost every drug to which humans become addicted. In the conditioned place preference paradigm, an animal repeatedly receives a drug in one of two distinctive compartments of a box; later, drug-free, it is allowed to wander between them, and the time it chooses to spend in the drug-paired compartment measures how rewarding the drug was. Both methods pointed to the nucleus accumbens. Rats will self-administer addictive drugs straight into the accumbens; microinjections there produce a place preference; lesions of the accumbens or of the VTA block both effects; and self-administered drugs, like food and sex, raise the level of dopamine outside the cells of the accumbens.

Key study: Di Chiara and Imperato's microdialysis experiment (1988)

At the University of Cagliari in Sardinia, Gaetano Di Chiara and Assunta Imperato used brain microdialysis, a fine probe that samples the fluid between neurons, to measure dopamine in freely moving rats. They compared two dopamine targets: the nucleus accumbens (a limbic area) and the dorsal caudate (a motor area). Drugs that humans abuse (opiates, alcohol, nicotine, amphetamine and cocaine) all raised extracellular dopamine in both areas, but especially in the accumbens. Drugs with unpleasant effects (kappa opioid agonists) lowered dopamine, and drugs that people do not abuse (the antidepressant imipramine, the anticholinergic atropine and the antihistamine diphenhydramine) left it unchanged. The conclusion, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences in 1988, was that raising dopamine in the mesolimbic system might be a fundamental property shared by drugs of abuse, however different their chemistry.

The drugs reach this common end by very different routes, which the drug sections of this chapter describe one by one. Cocaine blocks the reuptake of dopamine and the amphetamines force its release (Section 10.6). Nicotine excites VTA neurons directly through their nicotinic acetylcholine receptors (Section 10.6). Opioids act indirectly: VTA dopamine neurons are held in check by inhibitory GABA neurons, and opioids silence those GABA neurons, releasing the brake (Section 10.5). Alcohol, the benzodiazepines and cannabis also raise accumbens dopamine indirectly, through GABA, opioid and cannabinoid systems (Sections 10.3 and 10.7). The convergence is real, but two cautions are needed. Other transmitters, including the endogenous opioids, serotonin, noradrenaline and glutamate, play their own parts in reward; and most of this research used stimulants. When heroin was studied as carefully, lesions of the mesocorticolimbic pathway and dopamine antagonists that disrupted cocaine self-administration did not disrupt heroin self-administration, so the "one dopamine pathway for all drugs" view is a useful approximation rather than a law.

A deeper problem soon appeared. If dopamine simply meant pleasure, then addicted people should enjoy their drug more and more. They do not. Kuldeep's first glass "does nothing" for him, yet he wants it more than ever, and many heroin and cocaine users say the same: the drug has lost its magic but the craving has grown. The incentive-sensitisation theory of Terry Robinson and Kent Berridge (1993) explains this by separating two things that everyday language runs together. "Liking" is the pleasure actually felt when the drug is taken (its hedonic value). "Wanting" is the motivational pull of the drug and of everything that predicts it (its incentive salience, or positive-incentive value). Robinson and Berridge argued that mesolimbic dopamine carries wanting, not liking; liking depends on small "hedonic hotspots" that use opioids and other transmitters. With repeated use, in vulnerable people, the dopamine system becomes sensitised, more and more reactive to the drug and to the cues that signal it, while tolerance blunts the liking. The result is a person who craves intensely a drug he no longer much enjoys. The theory also explains why drug cues are so powerful: a stimulus that predicts a drug is enough on its own to trigger dopamine release in the accumbens, and the sensitised system turns that cue into an urge. And because sensitisation develops only in some users, it offers one answer to the question of why some people move from occasional to habitual use and others do not.

A second change makes drug use automatic. Barry Everitt and Trevor Robbins of Cambridge summarised a decade of animal work in 2005 under the title "from actions to habits to compulsion". Early drug taking is a goal-directed action: the person drinks in order to get a valued outcome, and the behaviour depends on the ventral striatum. With long repetition, control shifts towards the dorsal striatum, the structure that stores stimulus-response habits. The behaviour is now triggered by its cues whether or not the outcome is still wanted, just as you reach for a light switch in a dark room without deciding to. When the habit persists even in the face of punishment, it has become compulsion, the state DSM-5-TR describes as continued use despite knowledge of harm. At the same time the brakes weaken. Brain imaging by Rita Goldstein and Nora Volkow showed that in addicted people the prefrontal cortex, especially the orbitofrontal and anterior cingulate cortex, is underactive at rest yet overreacts to drug cues; they called the result impaired response inhibition and salience attribution (2002). Volkow's group also found reduced numbers of dopamine D2 receptors in the striatum of people addicted to cocaine, alcohol, methamphetamine and heroin, one reason why ordinary pleasures (food, sex, company, a good film) feel flat. This anhedonia persists long after the drug is stopped and pulls the abstinent user back. Are these brain differences caused by drugs, or were they there first? Probably both: in 2012 Karen Ersche and her colleagues found some of the same abnormalities in the non-using brothers and sisters of people dependent on stimulants, which suggests an inherited vulnerability that drug use then deepens.

The third change is the darkest. From 1997 onwards George Koob and Michel Le Moal argued that repeated drug use does not only sensitise the reward system; it also recruits the brain's stress and "anti-reward" systems, centred on the extended amygdala and the stress hormone corticotropin-releasing factor. The person's resting mood is pushed downwards, so that between doses he feels anxious, irritable and joyless, a shifted set point they later called allostasis. Koob and Volkow (2010) then described addiction as a repeating three-stage cycle: binge and intoxication (driven by the basal ganglia), withdrawal and negative affect (the extended amygdala) and preoccupation and anticipation, or craving (the prefrontal cortex). The second stage is the neural version of the opponent-process theory you will meet in Section 10.2.3, and it explains Kuldeep's own account of why he drinks now: not for pleasure, but so that his hands stop shaking and his mind stops troubling him.

Exam trap

The circuit most consistently altered in substance use disorders is the mesolimbic dopamine system: the ventral tegmental area projecting to the nucleus accumbens (ventral striatum), with the prefrontal cortex. The distractors are real structures with other jobs. The cerebellar vermis coordinates the trunk and gait (it does shrink in heavy drinkers, giving a broad-based, staggering walk, but that is alcohol toxicity, not the shared circuit of addiction). The lateral geniculate nucleus of the thalamus relays vision from the retina to the visual cortex. The vestibular nuclei of the brainstem handle balance and the reflexes that keep the eyes steady when the head moves. Also note: dopamine signals wanting, not simply pleasure or liking (Robinson and Berridge), and habitual use shifts control from the ventral to the dorsal striatum (Everitt and Robbins).

Learning: reinforcement, conditioning and the opponent process

Long before anyone measured dopamine, learning theorists had their own answer to the puzzle of addiction: drug taking is learned behaviour, acquired and maintained by the same laws as any other. The reward circuit is, after all, the machinery of reinforcement. Seen through the lens of operant conditioning, a drug is a powerful reinforcer, and it reinforces in two ways.

In positive reinforcement, a behaviour becomes more likely because it is followed by something pleasant: the rush of cocaine, the warmth of heroin, the relaxed talkativeness of the second drink. The evidence that drugs are positive reinforcers in their own right, apart from any social meaning, comes from animals. Rats and monkeys with a lever that delivers intravenous cocaine, amphetamine, opiates, alcohol or sedatives will learn to press it without any training beyond the drug itself. Given unlimited access, they can harm themselves badly. When Michael Bozarth and Roy Wise (1985) allowed rats to self-administer intravenous drugs around the clock for 30 days, the heroin rats kept grooming and kept their weight, but the cocaine rats took the drug in frenzied binges, stopped grooming, lost up to 47 percent of their body weight, and 90 percent of them died (against 36 percent of the heroin rats).

In negative reinforcement, a behaviour becomes more likely because it removes or prevents something unpleasant. Aspirin is a negative reinforcer: you take it not because it makes you feel good but because it stops your headache. Many drugs relieve withdrawal, physical pain, anxiety, loneliness or low mood, and each relief strengthens the habit. Kuldeep's drink taken to stop his hands shaking is negative reinforcement in its plainest form. The most influential version of this idea for alcohol is the tension-reduction hypothesis, formally proposed by the American psychologist John Conger in 1956 on the basis of earlier animal work, including his own experiments in which alcohol reduced the fear-driven avoidance of animals placed in conflict. The hypothesis has two parts: alcohol reduces tension, and people therefore drink more when they are tense. Both parts turned out to be only partly true. Alcohol does calm some people in some situations at some doses, but at other doses and later in the drinking episode it increases anxiety, and many people drink most when they are happy and celebrating, not when they are stressed. The idea survives in a more specific form as the self-medication hypothesis (Section 10.2.7).

One more operant principle explains why the long-term costs of drinking do so little to stop it. Reinforcement works best when it is immediate. The relief of a drink arrives in minutes; the cirrhosis, the lost job and the broken marriage arrive months or years later, uncertain and spread thin. People with addictions are especially prone to discount delayed consequences in favour of immediate ones, a pattern that laboratory choice tasks measure as steep delay discounting.

Classical (Pavlovian) conditioning adds a second layer. Every time a drug is taken, the stimuli that reliably come just before it (the place, the time of day, the friends, the bottle, the syringe, even the person's own mood) are paired with the drug's effects, and they become conditioned stimuli. The American psychiatrist Abraham Wikler (1910 to 1981), working at the federal narcotics hospital in Lexington, Kentucky, noticed in 1948 that former opioid addicts who had been drug-free for months would begin to yawn and shed tears, signs of opioid withdrawal, when they talked about drugs in group therapy. He called this conditioned withdrawal and proposed that drug-related cues, through Pavlovian learning, come to evoke a withdrawal-like state and craving, which the person then relieves by using; because such conditioned responses fade slowly, the risk of relapse outlasts any detoxification.

Clinical link: why Hamid relapsed on the way home

Hamid, a 29-year-old tailor from Bhopal, had smoked and later injected "brown sugar" (impure heroin) for six years. He completed six weeks in a de-addiction ward, felt well and was sure that this time he was finished with the drug. On the walk home from the bus stand he passed the lane where he used to buy. Within a minute his eyes were watering, he was yawning, his stomach cramped and he felt "the same restlessness as the first day in the ward". By evening he had used. Nothing in his body had changed since that morning; what had changed was the place. The lane had become a conditioned stimulus for withdrawal and craving, exactly as Wikler described. This is why relapse prevention plans map a person's high-risk places, people and moods (Book 2, Section 9.9.6), and why the most dangerous moment for overdose is the first use after detoxification, when tolerance has faded but the habit has not (see the next paragraphs and Section 10.5).

Conditioning also explains a strange feature of tolerance. Shepard Siegel, working at McMaster University in Canada, proposed that the body learns to anticipate a drug. If a drug lowers body temperature, slows the heart or depresses breathing, then the cues that regularly predict the drug come to elicit the opposite response in advance, a conditioned compensatory response that prepares the body for the drug's arrival and partly cancels its effect. Much of tolerance, on this view, is learned, and it should therefore be strongest in the usual setting. That is exactly what Siegel's group found. In one study (Crowell, Hinson and Siegel, 1981) rats received alcohol in a distinctive test room and saline in their colony room, or the reverse; tolerance to alcohol's temperature-lowering effect appeared only when the rats were tested in the room where they had always received alcohol. This situational specificity of tolerance has been shown many times, with many drugs.

Key study: Siegel's heroin "overdose" rats (1982)

Many heroin users found dead of an apparent overdose had taken no more than their usual dose. Siegel, Riley Hinson, Marvin Krank and Jane McCully asked whether the setting could be the missing factor. They built up heroin tolerance in rats with injections given in one distinctive environment, alternating with injections of a harmless sugar solution in another. Then every rat received a large dose of heroin (15 mg per kg). Of the tolerant rats given this dose in the familiar heroin environment, 32 percent died; of the equally tolerant rats given it in the environment that had been paired only with the sugar solution, 64 percent died; and of drug-naive control rats, 96 percent died. The same drug history, the same dose, and twice the death rate in an unfamiliar place, because the cues that triggered the protective compensatory response were missing. The study, published in Science in 1982, explains why users who inject in a new place, or in a hurry in an unusual way, are at special risk.

The cues need not be external. Interoceptive stimuli, such as the thoughts and feelings of the drug-taking ritual or the first effects of the drug itself, can serve as conditioned stimuli too, which is why simply thinking about a drug can trigger craving. Conditioning also helps explain one of the most striking natural experiments in the history of addiction. When Lee Robins and her colleagues interviewed American soldiers who had left Vietnam in September 1971, almost half of the general sample had tried narcotics there and about 20 percent reported having been addicted to opiates; yet after they came home, their drug use and addiction fell back almost to pre-war levels (Robins, Helzer and Davis, 1975). Removed from the place, the companions, the boredom and the cheap pure heroin of Vietnam, most simply stopped (Section 10.5.6 tells the study in full). Finally, the pull of cues grows rather than fades during the first weeks of abstinence. In rats trained to self-administer cocaine, a drug-paired cue provokes few lever presses a day after withdrawal but many more a month or two later, a phenomenon called the incubation of craving. Pinel lists three triggers of relapse that research keeps confirming: stress, a single "priming" dose of the drug, and exposure to drug-associated cues. Cue exposure therapy, which presents the cues repeatedly without the drug so that the conditioned craving can extinguish, is described in Book 2, Section 9.9.6.

The most elegant learning theory of addiction came from a teacher of James Olds. Richard Lester Solomon (1918 to 1995) of the University of Pennsylvania noticed that strong emotional states seem to set off their own opposites. With John Corbit he published the opponent-process theory of motivation, first applied to cigarette addiction (1973) and then set out in general form in the Psychological Review (1974); Solomon summarised it in 1980 under the subtitle "the costs of pleasure and the benefits of pain". The starting observation is a pattern of emotion that Solomon called the standard pattern of affective dynamics, with five features. When a strong stimulus begins, feeling rises quickly to a peak (State A). It then falls back a little to a steady level, even though the stimulus is still there. When the stimulus stops, feeling does not simply return to neutral: it swings past neutral into an opposite state (State B), the after-reaction, which reaches its own peak and then slowly decays back to baseline. In the laboratory Solomon saw it in dogs given a ten-second electric shock: the heart rate leapt, began to slow while the shock was still on, and after the shock dropped below its resting rate before slowly recovering. He found the same pattern in reports on military parachutists, who on their first jump were terrified during the free fall and, after landing, walked about stunned for a few minutes and then became talkative and elated.

Solomon explained the pattern by two processes inside the nervous system. The a-process is the primary reaction to the stimulus: it starts quickly, follows the stimulus closely, stops quickly when the stimulus ends, and does not change with repetition. The a-process automatically arouses the b-process, an opponent reaction of the opposite emotional sign. The b-process is sluggish: slow to start, slow to build and slow to fade. What a person actually feels at any moment is the difference between the two, a minus b. Early on, a outweighs b, so the person feels State A; when the stimulus ends, the a-process vanishes at once while the b-process lingers, and the person feels State B. The crucial assumption is that the b-process is strengthened by use and weakened by disuse. Each time the stimulus is repeated, b starts sooner, grows larger and lasts longer, while a stays the same. Figure 10.5 shows what follows.

Apply this to a drug. Solomon's own example was opiates. With the first few doses, the user moves from a resting state to a rush and then euphoria, followed by a mild craving that soon passes. After many doses, the before-state is already craving, the drug produces only "contentment" (normality), and the after-reaction has become what Solomon called abstinence agony: the full withdrawal syndrome, intense and long-lasting. Two things have happened at once. The pleasure has shrunk, which is tolerance, and the misery after the drug has grown, which is withdrawal. The motive for taking the drug has shifted from positive reinforcement (getting the high) to negative reinforcement (escaping the low), and the one thing that ends the low fastest is another dose. That is the trap of "the hair of the dog that bit you", and it is Kuldeep's trap. The same mathematics runs in reverse for frightening experiences: the experienced parachutist feels eagerness before the jump, a thrill during it and exhilaration for hours afterwards, because his once-small b-process (relief and elation) has grown while the terror has habituated. Solomon thought love, grief, jogging, sauna bathing and many acquired tastes followed the same rule, and the theory remains one of the clearest accounts of how tolerance, withdrawal and craving arise together.

The theory has limits. On its own it cannot explain why people relapse months or years after the b-process should have decayed through disuse. Here conditioning closes the gap: the b-process can itself be conditioned to drug cues, so that the lane, the workshop or the friends call up the opponent state (Wikler's conditioned withdrawal and Siegel's compensatory responses are both conditioned b-processes). And it says nothing about why one person is vulnerable and another is not, which is the business of genes and personality.

Exam trap

Negative reinforcement is not punishment. It strengthens behaviour by removing something unpleasant (drinking to stop withdrawal or anxiety); punishment weakens behaviour. In opponent-process theory the a-process is the fast, primary, unchanging reaction and the b-process is the slow opponent that grows with use; tolerance is the shrinking State A and withdrawal the enlarged State B. The authors are Solomon and Corbit (1974); do not confuse their theory with Ewald Hering's opponent-process theory of colour vision from the nineteenth century, which shares only the name and the idea of paired opposing processes. Siegel's conditioned compensatory response is opposite to the drug's effect, which is why tolerance is strongest, and overdose least likely, in the familiar setting.

Thinking and social learning: expectancies, models, peers and the gateway

Animals learn to take drugs without any beliefs about them, but people come to their first drink already full of beliefs. Long before an Indian boy tastes alcohol he has seen the film hero drink to forget a broken heart and the villain drink before a fight; he has seen uncles become loud and merry at weddings and his father become frightening at night. An alcohol expectancy is a belief about what alcohol will do to you: make you relaxed, sociable, witty, brave, sexually attractive, or angry. Children hold such beliefs years before they drink, learned from family, friends, films and advertising, and positive expectancies in early adolescence predict who starts drinking and who drinks more in the years that follow. Expectancies matter because they shape both the decision to drink and what drinking feels like.

How can you separate what alcohol does from what people expect it to do? The answer was a clever design introduced by G. Alan Marlatt (1941 to 2011) and his colleagues Demming and Reid in 1973. They wanted to test the gamma alcoholic's "loss of control", the disease-model claim that a single drink triggers an uncontrollable craving for more. They recruited alcoholics and social drinkers for what was presented as a taste-rating task: each person was given three supposedly different drinks to compare on adjectives such as "sweet" and "strong", and the experimenters quietly measured how much was drunk. The drinks were vodka and tonic, chosen because tonic water hides the taste of vodka so well that people cannot tell reliably whether it is there. Crossing what people were told with what they actually received produced the four cells of the balanced-placebo design in Table 10.10.

Table 10.10 The balanced-placebo design (Marlatt, Demming and Reid, 1973). How much people drank followed the row (what they believed), not the column (what was in the glass).
What the person is toldActually given vodka and tonicActually given tonic only
"This is vodka and tonic"Drug plus expectancy (ordinary drinking)Expectancy alone (placebo)
"This is plain tonic"Drug alone (hidden dose)Neither (control)

The result was a blow to the loss-of-control idea. The amount people drank depended on what they had been told, not on what they had been given. Alcoholics who believed they were drinking vodka drank more, even when there was none in the glass; alcoholics who unknowingly received alcohol showed no runaway drinking at all. The same pattern held for social drinkers, though less strongly. A first drink does not, by its chemistry alone, set off the craving that the disease model described; what the drinker believes about that drink matters as much. (Pharmacology is not irrelevant, though: when Tim Stockwell and colleagues repeated the study in 1982, the alcohol itself did increase craving in severely dependent drinkers.) The balanced-placebo design became a standard tool of alcohol research, and later studies using it found that many of alcohol's social effects (feeling sociable, sexually aroused or aggressive) follow what people think they drank, whereas its effects on coordination and reaction time follow the alcohol itself. Marlatt went on to build a cognitive-behavioural model of relapse, including the abstinence violation effect, the "I've blown it, I may as well go all the way" reaction that turns a single slip into a full relapse; that model and the therapy built on it are described in Book 2, Section 9.9.6. Kuldeep's "only one" at the workshop, followed by a week of heavy drinking, is a textbook example.

Expectancies are learned socially, and so is much of drinking itself. In the social learning theory of Albert Bandura (1925 to 2021), people learn by watching models, especially models who are similar, admired or rewarded. Marlatt showed the effect directly. In an experiment with B. D. Caudill (1975), male heavy social drinkers took part in a wine-tasting task alongside a confederate posing as another participant; when the confederate drank heavily, the real participants drank considerably more than when he drank lightly or was absent. In real life the most important models are parents and friends. Children of heavy-drinking parents see drinking as normal, learn their parents' expectancies and often have easier access to alcohol at home. Parents who are themselves using drugs also tend to supervise their children less, and poorly supervised adolescents drift towards friends who use. Peer use is one of the strongest predictors of adolescent substance use, through two processes: young people choose friends who share their habits (selection), and friends then shape one another (socialisation). For Kuldeep, the group at the workshop after closing time supplied all three: models, approval and a bottle.

Social learning also tells you something about the order in which young people take up drugs, and that order is the basis of the most famous, and most misunderstood, idea in the epidemiology of drug use.

Key study: Kandel's stages of adolescent drug use (1975)

Denise Kandel, a sociologist and epidemiologist at Columbia University, followed random samples of New York State high-school students in two longitudinal surveys. She found that involvement with drugs moved through four stages in a regular order: (1) beer or wine, or both; (2) cigarettes or hard liquor (spirits); (3) marijuana; and (4) other illicit drugs such as LSD, amphetamines or heroin. The legal drugs were necessary intermediates between non-use and marijuana: 27 percent of students who both smoked and drank moved on to marijuana within a five to six month follow-up, against only 2 percent of those who had used no legal drug. Marijuana in turn was a crucial step towards other illicit drugs: 26 percent of marijuana users progressed to LSD, amphetamines or heroin, against 1 percent of non-users and 4 percent of users of legal drugs only. The same order held in every year of high school and in the year after, and students who cut down retreated through the stages in reverse. Kandel published these findings in Science in 1975.

Kandel's sequence became the gateway hypothesis: the idea that using a "softer" drug opens the door to "harder" ones. Because so few heroin or cocaine users in her data had skipped the marijuana stage, marijuana became the drug most commonly cited as the gateway to hard drugs, and the phrase "gateway drug", popularised in the 1980s, became a slogan of drug policy. But notice what the data show. The first step on Kandel's ladder was not marijuana but alcohol and tobacco, and most people who use marijuana never go on to heroin or cocaine. A sequence is not a cause. The main alternative is the common liability model: the same underlying vulnerability (genes, impulsive temperament, troubled family, deviant friends, easy access) makes a young person likely to use any drug, and drugs are simply tried in order of their availability and social acceptability, cheapest and most legal first. The strongest evidence for this comes from comparing countries. When Louisa Degenhardt and her colleagues (2010) examined the order in which people began using alcohol, tobacco, cannabis and other illicit drugs in WHO World Mental Health Surveys from 17 countries, the order varied with how common each drug was locally, and people often broke the "normal" sequence where cannabis was easy to get; they concluded that the gateway pattern at least partly reflects shared causes rather than one drug causing the next. The causal version has not disappeared, however. In 2011 Kandel, her husband, the Nobel laureate neuroscientist Eric Kandel, and their colleagues reported that mice primed with nicotine showed a stronger response to cocaine, in behaviour and in gene expression in the striatum, than mice that were not, and in 2014 the Kandels argued that nicotine itself may act as a biological gateway.

Exam trap

Asked which drug is "most commonly cited" as the gateway to heroin or cocaine, answer marijuana (cannabis). The traps: alcohol and nicotine are in fact the first drugs in Kandel's sequence (beer or wine, then cigarettes or spirits), and the Kandels (2014) later argued for nicotine as a molecular gateway, but neither is the classic answer; ecstasy (MDMA, Section 10.6) is a later, "club" drug, not a gateway at all. Remember the order: beer or wine, cigarettes or hard liquor, marijuana, other illicit drugs. Kandel described a sequence; whether it is causal is exactly what the common liability model disputes.

Why some drugs capture more users than others

Reena is 27 and works as a data analyst in Bengaluru. She smoked her first cigarette at 19, in her college hostel, and hated it: she coughed, felt dizzy and wondered why anyone bothered. But her friends smoked at the tea stall between lectures, and she began to join them, "only with friends, only at parties". Within a year she was smoking fifteen cigarettes a day. She has tried to quit four times; each attempt ended within a week, usually at the afternoon chai break with her team. In the same years she tried alcohol (she drinks a glass of wine perhaps once a month) and, once, cannabis at a party, which made her anxious and which she has never wanted again. Three drugs, three exposures, one addiction. Why tobacco?

Drugs differ in their addictive liability, sometimes called their capture rate: the proportion of people who ever use a drug who go on to become dependent on it. The best-known estimate comes from the National Comorbidity Survey, a nationally representative American survey carried out between 1990 and 1992. James Anthony, Lynn Warner and Ronald Kessler (1994) took the 8,098 people aged 15 to 54 in the survey, found out which drugs each had ever used (outside medical use), and asked how many of the users had at some point met the criteria for dependence of the day (DSM-III-R). Table 10.11 gives the answer. Tobacco topped the list: almost one in three people who had ever smoked had become dependent, a higher proportion than for heroin, cocaine or alcohol, and more than three times the figure for cannabis.

Table 10.11 The proportion of users who had ever become dependent on each drug, National Comorbidity Survey, United States, 1990 to 1992 (Anthony, Warner and Kessler, 1994).
DrugEver used (percent of population)Users who became dependent (percent)
Tobacco75.631.9
Heroin1.523.1 (few users, so a wide margin of error)
Cocaine16.216.7
Alcohol91.515.4
Stimulants (other than cocaine)15.311.2
Sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics12.79.2
Cannabis46.39.1
Analgesics (opioid painkillers)9.77.5
Psychedelics10.64.9
Inhalants6.83.7

A later and larger American survey told the same story with lifetime follow-up. Using the National Epidemiologic Survey on Alcohol and Related Conditions, Catalina Lopez-Quintero and her colleagues (2011) estimated the cumulative probability that a user would ever become dependent: 67.5 percent for nicotine, 22.7 percent for alcohol, 20.9 percent for cocaine and 8.9 percent for cannabis. Pinel's textbook gives the same figures, rounded: about 68 percent of people who experiment with smoking become addicted, against 23 percent for alcohol and 9 percent for marijuana. The same study adds a twist worth remembering: capture is not the same as speed. Among those who did become dependent, half the cases of cocaine and cannabis dependence had appeared within about 4 and 5 years of first use, against about 13 years for alcohol and 27 years for nicotine. Cocaine captures fewer of its users, but it captures them fast.

Why should a legal drug that produces no intoxication be the most efficient trap of all? The answer lies in how the drug is delivered and how it fits into life. First, route and speed. Inhaled smoke carries nicotine from the lungs to the brain within seconds, and the faster a drug reaches the brain, the stronger its reinforcing effect; the same principle explains why smoked crack cocaine and injected heroin are more addictive than snorted cocaine or swallowed opium (Sections 10.5 and 10.6). Second, frequency. A smoker of twenty cigarettes a day, at roughly ten puffs a cigarette, delivers about two hundred small doses to the brain every day, and each puff is both a reinforcement and a conditioning trial that ties nicotine to tea, coffee, meals, the phone, the end of work and the company of friends. No heroin user gets two hundred doses a day. Third, nicotine is short-acting, so withdrawal (irritability, restlessness, poor concentration) begins within hours and every cigarette also relieves a little withdrawal: positive and negative reinforcement on every puff. Fourth, nicotine does not intoxicate. You can smoke while working, driving or studying, so use never has to stop. And fifth, tobacco is legal, cheap, sold at every pan shop and socially accepted almost everywhere. As Pinel puts it, can you think of any other psychoactive drug that people take almost continually, even while walking down the street?

Pinel tells the story of one famous man who could not stop. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) used cocaine for about three years from 1884 and stopped in 1887, apparently without difficulty, yet he never gave up the twenty or so cigars he smoked every day, despite his physician's orders, cancer of the mouth and 33 operations on his jaw and palate. The man who could walk away from cocaine could not walk away from cigars; the Key study in Section 10.6.1 tells the whole story.

Two qualifications keep this picture honest. Capture rates measure how often users become dependent, not how harmful or how intensely rewarding a single dose is. When David Nutt and his colleagues (2007) asked expert panels to rate drugs for their dependence potential, heroin (3.0 on a 0 to 3 scale) and cocaine (2.39) came above tobacco (2.21), which in turn came above alcohol (1.93) and cannabis (1.51). Heroin and cocaine are pharmacologically more compelling per dose; tobacco captures more of its users because of the five reasons above. And capture rates depend on context: they were measured mostly in Western samples, and a drug's liability changes with its price, purity, legal status and the way it is taken (Sections 10.5 and 10.6).

Exam trap

Which substance has the highest addictive liability, the largest proportion of users who become dependent? Nicotine (tobacco): about a third of users in the National Comorbidity Survey (Anthony, Warner and Kessler, 1994) and about two thirds over a lifetime in NESARC (Lopez-Quintero and colleagues, 2011). Cocaine is the tempting wrong answer: it is more intensely rewarding per dose and dependence develops faster, but a smaller share of its users become dependent. Alcohol captures about one in seven to one in four of its users, and marijuana about one in ten. If a question asks instead which drug expert panels rate highest for dependence potential, the answer is heroin.

Genes: families, adoptees, twins and inherited markers

Kuldeep's father drank himself to death, and Kuldeep is drinking heavily. Is that inheritance or upbringing? Alcohol use disorder clearly runs in families: DSM-5-TR puts the risk at three to four times higher in the close relatives of a person with the disorder, highest for those with many affected relatives, close genetic ties and severe problems in the family. But families share homes, habits and liquor cabinets as well as genes, so family studies alone cannot separate the two. The tools that can are twin and adoption studies, whose logic is explained in Chapter 1, Section 1.4. For alcohol, the adoption studies came first.

In Denmark, where national registers allowed adopted children to be traced back to their biological parents, the American psychiatrist Donald Goodwin and his Danish and American colleagues studied men who had been separated from their biological parents early in life and raised by unrelated adoptive families. In their 1973 report, 18 percent of the adopted-away sons of alcoholic biological parents had themselves become alcoholic, against 5 percent of adopted sons whose biological parents had no record of alcoholism. Being raised away from the alcoholic parent did not protect them. (In a later study by the same group, the adopted-away daughters of alcoholics did not show the same raised risk.)

The most influential adoption study was Swedish. C. Robert Cloninger, an American psychiatrist at Washington University in St Louis, worked with the Swedish researchers Michael Bohman and Sören Sigvardsson on the records of all the children born to single mothers in Stockholm between 1930 and 1949 whose fathers were known and who had been adopted by non-relatives at an early age. Sweden kept unusually complete records of alcohol abuse, because people who drank to excess were registered with local temperance boards, and of treatment and crime. In 1981 Cloninger, Bohman and Sigvardsson reported their cross-fostering analysis of 862 adopted men. Alcoholism in a biological parent raised a son's risk; alcoholism in an adoptive parent did not. But the pattern of inheritance was not uniform, and they separated two forms of alcoholism with different causes (Table 10.12).

Type I alcoholism, which they called milieu-limited, was the common form. The biological parents of these men had usually had mild, adult-onset alcohol problems without serious criminality, and the genetic predisposition expressed itself only when the adoptive environment also pushed (for example, when the adoptive father was an unskilled labourer); it was a joint product of genes and milieu, hence the name. A companion study of 913 adopted women found that their pattern of inheritance matched type I, so this form affects both sexes. Type II alcoholism, called male-limited, was less common but more strongly inherited. It ran in sons of biological fathers with severe alcoholism that had begun in adolescence, needed extensive treatment and went with serious criminality; the mothers were usually not alcoholic. Sons with this background were at high risk whatever kind of home they were raised in; the adoptive environment affected the severity of their drinking but not whether it appeared.

In 1987 Cloninger linked the two types to personality and to clinical pictures. Type I alcoholics typically begin drinking heavily after the age of 25, drink in binges with loss of control, feel guilty and afraid about their drinking, and are anxious, cautious, emotionally dependent people (high in harm avoidance and reward dependence, low in novelty seeking) who drink to relieve anxiety. Type II alcoholics begin before 25, cannot abstain, often fight and get arrested when drinking, feel little guilt, and are impulsive, thrill-seeking and socially detached (high in novelty seeking, low in harm avoidance and reward dependence) who drink for euphoria. You will recognise Kuldeep: an alcoholic, violent father with a police record, drinking from 14, fights and a police case by 22, and a restless boy who felt calm for the first time with a drink in his hand. He is a type II picture.

Table 10.12 Cloninger's type I and type II alcoholism (Cloninger, Bohman and Sigvardsson, 1981; Cloninger, 1987). The types are prototypes at the two ends of a continuum, not boxes into which every drinker fits.
FeatureType I (milieu-limited)Type II (male-limited)
CausesGenes plus environment; both neededMainly genetic; environment affects severity only
SexMen and womenMainly men
FrequencyCommonLess common
Biological parentsMild, adult-onset alcohol abuse, little crimeFather with severe, early, treated alcoholism and serious crime
Usual age of onsetAfter 25Before 25
Typical drinking problemLoss of control and binges; problems worsen over timeInability to abstain; drinking with fighting and arrests
Guilt and fear about drinkingFrequentInfrequent
PersonalityHigh harm avoidance and reward dependence, low novelty seekingHigh novelty seeking, low harm avoidance and reward dependence
Drinks mainly toRelieve anxietyGet high (euphoria)

The typology was criticised from the start. It was odd that type I covered both mild and severe abusers while type II covered moderate ones, and the "male-limited" label proved too strong, since some women fit the type II profile. A replication in Gothenburg, reported in 1996, confirmed both types in adopted men: the risk of severe type I alcoholism was raised about fourfold only when the genetic and the environmental risks were both present (11.4 percent against 3.0 percent), while the risk of type II alcoholism was raised about sixfold in sons with a type II genetic background, whatever their upbringing (10.7 percent against 2.0 percent). Other researchers found a similar divide by different routes: the American alcohol researcher Thomas Babor and colleagues (1992) used statistical clustering in treated alcoholics to separate a type A (later onset, fewer childhood risk factors, milder dependence, less psychopathology) from a type B (childhood risk factors, family history, early onset, severe dependence, use of other drugs and more psychopathology). The common message is that early-onset alcoholism with antisocial features is the more heritable and more severe form.

Twin studies agree that genes matter a great deal. A meta-analysis of 12 twin studies and 5 adoption studies by Bradley Verhulst, Michael Neale and Kenneth Kendler (2015) put the heritability of alcohol use disorder at about 0.49, the same in men and women, with a modest contribution of shared family environment (about 0.10); DSM-5-TR gives the range as 40 to 60 percent of the variance in risk. Kendler's twin work has also shown that much of the genetic risk is shared across drugs: what is inherited is largely a general vulnerability to substance use disorders, not a gene for whisky or a gene for heroin. Heritability is not fate, though. The risk is spread across a great many genes, each of which, DSM-5-TR notes, explains only about 1 to 2 percent of it, and genes and environment interact: genetic effects on drinking are stronger when parents monitor their children poorly or when alcohol is easy to get.

What exactly is inherited? Three kinds of answer have emerged. The first is the way the body handles alcohol. Alcohol is broken down to acetaldehyde, a toxic substance, and acetaldehyde is then broken down by the enzyme aldehyde dehydrogenase (ALDH2). A common variant of the ALDH2 gene, carried by roughly a third to a half of people of East Asian descent (some 540 million people, about 8 percent of the world's population) and almost absent elsewhere, produces an enzyme that barely works. When carriers drink, acetaldehyde builds up and they experience the alcohol flush reaction: a red, hot face, a racing heart, headache and nausea. Few people drink heavily through that, and carriers, especially those with two copies, are strongly protected against alcohol use disorder: in Japanese studies of several hundred alcoholics, people with two copies were almost never found. (Disulfiram, a drug used in treatment, works by producing the same reaction on purpose; Section 10.4.) Even this protection bends to culture. In Japan, as heavy drinking became more socially expected between 1979 and 1992, the protective effect of a single copy weakened, and more of the alcoholics treated at the end of that period carried the variant than at the start: a vivid example of genes and culture interacting.

The second answer is how strongly a person feels alcohol. Low level of response to alcohol was discovered by Marc Schuckit in the San Diego Prospective Study. At about the age of 20, his group gave a standard dose of alcohol to 227 sons of alcoholics and 227 matched young men without such a family history, and measured how intoxicated they felt and how much their bodies swayed. About 40 percent of the sons of alcoholics, but fewer than 10 percent of the comparison men, showed a low response: they felt and showed surprisingly little effect. Almost a decade later, when the first half of the group was about 30, a low response at 20 predicted a fourfold greater likelihood of alcoholism in both groups; among the sons of alcoholics, 56 percent of those with a low response had developed alcoholism, against 14 percent of those with a high response (Schuckit, 1994). The likely mechanism is simple: a person who needs more drinks to feel the effect drinks more, more often, and the heavier drinking leads on to dependence. Note the direction carefully, because it is counter-intuitive: the risk marker is feeling less from alcohol, not more. The third answer is temperament, a restless, impulsive, novelty-seeking disposition that shows itself in childhood, long before the first drink; it belongs to the next subsection. (Researchers have also found a smaller P300 brain-wave response in the sons of alcoholics, but because the same finding appears in other disorders it is probably a general marker of vulnerability rather than a specific one.)

Exam trap

Type I (milieu-limited): later onset (after 25), both sexes, genes plus environment, loss of control, guilt, anxious and harm-avoidant. Type II (male-limited): early onset (before 25), mainly men, highly heritable from fathers with severe alcoholism and criminality, inability to abstain, fights and arrests, novelty seeking. Do not reverse them, and do not equate them with Jellinek's gamma and delta (Jellinek's types were clinical patterns; Cloninger's came from adoption records). Goodwin's Danish study found the raised risk in adopted-away sons. Schuckit's marker is a low response to alcohol. The ALDH2 flushing variant protects against alcohol use disorder. Heritability of alcohol use disorder is about 50 percent (40 to 60 percent).

Personality, comorbidity and the psychodynamic view

For much of the twentieth century clinicians spoke confidently of an addictive personality: immature, dependent, manipulative, unable to tolerate frustration, the kind of person who would become addicted to something sooner or later. The idea has not survived research. Its first problem is the direction of cause. The traits were described in people who were already addicted, and many of them (lying, manipulation, self-centredness) are what years of addiction do to anyone, because sustaining a habit that others disapprove of requires them. Its second problem is that when children are followed prospectively, no single personality predicts all addictions. What prospective research does find is a small number of traits that raise the risk, and they fall on two quite different paths.

The first and stronger path runs through disinhibition. Children who are impulsive, restless, aggressive and poor at waiting for rewards are more likely to drink, smoke and use drugs early and to develop severe problems. Kenneth Sher and his colleagues (1991), comparing the children of alcoholics with other young people, called this behavioural undercontrol and showed that it was one of the routes by which risk passes from alcoholic parents to their children. It links substance use with childhood conduct problems, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (Chapter 3, Section 3.8) and antisocial personality disorder (Chapter 11) in what is now called the externalising spectrum; twin studies suggest that much of what these conditions share is inherited. DSM-5-TR notes that impulsivity raises the rates of all substance use disorders and of gambling disorder, and goes with earlier onset and greater severity. Closely related is sensation seeking, the need for varied, novel and intense experiences, studied by Marvin Zuckerman, and its cousin in Cloninger's 1987 theory, novelty seeking. Cloninger proposed three inherited dimensions of temperament, each tied to a transmitter: novelty seeking (dopamine), harm avoidance (serotonin) and reward dependence (noradrenaline); high novelty seeking marked his type II alcoholics, and his questionnaire is described in the Assessment book. Even rats show the pattern: animals that are more active in a novel environment, or that prefer sweetened water, are more likely to start self-administering cocaine.

The second path runs through negative emotion: anxious, easily distressed, depressed people who use drugs to feel better. This is the path of Cloninger's type I drinker, and it brings us to comorbidity. Substance use disorders very often co-occur with depression, anxiety disorders, post-traumatic stress disorder, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia and personality disorders. There are three ways such a link can arise, and in any one patient more than one may be at work. The drug may cause the other disorder: heavy drinking produces depression and anxiety that often clear within a few weeks of abstinence, which is why clinicians wait before diagnosing an independent depressive disorder in a drinker (Section 10.3). The other disorder may lead to drug use. Or a third factor, such as shared genes or childhood trauma, may cause both.

The second possibility has a name. In the self-medication hypothesis, the Harvard psychiatrist Edward Khantzian (1985) argued, from clinical work with heroin and cocaine users, that people are predisposed to addiction because they suffer painful feelings and related psychiatric problems, and that their drug of choice is not random. It is chosen by the interaction between what the drug does and the feeling the person cannot bear. Opioid users, he wrote, prefer opiates for their powerful muting of disorganising rage and aggression; cocaine appeals because it relieves the distress of depression, hypomania and hyperactivity. Hamid, the young man from Bhopal, describes it exactly: his father beat him throughout his childhood, he grew into a man with a short fuse, and the first time he smoked brown sugar "the anger went quiet, for the first time in my life". The idea fits many clinical histories, and it explains why people with schizophrenia smoke so heavily (nicotine may ease some of their cognitive difficulties and their inability to feel pleasure). Its weakness is that in prospective studies the drug use often comes first, and the drugs usually worsen, in the long run, the very feelings they are taken to relieve.

Khantzian's hypothesis is the modern heir of a much older tradition. Psychodynamic theories of addiction began with Freud, who thought addictions were rooted in the earliest stage of psychosexual development. In the oral stage, from birth to roughly 18 months, the infant's pleasure and its relationship with the world centre on the mouth: sucking, feeding and later biting. A child who is either frustrated or overindulged at this stage, Freud argued, becomes partly fixated there, leaving part of the libido invested in oral satisfactions. The adult with an oral fixation is dependent, passive and demanding, and seeks comfort through the mouth: eating, smoking, drinking, talking. In the Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality (1905) Freud suggested that children in whom the pleasure of the lips remains unusually strong grow into adults with a strong motive for drinking and smoking. Earlier still, in a letter to his friend Wilhelm Fliess dated 22 December 1897, he had called masturbation the "primal addiction" and suggested that the other addictions, to alcohol, morphine and tobacco, arise as substitutes for it. His follower Karl Abraham (1877 to 1925) wrote on the psychological relations between sexuality and alcoholism in 1908, and the Hungarian-born analyst Sandor Rado (1890 to 1972) described addiction in 1933 as pharmacothymia, a disorder of mood in which a state of tense depression is relieved by the drug's elation, only to be followed by a deeper depression and a renewed need for the drug; read in modern terms, Rado had described the opponent process forty years before Solomon. Later analysts, Khantzian among them, moved away from libido and fixation and towards the idea that addiction is a failure to regulate feelings, self-esteem, relationships and self-care.

How should you judge the psychodynamic view? The specific claim of oral fixation has little empirical support: there is no good evidence that the way an infant was fed predicts adult drinking, and the theory explains both too much and too little (any drinker can be called orally fixated after the fact). But the broader insight, that many people use drugs to manage feelings they cannot otherwise bear, has survived into the self-medication hypothesis, into the negative-reinforcement accounts of Section 10.2.3 and into the affect-regulation work of modern therapies (Book 2).

Exam trap

Freud linked alcohol dependence (and smoking and overeating) to fixation at the oral stage (birth to about 18 months; mouth; dependency). The distractors: anal fixation (about 18 months to 3 years; toilet training) gives the orderly, stingy, obstinate anal-retentive character, later linked to obsessional traits; phallic fixation (about 3 to 6 years; the Oedipus complex) is linked to vanity, recklessness and hysteria; latency (about 6 years to puberty) is a period of dormant sexual drives and is not a stage at which Freud placed fixations; and the genital stage (puberty onwards) is mature adult sexuality, the goal of development rather than a fixation point. Rado's term for drug addiction is pharmacothymia. The self-medication hypothesis is Khantzian's (1985).

Culture, society and the biopsychosocial synthesis

No one becomes dependent on a drug they cannot get, and no one drinks in a social vacuum. Society decides which drugs are legal, what they cost, where they are sold, who may use them, on which occasions and how much drunkenness is tolerated. DSM-5-TR's list of environmental risk factors for alcohol use disorder reads like a sociology syllabus: poverty and discrimination, unemployment, low education, cultural attitudes towards drinking and intoxication, the availability and price of alcohol, stress, heavy drinking among peers, exaggerated positive expectations of what alcohol will do, and poor ways of coping with stress. Of these, price and availability are the levers that public policy can pull most directly: when alcohol becomes cheaper or easier to buy, people drink more and suffer more harm, which is why taxes, licensing hours, the density of liquor outlets and the minimum drinking age matter (the Indian experience with prohibition is discussed in Section 10.3).

Culture shapes not only how much people drink but what drinking does to them. In some societies wine or beer is part of family meals, children see adults drink moderately with food, and drunkenness is frowned upon; in others alcohol is kept apart from daily life, drunk mainly by men in groups, often in spirits and often with the explicit aim of getting drunk. The same quantity of alcohol produces different behaviour in different places. Barlow and Durand cite the finding that Poland and Finland, with relatively low alcohol consumption, had more drinking-related conflicts and arrests for drunkenness than the Netherlands with similar consumption, and one explanation is the expectancies discussed in Section 10.2.4: in one culture "drink makes me aggressive", in another "drink makes me sleepy". Culture can even override biology, as the Japanese experience with the protective ALDH2 variant showed.

Indian context

India's relationship with alcohol is deeply ambivalent. Many religious and social traditions disapprove of drinking, the Constitution's Article 47 asks the State to work towards prohibition, and most Indian women do not drink at all; yet among the men who do drink, drinking is often heavy, done in all-male groups, with cheap country liquor and with intoxication as the goal (Section 10.1 gives the survey figures, and Section 10.3 the pattern of drinking). Other substances carry cultural sanction of their own: bhang (cannabis) is drunk at Holi and offered at Shiva festivals, and offering paan, often with tobacco in it, is a traditional gesture of hospitality in many regions. A clinician assessing a client therefore needs to ask not only how much the person uses, but what that use means in the person's community: a glass of bhang at Holi and a bottle of liquor every evening sit in very different places in Indian life.

The social environment matters for animals too. In standard self-administration studies a rat lives alone in a bare cage, where pressing a lever for a drug is the only interesting thing it can do. When rats are housed in groups, given enriched environments or offered other rewards, they are much less likely to take drugs. In the "Rat Park" studies of Bruce Alexander and his colleagues in Canada (1981), rats living in a large, comfortable colony with company and things to do drank much less morphine solution than rats kept in isolation (later attempts to repeat the result have been mixed). In a French study, given a choice between sweetened water and intravenous cocaine, only about 10 percent of rats preferred the cocaine. The lesson is not that drugs are harmless but that addiction flourishes where other rewards are scarce, and that treatment and prevention should add alternative rewards to people's lives: work, relationships, recognition, pleasure. The community reinforcement approach and contingency management, described in Sections 10.4 and 10.6 and in Book 2, are built on exactly this idea.

Now step back. Each theory in this section is true of something, and none is true of everything. The most useful way to combine them is to notice that they answer different questions, because different causes act at different stages of a drug career (Figure 10.6). Whether a person tries a drug at all depends mostly on availability, price, family and peer models, expectancies and a curious, sensation-seeking temperament. Whether use becomes regular depends on the drug's rewarding power and how quickly and often it is delivered (Sections 10.2.2 and 10.2.5), on positive and negative reinforcement, on a low response to alcohol or an absent flushing reaction, and on a culture and peer group that support heavy use. Whether regular use becomes a disorder depends on incentive sensitisation, the slide from action to habit, weakening prefrontal control, the growing b-process of the opponent-process theory, and the stresses and lack of alternative rewards in the person's life. And whether a person who has stopped relapses depends on conditioned cues, stress, a single priming dose and the abstinence violation effect, set against the strength of the person's new life. This is the biopsychosocial model, introduced in Chapter 1, applied to addiction; it is also a version of the diathesis-stress idea, with genes and temperament supplying the diathesis and drugs, cues and circumstances supplying the stress.

Return to Kuldeep and you can see every column of Figure 10.6 at work. He inherited a high genetic risk from a type II father, and with it a restless, novelty-seeking temperament (biology at stage 1). Alcohol was cheap, available at every wedding and approved of by the young men around him (society at stages 1 and 2).
