It relieved his restlessness, so it was negatively reinforcing from the first glass, and he probably felt less of its effect than his friends did, so he drank more (stage 2). Years of daily drinking sensitised his wanting, turned drinking at the workshop into a habit and grew a b-process so large that he now drinks mainly to escape the shakes and the misery of withdrawal (stage 3). And when he stopped, the workshop, the friends and one "harmless" drink brought him back (stage 4). Treating Kuldeep will mean working on every level: detoxification and medication (Section 10.4), help with his craving and his high-risk situations, involvement of his family, and a life that offers him something better to do at closing time.

Table 10.13 The main explanations of addiction at a glance.

Clinical link

The theories translate directly into an assessment. With any client who uses substances, ask about the family history (genes and modelling), the age and setting of first use (temperament, peers, availability), what the drug does for the person now (positive or negative reinforcement, self-medication of a comorbid disorder), the cues and situations that trigger use (conditioning), what the person believes the drug does (expectancies), and what else in life gives pleasure and meaning (alternative rewards). Each answer points to a part of the treatment plan: medication and detoxification for the biological level, cue-based and cognitive-behavioural work for the psychological level, and family, work and community for the social level (Section 10.4; Book 2, Sections 6.9 and 9.9.5).

Answers: (1) gamma, (2) intracranial self-stimulation, (3) nucleus accumbens, (4) wanting, (5) b-process, (6) unfamiliar (novel), (7) balanced-placebo, (8) marijuana (cannabis), (9) tobacco (nicotine), (10) II.

10.3 Alcohol: The Drug, Intoxication, Withdrawal and the Damage DoneLower yield · 1 Q

Of all the drugs in this chapter, only one is sold through government-licensed shops on most Indian streets, served at weddings and festivals, and taxed so heavily that its excise duty is one of the largest sources of many state governments' own revenue. It is also the drug whose withdrawal can kill, a leading contributor to deaths on the roads, and the drug that starved Jimmie G.'s brain of a vitamin until his memory stopped working. DSM-5-TR estimates that alcohol use accounts for about 2.8 million deaths a year worldwide and that about 237 million men and 46 million women have an alcohol use disorder. No other substance combines so much ordinary social use with so much damage. (It is not, however, the drug that captures the largest share of the people who try it; that distinction belongs to nicotine, as Section 10.2 explains.)

Because alcohol is legal and familiar, it is easy to forget that it is a drug in exactly the sense defined in Section 10.1: a psychoactive substance with a dose-response curve, tolerance, a withdrawal syndrome and a toxic dose. This section looks at it the way a pharmacologist, a physician and a psychiatrist would. It begins with what alcohol is and what the body does to it, moves to what it does to the brain at rising doses, and then follows the brain that has adapted to alcohol into withdrawal, where the most dramatic syndromes in this chapter (withdrawal seizures, alcoholic hallucinosis and delirium tremens) appear. It ends with the psychiatric and physical harm of years of heavy drinking, how problem drinking is recognised, and the place of alcohol in India. The theories of why some people become dependent and others do not are in Section 10.2; how alcohol use disorder is treated is the subject of Section 10.4.

Ethanol, the standard drink and what the body does with alcohol

The alcohol in every drink, from toddy to single malt, is the same molecule: ethanol (ethyl alcohol, C2H5OH). It is made when yeasts ferment the sugars in grain, fruit, palm sap, molasses or flowers. Fermentation stops when the alcohol reaches roughly 15 percent, because at that strength it kills the yeast, so beer, wine and toddy are all fairly weak. Stronger drinks (whisky, rum, brandy, vodka, arrack) are made by distillation, which boils off and concentrates the alcohol. Ethanol is not the only alcohol: methanol, used in industry, is a deadly poison, and you will meet it in the hooch tragedies of Section 10.3.9.

Ethanol is a small molecule that dissolves in both water and fat. That simple chemistry explains much of what follows. It crosses every membrane in the body without needing a transporter, so it passes freely into the brain and across the placenta to the fetus, and it spreads evenly through the body's water. A person with more body water dilutes the same amount of alcohol more; a person with less body water (a smaller person, an older person, or a woman, who on average has more fat and less water than a man of the same weight) reaches a higher concentration from the same drink.

Because drinks differ so much in strength, clinicians count alcohol in grams of pure ethanol, or in standard drinks. A standard drink is a conventional amount of pure alcohol used to compare drinks and to set safe-drinking advice. The World Health Organization (WHO), in the manual for its AUDIT questionnaire, uses 10 grams; DSM-5-TR describes a standard drink as about 10 to 12 grams; the United States uses 14 grams and the British "unit" is 8 grams. India has no official standard drink, so this book uses the WHO figure. The grams in any drink can be worked out from its volume and its strength: multiply the volume in millilitres by the percentage of alcohol by volume (divided by 100) and by 0.79, the density of ethanol. Table 10.14 does this for drinks you will hear about in an Indian clinic. Notice in particular the "quarter" (180 ml) of whisky, which many daily drinkers report as their usual amount: it contains about six standard drinks.

Table 10.14 How much alcohol is in common drinks (rounded; 1 standard drink = 10 g of ethanol).
DrinkVolumeAlcohol by volumeEthanol (g)Standard drinks

Strong beer, large bottle650 ml8%414
Wine, glass150 ml12%141.4

What happens after the drink is swallowed? A little alcohol is absorbed through the stomach wall, but most is absorbed from the small intestine, so anything that keeps the drink in the stomach slows absorption. On an empty stomach the blood level peaks roughly 30 to 90 minutes after drinking; a meal delays and flattens the peak, which is why the same two pegs feel very different before and after dinner. Some alcohol is broken down in the stomach wall and the liver before it ever reaches the general circulation, a process called first-pass metabolism; women have less of the stomach enzyme involved, which is a second reason (DSM-5-TR lists it alongside body weight, fat and water) why women reach higher blood levels than men from the same drink.

More than 90 percent of the alcohol is then metabolised by the liver; the small remainder leaves the body unchanged in breath, urine and sweat. The alcohol in the breath is in a fixed proportion to that in the blood, which is why a breath analyser can estimate the blood level. The liver's main route has two steps (Figure 10.7). First, the enzyme alcohol dehydrogenase (ADH) converts ethanol to acetaldehyde, a toxic and highly reactive chemical that causes flushing, nausea, a pounding heart and headache, and that damages tissues and DNA. Second, the enzyme aldehyde dehydrogenase (ALDH) converts acetaldehyde to acetate, which the body burns to carbon dioxide and water or turns into fat. Normally the second step is fast, so acetaldehyde never builds up. Two famous exceptions make this pathway a favourite of examiners. Many East Asian people carry a slow variant of the mitochondrial enzyme (ALDH2), so acetaldehyde accumulates and they flush, a reaction that protects them against alcohol use disorder (Section 10.2). And the drug disulfiram works by blocking ALDH deliberately, so that a drink produces the same unpleasant acetaldehyde reaction (Section 10.4.4).

Two further facts about this pathway matter clinically. Alcohol dehydrogenase becomes saturated at quite low blood levels, so the liver removes a roughly fixed amount of alcohol per hour however much is in the blood. Pharmacologists call this zero-order kinetics: elimination proceeds at a constant rate, not as a constant fraction. DSM-5-TR puts the rate at about one drink an hour, with the blood level falling by about 15 to 20 mg per 100 ml each hour. Black coffee, a cold shower or a walk in the night air do not speed it up; they produce a wide-awake drunk, not a sober one. Secondly, heavy drinkers recruit a second enzyme system in the liver, the microsomal ethanol-oxidising system (MEOS), whose main enzyme is cytochrome P450 2E1 (CYP2E1). Regular drinking induces it, so a heavy drinker clears alcohol somewhat faster than a non-drinker (one part of tolerance, Section 10.3.4). The same enzyme turns paracetamol into a liver-damaging product, which is why ordinary doses of paracetamol are more dangerous to a heavy drinker.

The amount of alcohol in the blood is expressed as the blood alcohol concentration (BAC), usually in milligrams of ethanol per 100 millilitres of blood (mg per 100 ml, which is the same as mg per decilitre and is sometimes written "mg%"). A BAC of 80 mg per 100 ml is the same as 0.08 percent. In India, Section 185 of the Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 makes it an offence to drive with more than 30 mg of alcohol per 100 ml of blood detected by a breath analyser (or another test); the limit in England and in most of the United States is 80. A useful approximation, worked out by the Swedish chemist Erik Widmark in 1932, estimates the peak BAC from the grams drunk divided by the body weight multiplied by the proportion of the body that is water (about 0.68 for men and 0.55 for women).

Push a case through it. A 70 kg man finishes a quarter of whisky (61 g) over the evening. His peak BAC is about 100 × 61 ÷ (0.68 × 70), or roughly 128 mg per 100 ml, more than four times the Indian driving limit. At 15 mg per hour, it will take him about six and a half hours to fall below 30; if he stops drinking at midnight he is still over the limit at six in the morning, the "morning-after driver" whom traffic police catch every day. A 55 kg woman who drank the same quarter would peak at about 200, the level at which DSM-5-TR notes that most people without tolerance are severely intoxicated. The same arithmetic explains the DSM-5-TR rule of thumb that each standard drink raises the BAC by about 20 mg per 100 ml.

Exam trap

Alcohol is eliminated by zero-order kinetics: a roughly constant amount (about one drink, or 15 to 20 mg per 100 ml of BAC) per hour, not a constant percentage, and nothing the drinker does speeds it up. The pathway is ethanol to acetaldehyde (by alcohol dehydrogenase) to acetate (by aldehyde dehydrogenase); the toxic product that causes flushing is acetaldehyde, not acetate. A "standard drink" is not one fixed amount: WHO uses 10 g, the United States 14 g, the British unit is 8 g, and India has no official definition. India's drink-driving limit is 30 mg per 100 ml, much lower than the 80 of England and most of the United States.

How alcohol acts on the brain

For most of the twentieth century pharmacologists thought alcohol worked like an anaesthetic gas, dissolving in the fatty membranes of neurons and making them sluggish. The theory explained why alcohol depresses everything at high doses, but not why some of its effects are so specific, or why benzodiazepines can stand in for it during withdrawal. From the 1980s onwards it became clear that alcohol acts on particular proteins in the membrane, and two of them carry most of the story: the main inhibitory receptor of the brain and one of the main excitatory ones.

The brain's chief inhibitory neurotransmitter is gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA). When GABA binds to the GABA-A receptor, a channel in the receptor opens and lets chloride ions flow into the neuron; the inside of the cell becomes more negative and the neuron becomes less likely to fire. You can think of GABA as the brain's brake. Alcohol does not bind where GABA binds. It binds at another site on the receptor complex and makes the channel respond more strongly when GABA arrives, so the brake works harder. A drug that acts like this is a positive allosteric modulator ("allosteric" means acting at another site). In ordinary exam language alcohol is called a GABA agonist, and functionally that is fair: its effect is to increase GABA's inhibition. Benzodiazepines and barbiturates act on the same receptor complex in the same way (barbiturates, at high doses, can even open the channel without GABA). This shared target is the reason alcohol, benzodiazepines and barbiturates show cross-tolerance and cross-dependence (Section 10.1), the reason a benzodiazepine can replace alcohol during detoxification (Section 10.4.3), and the reason that alcohol combined with sedative tablets is a common route to fatal overdose.

The brain's chief excitatory neurotransmitter is glutamate, and one of its receptors, the NMDA receptor (named after N-methyl-D-aspartate, a chemical that activates it selectively), is the accelerator. Alcohol blocks it: it is an NMDA antagonist. The NMDA receptor is also essential for the strengthening of synapses that underlies new learning in the hippocampus, so its blockade is thought to explain why heavy drinking disrupts the laying down of new memories and produces blackouts (Section 10.3.3). So alcohol presses the brake and lifts the foot off the accelerator at the same time. Every depressant effect of drinking, from relaxation to coma, follows from that double action, and so, as you will see in Section 10.3.4, does the overexcited brain of withdrawal.

Alcohol also acts on several other systems, and these explain its pleasures rather than its sedation. It increases the release of dopamine in the nucleus accumbens, the common pathway of drug reward described in Section 10.2, partly because it triggers the release of the brain's own opioids (endorphins), which in turn release the brakes on dopamine neurons. That opioid link is the basis of naltrexone treatment (Section 10.4.5). Alcohol enhances the action of one serotonin receptor (the 5-HT3 receptor) and raises serotonin release, and it affects glycine receptors, nicotinic acetylcholine receptors and several ion channels. Barlow and Durand's textbook sums up the result: because alcohol affects so many neurotransmitter systems, we should not be surprised that its effects are so widespread and complex. Notice, though, the difference in status. The GABA-A and NMDA actions are direct and central; the dopamine, endorphin and serotonin effects are mostly downstream consequences or secondary targets.

Exam trap

"For which neurotransmitter is alcohol an agonist?" The answer is GABA: alcohol enhances inhibition at the GABA-A receptor (strictly, it is a positive allosteric modulator, the same family as benzodiazepines and barbiturates). Its second core action is to block the NMDA glutamate receptor, so if a question asks which receptor alcohol antagonises, the answer is NMDA. The distractors are not meaningless: alcohol does release endorphins and dopamine and does enhance one serotonin receptor, but it is not described as a serotonin, acetylcholine or endorphin agonist. Nicotine is the acetylcholine (nicotinic) agonist (Section 10.6); morphine and heroin are the opioid agonists (Section 10.5).

Intoxication: from relaxed to comatose

Watch the guests at a long wedding reception and you can see the whole dose-response curve of alcohol in one evening. Early on, the drinkers are warmer and more talkative than usual. A little later some are loud, a few are dancing who never dance, and one uncle is telling a story he would never tell sober. Later still, someone is slurring and swaying, someone has picked a quarrel over nothing, and someone is being helped to a car (where, one hopes, someone else is driving). Each stage corresponds roughly to a range of blood alcohol concentration, and each tells you something about how the drug acts.

DSM-5-TR describes alcohol intoxication through four requirements, and each has a reason. There must be recent drinking. There must be clinically significant problematic changes in behaviour or psychological state that arise during or soon after drinking: inappropriate sexual or aggressive behaviour, swings of mood, impaired judgement. This requirement exists because having had a drink is not a disorder; the diagnosis is meant for intoxication that causes trouble. There must be at least one of six signs of a depressed nervous system: slurred speech, incoordination, an unsteady gait, nystagmus (jerky involuntary eye movements), impaired attention or memory, or stupor or coma. And the picture must not be better explained by something else. That last rule matters more in practice than any other. Low blood sugar, diabetic ketoacidosis, a head injury, cerebellar disease, multiple sclerosis and intoxication with sedative tablets can all look like drunkenness, and the drunk man in the casualty department who smells of alcohol and is "sleeping it off" is the classic person in whom a bleed inside the skull is missed.

Table 10.15 lists the effects usually seen at each blood level in a person who has not developed tolerance. Two qualifications apply to every row. First, tolerance shifts the whole table: DSM-5-TR points out that a person with a BAC of 150 who shows no signs of intoxication must have acquired some tolerance, and dependent drinkers are sometimes found walking and talking at levels that would put a non-drinker into a coma. Second, intoxication is more intense while the level is rising than when it is falling.

Table 10.15 Blood alcohol concentration and the stages of intoxication in a person without tolerance (bands are approximate; individuals vary).
BAC (mg per 100 ml)What is usually seenLandmarks
20 to 50Relaxation, warmth, talkativeness, mild euphoria; judgement begins to slipIndia's driving limit is 30
50 to 100Disinhibition, louder speech, poorer judgement and slower reactionsDriving skills deteriorate from about 50; limit in England and most of the United States is 80
100 to 200Obvious drunkenness: slurred speech, unsteady gait, nystagmus, labile mood, impaired attention and memoryMost non-tolerant people are severely intoxicated by 200
200 to 300Nausea and vomiting, double vision, confusion, blackouts; falls asleepDSM-5-TR: a first stage of anaesthesia
300 to 400 and aboveStupor and coma, low body temperature, slowed breathing and pulseDeath possible above 300 to 400; Pinel gives 500 (0.5 percent) as a level with a risk of death from respiratory depression

Why does a depressant drug make people lively at first? Partly because the first systems to be depressed are inhibitory ones. The frontal and social controls that normally hold behaviour in check are released before anything else is slowed, so the drinker is disinhibited, not stimulated. And partly because alcohol's effects are genuinely biphasic. DSM-5-TR notes that while blood levels are rising, the effects often reflect stimulation (talkativeness, a sense of well-being, a bright, expansive mood), whereas when levels are falling the drinker becomes progressively more depressed, withdrawn and cognitively impaired. The pleasant phase belongs to the rising limb of the blood alcohol curve and the unpleasant phase to the falling limb, which creates an obvious temptation: one more drink to get back to the rising limb.

How can the same drug make one person aggressive, another tearful, a third generous and a fourth amorous? A strong answer was given by the social psychologist Claude M. Steele (born 1946) and Robert A. Josephs in a 1990 paper in American Psychologist called "Alcohol myopia: its prized and dangerous effects". Their idea of alcohol myopia is that alcohol narrows attention, like short sight, to the most salient cues of the moment, and leaves the drinker unable to process the more distant, abstract cues (consequences, rules, other people's feelings, tomorrow) that normally restrain behaviour. Steele and Josephs drew three conclusions. First, alcohol makes social behaviour more extreme by blocking a form of response conflict: a sober man who is insulted at a party feels the pull to hit back but is restrained by the thought of his job and his family; drunk, he sees only the insult. If the most salient cue happens to be a person asking for help, the same narrowing can make him unusually helpful. Secondly, the same process can inflate self-evaluation, which is why drinkers feel more attractive and capable than they are. Thirdly, alcohol myopia combined with a distracting activity reliably reduces anxiety and depression, because it becomes hard to attend to the thoughts that cause them; but a person who drinks alone with nothing to do but brood may feel worse. The theory explains the dangerous effects (violence, unsafe sex, drunk driving) and the prized ones, and it does so through what alcohol does to perception and thought rather than through a direct chemical "aggression effect" or through expectations alone. The role of expectations, studied with the balanced-placebo design, is described in Section 10.2.

Heavy drinking can also wipe out memory for part of the evening. A blackout is amnesia for events that occurred during intoxication. It is not passing out: during a blackout the person was awake, talking, walking, perhaps driving, and to those around him he may not have seemed especially drunk. In 1969 the psychiatrists Donald W. Goodwin, J. Bruce Crane and Samuel Guze reviewed the subject and interviewed 100 hospitalised alcoholics about their blackouts, and they described two kinds. In an en bloc blackout a whole stretch of time, with a sharp beginning and end, is simply missing and cannot be recovered even with reminders. In a fragmentary blackout (sometimes called a grey-out) the memory has holes: islands of recollection remain, and reminders can bring back some of the rest. Blackouts reflect a failure to lay down new memories rather than a loss of memories already stored; the NMDA blockade described in Section 10.3.2 disrupts the hippocampal processes that consolidate new events. DSM-5-TR links them to high blood levels and perhaps to the speed with which the level rises, which is why gulping spirits on an empty stomach is the classic setting, and notes that women who drink heavily may be more vulnerable to them than men.

Two rarer pictures complete the account of intoxication. The first is pathological intoxication, known in older texts by the Latin name mania a potu ("madness from drink"). ICD-10 lists it as a complication of acute intoxication (code F10.07) that applies only to alcohol: a sudden outburst of aggressive, often violent behaviour, quite unlike the person's sober character, after an amount of alcohol that would not intoxicate most people, usually followed by sleep and amnesia for the episode. DSM-III and DSM-III-R had a similar category (alcohol idiosyncratic intoxication), but DSM-IV dropped it, because the evidence that such a syndrome exists separately from ordinary drunken violence, epilepsy or other causes is thin. It is best regarded as a doubtful entity that is still examined because ICD-10 retains it.

The second is acute alcohol poisoning, which is not rare at all. It follows the rapid drinking of large amounts, typically in drinking games, competitive "bottoms-up" sessions in hostels, or by a teenager drinking spirits for the first time, and it pushes the BAC into the range at which breathing slows and the gag reflex is lost. Death comes from respiratory depression, from inhaling vomit, from hypothermia, or from a fall in blood sugar (alcohol blocks the liver's production of glucose, a particular danger for children and the malnourished). The practical rules follow from the pharmacology: a person who cannot be roused is a medical emergency, not someone to be left to sleep it off; he should be placed on his side so that vomit drains out of the mouth, never left alone, and taken to hospital, where blood sugar is checked and corrected (with thiamine, Section 10.4.3) and other causes of coma, including head injury and sedative overdose, are looked for.

Exam trap

A blackout is amnesia for a period of intoxication during which the person was awake and acting; it is not fainting or passing out. En bloc blackouts are complete, with sharp edges, and unrecoverable; fragmentary ones are patchy, and cues can recover parts of them. Pathological intoxication (mania a potu, ICD-10 F10.07) means violent behaviour after a small amount of alcohol, out of keeping with the sober person; it is not simply heavy drunkenness, and modern DSM does not include it. And remember the biphasic rule: stimulation on the rising limb of the BAC curve, depression on the falling limb.

Tolerance, neuroadaptation and kindling

A man who has been drinking a quarter of whisky every night for ten years does not behave like the 70 kg man in Section 10.3.1 who drank the same quarter for the first time. He has developed tolerance, the need for markedly more of a drug to get the same effect, or a smaller effect from the same amount (Section 10.1). With alcohol, tolerance comes in several forms. A small part is metabolic: as you saw, regular drinking induces the liver's CYP2E1 system, so alcohol is cleared somewhat faster. But Pinel's textbook stresses that this increased metabolic efficiency contributes only slightly to overall alcohol tolerance; most alcohol tolerance is functional tolerance, a change in the brain itself so that the same blood level has less effect. There is even acute tolerance within a single drinking session: at the same blood level, a drinker is less impaired on the falling limb of the curve than he was on the rising limb, a finding known as the Mellanby effect after the British physiologist Edward Mellanby. Learned and situation-specific tolerance, the part played by conditioning, is described in Section 10.2. Because alcohol, benzodiazepines and barbiturates share a receptor, tolerance to one brings tolerance to the others (cross-tolerance, Section 10.1): heavy drinkers often need larger doses of sedatives and anaesthetics.

Tolerance can also be lost. Late in a long drinking career, when the liver is failing or the brain has been damaged, some drinkers become drunk on far less than they used to drink. This late loss of tolerance is sometimes called reverse tolerance, but be careful with the term: Section 10.1 uses reverse tolerance for sensitisation, the increasing response to repeated doses of stimulants, and the two meanings should not be confused.

What exactly changes in the brain? The answer, called neuroadaptation, is that the brain adjusts its own chemistry to cancel out a drug that is always present. Recall that alcohol strengthens the GABA brake and weakens the glutamate accelerator. A brain bathed in alcohol every day responds by making its GABA-A receptors fewer and less sensitive and by making more NMDA receptors, and the noradrenaline system of the brain stem, which drives arousal, sweating and a racing heart, becomes more active. With alcohol present these changes restore something close to normal function: that is tolerance. But the brain is now balanced only as long as the alcohol keeps coming (Figure 10.8). Take the alcohol away suddenly and the weakened brake and the strengthened accelerator are left unopposed. The result is a hyperexcitable nervous system: tremor, anxiety, sweating and a fast pulse first, then seizures and, at the extreme, delirium. This is why alcohol withdrawal is the mirror image of alcohol intoxication, and why it is relieved by alcohol itself or by a drug that acts on the same GABA-A receptor, such as a benzodiazepine.

Even a single heavy evening produces a small version of this. Pinel's textbook describes the hangover as a withdrawal syndrome euphemistically named: headache, nausea, vomiting and tremor the morning after a single bout of drinking. Dehydration adds to it, because alcohol is a diuretic, and so do low blood sugar, acetaldehyde, poor sleep and the flavouring chemicals (congeners) in dark spirits. The hangover matters clinically because a drink relieves it. The morning drink taken to steady the hands, the "eye-opener", is relief drinking, one of the elements of the dependence syndrome described by Griffith Edwards and Milton Gross (Section 10.1), and it is one of the four CAGE questions you will meet in Section 10.3.8.

Something else changes with each episode of withdrawal. In the late 1960s researchers discovered that if a weak electrical current is passed into a rat's amygdala once a day, at first nothing happens, but after many days the same weak current produces a full convulsion; the brain has been permanently sensitised. This phenomenon was called kindling, by analogy with the small sticks that light a fire. In 1978 the American psychiatrists James C. Ballenger and Robert M. Post proposed in the British Journal of Psychiatry that alcohol withdrawal works the same way. They reviewed evidence that the severity of withdrawal increases over years of drinking in a stepwise fashion, and argued that the overexcitement of each withdrawal kindles the brain a little further, so that a drinker whose early withdrawals were only the shakes may, years later, have seizures and delirium tremens. The kindling model fits a clinical observation that DSM-5-TR also records: people who have had more withdrawal episodes in the past have more severe withdrawal now. It also has a practical implication, which Section 10.4.3 takes up: every withdrawal should be treated properly, and repeated unsupervised "drying out" at home may be doing harm.

Clinical link

When you take a drinking history, ask about previous withdrawals, not just about drinking. "Have you ever had the shakes in the morning? Have you ever had a fit, or seen or heard things, when you stopped?" A history of withdrawal seizures or delirium tremens is the strongest single warning that the next withdrawal may be dangerous, and it is the main reason a patient is detoxified in hospital rather than at home.

Exam trap

Most alcohol tolerance is functional (pharmacodynamic), a change in the brain, not metabolic; the liver's faster clearance contributes only a little. Withdrawal is the mirror image of intoxication because of neuroadaptation: fewer and less sensitive GABA-A receptors, more NMDA receptors and an overactive noradrenaline system. The kindling model of increasingly severe withdrawals is Ballenger and Post (1978). And "reverse tolerance" has two meanings: sensitisation to stimulants (Section 10.1), and the late loss of tolerance in a drinker with a failing liver.

The alcohol withdrawal syndrome: from the shakes to delirium tremens

Jaswinder is 52, a long-distance truck driver from Ludhiana. He is admitted to the orthopaedic ward of a district hospital after his truck overturns on the highway, and the next morning a surgeon pins his broken thigh bone. Nobody asks about alcohol. On his second evening in hospital his hands tremble so much that he spills his tea, he is sweating, his pulse is 110 and he cannot sleep; the nurse puts it down to pain. On the third night, about sixty hours after his last drink, he is a different man. He pulls out his drip and tries to climb out of bed, shouting that he must unload the truck before the dhaba closes. He brushes furiously at his arms because, he says, ants are crawling all over him, and he points at small men in khaki uniforms climbing the bed rails. He does not know where he is or what day it is, and he cannot keep his attention on the doctor's questions for more than a few seconds. His temperature is 38.6 °C, his pulse 136, and he is drenched in sweat and shaking. During the next morning he is quieter and half-recognises his son, but by evening he is worse again. Only now does his wife tell the duty psychiatrist that he has drunk a 375 ml bottle of whisky (about thirteen standard drinks) every evening for fifteen years. Jaswinder has delirium tremens, and he is moved to the intensive care unit.

For a long time doctors argued about what caused pictures like this. Some blamed malnutrition, some infection or head injury, and some the alcohol itself; delirium tremens often appeared when a drinker had stopped, but stopping was usually forced by an illness or injury that might have been the real cause. The question was settled by an experiment that could not be done today.

Key study: Isbell and the experimental "rum fits" (1955)

At the Addiction Research Center in Lexington, Kentucky, Harris Isbell (1910 to 1994) and his colleagues gave large amounts of alcohol to volunteers, former narcotic users at the centre. Of ten men who began, six were kept continuously and moderately intoxicated for between 48 and 87 days. Then the alcohol was stopped abruptly. All six developed tremor and weakness, two had convulsions, and four of the six developed hallucinations or delirium; one man's hallucinations included a shrunken, disembodied head whose eyes followed him and snakes coming up out of the floor. Because the seizures and delirium appeared only when the drinking stopped, after weeks of continuous intoxication had produced none, the study, published in 1955 as "An experimental study of the etiology of 'rum fits' and delirium tremens", showed that alcohol produces physical dependence and that withdrawal, not intoxication, precipitates seizures and delirium tremens. The criticism is ethical: volunteers were deliberately made dependent and then put through a withdrawal known to be dangerous, an experiment no ethics committee would approve today.

Two years earlier, in 1953, the neurologists Maurice Victor and Raymond D. Adams had studied 226 men admitted to Boston City Hospital with alcohol-related illness, observing them from six to eight hours after their last drink until three or four days later. They described a spectrum that ran from mild tremor (the shakes or jitters) through seizures and hallucinations to delirium tremens, and they described the typical times at which each appeared. Their timetable, refined since, is the one clinicians still use.

DSM-5-TR defines alcohol withdrawal by four requirements. There must have been heavy and prolonged drinking that has stopped or been cut down. Within several hours to a few days, at least two of eight signs must appear: autonomic hyperactivity (sweating, or a pulse over 100), increased hand tremor, insomnia, nausea or vomiting, transient visual, tactile or auditory hallucinations or illusions, psychomotor agitation, anxiety, and generalised tonic-clonic seizures. Read the list and you can see the hyperexcitable brain of Figure 10.8: everything on it is overactivity. The signs must cause distress or impairment, and they must not be better explained by another illness or by withdrawal from another drug (sedative withdrawal looks almost identical). The specifier "with perceptual disturbances" is used in the uncommon case in which hallucinations occur while the person knows they are not real (intact reality testing), or illusions occur without a delirium.

DSM-5-TR also gives the timetable. Symptoms begin when the blood level falls sharply, within about 4 to 12 hours of the last drink; reflecting the fast metabolism of alcohol, they usually peak during the second day of abstinence and improve markedly by the fourth or fifth day. Fewer than 10 percent of people who develop withdrawal ever have dramatic symptoms (severe autonomic overactivity, marked tremor, delirium), and fewer than 3 percent have seizures. Withdrawal is more likely the more and the longer a person has drunk (DSM-5-TR says most people with the condition have been drinking more than about eight drinks a day for many days), is uncommon under the age of 30, and becomes more frequent and severe with age. Figure 10.9 lays out the four syndromes on this clock. Treat its windows as typical rather than fixed: different textbooks give slightly different hours (Pinel's, for example, places the onset of delirium tremens at three to five days), and individuals vary.

Minor withdrawal. Most people who go through withdrawal have only this stage, the shakes. Within the first day the hands (and sometimes the tongue and eyelids) develop a coarse tremor that is worse when the person tries to hold something; the drinker feels jittery and on edge, sweats, has a fast pulse and a raised blood pressure, loses his appetite, feels sick or vomits, and lies awake or sleeps fitfully with vivid dreams. He startles easily and is irritable and anxious. Brief illusions are common (a coat on a hook seems to move), and some people have fleeting hallucinations that they recognise as unreal. A drink abolishes all of it within minutes, which is exactly why a dependent drinker's first act of the day may be to drink. After the acute stage has passed, a milder protracted withdrawal may linger: DSM-5-TR notes that anxiety, insomnia and autonomic instability can persist at low intensity for 3 to 6 months, and these lingering symptoms are a common trigger of relapse (and the target of acamprosate, Section 10.4.5).

Withdrawal seizures. Between about 12 and 48 hours after the last drink, a small minority have generalised tonic-clonic convulsions, which drinkers and older textbooks call rum fits. Usually there is a single seizure or a short burst of two or three. The risk is higher in people who have had withdrawal seizures before (the kindling of Section 10.3.4) and, DSM-5-TR notes, in people with epilepsy or a past severe head injury. A seizure that does not fit the pattern (one that is focal, that starts well after the second day, or that does not stop) should make the doctor look for another cause: a head injury with bleeding inside the skull, low blood sugar, low sodium, or meningitis. A withdrawal seizure is also a warning sign that the withdrawal is a severe one, and it calls for treatment, not just observation (Section 10.4.3).

Alcoholic hallucinosis. Gopi, 46, a carpenter in Coimbatore, has drunk arrack heavily for twenty years. Two days after he stops drinking because of stomach pain, he begins to hear men's voices outside his window. They talk about him in the third person: "That drunkard is finished. Tonight we will come for him." They accuse him of stealing from his employer and say his wife has gone to the police. Gopi is terrified. He barricades the door and later runs to the neighbour's house to hide, and his family bring him to hospital. On examination he is fully alert. He knows the date, the place and the doctor's name, his attention is good, and he can give a coherent account of the voices, which he is quite sure are real. This is alcoholic hallucinosis, a term coined in 1900 by the German neuropsychiatrist Carl Wernicke (1848 to 1905), the same Wernicke whose encephalopathy you will meet in Section 10.11. Its defining features are vivid hallucinations, mostly auditory and often threatening, accusing or insulting, occurring in clear consciousness, with orientation and attention intact. The voices frequently comment on the patient in the third person, and the patient may act on them in fear: barricading himself in, fleeing, calling the police, attacking the supposed persecutors, or attempting suicide. Delusions of persecution may grow out of the voices. It usually begins within about 12 to 48 hours of stopping or sharply reducing heavy drinking, though it can occur during continued drinking.

In DSM-5-TR alcoholic hallucinosis is diagnosed as alcohol-induced psychotic disorder (with hallucinations); the manual notes that this usually occurs only after prolonged, heavy drinking in people with moderate to severe alcohol use disorder and that the hallucinations are generally auditory, and it estimates that alcohol-induced psychotic episodes are seen in fewer than 5 percent of people with alcohol use disorder. ICD-10 codes it within the psychotic disorders due to alcohol as F10.52, the predominantly hallucinatory form; the same F10.5 group includes alcoholic jealousy and alcoholic paranoia. Most episodes resolve within days to a few weeks of abstinence, and a minority persist for months. Because the picture can look exactly like paranoid schizophrenia, the distinction rests on the history: a long period of heavy drinking, onset when drinking stopped or fell, no earlier psychotic illness, and recovery with abstinence. Eugen Bleuler suspected that such cases were really schizophrenia released by alcohol, and the minority that become chronic keep that debate alive; substance-induced psychosis in general is discussed in Chapter 4, Section 4.5.6.

Delirium tremens. The most dangerous withdrawal syndrome was named in 1813 by the English physician Thomas Sutton, who described it in his Tracts on Delirium Tremens and separated it from phrenitis, the delirium of fever and brain inflammation; the name means "trembling delirium". Delirium tremens (the DTs) is a delirium, a disturbance of consciousness and attention (Section 10.8 describes delirium in general), produced by alcohol withdrawal, and Jaswinder showed every part of it. Consciousness is clouded and fluctuates, worse at night; the patient is disoriented in time and place, cannot sustain attention, and misidentifies people and surroundings. Hallucinations are vivid and mainly visual and tactile: insects, snakes, rats and other animals, often moving, and sometimes tiny people or animals, the Lilliputian hallucinations named after the miniature inhabitants of Lilliput in Jonathan Swift's Gulliver's Travels and famously described in 1909 by the French psychiatrist Raoul Leroy. The feeling of insects crawling on or under the skin is formication (from the Latin formica, ant), a tactile hallucination that is also typical of stimulant intoxication (Section 10.6 and Chapter 2, Section 2.3). Many patients act out their ordinary work, as Jaswinder tried to unload his truck, a picture called occupational delirium. Fear and agitation are marked, and patients are highly suggestible. Around the mental state is a storm of autonomic overactivity: coarse tremor, profuse sweating, fever, a racing pulse, raised blood pressure and complete sleeplessness, with the dehydration and loss of potassium and magnesium that follow.

Delirium tremens usually begins about 48 to 72 hours after the last drink (the second or third day of abstinence), occasionally later, up to about 96 hours, and typically lasts two or three days, sometimes up to a week, often ending in a long, deep sleep. DSM-5-TR classes it as alcohol withdrawal delirium, one of the substance withdrawal deliriums in its neurocognitive disorders chapter, and ICD-10 as withdrawal state with delirium (F10.4). Fewer than one in ten people in withdrawal reach this stage, but it kills. Early studies reported death in as many as 15 percent of cases; with modern treatment deaths have become uncommon (the 2004 practice guideline of the American Society of Addiction Medicine cites recent series with a mortality of 0 to 1 percent, although textbooks often still quote a few percent). When death occurs it comes from cardiac arrhythmia, high fever, dehydration and electrolyte collapse, pneumonia and injuries. DSM-5-TR lists the predictors of severe withdrawal (a previous delirium or severe withdrawal, a low blood potassium, a low platelet count, and high systolic blood pressure) and warns that when withdrawal delirium develops, a clinically relevant medical condition is likely to be present as well: liver failure, pneumonia, gastrointestinal bleeding, the effects of a head injury, low blood sugar, an electrolyte imbalance or the aftermath of surgery. Jaswinder had one of these, a recent operation, and his doctors also had to rule out a head injury from the crash. Thiamine deficiency may also be present, and Wernicke's encephalopathy (Section 10.11) can hide inside the confusion of delirium tremens.

Table 10.16 The four alcohol withdrawal syndromes compared.
FeatureMinor withdrawalWithdrawal seizuresAlcoholic hallucinosisDelirium tremens
Usual onset after last drink6 to 24 hours12 to 48 hours12 to 48 hours48 to 72 hours (up to about 96)
ConsciousnessClearLost during the fitClear; orientedClouded, fluctuating; disoriented
HallucinationsBrief illusions, usually with insightNoneMainly auditory: threatening, accusing voicesMainly visual and tactile: animals, insects, Lilliputian figures, formication
Autonomic signsMild to moderateVariableMildSevere: fever, tachycardia, sweating
DangerLowInjury; may herald deliriumActs on voices; suicideLife-threatening
ClassificationDSM-5-TR alcohol withdrawal; ICD-10 F10.3A sign of alcohol withdrawalAlcohol-induced psychotic disorder; ICD-10 F10.52Alcohol withdrawal delirium; ICD-10 F10.4

On the ward, the severity of withdrawal is tracked with a rating scale, most often the revised Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol (CIWA-Ar) of John Sullivan and colleagues (1989), a ten-item scale scored from 0 to 67 by a nurse or doctor at the bedside; its details belong to the Assessment book, and its use in deciding medication doses is described in Section 10.4.3.

Clinical link

Jaswinder's case is not unusual. DSM-5-TR warns that unanticipated alcohol withdrawal in hospitalised people whose alcohol use disorder has been overlooked adds to the risks and costs of their stay. Surgical, orthopaedic and medical wards are full of hidden drinkers, and their withdrawal typically appears on the second or third day after admission, just when an operation or injury seems to explain the confusion. Asking every newly admitted adult how much they drink, and asking a relative when the patient cannot answer, is one of the cheapest life-saving habits in medicine.

Exam trap

Do not mix up the two hallucinatory syndromes. Alcoholic hallucinosis: mainly auditory hallucinations (threatening voices) in clear consciousness, orientation intact, little autonomic disturbance, usually 12 to 48 hours after stopping. Delirium tremens: mainly visual and tactile hallucinations (animals, insects, Lilliputian people, formication) with clouded consciousness, disorientation, coarse tremor and severe autonomic overactivity, usually 48 to 72 hours after stopping, and potentially fatal. The order on the clock is tremor first, then seizures and hallucinosis, then delirium tremens. Thomas Sutton named delirium tremens (1813); Carl Wernicke named alcoholic hallucinosis (1900).

Alcohol and the mind: depression, anxiety, jealousy, suicide and violence

Sushila, 41, teaches mathematics at a school in Pune. After her mother died three years ago she began taking a glass of wine to help her sleep; now she drinks most of a 750 ml bottle of vodka over two or three nights, kept behind the saris in her cupboard. She comes to a psychiatrist complaining of depression, and on the face of it she has a major depressive episode: low mood most of the day, loss of interest in her classes, early waking, poor concentration, guilt, and the thought that her family would be better off without her. Only on careful questioning does the drinking emerge. Her psychiatrist does something that surprises her: after arranging detoxification and making sure she is safe, she decides to wait before prescribing an antidepressant. By the third week without alcohol Sushila is sleeping through the night, her mood has lifted and the thoughts of death have gone. She did have a depressive syndrome, but it was produced by the alcohol.

Sushila's story illustrates the most important rule about the psychiatric syndromes that heavy drinking produces. DSM-5-TR describes a family of alcohol-induced mental disorders (alcohol-induced depressive, bipolar, anxiety, psychotic, sleep, sexual and neurocognitive disorders, each listed in the chapter of the disorder it resembles), diagnosed when the symptoms are severe enough to need attention in their own right. They look exactly like the independent disorders, and they can have the same grave consequences, including suicide attempts. What separates them is time. An independent disorder is more likely if the symptoms began before the heavy drinking or continue for more than a month after severe intoxication or withdrawal has ended; alcohol-induced disorders, in contrast, are likely to improve without specific treatment within days to weeks of stopping. The numbers show why this matters. DSM-5-TR estimates that about 40 percent of people with alcohol use disorder have a major depressive episode at some point in their lives, but that only about a third to a half of these are independent depressions occurring outside the context of intoxication. In practice, therefore, clinicians usually wait for two to four weeks of abstinence before diagnosing an independent depressive or anxiety disorder and starting long-term treatment for it, unless the depression is severe, the person is at high risk of suicide, or there is a clear history of depression before the drinking began.

Anxiety follows the same logic. The overactive nervous system of withdrawal produces tremor, a racing heart, restlessness and panic attacks, and a person who drinks every evening goes through a small withdrawal every morning. Drinking also relieves anxiety in the short term, so people with social anxiety or panic may use alcohol as self-medication (Section 10.2 and Chapter 6), and the two problems can feed each other. Alcohol disturbs sleep too: it helps a person fall asleep, but as the blood level falls in the second half of the night sleep becomes fragmented and dream (REM) sleep rebounds, and insomnia often persists for months after stopping (Chapter 8, Section 8.10). Sexual function suffers in the way the porter in Shakespeare's Macbeth described: drink "provokes the desire, but it takes away the performance". Alcoholic hallucinosis, the commonest alcohol-induced psychotic disorder, was described in Section 10.3.5.

One alcohol-related syndrome is notorious for its danger to other people. Morbid jealousy (pathological jealousy) is a preoccupation with a partner's supposed infidelity that is held on inadequate evidence and drives the person's behaviour. In 1955 the British psychiatrists John Todd and Kenneth Dewhurst called it the Othello syndrome, after Shakespeare's Moor, who murders his innocent wife Desdemona on the strength of a planted handkerchief. When it arises in a heavy drinker it is often called alcoholic jealousy, and ICD-10 lists it among the psychotic disorders due to alcohol (F10.5). The belief may be a true delusion or an overvalued idea. A 48-year-old mill worker becomes convinced that his wife is meeting a neighbour while he is at work; he checks her phone, inspects her clothes and bedsheets for stains, times her trips to the market, questions the children, follows her, and demands a confession, which (when a frightened wife finally gives a false one to stop the interrogation) only confirms his belief. Several things in a drinker's life feed the idea: alcohol-induced impotence, a wife who has understandably withdrawn from a drunken husband, and the suspicion and irritability of withdrawal. The jealousy does not always disappear with abstinence. It carries a real risk of assault and of homicide, so assessment always includes direct questions about threats, weapons and the partner's safety, and separation is sometimes the safest advice. Delusional jealousy as a feature of delusional disorder is taught in Chapter 4, Section 4.5.

Alcohol is also tightly linked with suicide. DSM-5-TR summarises the evidence: both intoxication and chronic heavy drinking are associated with suicide; in a meta-analysis, acute alcohol use was associated with a nearly sevenfold increase in the risk of a suicide attempt compared with not drinking; and an international study of emergency departments in 17 countries found that each drink raised the risk of an attempt by about 30 percent. Intoxication removes the restraint that holds a suicidal impulse back (alcohol myopia again), chronic drinking brings depression, debt, job loss and broken relationships, and the combination of alcohol with sedative tablets is especially lethal. Violence follows a similar pattern. DSM-5-TR calls intoxication an important contributor to interpersonal violence and notes that severe alcohol use disorder, especially with antisocial personality disorder, is associated with criminal acts including homicide. In India, surveys of domestic violence consistently find a husband's heavy drinking among the strongest correlates of violence against his wife. Suicide risk assessment is taught in Chapter 5.

Clinical link

When a heavy drinker presents with depression, treat the safety first and the diagnosis second. Assess suicide risk at once, because intoxicated and recently detoxified drinkers are at high risk. Then, unless the depression is severe or clearly predates the drinking, review the mood after two to four weeks of abstinence before committing the person to an antidepressant. Many depressions will have lifted; the ones that have not are probably independent and need treatment in their own right.

Exam trap

The Othello syndrome (Todd and Dewhurst, 1955) is morbid jealousy, a delusion or overvalued idea that the partner is unfaithful; in drinkers it is called alcoholic jealousy. Do not confuse it with erotomania (de Clérambault's syndrome, the delusion that someone, usually of higher status, is in love with the patient) or with the Capgras delusion (a familiar person has been replaced by an impostor). For alcohol-induced depression, DSM-5-TR's dividing line is about one month after stopping; clinically, wait two to four weeks of abstinence before diagnosing independent depression.

The damage done to the body

Pinel's textbook puts it bluntly: alcohol attacks almost every tissue in the body. It does so in three ways. It is directly toxic, and so is acetaldehyde; it displaces food and blocks the absorption of vitamins, especially thiamine and folate; and it causes accidents, falls and fights. Table 10.17 lists the main harms system by system. DSM-5-TR adds a sense of proportion: gastritis and ulcers are common, about 15 percent of people who drink heavily develop cirrhosis of the liver, pancreatitis or both, and mild high blood pressure is one of the most commonly associated conditions.

Table 10.17 The physical harm of heavy drinking.
SystemMain harmsPoints to note
LiverFatty liver; alcoholic hepatitis; cirrhosis; liver cancerFatty liver reverses with abstinence; cirrhosis is scarring and is permanent. Cirrhosis brings portal hypertension (oesophageal varices that can bleed, ascites) and hepatic encephalopathy (Section 10.8). Pinel calls cirrhosis the major cause of death among heavy drinkers
Gut and pancreasGastritis, ulcers, acute and chronic pancreatitis, malabsorptionChronic pancreatitis can cause diabetes; poor absorption deepens thiamine and folate deficiency
Heart and circulationHigh blood pressure, cardiomyopathy, irregular heart rhythm, strokeRaised triglycerides and LDL cholesterol add to heart disease risk (DSM-5-TR)
Nervous systemPeripheral neuropathy; cerebellar degeneration; brain shrinkage and cognitive decline; Wernicke-Korsakoff syndromeNeuropathy gives numb, burning feet and weakness; cerebellar damage gives a broad-based, unsteady gait. Rarer: Marchiafava-Bignami disease and central pontine myelinolysis
CancerMouth, throat, oesophagus, stomach, liver, bowel, breastPinel notes raised risk even at one or two drinks a day
Blood and immunityLarge red cells (raised MCV), anaemia, low platelets, lowered immunityHeavy drinkers are prone to pneumonia and tuberculosis
Hormones and metabolismLow blood sugar; low testosterone, shrunken testes, erectile dysfunction; menstrual irregularityAlcohol blocks the liver's production of glucose, so a fasting drinker can become hypoglycaemic
PregnancyMiscarriage; fetal alcohol spectrum disorderNo safe amount or time in pregnancy has been established (Chapter 3, Section 3.3.5)

Two rare brain disorders appear in the table because examiners like them. Marchiafava-Bignami disease is a destruction of the myelin and nerve fibres of the corpus callosum, the great bridge between the two hemispheres. The Italian pathologists Ettore Marchiafava and Amico Bignami described it in 1903 in a drinker of Chianti red wine; it causes confusion, dementia and signs that the hemispheres have been disconnected, such as apraxia of one hand. Central pontine myelinolysis, described by Raymond Adams, Maurice Victor and Elliott Mancall in 1959 in alcoholic and malnourished patients, is a loss of myelin in the pons of the brain stem, causing paralysis of the limbs, difficulty swallowing and speaking, and sometimes a locked-in state; it is now known to follow correcting a low blood sodium too quickly, a common problem in drinkers. The most important alcohol-related brain disorder of all, Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome, belongs to Section 10.11, and the general shrinkage of the brain and alcohol-related dementia are also discussed there.

Is a little alcohol good for you? For decades studies seemed to show a J-shaped curve: moderate drinkers had fewer heart attacks than abstainers, and some studies even suggested that moderate drinking protected the ageing brain. The finding has weakened under scrutiny. Many "abstainers" in such studies are former drinkers who stopped because they were already ill, which makes non-drinking look harmful; when studies correct for this, the protective effect largely disappears. Meanwhile the cancer risk starts at low levels of drinking, as Pinel notes. In 2023 the WHO's European office went so far as to state that no level of alcohol consumption is safe for health. For a clinician, the practical message is simpler: there is no medical reason to advise a non-drinker to start.

Exam trap

Match the alcohol-related brain disorders to their sites and causes. Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome: thiamine deficiency; mammillary bodies and thalamus (Section 10.11). Marchiafava-Bignami disease: the corpus callosum (Italian red-wine drinkers, 1903). Central pontine myelinolysis: the pons, after over-rapid correction of low sodium. Cerebellar degeneration: the cerebellum (unsteady, broad-based gait). Hepatic encephalopathy: a delirium caused by liver failure, with a flapping tremor (asterixis). Fetal alcohol spectrum disorder is taught in Chapter 3, not here.

Spotting problem drinking: questions and blood tests

Most people with alcohol problems are never asked about them, and those who are asked often minimise. Two kinds of tool help: short questionnaires and laboratory markers. Both belong in detail to the Assessment book; what follows is what a clinician needs to know to use and interpret them.

The shortest questionnaire is the CAGE questionnaire, published in the Journal of the American Medical Association in 1984 by the psychiatrist John A. Ewing as "Detecting alcoholism: the CAGE questionnaire". Its name is a mnemonic for four questions:

  • Cut down: Have you ever felt you should cut down on your drinking?
  • Annoyed: Have people annoyed you by criticising your drinking?
  • Guilty: Have you ever felt bad or guilty about your drinking?
  • Eye-opener: Have you ever had a drink first thing in the morning to steady your nerves or get rid of a hangover?

Two or more "yes" answers are usually taken as clinically significant. The CAGE asks about the whole of life rather than recent drinking and is good at picking up dependence, but it misses people whose drinking is risky without yet being dependent. For that the WHO developed the Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test (AUDIT), first issued in 1989 and described in its development paper by John Saunders and colleagues in 1993. It has ten items: three about how much and how often the person drinks, three about signs of dependence, and four about harm. Scores range from 0 to 40, and a score of 8 or more usually indicates hazardous or harmful drinking. Its first three items alone form the AUDIT-C, a quick screen used in busy clinics.

Blood tests add objective evidence, though DSM-5-TR stresses that they do not establish a diagnosis. The direct measure is the blood (or breath) alcohol concentration, which also reveals tolerance: a person who is talking normally at a BAC of 150 is a heavy drinker. Gamma-glutamyltransferase (GGT), a liver enzyme, is a sensitive marker: DSM-5-TR notes that a modest elevation (above about 35 units) may be the only abnormal finding, and that at least 70 percent of people with a high GGT are persistent heavy drinkers. Carbohydrate-deficient transferrin (CDT), a form of an iron-carrying blood protein that rises with regular heavy drinking (levels of 20 units or more), is at least as sensitive and specific, and the two together are better than either alone. Because both fall towards normal within days to weeks of stopping, a rise during follow-up is a sign that drinking has resumed. The mean corpuscular volume (MCV), the average size of the red blood cells, rises because alcohol is toxic to the bone marrow; it helps to identify heavy drinkers but is a poor way to monitor abstinence, because red cells live for about four months and the MCV changes slowly. Newer tests detect breakdown products of alcohol itself, such as ethyl glucuronide in urine and phosphatidylethanol in blood, and can show recent drinking even after the alcohol has gone. Questionnaires, blood tests and a relative's account together give the most accurate picture.

Exam trap

CAGE stands for Cut down, Annoyed, Guilty, Eye-opener (Ewing, 1984); the E is not "evening" or "excess". A score of 2 or more is significant. The AUDIT is the WHO's ten-item test (score 8 or more suggests hazardous drinking). Among blood markers, GGT and CDT return to normal within days to weeks and are good for monitoring; MCV changes slowly and is poor for monitoring. None of these tests makes the diagnosis on its own.

Alcohol in India

The national survey Magnitude of Substance Use in India, conducted in 2018 by the National Drug Dependence Treatment Centre (NDDTC) of the All India Institute of Medical Sciences for the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment and released in 2019 (its design is described in Section 10.1), found that alcohol is the substance Indians use most. About 14.6 percent of people aged 10 to 75, roughly 16 crore people, were current users of alcohol, and use was 17 times commoner among men than women. About 5.2 percent of Indians, more than 5.7 crore people, were estimated to be affected by harmful or dependent drinking; in the report's words, every third alcohol user in India needs help for alcohol-related problems. The states with the highest prevalence of alcohol use were Chhattisgarh, Tripura, Punjab, Arunachal Pradesh and Goa, and those in which more than 10 percent of people had alcohol use disorders were Tripura, Andhra Pradesh, Punjab, Chhattisgarh and Arunachal Pradesh.

What do Indians drink? The same survey found that country liquor ("desi" liquor, a cheap distilled spirit made under government licence, of varying strength) and Indian-made foreign liquor (IMFL: whisky, rum, brandy and gin manufactured in India) were each the main drink of about 30 percent of drinkers. Beer and wine make up much of the rest, alongside a large world of traditional and local drinks: toddy (fermented palm sap) in the south and east, arrack (a distilled spirit), mahua liquor made from the flowers of the mahua tree in the tribal belt of central India, rice beers such as handia, and feni in Goa.

Outside the licensed trade is illicitly distilled liquor, known as hooch, and it is responsible for the periodic tragedies in which dozens of people die after a single batch. The killer is usually methanol, industrial alcohol added to raise the strength or distilled by mistake. Methanol is metabolised by the same enzyme as ethanol, alcohol dehydrogenase, but its products are formaldehyde and formic acid, which cause severe acidosis of the blood and destroy the optic nerve; survivors may be left blind. The pharmacology of Section 10.3.1 explains the antidote. Alcohol dehydrogenase prefers ethanol to methanol, so giving ethanol (or fomepizole, a drug that blocks the enzyme) slows the conversion of methanol into its poisons while dialysis removes it. In June 2024 more than 60 people died in Kallakurichi district of Tamil Nadu after drinking methanol-laced illicit liquor; similar disasters have struck many states, including states where alcohol is banned.

That last point brings in prohibition. Article 47 of the Constitution of India directs the State to endeavour to bring about the prohibition of intoxicating drinks except for medicinal purposes, and several states have tried. Gujarat has had prohibition since it was formed in 1960, Nagaland since 1989, and Bihar since April 2016; Mizoram's current prohibition law dates from 2019, and the Union Territory of Lakshadweep is also dry. Supporters point to reduced domestic violence and spending on drink; critics point to bootlegging, hooch deaths, the criminalisation of poor drinkers and the loss of revenue. For the clinician, the lesson of prohibition states is that a ban changes where and what people drink more reliably than whether they drink, and that the people who need treatment are still there.

Indian context

The figures above matter in interviews. Alcohol is the commonest substance used in India (about 14.6 percent of 10 to 75 year olds), every third user needs help, and men use it far more than women. Two practical consequences follow. First, an Indian clinical psychologist in almost any setting, a general hospital, a school, a family court, an industrial clinic, will meet alcohol problems every week, often presented as something else (marital conflict, depression, a child's school failure, domestic violence). Secondly, the treatment gap is enormous, as Section 10.4.8 shows.

10.4 Treating Alcohol Use Disorder: Detoxification, Drugs and Staying SoberLower yield · 1 Q

Treating a person with alcohol use disorder involves two quite different jobs, and confusing them is the commonest mistake in the field. The first is to get the person safely off alcohol: to manage a withdrawal that, as Section 10.3.5 showed, can produce seizures and a delirium that kills. That job takes about a week and is mainly medical. The second is to keep the person off alcohol, or at least out of harm, for the months and years afterwards, when craving, old habits, friends, stress and the shop on the way home all pull him back. That job is longer, harder and mainly psychological and social, although several drugs help with it. This section begins with what happens to people with alcohol use disorder over the course of their lives, with and without treatment, then follows the treatment ladder from a brief conversation in a clinic to detoxification in hospital, the drugs that help people stay stopped, the psychological treatments (which Book 2 teaches in full), the debate about whether the goal must be total abstinence, and finally the services that exist in India.

The course of alcohol use disorder: who gets better?

The popular picture of alcoholism is a steady slide: social drinking, heavier drinking, loss of control, and ruin, unless the drinker hits "rock bottom" and is saved. Section 10.2 describes how E. M. Jellinek's stages gave scientific form to this picture. The best evidence on what actually happens comes from studies that followed large groups of people for decades, and the most famous of them was conducted by the American psychiatrist George E. Vaillant (born 1934).

Key study: Vaillant's natural history of alcoholism (1983)

Vaillant drew on two groups of men followed since the 1940s: 268 Harvard undergraduates (the college sample) and 456 socially disadvantaged but non-delinquent boys from inner-city Boston, originally recruited as the comparison group of a study of delinquency (the core city sample). Because the men were studied prospectively, by repeated interviews, questionnaires and medical examinations, from adolescence onwards, Vaillant could see what drinkers were like before their drinking became a problem, something no study of patients in treatment can do. He published the first full analysis in The Natural History of Alcoholism (1983) and later follow-ups to age 70 and 80. At some point in their lives 20 percent of the college men and 31 percent of the core city men met criteria for alcohol abuse. Much of the anxiety, depression and instability seen in alcoholic men, Vaillant argued, was more often the result of years of drinking than its cause. By the age of 70, of the alcohol-dependent core city men who could be followed, 54 percent had died, 32 percent were abstinent, 1 percent were drinking in a controlled way and only 12 percent were still abusing alcohol. Alcohol abuse could persist for decades without either remitting or progressing to dependence; previous dependence and attendance at Alcoholics Anonymous were the two best predictors of sustained abstinence; once abstinence had lasted five years relapse was rare; and the men who managed a lasting return to controlled drinking were those who had had few symptoms of abuse. The criticism is that the samples were all men, all American and all born in the 1920s and early 1930s, so the findings may not apply to women or to other cultures and generations.

Vaillant's results fit what DSM-5-TR says about the course of the disorder. It is variable, with periods of remission and relapse. A decision to stop, often in response to a crisis, is typically followed by weeks or more of abstinence, then by limited periods of controlled drinking; but once drinking resumes it is highly likely to escalate rapidly back to the old level. DSM-5-TR also corrects the gloomy stereotype. The disorder is often wrongly seen as intractable because the people clinicians meet in treatment have usually had many years of severe problems, but these most severe cases are only a minority, and the typical person with alcohol use disorder has a much more promising prognosis. Many people recover with no formal treatment at all, a process called natural recovery (or spontaneous remission); Barlow and Durand estimate that about 20 percent of people with severe alcohol dependence stop drinking on their own and do not develop problems again. Life events often trigger it: a health scare, a new relationship, a religious commitment, a new job, a child.

Two lessons follow for treatment. First, the course is not fixed, so treatment is worth attempting at every stage, and the people who come to treatment are not a hopeless group. Secondly, relapse is part of the natural history, not proof of failure; a treatment plan should expect it and prepare for it, which is what relapse prevention does (Section 10.4.6).

The treatment ladder and brief intervention

Not every person whose drinking is a problem needs a de-addiction ward. Most clinicians now think of treatment as a ladder, matched to severity and stepped up if the lower rungs fail (an approach called stepped care). At the bottom are the large numbers of people whose drinking is hazardous (risky) or harmful without being severely dependent. For them the evidence supports a brief intervention: one to a few short conversations, often of 5 to 30 minutes, delivered by a general practitioner, nurse or counsellor in primary care, in an emergency department after an injury, or in a workplace clinic. Many brief interventions follow an outline that William R. Miller and his colleagues summarised with the acronym FRAMES: Feedback about the person's own risk (for example, his AUDIT score or his GGT result), emphasis on personal Responsibility for change, clear Advice to cut down or stop, a Menu of options for how to do it, an Empathic style, and support for Self-efficacy, the person's belief that he can succeed. Reviews of many trials show that brief interventions produce modest but real reductions in drinking among non-dependent drinkers, which, multiplied over a population, prevent a great deal of harm. They are not enough for severe dependence. Brief interventions borrow heavily from motivational interviewing and from the stages of change of the transtheoretical model, both of which are taught in Book 2, Section 6.9 (the stages in Section 6.9.6).

Higher up the ladder are outpatient treatment for dependence (medication and psychological treatment while the person lives at home), detoxification (at home, as an outpatient, or in hospital), and residential rehabilitation followed by aftercare. How intensive should treatment be? Barlow and Durand summarise the evidence that outcomes after intensive residential programmes are often no better than after good outpatient care, which can cost a small fraction as much. Inpatient care is therefore kept for people who need it for safety: those with a history of withdrawal seizures or delirium tremens, severe current withdrawal, serious physical or psychiatric illness (including suicidal risk and psychosis), dependence on sedatives as well as alcohol, pregnancy, very heavy daily drinking that starts in the morning, no one at home who can supervise, or previous failed attempts at outpatient detoxification. The sequence of psychological care that follows detoxification, from engagement through active treatment to aftercare, is described in Book 2, Section 9.9.5.

Detoxification: getting safely through withdrawal

Ravinder, 45, runs a hardware shop in Kanpur. For eight years he has drunk two quarters of whisky (360 ml, about twelve standard drinks) every day, and for the last two years his hands have shaken in the mornings until he has his first drink. Two years ago, during a pilgrimage on which he could not drink, he had a fit. He comes to the de-addiction clinic because his wife has threatened to leave after he fell asleep at the shop counter in front of customers. Because of his previous seizure, the team admits him. His last drink is at nine at night; by eight the next morning he is tremulous, sweating, anxious and nauseated, with a pulse of 108. How should he be detoxified?

Detoxification (detox) is the medically supervised management of withdrawal. Its principle follows directly from the pharmacology of Sections 10.3.2 and 10.3.4. Ravinder's brain has adapted to a daily dose of a drug that enhances GABA-A inhibition. Stopping suddenly removes that support and leaves an overexcited brain. The answer is to replace alcohol with another drug that acts on the same GABA-A receptor, and then to withdraw that drug gradually over about a week, letting the brain readjust in small steps. The replacement works because of cross-tolerance and cross-dependence (Section 10.1): a benzodiazepine can suppress alcohol withdrawal because the two act on the same receptor complex. DSM-5-TR notes that the symptoms of alcohol withdrawal can be relieved by giving alcohol or benzodiazepines; alcohol itself is never used, because it is the problem and is toxic, but the benzodiazepines are the treatment of choice. A meta-analysis carried out by Michael F. Mayo-Smith for the American Society of Addiction Medicine in 1997 confirmed it: benzodiazepines reduce the severity of withdrawal and significantly reduce both delirium (by about 5 cases per 100 patients) and seizures (by about 8 per 100). Other drugs (beta-blockers, clonidine, carbamazepine) eased withdrawal symptoms, but the evidence was inadequate to show that they prevented delirium or seizures, and antipsychotics of the phenothiazine type were less effective than benzodiazepines at preventing both.

Which benzodiazepine? The usual choices are the long-acting ones, chlordiazepoxide (the first benzodiazepine, synthesised by Leo Sternbach at the Hoffmann-La Roche company and marketed in 1960) and diazepam. Both are broken down into active products that themselves last for days, so the blood level falls smoothly as the dose is reduced, the drug in effect tapers itself, and protection against seizures is good. Their disadvantage is the same property: in a person whose liver is failing, or in an elderly patient, they can accumulate and cause oversedation. For such patients clinicians use lorazepam or oxazepam, which are cleared by a simple chemical step (glucuronidation) that a damaged liver can still manage, and have no long-lived active products. Table 10.18 compares them.

Table 10.18 Benzodiazepines used in alcohol detoxification.
DrugDuration of actionLong-lived active productsTypical use in detoxification
ChlordiazepoxideLongYesStandard oral choice for uncomplicated withdrawal; smooth taper
DiazepamLong, fast onsetYesOral or intravenous; loading doses; seizures and delirium tremens
LorazepamIntermediateNoLiver disease, elderly patients; can be given by injection (also for seizures)
OxazepamShortNoLiver disease, elderly patients; by mouth only

How much, and on what schedule? There are three approaches. In a fixed-schedule regimen, the patient receives a set dose at set times (four times a day, for example) on day one, reduced step by step over five to ten days, with extra doses if symptoms break through. In a symptom-triggered regimen, nurses rate the withdrawal at regular intervals with the CIWA-Ar (Section 10.3.5), and a dose is given only when the score rises above a set threshold. In front-loading, large doses of a long-acting drug such as diazepam are given every hour or two until the patient is calm, after which the drug's long half-life carries him through. The symptom-triggered approach was tested in a classic trial.

Key study: Saitz and symptom-triggered detoxification (1994)

Richard Saitz and his colleagues, working in the detoxification unit of a Veterans Affairs medical centre in the United States, randomly assigned 101 patients admitted for alcohol withdrawal, in a double-blind trial, either to a standard fixed course of chlordiazepoxide four times daily (with extra doses as needed) or to chlordiazepoxide given only when a validated severity scale showed signs and symptoms of withdrawal. The median duration of treatment was 9 hours in the symptom-triggered group against 68 hours in the fixed-schedule group, and the total dose of chlordiazepoxide was 100 mg against 425 mg. There were no significant differences in the severity of withdrawal or in the number of seizures or cases of delirium tremens. The conclusion, published in the Journal of the American Medical Association, was that symptom-triggered therapy individualises treatment and gives less drug for a shorter time with equal safety. The limitation is in the patients: those with a history of seizures were excluded, and the method needs trained staff who check the patient regularly and patients who can describe their symptoms.

Symptom-triggered treatment is therefore preferred where nurses can monitor closely. A fixed schedule is safer where monitoring is thin, for patients who cannot report their symptoms reliably, and for those, like Ravinder, with a history of seizures or delirium, in whom one does not wait for symptoms to appear. Ravinder receives a fixed reducing course of chlordiazepoxide over a week, with extra doses allowed. His symptoms peak on the second day, as Figure 10.9 predicts, he has no fit, and by the fifth day he is eating and sleeping.

Benzodiazepines are not the whole of detoxification. Heavy drinkers are frequently short of thiamine (vitamin B1), and a brain short of thiamine can develop Wernicke's encephalopathy, the acute confusion, unsteadiness and eye-movement paralysis described in Section 10.11.
