Every patient in detoxification therefore receives thiamine, by injection when the risk is high (malnutrition, vomiting, any sign of Wernicke's encephalopathy), and, a rule every examiner knows, thiamine is given before glucose: the metabolism of glucose uses up thiamine, so a glucose drip given to a thiamine-depleted drinker can precipitate the encephalopathy. Fluids, a nourishing diet, other vitamins, and correction of low potassium, magnesium and sodium (slowly, because of central pontine myelinolysis, Section 10.3.7) complete the medical care, along with a calm, well-lit room, reassurance, and checks of pulse, blood pressure and temperature.

Seizures and delirium tremens need specific treatment. A withdrawal seizure is treated with a benzodiazepine. In a trial in Boston emergency departments published in 1999, Gail D'Onofrio and her colleagues gave patients who had just had an alcohol-related seizure either 2 mg of lorazepam intravenously or salt water; 3 percent of the lorazepam group had a second seizure in the next six hours, against 24 percent of the placebo group. The anti-epileptic drug phenytoin, by contrast, does not prevent withdrawal seizures: in a 1989 placebo-controlled trial by Brian K. Alldredge and colleagues, 6 of 45 patients had a further seizure on phenytoin and 6 of 45 on placebo. The reason is that withdrawal seizures arise from a brain-wide state of overexcitement, not from an epileptic focus. Delirium tremens is a medical emergency, usually managed in an intensive care unit. The core is high doses of benzodiazepines (often intravenous diazepam or lorazepam) given until the patient is lightly sedated, with intravenous fluids, correction of electrolytes, thiamine, cooling of fever, and a search for the medical conditions that so often accompany it (infection, bleeding, head injury, low blood sugar). An antipsychotic such as haloperidol may be added for severe agitation or hallucinations, but only alongside adequate benzodiazepine, because antipsychotics lower the seizure threshold and are less effective than benzodiazepines on their own. In the rare cases that do not respond, intensive care units use phenobarbital or anaesthetic drugs.

Not everyone needs a hospital bed. People with mild to moderate withdrawal, no history of seizures or delirium, no serious illness and a responsible relative at home can be detoxified as outpatients or at home, with daily review and small supplies of medication (a benzodiazepine taken with alcohol is dangerous, so drinking must stop). Whatever the setting, the most important fact about detoxification is what it does not do. It makes stopping safe and bearable, but on its own it does almost nothing to prevent relapse. Book 2, Section 9.9.5 puts it bluntly: detoxification is the doorway to treatment, not treatment itself. When Ravinder is ready for discharge, the real question is how he will stay sober.

Exam trap

Alcohol detoxification uses long-acting benzodiazepines (chlordiazepoxide, diazepam) because of cross-tolerance at the GABA-A receptor; in liver disease or old age use lorazepam or oxazepam. Give thiamine before glucose. Symptom-triggered dosing with the CIWA-Ar gives less drug for a shorter time with equal safety (Saitz, 1994). Withdrawal seizures respond to benzodiazepines, not to phenytoin. Antipsychotics are only an add-on in delirium tremens, never the main treatment. And detoxification is not treatment: it does not prevent relapse.

Disulfiram: the deterrent

On the day of discharge Ravinder's wife asks the question families in every de-addiction clinic ask: is there a tablet that will make him hate alcohol? There is something close to it, and its discovery was an accident. In 1937 a physician working in the American rubber industry reported that workers exposed to certain sulphur compounds used in making rubber became ill when they drank alcohol. A decade later, in Copenhagen, the pharmacologists Jens Hald and Erik Jacobsen of the drug company Medicinalco were testing one of those compounds, tetraethylthiuram disulphide, as a possible treatment for intestinal worms, and, as pharmacologists then often did, they took it themselves. Both found that after taking it they had become strikingly sensitive to alcohol: an ordinary drink made them unwell. Working with the psychiatrist Oluf Martensen-Larsen, they tried the drug on patients with alcoholism, and in December 1948 they reported their findings in The Lancet under the title "A drug sensitizing the organism to ethyl alcohol". The drug, disulfiram, was sold as Antabuse ("anti-abuse"), and the United States Food and Drug Administration approved it in 1951. It was the first medicine for alcohol dependence, and for decades it was the only one.

Disulfiram works on the second step of the pathway in Figure 10.7. Its breakdown products block aldehyde dehydrogenase irreversibly, so when a person taking it drinks, alcohol is converted to acetaldehyde as usual, but the acetaldehyde cannot be cleared and accumulates in the blood. Within minutes the disulfiram-ethanol reaction begins: the face and neck flush red and hot, the head throbs, the heart pounds and races, the person feels sick and vomits, sweats, becomes breathless and thirsty, and the blood pressure falls, with dizziness and sometimes fainting. The reaction usually lasts from half an hour to several hours, and its severity rises with the amount drunk. Severe reactions can include chest pain, irregular heart rhythm, heart failure, collapse, convulsions and, rarely, death. Because the enzyme is blocked irreversibly, the body must make new enzyme before alcohol is safe again, so a reaction can occur for up to about two weeks after the last tablet. For the same reason the first dose must not be taken until the person has been free of alcohol for at least 12 hours (many clinicians wait 24). The usual dose is a single tablet of about 250 mg each morning.

How does a drug that does nothing to craving help people stay sober? Not, for most patients, by making them ill: most people taking disulfiram as prescribed never experience the reaction, because they do not drink. Disulfiram is a deterrent, and it works through the person's knowledge and fear of what would happen. Clinicians often describe its effect as changing the decision. Without it, a person recovering from dependence must decide not to drink many times every day, at every craving, every invitation, every passing liquor shop. With it, he has to make only one decision, once a day, in the morning when his resolve is strongest: will I take my tablet? Once it is taken, drinking is off the menu for the day. This also explains its great weakness. Barlow and Durand note that non-compliance is a major concern: a person who stops the tablets for a few days can drink again, and many who mean to drink simply stop taking it. Disulfiram therefore works best when someone else watches it being swallowed: a spouse, a parent, a pharmacist or a nurse, often under an explicit agreement made in therapy (behavioural couples therapy, Section 10.4.6, frequently builds a daily supervised dose into the contract). Supervision is the single most important determinant of whether disulfiram works.

Key study: Does disulfiram work? Fuller (1986) and the De Sousa trials (2004 and 2005)

In the largest blinded trial, Richard K. Fuller and colleagues in the United States Veterans Administration randomly assigned 605 men to 250 mg of disulfiram, to 1 mg (a dose too small to block the enzyme, but which still carried the threat of a reaction), or to no disulfiram; all received counselling. After a year there were no differences in total abstinence or time to first drink. Men on 250 mg who did drink reported fewer drinking days, and in every group the men who took their tablets faithfully were more likely to stay abstinent. The puzzle is in the design: in a blinded trial every patient fears the reaction, so the deterrent is spread across all groups. A 2014 meta-analysis of 22 trials by M. D. Skinner and colleagues found exactly this pattern: blinded trials showed no benefit, but open-label trials, in which patients know what they are taking, showed disulfiram superior to control conditions and to naltrexone and acamprosate. Two open trials from Mumbai by Avinash De Sousa and A. De Sousa, in alcohol-dependent men whose family members agreed to accompany them to follow-up, illustrate it. Against naltrexone over a year (2004), 86 percent of the men on disulfiram stayed abstinent against 44 percent on naltrexone, and relapse came later (at a mean of 119 days against 63). Against acamprosate over eight months (2005), the figures were 88 against 46 percent. In both trials, however, craving was lower on the comparison drug. The criticism of open trials is obvious: patients and doctors knew the treatment, and the men were a selected, well-supported group.

Disulfiram has side effects of its own even without alcohol: drowsiness and fatigue, a metallic or garlic-like taste and bad breath, headache and skin rash. More serious but rare are inflammation of the liver (so liver tests are checked before and during treatment), damage to peripheral and optic nerves, and psychiatric effects. Disulfiram also blocks dopamine beta-hydroxylase, the enzyme that turns dopamine into noradrenaline, so dopamine levels rise; this can precipitate psychosis or worsen depression in vulnerable people, and it is why the drug has also been tried for cocaine dependence. It interacts with some other medicines, such as warfarin and phenytoin. It should not be given to people with coronary heart disease, heart failure or cardiomyopathy, uncontrolled high blood pressure or a past stroke (all of whom might not survive a severe reaction), psychosis, severe liver disease or pregnancy, to people at high risk of suicide or with marked impulsivity (who may drink deliberately), to those with cognitive impairment who cannot understand the risk, and to anyone who has not given fully informed consent.

Patients must also be warned about hidden alcohol, because even small amounts can produce a reaction: cough syrups and "tonics", many Ayurvedic asavas and arishtas (which contain alcohol produced by fermentation during their preparation), some mouthwashes, aftershave and perfumes on the skin, vinegar, some sauces, and desserts made with liqueurs. Ravinder agrees to take disulfiram each morning in front of his wife, who has been taught what the reaction looks like; the team gives him a card to carry stating that he takes the drug, in case he is ever brought to a hospital unconscious.

Clinical link

Families in India sometimes ask for disulfiram tablets that they can crush into the patient's food without telling him. The answer must be no. A person who does not know he has taken disulfiram may drink heavily and suffer a severe, even fatal, reaction without anyone understanding why; and secret medication violates the patient's right to informed consent, which the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 makes explicit for people with mental illness, a term that, under Section 2(1)(s) of the Act, includes mental conditions associated with the abuse of alcohol and drugs. A family's desperation is real and deserves a response, but the response is to bring the family into treatment openly, not to drug the patient behind his back.

Exam trap

Disulfiram works because it inhibits aldehyde dehydrogenase, so acetaldehyde accumulates and drinking produces aversive symptoms (flushing, throbbing headache, nausea and vomiting, palpitations, a fall in blood pressure). It is a deterrent. Each wrong option in the usual question belongs to another treatment. "Reduces dopaminergic reward signalling in the nucleus accumbens" describes naltrexone, which blocks the opioid-driven release of dopamine. "Blocks NMDA receptors to reduce craving" is closest to acamprosate, which dampens glutamate (NMDA) overactivity. "Acts as a partial agonist at GABA-A receptors" describes none of the standard alcohol drugs: the drugs that act on the GABA-A receptor are the benzodiazepines used in detoxification (and alcohol itself), and they are positive allosteric modulators that strengthen GABA's own action, not partial agonists; baclofen is an agonist at a different GABA receptor, GABA-B. Disulfiram does not reduce craving, does not block opioid receptors and is not a GABA agonist.

Naltrexone, acamprosate and the newer drugs

Disulfiram makes drinking unpleasant. The drugs developed since the 1990s try to do something subtler: to make drinking less rewarding, or to quieten the craving that drives relapse. The first was an old drug put to a new use. Naltrexone is an opioid receptor antagonist, a drug that occupies the opioid receptors (mainly the mu receptor) and blocks them, originally developed to treat heroin dependence. Recall from Section 10.3.2 that part of alcohol's pleasure comes from the brain's own opioids: alcohol releases endorphins, which act on mu receptors and release dopamine in the nucleus accumbens. Researchers reasoned that blocking those receptors should blunt the reward of drinking. In 1992 two research groups, one in Philadelphia led by Joseph R. Volpicelli and one at Yale led by Stephanie S. O'Malley, published placebo-controlled trials in the same issue of the Archives of General Psychiatry showing that alcohol-dependent patients given naltrexone relapsed to heavy drinking less often than those given placebo. On the strength of these results the Food and Drug Administration approved naltrexone for alcohol dependence in 1994, and a once-a-month injectable form followed in 2006.

What naltrexone does is easiest to see in a person who slips. A patient on naltrexone who takes a drink finds it less rewarding and feels less of the familiar pull to have another and another; a lapse is therefore less likely to turn into a full relapse. Naltrexone is consequently better at reducing heavy drinking than at producing total abstinence, and it can suit people whose goal is to cut down as well as those who want to stop. It is usually taken as a 50 mg tablet once a day. Its common side effects are nausea, headache, dizziness and tiredness. Its critical caution follows from its mechanism: because it blocks opioid receptors, it throws anyone who is physically dependent on opioids (heroin, morphine, tramadol, codeine cough syrups) into immediate withdrawal and blocks the pain relief of opioid analgesics, so patients must be opioid-free before starting and should carry a card saying they take it. Its use in opioid dependence itself is described in Section 10.5. It is avoided in acute hepatitis and liver failure, and liver tests are monitored.

Acamprosate (calcium acetylhomotaurinate) works on the other side of the balance. A chemical relative of GABA and of the amino acid taurine, it is thought to dampen the excess glutamate activity at NMDA receptors that persists for months after detoxification, the neurochemical basis of the protracted withdrawal of Section 10.3.5, with its restlessness, poor sleep and anxiety that drive "relief" craving. Barlow and Durand note that the brain mechanisms of its effects are still not well understood. Acamprosate does not interact with alcohol and does not make drinking unpleasant; it is used to help people who have already stopped to stay stopped. It was first approved in France in 1989 and in the United States in 2004. It is taken as two 333 mg tablets three times a day by adults over about 60 kg, and its common side effect is diarrhoea. It has a pharmacological virtue that matters a great deal in heavy drinkers: it is not broken down by the liver but excreted unchanged by the kidneys. It can therefore be used in liver disease, but it must be avoided in severe kidney disease. In European trials it modestly increased the number of people who stayed abstinent; in the largest American trial, it did not.

Key study: the COMBINE trial (2006)

Which works better, naltrexone, acamprosate or a psychological treatment, and do they add up? The COMBINE study, led by Raymond F. Anton and funded by the US National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism, randomised 1,383 recently abstinent alcohol-dependent volunteers at 11 American centres between 2001 and 2004. For 16 weeks, eight groups received medical management (regular brief meetings with a health professional) with naltrexone, acamprosate, both, or placebos, with or without a combined behavioural intervention delivered by specialist therapists; a ninth group received the behavioural intervention with no pills at all. Drinking fell substantially in every group. Patients given naltrexone, the behavioural intervention, or both did better than those given placebo and medical management alone (for example, about 80 against 75 percent of days abstinent), and naltrexone reduced the risk of a heavy drinking day. Acamprosate showed no effect, alone or in any combination, and no combination beat naltrexone or the behavioural intervention alone. Strikingly, the group with the behavioural intervention but no pills did worse than the groups given placebo pills and medical management: simply taking a pill and seeing a professional regularly had a therapeutic effect. The conclusion, published in the Journal of the American Medical Association, was that naltrexone with medical management could be delivered in ordinary health-care settings. The criticisms are that the participants were motivated volunteers who were already abstinent, and that the acamprosate result contradicts many European trials, perhaps because the European patients were more severely dependent and had been abstinent longer.

Several other drugs have entered practice more recently, and examiners now ask about them:

  • Nalmefene is an opioid antagonist related to naltrexone. In 2013 the European Medicines Agency approved it for reducing alcohol consumption in dependent people who drink at high-risk levels but do not need immediate detoxification. It is taken "as needed", on days when the person expects to drink, one to two hours beforehand, so it fits a goal of cutting down rather than stopping. It is not approved for alcohol use disorder in the United States.
  • Baclofen, a muscle relaxant used for spasticity, stimulates the GABA-B receptor (a different GABA receptor from the GABA-A target of alcohol and benzodiazepines). Its use for alcohol dependence was popularised by the French cardiologist Olivier Ameisen, who in 2005 described the suppression of his own alcohol dependence with high doses and in 2008 wrote a best-selling book about it, Le Dernier Verre ("The Last Glass"). After a wave of off-label prescribing, France allowed its use under a temporary recommendation in 2014 and in October 2018 became the first country to grant it a marketing authorisation for alcohol dependence, at doses up to 80 mg a day. Trial results are mixed; its risks include sedation, dangerous overdose, especially with alcohol, and withdrawal seizures if it is stopped suddenly. Because it is cleared mainly by the kidneys, it has been studied in patients with liver disease.
  • Topiramate, an anticonvulsant that enhances GABA-A activity and blocks some glutamate receptors, reduced heavy drinking in a trial published in The Lancet in 2003 by Bankole A. Johnson and colleagues. It is used off-label (not formally licensed for this purpose); its side effects include tingling, slowed thinking and word-finding difficulty, weight loss and kidney stones. Gabapentin, another anticonvulsant, is also used off-label.
  • Ondansetron, the anti-vomiting drug that blocks the 5-HT3 serotonin receptor, reduced drinking in a trial by Johnson and colleagues in 2000, but only in people whose alcoholism had begun early in life, a finding that connects with the early-onset type of alcoholism described in Section 10.2. It remains experimental.

Table 10.19 brings the relapse-prevention drugs together. Notice that choosing among them is mostly a matter of matching the drug's mechanism and route of elimination to the patient: whether the goal is abstinence or reduction, whether someone can supervise, whether the liver or the kidneys are damaged, and whether the person also uses opioids.

Table 10.19 Drugs that help people stop drinking and stay stopped.
DrugMechanismWhat it doesMain cautions
Disulfiram (Antabuse)Irreversibly blocks aldehyde dehydrogenase; acetaldehyde builds up after a drinkDeterrent: drinking causes the disulfiram-ethanol reaction; supports abstinence, best when supervisedHeart disease, psychosis, severe liver disease, pregnancy, suicide risk; informed consent essential; hidden alcohol; reaction possible for about 2 weeks after stopping
Naltrexone (oral daily; monthly injection)Opioid (mu) receptor antagonist; blunts alcohol-induced endorphin and dopamine rewardLess reward and craving after a drink; reduces relapse to heavy drinkingPrecipitates withdrawal in opioid users and blocks opioid pain relief; avoid in acute hepatitis or liver failure; nausea
AcamprosateDampens glutamate (NMDA) overactivity of protracted withdrawalHelps people already abstinent to stay abstinentExcreted by kidneys: avoid in severe renal impairment (safe in liver disease); diarrhoea; three times a day
NalmefeneOpioid receptor antagonist (related to naltrexone)Taken as needed to reduce drinking; approved in Europe in 2013As for naltrexone; not for people needing detoxification

Return to Sushila from Section 10.3.6. After detoxification her mood has recovered, but she craves a drink every evening when she comes home to an empty flat (her husband works in another city during the week). Her liver is enlarged and her liver enzymes are markedly raised, a reason for caution with naltrexone; there is no one at home to supervise disulfiram; and her goal is complete abstinence. Her psychiatrist prescribes acamprosate, safe for her liver, and refers her to a cognitive-behavioural group to plan her evenings and handle the craving, the subject of the next subsection.

Exam trap

Match each drug to its action. Disulfiram: blocks aldehyde dehydrogenase (aversion or deterrence). Naltrexone: opioid antagonist (reduces the reward and craving; FDA 1994); never in someone using opioids. Acamprosate: glutamate and NMDA modulation (maintains abstinence); excreted by the kidneys, so usable in liver disease. Nalmefene: as-needed opioid antagonist for cutting down. Baclofen: GABA-B agonist. Benzodiazepines are for detoxification, not for long-term relapse prevention (they are themselves addictive and dangerous with alcohol). In the COMBINE trial naltrexone helped and acamprosate did not.

The psychological treatments and mutual help

Every drug in Table 10.19 works better with psychological treatment than without it. Barlow and Durand conclude that no biological treatment alone succeeds for most people, and the COMBINE trial found that even a placebo pill helped when it came with regular meetings with a professional. The psychological treatments for alcohol problems share four aims: to build and keep the motivation to change, to teach the skills of not drinking, to rearrange the person's life so that sobriety pays better than drinking, and to mobilise the support of family and peers. How each treatment is actually delivered is taught in Book 2; this subsection says what each one is and what the evidence shows for alcohol, and Table 10.20 summarises them.

Motivational interviewing and motivational enhancement therapy. Ambivalence is the rule in alcohol problems: the drinker both wants to stop and wants to drink. Motivational interviewing, developed by William R. Miller in work with problem drinkers in the early 1980s and later with Stephen Rollnick, is a collaborative style of conversation that draws out the person's own reasons for change instead of confronting him. Motivational enhancement therapy (MET) is its structured, four-session form, built around personalised feedback from an assessment; in Project MATCH it did as well as twelve sessions of other treatments. Both are taught in Book 2, Section 6.9.

Cognitive-behavioural coping skills and relapse prevention. Cognitive-behavioural treatment teaches the drinker to recognise the situations, moods and thoughts that trigger drinking, to refuse drinks, to manage craving and negative emotions, and to solve the problems that drinking used to numb. Its best-known branch is relapse prevention, set out by G. Alan Marlatt (1941 to 2011) and Judith Gordon in their book Relapse Prevention (1985). Marlatt distinguished a lapse (a single slip) from a relapse (a return to the old pattern), identified high-risk situations, and described the abstinence violation effect, the "I've blown it, so I may as well keep going" reaction that turns a lapse into a relapse. Relapse prevention is taught in Book 2, Section 9.9.6. A related technique, cue exposure, repeatedly exposes the person to the sight and smell of his drink without drinking, so that the conditioned craving described in Section 10.2 can extinguish; its results for alcohol have been modest.

Aversion therapy. In the middle of the twentieth century some clinics tried to condition a distaste for alcohol by pairing drinks with nausea produced by an emetic drug, or with electric shocks; covert sensitisation, introduced by Joseph Cautela in 1966, pairs imagined drinking with imagined disgusting scenes. These methods are rarely used now. Do not confuse them with disulfiram, which works as a deterrent rather than by conditioning, since most people taking it never drink and so never experience the reaction.

The community reinforcement approach and contingency management. In 1973 George M. Hunt and Nathan H. Azrin described the community reinforcement approach (CRA), which treats drinking as behaviour maintained by its rewards and sets out to make a sober life more rewarding: help with finding and keeping work, relationship counselling, new recreation and social activities, and training in the skills to enjoy them. Its family version, community reinforcement and family training, teaches relatives how to encourage a reluctant drinker into treatment. Contingency management rewards verified abstinence directly, typically with vouchers or small prizes; its strongest evidence comes from stimulant dependence, and its use for alcohol has been limited by the difficulty of proving abstinence, which breath tests and the newer urine markers of Section 10.3.8 are beginning to solve. Both are described in Book 2, Section 9.9.5.

Couples and family treatment. Alcohol problems are family problems, and the family can be a powerful ally in treatment. In behavioural couples therapy, developed for alcohol problems by Timothy O'Farrell and William Fals-Stewart, the couple make a daily "sobriety contract" (often including a supervised disulfiram dose of the kind Ravinder takes), increase shared positive activities and learn to communicate without accusations; trials have found better drinking and relationship outcomes than with individual treatment alone. Relatives' own distress is addressed in family groups such as Al-Anon, founded in 1951 for the families and friends of alcoholics.

Alcoholics Anonymous and twelve-step facilitation. The most widespread help of all is free and is not run by professionals. Alcoholics Anonymous (AA) was founded in Akron, Ohio, in 1935 by two men who had failed to stop drinking on their own: Bill Wilson (1895 to 1971), a New York stockbroker, and Robert Holbrook Smith (1879 to 1950), an Akron surgeon known as Dr Bob. Its programme, set out in the book Alcoholics Anonymous (the "Big Book", 1939), follows twelve steps that begin with admitting powerlessness over alcohol and include a moral inventory, making amends and carrying the message to other alcoholics. Members attend meetings, share their stories anonymously, and are guided by a sponsor, a member with longer sobriety. AA regards alcoholism as an illness that can be arrested by lifelong abstinence but never cured. Because members come and go anonymously it was long difficult to study, but the evidence has improved. Vaillant found that AA attendance was one of the two best predictors of sustained abstinence (Section 10.4.1), and a 2020 Cochrane review of 27 studies by John Kelly, Keith Humphreys and Marica Ferri concluded that manualised AA and twelve-step facilitation (TSF, a professional therapy that encourages active AA involvement) produced higher rates of continuous abstinence than other treatments such as cognitive-behavioural therapy, and performed about as well on other outcomes. Mutual-help groups are taught in Book 2, Section 7.11.7. Alternatives exist for people who do not want a spiritual or abstinence-based programme, such as SMART Recovery and Moderation Management.

Therapeutic communities. A therapeutic community is a residential programme, lasting months, in which the community of residents and staff is itself the main instrument of treatment, with residents taking on graded responsibilities and confronting one another's behaviour. Therapeutic communities are used more for drug than for alcohol dependence; they are described in Book 2, Sections 7.11.8 and 9.9.7.

Does it matter which of these treatments a person receives? The largest test of the question, Project MATCH (1997), randomly assigned 1,726 people with alcohol problems to cognitive-behavioural coping skills therapy, motivational enhancement therapy or twelve-step facilitation. All three groups improved substantially and about equally, and very few of the predicted "matches" between client characteristics and treatments appeared. The trial is described in Book 2, Section 8.8.2. Its message for this chapter is that well-delivered structured treatments for alcohol use disorder work about equally well, which makes it sensible to offer the one a person will actually attend, and to combine it with medication where medication fits.

Table 10.20 The psychological treatments for alcohol use disorder at a glance.
TreatmentCore ideaEvidence for alcoholTaught in
Brief intervention (FRAMES)Short advice and feedback in primary careModest reductions in non-dependent drinkersBook 2, 6.9
Motivational enhancement therapyResolve ambivalence; four sessionsAs good as longer therapies in Project MATCHBook 2, 6.9
CBT coping skills and relapse preventionSkills for triggers, craving and lapsesWell supported; equal to MET and TSF in MATCHBook 2, 9.9.6

Exam trap

Project MATCH compared CBT, MET and TSF and found them about equally effective, with few client-treatment matches; MET achieved this in only four sessions. AA was founded in 1935 in Akron, Ohio, by Bill Wilson and Dr Bob Smith; it is a mutual-help group, not a therapeutic community and not a professional therapy (TSF is the professional therapy that links people to AA). The community reinforcement approach is Hunt and Azrin (1973); relapse prevention and the abstinence violation effect are Marlatt and Gordon (1985).

Abstinence or controlled drinking?

AA, and most treatment in India, insists that the only safe goal for a person with alcohol dependence is total, lifelong abstinence. Is that true? The question has caused one of the bitterest disputes in the history of clinical psychology. In 1962 D. L. Davies of the Maudsley Hospital in London reported that 7 of 93 men treated there for alcohol addiction had gone on to drink normally for years, a finding that contradicted the disease model's claim that one drink inevitably leads to loss of control. A decade later the issue became a scientific battle.

Key study: the Sobells, Pendery and the controlled-drinking controversy (1973 to 1982)

At Patton State Hospital in California, Mark B. Sobell and Linda C. Sobell gave hospitalised men with severe (physically dependent) alcohol dependence an individualised behaviour therapy of 17 sessions aimed at controlled drinking: they practised drinking moderately in a simulated bar, where drinking in an uncontrolled way was followed by an electric shock, received feedback from videotapes of their drunken behaviour, and learned to cope with situations that triggered heavy drinking. Compared with controls given conventional abstinence-oriented treatment, the controlled-drinking group did better; in the second year after treatment, as Barlow and Durand summarise it, the men in the controlled-drinking group were functioning well 85 percent of the time against 42 percent for the abstinence group. In 1982 Mary L. Pendery, Irving Maltzman and Louis Jolyon West published a re-examination in Science. Following the 20 men of the controlled-drinking group for about ten years, they reported that only one, who apparently had never had physical withdrawal symptoms, had maintained controlled drinking; eight had continued to drink excessively, six had become abstinent, four had died of alcohol-related causes and one was missing. The paper made headlines and the Sobells were accused of misrepresenting their results. Formal inquiries did not find misconduct, and the decisive criticism of the re-examination was methodological: it gave no comparable ten-year data on the abstinence group, so it could not show that controlled-drinking training had done worse than the usual treatment. The controversy had a lasting chilling effect on controlled-drinking research in the United States, while the approach remained widely accepted in Britain.

Where does the debate stand now? Barlow and Durand summarise later research as showing that controlled drinking is at least as effective as abstinence as a goal, but that neither succeeds over the long term for most people with alcohol dependence, and Vaillant's data show who can drink moderately again: those whose problems were few and mild. Clinical practice therefore matches the goal to the person. Abstinence is the advice for people with severe dependence, liver disease or other alcohol-related organ damage (including the brain), pregnancy, a psychiatric illness that alcohol worsens, medication that interacts with alcohol, or repeated failures at moderation. A goal of reduced, controlled drinking is reasonable for people with hazardous or harmful drinking and milder disorders, and as a first step for people who will not yet accept abstinence. This is part of a wider philosophy called harm reduction: reducing the damage drinking does to the person and to others, even when the person is not ready to stop entirely. Nalmefene's European licence for reducing drinking, and naltrexone's effect on heavy drinking days, are its pharmacological expression.

Exam trap

The controlled-drinking study was by Mark and Linda Sobell (Patton State Hospital, early 1970s); the famous re-evaluation in Science (1982) was by Pendery, Maltzman and West, and its weakness was the lack of a comparable follow-up of the abstinence group. D. L. Davies (1962) first reported normal drinking in recovered alcohol addicts. Controlled drinking is a reasonable goal for less severe problems, not for severe dependence or alcohol-related organ damage.

De-addiction care in India

Whatever the treatment, it helps only the people who receive it, and in India very few do. The Magnitude of Substance Use in India survey (Section 10.3.9) found that only about one in 38 people with alcohol dependence reported getting any treatment, and only about one in 180 had ever received inpatient treatment or hospitalisation for an alcohol problem. Closing this gap is the main task of India's de-addiction services.

Those services come from two ministries. The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare runs the Drug De-Addiction Programme, under which de-addiction centres were set up in government hospitals. Its national resource centre is the National Drug Dependence Treatment Centre of AIIMS, located at Ghaziabad near Delhi, which began in 1988 as the Drug Dependence Treatment Centre and was upgraded to a national centre, working from its Ghaziabad campus since 2003; it is also a WHO collaborating centre and coordinates Drug Treatment Clinics in government hospitals that offer outpatient care, including long-term medication. The Centre for Addiction Medicine at NIMHANS in Bengaluru is the other major national centre for treatment, training and research. The Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment funds the social side of treatment. In 2018 it launched the National Action Plan for Drug Demand Reduction (NAPDDR), with a roadmap to 2025, under which non-governmental Integrated Rehabilitation Centres for Addicts (IRCAs) are funded and Addiction Treatment Facilities are being set up in government hospitals with the NDDTC as coordinator; and on 15 August 2020 it launched the Nasha Mukt Bharat Abhiyaan ("drug-free India campaign"), which began in 272 districts identified as most vulnerable. Alongside these are a large number of private de-addiction and "rehab" centres of very uneven quality, some of which have been exposed for locking up and beating residents (Book 2, Section 9.9.5). Because the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 includes conditions associated with alcohol and drug use within its definition of mental illness, people in all such centres have the Act's rights, including informed consent and freedom from chaining and cruel treatment.

Indian de-addiction care has one feature that Western textbooks rarely describe: the family is usually present. Patients are typically brought by relatives, often stay in the ward with a family member, and return for follow-up with them, and Indian clinicians have learned to use this. Families are taught about withdrawal, relapse and the drugs (Ravinder's wife learned to supervise his disulfiram, and the De Sousa trials of Section 10.4.4 recruited only men whose relatives would accompany them); spouses' own distress, depression and exposure to violence are assessed and treated; and family members are helped to stop the patterns, from covering up absences at work to paying off liquor debts, that unwittingly keep the drinking going. Community-based camps, run by hospitals and voluntary organisations, have taken detoxification and follow-up to villages and workplaces where no clinic exists.

Gopi, the carpenter with alcoholic hallucinosis from Section 10.3.5, shows the whole pathway. His voices faded within ten days on a short course of an antipsychotic and a benzodiazepine, under thiamine cover. Before discharge from the medical college's de-addiction ward, his wife and elder son joined a family session in which the team explained what had happened to him and what would help. He chose disulfiram, taken each morning in front of his son, attended a weekly relapse-prevention group for six months, and went back to work. A year later he had had one lapse, at a cousin's wedding, which he and his family recognised, discussed at the group and stopped. That is what success in this field usually looks like: not a single dramatic cure, but a lapse caught early.

Indian context

For interviews, know the institutions and the dates: the NDDTC of AIIMS (Ghaziabad; begun in 1988, national centre since 2003) under the Ministry of Health's Drug De-Addiction Programme; the NAPDDR (2018) and the Nasha Mukt Bharat Abhiyaan (launched 15 August 2020 in 272 districts) under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment; IRCAs run by non-governmental organisations; and the treatment gap of about one in 38 for alcohol dependence. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 brings alcohol and drug conditions within "mental illness", with all the rights that follow.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

Answers: (1) zero-order, (2) acetaldehyde, (3) GABA-A; NMDA, (4) kindling, (5) hallucinosis, (6) 48; 72, (7) Othello, (8) thiamine, (9) oxazepam, (10) naltrexone.

10.5 Opioids: From Opium to Heroin, and Their TreatmentMedium yield · 2 Qs

Few drugs show both faces of pharmacology as plainly as the opioids. The same molecule that lets a surgeon operate, a soldier survive a shattered leg and a woman with cancer die without agony can also take over a young man's life within a year and stop his breathing in a single evening. The biopsychologist John Pinel calls this their Jekyll-and-Hyde character: on the kindly side, the best painkillers medicine has ever had, and excellent treatments for cough and diarrhoea; on the other, a powerful capacity to produce addiction. In India this is not a distant problem. The national survey of 2019 found that about 2 percent of Indians aged 10 to 75 had used an opioid in the previous year, and that roughly 60 lakh people had an opioid use disorder (Section 10.5.6 gives the details). This section follows the family from the poppy field to the laboratory, explains how opioids act on the brain, describes what intoxication, overdose and withdrawal look like, and ends with the treatments, which are among the best supported by evidence anywhere in psychiatry.

From the poppy to the pharmacy: a short history

Opium is the dried milky latex of the opium poppy, Papaver somniferum, whose Latin name means "the poppy that brings sleep". When the petals have fallen, the farmer makes shallow cuts in the unripe green seed capsule in the late afternoon. A white sap oozes out, darkens and thickens overnight, and is scraped off the next morning as raw opium gum. The dried capsules left behind after the latex has been collected are poppy husk or poppy straw, known in north India as doda or post; they still contain enough of the active chemicals to be crushed, soaked and drunk. The ripe seeds, the khus khus of Indian kitchens, contain only traces.

People have used opium to relieve pain, bring sleep, stop diarrhoea and produce pleasure for more than four thousand years. In seventeenth-century England the physician Thomas Sydenham (1624 to 1689) popularised laudanum, a tincture of opium dissolved in alcohol, and for the next two hundred years laudanum and other opium mixtures were sold freely in Europe and North America. Labourers took them for aches, ladies for "nerves", and nursemaids spooned "soothing syrups" into crying babies. In 1821 the English writer Thomas De Quincey (1785 to 1859) published Confessions of an English Opium-Eater, first in a London magazine and then as a book. It was the first great first-person account of addiction, and its two halves, "the pleasures of opium" and "the pains of opium", describe a life that is still recognisable in any de-addiction ward: a glorious beginning, then years of dependence, tormenting dreams and failed attempts to stop.

India sits near the centre of this history. In the late eighteenth century the British East India Company took control of opium production in Bengal and Bihar and turned it into one of the most profitable trades in the world: opium grown on the Gangetic plain was shipped to China and smuggled in, in exchange for tea and silver. When the Chinese government tried to stop the trade, Britain went to war. The First Opium War (1839 to 1842) ended with the Treaty of Nanking, which opened Chinese ports to British trade and handed Hong Kong to Britain, and a Second Opium War (1856 to 1860) followed. The opium factory that the Company's Benares Opium Agency opened at Ghazipur in 1820 is still working today as a government factory, alongside a second at Neemuch in Madhya Pradesh. India is now the only country in the world that legally produces opium gum: licensed farmers in selected tracts of Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan and Uttar Pradesh grow the poppy under the supervision of the Central Bureau of Narcotics, and the gum is processed into morphine and codeine for medicine.

The step that turned a plant into modern pharmacology came in 1804 (Pinel gives 1803; sources differ slightly), when Friedrich Wilhelm Adam Sertürner (1783 to 1841), a young pharmacist's assistant in Paderborn, Germany, extracted the main active ingredient of opium in pure form. He called it morphium, after Morpheus, the Greek god of dreams, because of the deep sleep it produced, and we know it as morphine. It was the first alkaloid to be isolated from a plant: an alkaloid is a nitrogen-containing compound made by a plant that has marked effects on animals (caffeine, nicotine, cocaine and atropine are others). The firm of Merck in Darmstadt began selling morphine commercially in 1827, and a second opium alkaloid, codeine, was isolated in 1832. Pure morphine could be given in an exact dose, and once the hypodermic syringe was developed in the 1850s it could be injected straight into the body, which made it act faster and more powerfully than opium ever had. Morphine was given freely to the wounded of the American Civil War (1861 to 1865), and a story later grew up that so many veterans were left addicted that morphine dependence became known as "the soldier's disease". Historians now regard the phrase and the size of that epidemic as largely legend, although the war was one of several reasons that opioid addiction rose so steeply in late nineteenth-century America.

Then came heroin. In 1874 (Pinel gives 1870, but most sources give 1874) an English chemist working in London, C. R. Alder Wright (1844 to 1894), first made diacetylmorphine from morphine, but nobody took much notice. In August 1897 Felix Hoffmann (1868 to 1946), a chemist at the Bayer company in Elberfeld, Germany, made the same compound again, less than two weeks after he had made aspirin. From 1898 to 1910 Bayer sold it over the counter, under the trade name Heroin (from the German heroisch, "heroic" or "strong"), as a cough suppressant and as a supposedly non-addictive substitute for morphine. It was nothing of the kind. Adding the two acetyl groups makes the molecule far more fat-soluble, so it crosses the blood-brain barrier much faster than morphine; inside the brain it is converted back to morphine. Heroin is therefore a "fast morphine", and speed of arrival in the brain is one of the strongest predictors of how addictive a drug will be (Section 10.2). In the United States the Harrison Narcotics Act of 1914 restricted opium, coca and their derivatives, heroin among them, to sale on a doctor's prescription, apart from weak over-the-counter remedies. (Pinel writes that heroin was left out of the Act and that users switched to it, but the Act covered all opium "salts, derivatives, or preparations".) In 1924 Congress banned heroin's manufacture and import altogether, even for medical use.

Exam trap

Sertürner isolated morphine (about 1804), not heroin. Heroin (diacetylmorphine) was first made by C. R. Alder Wright in 1874 and marketed by Bayer from 1898, ironically as a non-addictive cough medicine. Opium is the dried latex of the unripe capsule of Papaver somniferum; poppy husk (doda) is the dried capsule, and poppy seeds contain only traces of the alkaloids. Thomas De Quincey's Confessions of an English Opium-Eater is 1821.

The opioid family: natural, semi-synthetic, synthetic and endogenous

Three overlapping words are used for these drugs, and examiners like to test them. An opiate, strictly, is one of the natural alkaloids of opium, such as morphine and codeine. An opioid is any substance, natural, semi-synthetic, synthetic or made by the body, that acts on the opioid receptors; it is the wider and now preferred term, and DSM-5-TR calls the whole class "opioids". Narcotic comes from the Greek narke, numbness, and once meant any drug that dulls the senses and brings sleep; today it is mainly a legal word. India's Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances (NDPS) Act, 1985 (Section 10.1) counts coca leaf and cocaine as "narcotic drugs" along with opium and cannabis, even though cocaine is a stimulant, so "narcotic" is best avoided as a clinical term.

Opium contains a couple of dozen alkaloids, and they fall into two chemical groups with very different effects. The first group, the phenanthrene alkaloids, act on the opioid receptors. Morphine is the most abundant, making up roughly 8 to 14 percent of the dry weight of opium; it is the standard against which every other opioid's strength is measured. Codeine is a much weaker relative, used for dry cough and mild pain. Codeine itself barely binds the receptor; it works because an enzyme in the liver (CYP2D6) converts a small part of each dose into morphine. People who inherit an inactive form of the enzyme get little relief from codeine, while "ultra-rapid metabolisers" turn it into morphine so fast that ordinary doses have caused dangerous breathing problems, especially in children, which is why many countries now restrict codeine for the young. Thebaine has little painkilling effect of its own and causes convulsions at high doses, but it is the raw material from which industry makes several important semi-synthetic drugs. The second group, the benzylisoquinoline alkaloids, do not act on opioid receptors at all, produce no euphoria and cause no dependence. Papaverine relaxes smooth muscle and widens blood vessels; it is used to relieve spasm of arteries and, injected into the penis, to treat erectile dysfunction (Chapter 9). Noscapine (once called narcotine, despite not being narcotic) suppresses cough and is found in cough remedies. So "natural opium alkaloid" does not mean "addictive": papaverine and noscapine are natural opium alkaloids that are neither.

A semi-synthetic opioid is made in a laboratory by chemically modifying one of the natural alkaloids. Heroin is the classic example, made from morphine by adding two acetyl groups. Others are hydromorphone (from morphine), hydrocodone (from codeine or thebaine), oxycodone (from thebaine; first made in Germany in 1916 and, as the slow-release tablet OxyContin, at the centre of the North American crisis described in Section 10.5.6), buprenorphine (from thebaine, first made in 1966), and the opioid antagonists naloxone and naltrexone, which are also made from thebaine. A synthetic opioid is built from scratch in the laboratory and owes nothing to the poppy except its effects. Pethidine (meperidine in American texts) and methadone were both made in Germany in the late 1930s; methadone came from the chemists Gustav Ehrhart and Max Bockmühl at the IG Farben company in 1937. (You may read that its American brand name, Dolophine, honoured Adolf Hitler. That story is an urban myth; the name comes from the Latin dolor, pain, and finis, end.) Fentanyl was made by Paul Janssen (1926 to 2003) in Belgium around 1960; it is 50 to 100 times as potent as morphine, and its relative carfentanil, which vets use to sedate elephants and other large animals, is about 100 times more potent again. Tramadol is a weak synthetic opioid that also blocks the reuptake of serotonin and noradrenaline; it can cause seizures and serotonin toxicity, it is misused in India and has been smuggled from India to countries where it is banned, and since April 2018 it has been controlled as a psychotropic substance under the NDPS Act. Tapentadol works in a similar double way. Loperamide, the familiar diarrhoea tablet, is a synthetic opioid that barely enters the brain at normal doses. The fourth member of the family is the body's own: the endogenous opioids, the endorphins, enkephalins and dynorphins described in the next subsection. Table 10.21 puts the family together.

Table 10.21 The opioid family. Heroin is the odd one out among the drugs that examiners list beside morphine and codeine: it is semi-synthetic.
ClassHow it is obtainedExamplesPoints to remember
Natural opium alkaloids (opiates)Occur in the latex of Papaver somniferumMorphine, codeine, thebaine; papaverine and noscapineMorphine is the main alkaloid; codeine acts after conversion to morphine; papaverine and noscapine are not narcotic
Semi-synthetic opioidsA natural alkaloid chemically modifiedHeroin (diacetylmorphine), hydromorphone, hydrocodone, oxycodone, buprenorphine; naloxone, naltrexoneHeroin is made from morphine; buprenorphine, oxycodone and the antagonists from thebaine
Synthetic opioidsMade entirely in the laboratoryMethadone, pethidine (meperidine), fentanyl, tramadol, tapentadol, pentazocine, loperamideFentanyl is 50 to 100 times as potent as morphine; methadone is long-acting
Endogenous opioidsPeptides made by the bodyBeta-endorphin, enkephalins, dynorphins"Endorphin" means endogenous morphine
Exam trap

"Which is NOT a natural alkaloid of opium: morphine, codeine, heroin, papaverine?" The answer is heroin, because it does not occur in the poppy: it is semi-synthetic, made by acetylating morphine. Every other option is natural. Morphine is the principal natural alkaloid and the reference opioid. Codeine is natural too (even though much medicinal codeine is in practice made from morphine, the molecule occurs naturally in opium). Papaverine is the option students wrongly reject: it is a genuine natural opium alkaloid, merely one that is not an opioid analgesic and causes no dependence. The same logic answers the twin question "which is not a natural alkaloid compound?": heroin. Do not confuse the classes the other way either: methadone and fentanyl are fully synthetic, not semi-synthetic, and buprenorphine is semi-synthetic (from thebaine).

Receptors and what opioids do in the body

By the early 1970s pharmacologists knew that opioids must act on some specific target in the nervous system. Tiny doses had huge effects; the effects depended on the exact three-dimensional shape of the molecule (a drug and its mirror image did not act alike); and the drug naloxone could reverse a heroin overdose within a minute or two, as though it were pushing the opioid off a site. But nobody had ever seen that site. The question was settled in 1973, and the way it was settled is one of the classic stories of neuroscience.

Key study: finding the opiate receptor and the brain's own opioids (1973 to 1975)

Candace Pert (1946 to 2013), a graduate student at Johns Hopkins University, and her supervisor Solomon Snyder (born 1938) made naloxone radioactive, mixed it with ground-up brain tissue, and washed away everything that was not firmly stuck. A small amount of radioactivity stayed bound, it could be displaced by opioids in proportion to their known strength as painkillers, and it was found in nervous tissue. They had found the opiate receptor, and they reported it in Science in March 1973; Eric Simon in New York and Lars Terenius in Sweden found it independently the same year. The discovery raised an obvious question: why would the human brain carry receptors for the sap of a poppy? The answer had to be that the brain makes its own opioid-like substances. In 1975 John Hughes and Hans Kosterlitz (1903 to 1996) at the University of Aberdeen extracted two small peptides from pig brain that acted like morphine and were blocked by naloxone. They named them enkephalins (from the Greek for "in the head"). Beta-endorphin and the dynorphins followed, and "endorphin", a contraction of "endogenous morphine", became the name for the whole group. The lesson for psychiatry is that addictive drugs work by hijacking systems the brain already has for its own purposes: here, for damping pain and stress and for signalling reward.

There are three classical opioid receptors, named with Greek letters: mu, delta and kappa (a fourth, the nociceptin receptor, is a close cousin). All three are metabotropic receptors linked to inhibitory G proteins. When an opioid binds, the neuron's internal messenger cyclic AMP falls, potassium channels open and calcium channels close, so the neuron becomes harder to fire and releases less transmitter. Opioids are therefore, at the level of the single neuron, mostly inhibitory, and their effects depend on which neurons carry the receptors.

The mu receptor is the one that matters most. Morphine, heroin, methadone and fentanyl are all mu agonists ("mu for morphine" is a useful memory hook), and beta-endorphin is its main natural ligand. Nearly everything an opioid does, good and bad, follows from where mu receptors sit:

  • Pain relief. Mu receptors in the periaqueductal grey of the midbrain, in the brainstem and in the dorsal horn of the spinal cord block the transmission of pain signals. Opioids blunt the emotional sting of pain as well as its intensity; patients often say the pain is still there but no longer bothers them.
  • Euphoria and reward. In the ventral tegmental area, mu receptors sit on GABA interneurons that normally hold the dopamine neurons in check. Opioids inhibit these brake cells, the dopamine neurons fire, and dopamine floods the nucleus accumbens; opioids also act directly in the accumbens. This is how opioids plug into the common reward circuit described in Section 10.2.
  • Respiratory depression. Mu receptors in the breathing centres of the medulla make them less responsive to rising carbon dioxide, so breathing becomes slow and shallow. This is what kills in overdose.
  • Pinpoint pupils. Opioids increase the activity of the Edinger-Westphal nucleus, which drives the muscle that constricts the pupil, producing the tiny "pinpoint" pupils (miosis) that are the single most useful sign of opioid use.
  • Cough suppression, nausea and constipation. Opioids depress the cough centre in the medulla (hence codeine linctus), stimulate the chemoreceptor trigger zone that causes vomiting, and slow the gut by acting on the nerve plexus in its wall (hence their use for diarrhoea, and the constipation that every long-term user knows).
  • Hormonal and other effects. Opioids suppress the hormones that drive the gonads, reducing sex drive and disturbing menstruation; morphine releases histamine, causing itching and flushing; and opioids lower body temperature slightly.

The kappa receptor, whose natural ligand is dynorphin, also relieves pain but produces dysphoria rather than euphoria, sometimes with perceptual distortions; the hallucinogen salvinorin A acts here. Delta receptors, the main target of the enkephalins, contribute to pain relief and mood.

Two pharmacological facts explain much of what clinicians see. The first is that tolerance to opioids is uneven. With repeated use, tolerance develops quickly to the euphoria, the pain relief, the sedation and the nausea, and to some extent to the respiratory depression, although the evidence suggests that this last tolerance develops more slowly and is lost quickly when use stops. Almost no tolerance develops to the constriction of the pupils or to the constipation. That is why a man who has injected heroin daily for ten years still has pinpoint pupils and is still constipated, even though the "high" has long since faded. The second fact is the adaptation that produces physical dependence. Neurons exposed to an opioid day after day compensate for its inhibitory effect by turning up their internal machinery, especially the cyclic AMP system. This is clearest in the locus coeruleus, the brainstem nucleus that supplies most of the brain's noradrenaline: under the drug its neurons fire at a near-normal rate only because the compensating machinery is working hard against the opioid. Remove the drug, and the compensation is suddenly unopposed: the locus coeruleus fires furiously, and much of the opioid withdrawal syndrome (anxiety, sweating, racing heart, gooseflesh) is this noradrenergic storm. Tolerance, cross-tolerance and the difference between physical dependence and addiction are defined in Section 10.1; a cancer patient on regular morphine is physically dependent but not addicted, which is why DSM-5-TR does not count tolerance or withdrawal towards opioid use disorder when the drug is taken as prescribed.

Exam trap

The euphoria, analgesia, respiratory depression, miosis and constipation of morphine and heroin come mainly from the mu receptor; kappa (dynorphin) produces dysphoria. The opiate receptor was demonstrated by Pert and Snyder in 1973; the first endogenous opioids, the enkephalins, were isolated by Hughes and Kosterlitz in 1975. Endorphins are the body's own opioid agonists; naloxone is an antagonist. Little or no tolerance develops to miosis and constipation, so pinpoint pupils persist even in highly tolerant users.

Intoxication and overdose

Ask a person who injects heroin what the first seconds feel like and you will hear about the rush: a wave of warmth and intense pleasure that starts in the abdomen and spreads through the body, often compared to orgasm, which settles into a calm, drowsy, contented state in which nothing seems to matter. Smoking heroin from foil ("chasing the dragon") gives a slightly gentler version; swallowing opium or drinking poppy husk tea gives a slow, mild warmth without any rush at all. After the rush come the signs of intoxication. The person becomes drowsy and slips in and out of a light sleep, head dropping and jerking up again, which users call being "on the nod". Speech is slow and slurred, attention and memory are poor, and the person may scratch at the face and nose because of histamine release. The pupils are small, often pinpoint, even in a dim room.

DSM-5-TR's criteria for opioid intoxication put that picture into a rule with three parts. There must be recent opioid use; there must be clinically significant behavioural or psychological change, typically an initial euphoria followed by apathy, dysphoria, slowing or agitation, and impaired judgement; and there must be pupillary constriction together with at least one of drowsiness or coma, slurred speech, or impaired attention or memory. The pupils are made a required sign because they are so reliable, and the manual adds one exception you should know: in a severe overdose the brain is starved of oxygen, and anoxia dilates the pupils, so a person close to death from an opioid can have wide pupils. The rule also excludes other explanations. Drunkenness and sedative intoxication can look much the same, but they do not shrink the pupils, and they do not respond to naloxone.

An opioid overdose is intoxication that has gone far enough to stop the person breathing adequately. Its classic triad is unconsciousness (coma), pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression. Breathing slows to a few shallow breaths a minute, sometimes with a snoring or gurgling sound; the lips and fingertips turn blue (cyanosis); the pulse slows and the skin is cold and clammy; fluid may fill the lungs. Death comes from respiratory arrest, within minutes of a dose of fentanyl and usually within a few hours of heroin, which is why bystanders matter so much. Overdose rarely happens at random. It clusters in four situations. The first is loss of tolerance: after a few weeks without opioids, in a hospital, a de-addiction centre or a prison, a person's tolerance to the respiratory effect falls away, but the habit of taking the old dose does not, so the days after discharge or release are among the most dangerous in a user's life. The second is mixing: alcohol, benzodiazepines and other sedatives depress breathing by different mechanisms, and the combination with an opioid is far more lethal than either alone (Section 10.7). The third is unknown purity: street heroin is cut to an unknown degree, and when a batch is purer than usual, or has been cut with fentanyl, a familiar dose becomes a fatal one. The fourth is using alone, with nobody to call for help.

The emergency treatment is naloxone, a pure opioid antagonist that binds the mu receptor more tightly than heroin or morphine, pushes them off and switches the receptor off. Given into a vein, into a muscle or as a nasal spray, it can restore breathing within minutes, and it is so safe that many countries put it in the hands of drug users, families and police. Three cautions go with it. Naloxone wears off faster than many opioids (its action often lasts only about an hour), so a person who wakes up can slip back into respiratory depression as the naloxone fades while the heroin, methadone or fentanyl is still present; anyone revived must be watched for several hours. Naloxone instantly throws a dependent person into withdrawal, so people often wake vomiting, shivering and angry. And naloxone reverses only the opioid part of a mixed overdose: it does nothing for alcohol or benzodiazepines.

Manpreet, a 24-year-old from a village near Amritsar, had started smoking heroin (chitta, as it is called in Punjab) on foil with friends at 19, and within two years was injecting it two or three times a day. After his mother sold her jewellery to pay for it, his family took him to a private de-addiction centre, where he was kept for six weeks without medication and discharged "clean". On the evening he came home he met an old friend, bought the amount he used to inject before admission and used it alone in a fields shed. His younger brother found him an hour later, unconscious, breathing perhaps four times a minute with a snoring sound, his lips blue and his pupils the size of pinheads. The ambulance crew gave naloxone, and within two minutes Manpreet was sitting up, retching, sweating and demanding to know why they had "ruined" his high. Two hours later he became drowsy again and needed a second dose. Every element of the overdose is there: the triad of coma, pinpoint pupils and slowed breathing; the loss of tolerance after six weeks of abstinence; using alone; the rapid response to naloxone; the precipitated withdrawal; and the second dip as the naloxone wore off.

Exam trap

Pinpoint pupils mean opioid intoxication (the only exception is anoxia in a severe overdose, which dilates them); dilated pupils mean opioid withdrawal, stimulant intoxication or hallucinogen intoxication. The overdose triad is coma, pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression. Do not mix up the antagonists: naloxone is the short-acting emergency reversal agent; naltrexone is the long-acting antagonist taken to prevent relapse (Section 10.5.7); flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines, not opioids (Section 10.7). The most dangerous moment is not the first use but the return to the old dose after a period of abstinence.

Withdrawal: "cold turkey"

Opioid withdrawal is one of the most recognisable syndromes in medicine, and it follows a simple rule that you met in Section 10.1: a withdrawal state is roughly the mirror image of the drug's acute effects. Opioids constrict the pupils, dry up secretions, slow the gut, relieve pain, calm, sedate and produce euphoria. Take them away from a body that has adapted to them, and the pupils dilate, the eyes and nose stream, the gut cramps and empties, every muscle aches, sleep vanishes, and the person feels restless, anxious and wretched. Once you know the effects, you can work out the withdrawal.

The timing depends on the half-life of the drug, as Figure 10.10 shows. DSM-5-TR describes the usual course: most people dependent on a short-acting opioid such as heroin begin to have symptoms within 6 to 12 hours of the last dose; the acute symptoms peak within 1 to 3 days and subside gradually over 5 to 7 days. With longer-acting drugs such as methadone or buprenorphine, symptoms may take 2 to 4 days to emerge and last longer. Even after the acute phase has passed, a quieter "protracted" phase of anxiety, low mood, inability to enjoy anything, poor sleep and craving can continue for weeks or months, and it is this phase, not the dramatic first week, that drives many relapses.

The first symptoms are subjective: anxiety, restlessness, irritability and an "achy" feeling, often in the back and legs. Then come yawning, watering eyes (lacrimation), a running nose (rhinorrhoea) and sweating. Many people fall into a fitful sleep for a few hours and wake worse. At the peak the original symptoms are joined by chills, shivering, piloerection (gooseflesh, the hairs standing on end), nausea, vomiting, abdominal cramps, diarrhoea, dilated pupils, tremor, muscle pains and spasms, and fever in severe cases. The skin covered in goosebumps, which looks like a plucked bird, is the origin of the expression "going cold turkey" for stopping abruptly, and the involuntary kicking movements of the legs gave English "kicking the habit". DSM-5-TR's criteria for opioid withdrawal require either the cessation or reduction of heavy, prolonged use (several weeks or more) or the giving of an opioid antagonist after a period of use, followed within minutes to several days by at least three of nine signs: dysphoric mood; nausea or vomiting; muscle aches; lacrimation or rhinorrhoea; pupillary dilation, piloerection or sweating; diarrhoea; yawning; fever; and insomnia. The symptoms must cause significant distress or impairment and not be better explained by something else. The manual notes that piloerection and fever are seen mainly in severe withdrawal, which clinicians rarely see because dependent people usually find a dose before withdrawal gets that far.

Withdrawal can be produced in two ways, and the second matters for treatment. Spontaneous withdrawal follows stopping the drug and builds over hours. Precipitated withdrawal is produced in minutes when an antagonist such as naloxone or naltrexone is given to someone dependent on opioids, or when buprenorphine, which binds more tightly than heroin but activates the receptor less (Section 10.5.7), displaces a full agonist. It is sudden and intense, which is why a revived overdose victim wakes in misery and why buprenorphine must be started only once withdrawal has already begun.

How dangerous is opioid withdrawal? Far less than its reputation. The film image of the addict screaming and convulsing on the floor is misleading, and Pinel quotes the psychologist William McKim's verdict that even in its most severe form opioid withdrawal "is not as dangerous or terrifying as withdrawal from barbiturates or alcohol"; Pinel himself compares it to a bad case of the flu. Seizures are not part of the opioid withdrawal syndrome, delirium is rare (DSM-5-TR does allow a diagnosis of opioid withdrawal delirium, but it is uncommon), and in healthy adults the syndrome is very rarely fatal. The exceptions are worth knowing: severe vomiting and diarrhoea can dehydrate a frail or ill person; in pregnancy, withdrawal stresses the fetus, so pregnant women dependent on opioids are generally stabilised on methadone or buprenorphine rather than detoxified; and babies born to dependent mothers can develop a neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome of irritability, a high-pitched cry, tremor, poor feeding and diarrhoea in the days after birth. The real danger of withdrawal is indirect: it is a powerful reason to use again, and the person who gets through it has lost tolerance and is at high risk of overdose if he relapses, as Manpreet did.

Table 10.22 sets withdrawal beside intoxication, and if you read across each row you will see the mirror-image rule at work. The Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale (COWS) is used to rate severity (Assessment book).

Table 10.22 Opioid intoxication and overdose set against opioid withdrawal. Most rows are mirror images.
FeatureIntoxication and overdoseWithdrawal
PupilsConstricted, pinpoint (dilated only with anoxia in severe overdose)Dilated
MoodEuphoria, then apathy and calmDysphoria, anxiety, irritability, craving
ArousalDrowsy, "on the nod"; coma in overdoseRestless, pacing, insomnia
BreathingSlow and shallow; arrest in overdoseNormal or fast; repeated yawning
SecretionsDryLacrimation, rhinorrhoea, sweating
SkinWarm, flushed, itchyGooseflesh (piloerection), chills
