What reverses or relieves itNaloxoneAn opioid; clonidine or lofexidine ease the symptoms

Bablu, a 56-year-old farmer from a village in Barmer district of western Rajasthan, had drunk poppy husk (doda post) soaked in water every morning and evening for more than twenty years. He had started, like many men in his area, to keep going through long days in the fields, and later took it "for strength" and to be sociable: opium was offered to guests at weddings in his village, as it had been in his father's time. When the licensed shops that sold poppy husk in Rajasthan closed at the end of March 2016 and he could not afford the black-market price, he came to the district hospital on the second day without it. He sat yawning repeatedly, his eyes and nose streaming, wiping his face with his turban cloth; his forearms were covered in gooseflesh although the ward was warm; he complained of cramps, loose motions six times since the night before, aching legs that would not keep still, and no sleep at all; his pupils were wide. He was sure he was going to die. The doctor reassured him that he would not, began a short course of buprenorphine with naloxone to relieve the symptoms, and talked with him and his son about whether he wanted to come off opioids altogether or to stay on a maintenance dose (Section 10.5.7). Bablu's withdrawal is textbook: spontaneous onset after the last dose, a peak on the second or third day, and nearly every sign in Table 10.22, from yawning and lacrimation to piloerection, diarrhoea and dilated pupils.

Exam trap

The signs that identify opioid withdrawal are yawning, lacrimation, rhinorrhoea, dilated pupils, piloerection, sweating, diarrhoea and muscle aches. Seizures and delirium are not features of uncomplicated opioid withdrawal; they point to alcohol or sedative withdrawal, which, unlike opioid withdrawal, can kill (Sections 10.3 and 10.7). DSM-5-TR itself uses the runny nose, streaming eyes and dilated pupils to tell opioid withdrawal from sedative withdrawal, whose anxiety and restlessness look similar. Heroin withdrawal starts in 6 to 12 hours and peaks at 1 to 3 days; methadone withdrawal starts later (2 to 4 days) and lasts longer. "Cold turkey" comes from the gooseflesh, "kicking the habit" from the leg movements.

Course, complications and the opioid epidemics

How does opioid dependence usually unfold over a lifetime? The most surprising answer came from a war. In 1971 reports reached Washington that large numbers of American soldiers in Vietnam were using heroin, which there was cheap, pure and sold openly. The government feared that hundreds of thousands of addicted men were about to come home. It asked Lee Robins (1922 to 2009), a psychiatric epidemiologist at Washington University in St Louis, to find out what happened to them. Robins and her colleagues interviewed a representative sample of returning enlisted men, checked their accounts against urine tests and military records, and followed them up. About a third had used heroin in Vietnam and about one in five had had symptoms of dependence there. Yet in their first year back in the United States only about 1 percent became re-addicted, although about 10 percent tried heroin again (these are the figures as Wayne Hall and Megan Weier summarised them in 2016). DSM-5-TR sums up the same work by saying that over 90 percent of the soldiers who had become dependent in Vietnam stayed off opioids in the long term, although many later had problems with alcohol, amphetamines or suicidal thoughts. The study did not show that heroin is harmless. It showed how powerfully the environment controls drug use: the men left behind the cheap supply, the boredom and terror, the drug-using companions and every cue linked to using, and most of them simply stopped. The conditioning of craving and tolerance to places and cues is explained in Section 10.2.

People who come to clinics in their home towns, where the cues and the supply never go away, tell a gloomier story. For them DSM-5-TR describes opioid use disorder as a condition that, once it needs treatment, often continues for many years, with brief periods of abstinence and long-term abstinence in only a minority. A 33-year follow-up of 86 people treated for injected heroin dependence in 1966 and 1967 in a small town in south-east England (Rathod, Addenbrooke and Rosenbach, 2005) found that 22 percent had died, about twice the rate expected, more than half of them from overdose; but 42 percent of the whole group had been abstinent for at least ten years, and another 10 percent were being maintained on methadone. So the natural history has two faces: high mortality, and a slow drift towards recovery among those who survive.

Most of the physical harm of opioid dependence, oddly, does not come from the drug itself. Pinel points out that the direct effects of long-term use of pure opioids are surprisingly minor: constipation, small pupils, irregular periods and reduced sex drive. The great American surgeon William Stewart Halsted (1852 to 1922), one of the founders of the Johns Hopkins medical school, was secretly dependent on morphine for most of his brilliant career. The killers are overdose, the circumstances of illegal use and the needle. A person who injects street drugs never knows their strength or what they have been cut with, shares needles and syringes, and injects into ever smaller and more damaged veins. The results are abscesses and cellulitis at injection sites, infection of the heart valves (endocarditis), clots in deep veins, gangrene after accidental injection into an artery, and above all blood-borne infections: hepatitis B and C and the human immunodeficiency virus (HIV). In India the link between injecting and HIV was seen most starkly in Manipur and the other north-eastern states, where HIV spread rapidly among people injecting heroin from about 1990: reports that year described its prevalence among them rising from almost nil to about half within six months, and in the following years it spread to their sexual partners and to babies born to infected mothers. Add poverty, poor nutrition, crime to pay for the drug, imprisonment and violence, and the high mortality is easy to understand. DSM-5-TR also notes a raised risk of suicide, and it warns that an accidental overdose and a suicide attempt are different things that are easy to confuse.

This analysis is the basis of harm reduction: policies and services that aim to reduce the damage drug use does, to the user and to others, without requiring the person to stop using first. Needle and syringe programmes give out sterile injecting equipment and collect used equipment, and they reduce HIV and hepatitis C transmission; opioid substitution treatment (Section 10.5.7) is itself one of the most effective harm-reduction measures; naloxone given to users and their families prevents overdose deaths; and some countries run supervised injection facilities. In 1994 Switzerland went further and opened clinics where long-term heroin users who had failed other treatments could receive pharmaceutical heroin from a doctor, as part of a package of medical and social care. The evaluations, which Pinel describes, found that most users gave up their criminal lifestyles and improved in health and social functioning, and similar trials followed in Germany, the Netherlands, Spain, Britain and Canada, where supervised heroin-assisted treatment proved more effective than methadone for this hard-to-treat group.

The worst opioid epidemic of modern times began not in a back street but in doctors' surgeries. In the United States in the 1990s, pharmaceutical companies and medical bodies argued that pain was being undertreated and that addiction was rare when opioids were prescribed for pain. In 1996 Purdue Pharma launched OxyContin, a slow-release form of oxycodone, and marketed it as carrying a low risk of addiction; the company later pleaded guilty to federal criminal charges over its marketing and sale of the drug (in 2007, and again in 2020). Prescriptions soared, and DSM-5-TR describes what followed in three waves: until 2009 most overdose deaths involved prescribed opioids; from 2010, as prescribing was tightened, many dependent people turned to cheaper heroin and heroin deaths rose sharply; and from 2015 deaths from synthetic opioids other than methadone, mostly illicitly made fentanyl mixed into the heroin and counterfeit pill supply, outnumbered those from prescribed opioids. Deaths involving synthetic opioids in the United States rose from about 2,600 in 2011 to more than 70,000 in 2021. Canada went through the same waves. The epidemic is a lesson in how supply, marketing and medical fashion can create addiction on a mass scale, just as the opium trade did in nineteenth-century China.

Indian context

The national survey Magnitude of Substance Use in India (carried out by the National Drug Dependence Treatment Centre (NDDTC), AIIMS, for the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, and released in 2019) found that 2.06 percent of Indians aged 10 to 75 were current users of opioids. Heroin was the most used (1.14 percent), followed by pharmaceutical opioids (0.96 percent) and opium (0.52 percent), and about 0.55 percent of Indians, roughly 60 lakh people, needed help for harmful use or dependence. More than half of them lived in a few large states: Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, Haryana, Delhi, Maharashtra, Rajasthan, Andhra Pradesh and Gujarat. In proportion to population, the worst affected were the north-eastern states (Mizoram, Nagaland, Arunachal Pradesh, Sikkim, Manipur), together with Punjab, Haryana and Delhi. There were an estimated 8.5 lakh people who inject drugs, and the drugs they injected were almost all opioids (heroin and pharmaceutical opioids in equal shares). Only about one in 20 people dependent on illicit drugs had ever received inpatient treatment.

The patterns are regional. In western Rajasthan, opium and poppy husk have a long ceremonial history: at weddings and other gatherings guests may be offered opium in the amal ceremony (called the riyan in Jaisalmer and Barmer), and many older men are dependent on daily doda. In Punjab, heroin, usually called chitta, has replaced the older opium and poppy husk among young men. In the north-east, heroin (sometimes as impure "brown sugar") arrives from Myanmar, on the route from the opium-growing Golden Triangle. Across the country pharmaceutical opioids are misused: codeine-containing cough syrups, tramadol (controlled under the NDPS Act since 2018), and injected buprenorphine, often mixed with the antihistamine pheniramine.

Exam trap

Lee Robins's Vietnam study found that most soldiers who became dependent on heroin in Vietnam stopped after coming home: the lesson is the power of environment and cues, not that heroin is non-addictive. Most medical harm in opioid dependence is indirect (overdose, infection from injecting, impurities, poverty) rather than direct toxicity. Harm reduction (needle and syringe programmes, opioid substitution, naloxone) does not require abstinence. In India, the most used opioid in the 2019 national survey was heroin, ahead of pharmaceutical opioids and opium.

Treatment: detoxification, substitution and blockade

Imagine three ways of helping Manpreet. You could take him off opioids altogether, as safely and comfortably as possible, and hope he stays off. You could replace the injected street heroin, which he must buy several times a day, with a legal, long-acting opioid taken by mouth once a day under supervision. Or you could give him a drug that blocks the opioid receptors, so that heroin no longer has any effect. These are the three medical strategies, detoxification, agonist maintenance and antagonist treatment, and each suits different people. All three work better with psychological and social support, and the most important practical lesson of the last fifty years is that the second one, unfashionable as it sounds, saves the most lives.

Detoxification. Detoxification is the medically supervised management of withdrawal. The most comfortable method is to substitute an opioid and then reduce it: the person is stabilised on buprenorphine or methadone, and the dose is tapered over days to weeks. The alternative is to treat the withdrawal symptoms without an opioid. In 1978 Mark Gold, D. Eugene Redmond and Herbert Kleber (1934 to 2018) reported in The Lancet that clonidine, a blood-pressure drug, blocks acute opiate withdrawal symptoms. Clonidine is an alpha-2 adrenergic agonist: it stimulates the receptors that act as a brake on the locus coeruleus, and so calms the noradrenergic storm described in Section 10.5.3. It relieves sweating, gooseflesh, cramps, diarrhoea and anxiety, but helps less with aches, insomnia and craving, and it can drop the blood pressure. Lofexidine works the same way with less effect on blood pressure; it has been licensed in Britain since the 1990s and in the United States since 2018. Symptomatic medicines (loperamide for diarrhoea, an anti-emetic, simple painkillers) complete the regimen. The Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale guides dosing.

Detoxification is safe and it is necessary for anyone who wants to be opioid-free, but on its own it rarely produces lasting abstinence. Most people relapse within weeks or months, and because detoxification removes tolerance, relapse after it carries a high risk of overdose: Manpreet's story is common. Detoxification is therefore best seen as the doorway into treatment, not the treatment itself (Book 2, Section 9.9.5).

Key study: Dole and Nyswander's methadone maintenance (1965)

In the early 1960s Vincent Dole (1913 to 2006), a specialist in metabolism at the Rockefeller Institute in New York, teamed up with the psychiatrist Marie Nyswander (1919 to 1986), who had years of experience treating heroin users and had concluded that conventional psychotherapy and detoxification almost always failed. Dole's hypothesis was that long-term heroin use produces a lasting metabolic change, so that the dependent person needs an opioid somewhat as a person with diabetes needs insulin. They admitted a small group of men with long histories of heroin dependence and many failed treatments to a hospital ward and gave them oral methadone in slowly rising doses, once a day. The results surprised them. On a stable daily dose the men stopped craving heroin, did not appear intoxicated, and when they were given heroin experimentally they felt little from it, because the cross-tolerance produced by methadone blocked its euphoria. Discharged to the community on daily methadone, most stayed off heroin, went back to work or school, and stopped committing crimes. They published the results in 1965 in the Journal of the American Medical Association as "A medical treatment for diacetylmorphine (heroin) addiction". Criticism came at once and has never quite stopped: methadone is itself an opioid and produces dependence, so critics said the patients had merely swapped one addiction for another; the metabolic theory has never been proved; and some methadone leaks onto the street. But in hundreds of later studies, methadone maintenance has kept people in treatment, reduced heroin use, crime and HIV transmission, and lowered death rates, and it became the model for all agonist treatment.

Agonist maintenance with methadone. The logic of opioid substitution therapy (also called opioid agonist treatment or maintenance treatment) follows from pharmacology. Methadone is a full mu agonist, like heroin, but it differs in the ways that matter. It is taken by mouth, so there is no injection and no rush. It has a long half-life (about a day on average, though it varies widely between people), so a single daily dose keeps the level steady for 24 hours, with none of the cycle of highs and withdrawals that a heroin user goes through several times a day. And at an adequate dose its cross-tolerance blunts the effect of any heroin taken on top. The person's life no longer has to be organised around getting the next dose. Methadone has real dangers. Because it accumulates over the first days, the induction period is risky, and doses are started low and raised slowly; it prolongs the QT interval of the heart and can cause dangerous rhythm disturbances; combined with alcohol or benzodiazepines it can still stop breathing; and take-home doses can be diverted. For these reasons it is usually dispensed daily under supervision in a clinic, with take-home doses earned by stability.

Agonist maintenance with buprenorphine. Buprenorphine, a semi-synthetic opioid made from thebaine, is a partial agonist at the mu receptor (it is also a kappa antagonist). Two of its properties make it unusual. It binds the mu receptor extremely tightly and comes off it slowly, so its action is long and it displaces other opioids; and because it can only partly activate the receptor, its effects rise with dose only up to a ceiling, as Figure 10.11 shows. The ceiling means enough opioid effect to prevent withdrawal and craving, but much less respiratory depression than a full agonist, so buprenorphine taken on its own is far safer in overdose than methadone or heroin (though not when mixed with sedatives or alcohol). The tight binding means that a dose given while a full agonist is still on the receptors pushes it off and replaces a large effect with a smaller one: precipitated withdrawal. Buprenorphine is therefore started only when the person is already in moderate withdrawal, usually at least 12 hours after the last heroin and longer after methadone. It is taken as a tablet or film under the tongue, often combined with naloxone in a ratio of four to one. Taken correctly, the naloxone is barely absorbed and does nothing; if the tablet is crushed and injected, the naloxone acts and precipitates withdrawal, which discourages injection. Monthly slow-release injections are now available in some countries. Buprenorphine has fewer side effects than methadone, but in trials in which the dose could be adjusted it kept fewer people in treatment than methadone did.

Antagonist treatment. Naltrexone is a long-acting opioid antagonist, taken by mouth daily or given as a monthly depot injection. While it is on board, heroin produces no euphoria and no respiratory depression, so using is pointless; naltrexone itself produces no high, no dependence and has no street value. Its weaknesses are practical. The person must be completely free of opioids for about 7 to 10 days before starting, or the first dose precipitates severe withdrawal, and many dependent people cannot get through that gap. Many stop taking the tablets once motivation dips, and if they then return to their old dose without tolerance they are at high risk of overdose. Naltrexone works best for people with strong reasons to stay abstinent and someone checking that they take it: doctors and nurses whose licence depends on it, or people on probation. The depot injection improves adherence. Naltrexone's use for alcohol dependence is described in Section 10.4.

What the evidence shows. Systematic reviews of randomised trials (the Cochrane reviews led by Richard Mattick and colleagues) find that methadone maintenance keeps people in treatment and reduces heroin use far better than detoxification or treatment without medication, and that buprenorphine at adequate doses suppresses heroin use about as well, although flexible-dose buprenorphine retains fewer patients. The most important outcome is survival. In 2017 Luis Sordo and colleagues pooled cohort studies of more than 138,000 people treated with methadone or buprenorphine and found that the all-cause death rate was 11.3 per 1,000 person-years while people were on methadone and 36.1 after they left it, about a threefold difference; the corresponding figures for buprenorphine were 4.3 and 9.5. The two most dangerous periods were the first four weeks of methadone treatment and the weeks immediately after leaving either treatment. Agonist treatment also reduces injecting, HIV infection and crime, and both methadone and buprenorphine are on the World Health Organization's list of essential medicines. Medication works best alongside psychosocial help: contingency management (Book 2, Section 4.5.7), counselling and cognitive-behavioural therapy, family work, Narcotics Anonymous (Book 2, Section 7.11.7), relapse prevention (Book 2, Section 9.9.6) and therapeutic communities (Book 2, Section 9.9.7). Table 10.23 compares the medical options.

Table 10.23 The medical treatments of opioid dependence compared.
ApproachDrugsHow it worksStrengthsCautions
DetoxificationBuprenorphine or methadone taper; clonidine, lofexidine; symptomatic drugsEases withdrawal while the opioid is withdrawn; alpha-2 agonists calm the locus coeruleusSafe; needed before antagonist treatmentHigh relapse when used alone; lost tolerance raises overdose risk
Full agonist maintenanceMethadone (oral, once daily)Replaces heroin with a long-acting oral opioid; cross-tolerance blunts heroin's effectBest retention in treatment; large fall in deaths, crime and HIV riskRisky induction; QT prolongation; deadly with sedatives; diversion
Partial agonist maintenanceBuprenorphine, often with naloxone (sublingual); monthly depotsTight-binding partial mu agonist with a ceiling effectSafer in overdose; fewer side effects; office or clinic basedPrecipitated withdrawal if started too early; slightly lower retention
Antagonist treatmentNaltrexone (oral daily or monthly depot)Blocks the mu receptor so opioids have no effectNo dependence or street valueNeeds 7 to 10 opioid-free days first; poor adherence; overdose risk after stopping
