Indian context

India came to opioid substitution through HIV prevention. The National AIDS Control Organisation (NACO) began offering opioid substitution therapy, mainly with sublingual buprenorphine, to people who inject drugs in the late 2000s, at first through a few dozen centres run by non-governmental organisations, later through government hospitals as well; methadone was added in some centres later and remains less widely available. Government hospitals linked to the NDDTC at AIIMS also provide buprenorphine-based treatment. Punjab, facing the heroin epidemic among its young men, launched a large network of Outpatient Opioid Assisted Treatment (OOAT) clinics from October 2017, dispensing buprenorphine with naloxone, and in 2019 began allowing take-home doses. The national plan for drug demand reduction and India's de-addiction services are described in Section 10.4, and the NDPS Act in Section 10.1.

Clinical link

Families, and sometimes de-addiction staff, object that maintenance treatment "just swaps one addiction for another". A clinical psychologist should be able to answer that. The difference is between the compulsive use of a short-acting, injected, illegal drug of unknown strength, which organises the whole of a person's life and carries a high risk of death, and the supervised use of a long-acting oral medicine at a steady dose, which frees the person to work, look after children and engage in therapy. DSM-5-TR builds this into the diagnosis: a person who is taking methadone or buprenorphine and meets no criteria for opioid use disorder (apart from tolerance to and withdrawal from the medicine) is coded as being in remission, with the specifier on maintenance therapy. Pushing a stable patient to stop maintenance too soon is one of the commonest routes back to overdose.

Exam trap

Methadone is a full agonist (synthetic, oral, long-acting); buprenorphine is a partial agonist with a ceiling effect (semi-synthetic, sublingual, high affinity); naltrexone is a long-acting antagonist used after detoxification; naloxone is the short-acting antagonist for overdose, and it is added to buprenorphine tablets to deter injection. Clonidine and lofexidine are not opioids: they are alpha-2 adrenergic agonists that ease withdrawal. Methadone maintenance was introduced by Dole and Nyswander in 1965. Starting buprenorphine too soon after heroin, or naltrexone before 7 to 10 opioid-free days, causes precipitated withdrawal. Detoxification alone is not an adequate treatment for opioid dependence.

10.6 Stimulants, Tobacco and CaffeineLower yield · 1 Q

Opioids slow the brain down; the drugs of this section speed it up. A stimulant is a drug whose main effect is a general increase in neural and behavioural activity: more alertness, energy and confidence, less need for food and sleep. The family runs from the cup of filter coffee that starts a working day in Chennai, through the bidi smoked at a roadside tea stall and the tablet a college student takes before an examination, to crack cocaine and crystal methamphetamine. They differ enormously in potency, and the way the drug reaches the brain matters as much as the drug itself: coca leaves chewed in the Andes and crack smoked in a pipe contain the same molecule. DSM-5-TR separates them into three classes, each with its own rules: stimulant-related disorders (cocaine and the amphetamine-type substances), tobacco-related disorders and caffeine-related disorders. This section takes them in that order, then looks at MDMA (ecstasy), which DSM-5-TR actually files among the hallucinogens, and at the synthetic cathinones.

Cocaine: from the coca leaf to crack

The coca shrub, Erythroxylum coca, grows on the eastern slopes of the Andes in Peru and Bolivia, and the peoples of the region have chewed its leaves for thousands of years, usually with a pinch of lime or plant ash that helps release the active alkaloid. Chewed leaf eases hunger, fatigue and the breathlessness of high altitude, and because the cocaine is absorbed slowly and in small amounts, it causes few problems; coca tea is still served to tourists arriving in La Paz. The trouble began when chemistry concentrated the leaf. In 1860 Albert Niemann (1834 to 1861), a young chemist at the University of Göttingen, described the purified alkaloid in his doctoral dissertation and named it cocaine, noting that it numbed his tongue. Within a few years cocaine was everywhere. From 1863 a Corsican chemist, Angelo Mariani, sold Vin Mariani, a Bordeaux wine steeped in coca leaves that was endorsed by a pope, and in 1886 an Atlanta pharmacist, John Pemberton, created Coca-Cola, whose original recipe contained coca extract. In the early 1900s the company switched to coca leaves from which the cocaine had been removed; its stimulant effect today comes from caffeine.

Key study: Sigmund Freud, cocaine and cigars

In 1884 Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), then a young and ambitious neurologist in Vienna, read that a German army doctor had given cocaine to soldiers to help them endure manoeuvres. He obtained some, took it himself and was delighted. He pressed it on friends, colleagues and patients, sent some to his fiancée, and in July 1884 published "Über Coca" ("On coca"), an essay so enthusiastic about the drug that Pinel calls it a "song of praise". He even tried to cure his friend, the physiologist Ernst von Fleischl-Marxow, of morphine dependence by giving him cocaine; Fleischl became dependent on cocaine as well, developed a cocaine psychosis and died in 1891. Freud's colleague Carl Koller (1857 to 1944) took more careful notice of the numbing. In September 1884 a report of his experiments, read at the ophthalmological congress in Heidelberg, showed that a few drops of cocaine solution made the eye insensitive enough for surgery, and the age of local anaesthesia began. (The New York surgeon William Halsted developed cocaine nerve blocks in 1885 by experimenting on himself and became dependent on cocaine, and later on morphine.) As reports of cocaine dependence and psychosis accumulated, Freud was widely criticised. He stopped using cocaine around 1887 and seems to have had no difficulty doing so. His other drug was a different story. Freud smoked about 20 cigars a day. Told in 1894, at 38, to stop because of an irregular heartbeat, he managed seven weeks; once he stopped for fourteen months, and relapsed. In 1923, aged 67, he developed cancer of the mouth, and although he wrote that smoking had caused it, he kept smoking through attacks of chest pain whenever he smoked (tobacco angina) and through 33 operations by 1936, which cut away much of his upper jaw and palate and left him with an artificial prosthesis, so that he could swallow and speak only with difficulty. At 73, admitted to hospital for his heart, he stopped, and 23 days later he started again. He died in London in 1939, when his physician, at Freud's request, gave him the doses of morphine that ended the suffering of his incurable cancer. Pinel tells the story to make two points: nobody, however intelligent, is immune to addiction; and the drug that defeated Freud was not the dramatic, illegal one but tobacco.

Cocaine comes in several forms, and the differences are mainly about speed. Coca leaves are soaked and treated to make a crude coca paste, which is purified into cocaine hydrochloride, the white powder usually meant by "cocaine". Because it is a salt, the powder dissolves in water and can be sniffed ("snorted") so that it is absorbed through the lining of the nose, or injected; but heat destroys it, so it cannot be smoked. The base form of cocaine, by contrast, vaporises when heated and can be smoked. Freebase and crack are smokeable base forms; crack, named after the crackling sound it makes when smoked, is the cheap, impure version that spread through American cities from the mid-1980s. Smoked cocaine reaches the brain within seconds, as fast as an injection, while snorted cocaine takes minutes and coca leaf much longer. The faster the rise in the brain, the more intense the high and the stronger the compulsion to repeat it, a rule explained in Section 10.2. Cocaine also has a short half-life of about an hour, so the high fades quickly and users redose again and again, often in binges that last until the money or the drug runs out.

Cocaine has two actions that have nothing to do with pleasure but explain several of its dangers. It blocks sodium channels in nerve membranes, which is why it is a local anaesthetic (it was replaced in most medical uses by synthetic relatives such as procaine and lidocaine, whose names still end in its "-caine"); and it constricts blood vessels, which is why surgeons once used it to reduce bleeding in the nose and why chronic snorting can destroy the cartilage between the nostrils.

The amphetamines, and how the stimulants act

Amphetamine was first made in 1887 in Germany by the Romanian chemist Lazăr Edeleanu (1861 to 1941), who called it phenylisopropylamine and did not study its effects. In 1893 the Japanese chemist Nagai Nagayoshi made methamphetamine from ephedrine, the active ingredient of the Chinese herb ma huang, and in 1919 Akira Ogata produced it in crystalline form. Amphetamine's effects on the body were rediscovered in 1927 by the American chemist Gordon Alles, who was looking for a substitute for ephedrine as an asthma treatment and tested it on himself, and in the early 1930s the firm Smith, Kline and French sold it as the Benzedrine inhaler for blocked noses. Users soon discovered that they could break open the inhaler and swallow the amphetamine-soaked paper strip inside. In the Second World War amphetamines were given to soldiers and pilots on both sides to keep them awake: German forces received millions of tablets of methamphetamine under the brand name Pervitin, and in Japan it was sold as Philopon, which after the war led to a large epidemic of methamphetamine use there. In the decades that followed, amphetamines were prescribed for slimming, for depression and as "pep pills". Today their main medical uses are attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and narcolepsy (Chapter 3, Section 3.8, describes the prescription stimulants for ADHD), but illicit methamphetamine, as tablets, powder or the smokeable crystals called "ice" or "crystal meth", is a major drug of misuse; Pinel notes that the amphetamine-type stimulants are now more widely misused worldwide than cocaine. DSM-5-TR's "amphetamine-type substances" include amphetamine, dextroamphetamine and methamphetamine, and also drugs with different structures but similar effects such as methylphenidate and modafinil, and the plant stimulant khat.

How do these drugs act? Recall from Section 10.2 that after a dopamine neuron releases dopamine into the synapse, the signal is ended mainly by reuptake: a protein in the presynaptic membrane called the dopamine transporter pumps the dopamine back into the neuron. Similar transporters clear noradrenaline and serotonin. Cocaine and amphetamine both raise the level of these monoamines in the synapse, but in different ways.

  • Cocaine is a reuptake blocker. It sits on the dopamine, noradrenaline and serotonin transporters and jams them, so transmitter that the neuron releases stays in the synapse longer and at higher concentration. Cocaine therefore amplifies the neuron's own activity: its effect depends on the neuron firing.
  • Amphetamine is a releaser. It is similar enough in shape to dopamine and noradrenaline to be carried into the nerve terminal by the transporters. Once inside, it disrupts the storage of transmitter in vesicles and makes the transporters run backwards, so dopamine and noradrenaline pour out into the synapse whether or not the neuron is firing. At high doses it also inhibits monoamine oxidase, the enzyme that breaks these transmitters down. Methamphetamine does the same, enters the brain more readily and lasts longer.

In both cases the extra dopamine in the nucleus accumbens produces the euphoria and the drive to repeat, and the extra noradrenaline produces the "fight or flight" effects on the body: racing heart, raised blood pressure, dilated pupils, sweating and loss of appetite. Table 10.24 compares the two drugs. One more principle links them. Repeated doses produce tolerance to the euphoria, so users need more to get high, but they produce the opposite, sensitisation (reverse tolerance, defined in Section 10.1), to some other effects: repetitive movements, agitation and paranoia appear at lower doses after repeated binges. Sensitisation is one reason stimulant psychosis tends to come back more easily each time.

Table 10.24 Cocaine and the amphetamines compared.
FeatureCocaineAmphetamines (including methamphetamine)
OriginNatural alkaloid of the coca leaf (isolated 1860)Synthetic (amphetamine 1887; methamphetamine 1893)
Main actionBlocks the dopamine, noradrenaline and serotonin transporters (reuptake blocker)Enters the terminal and reverses the transporters, releasing dopamine and noradrenaline (releaser)
Other actionsLocal anaesthetic (sodium channel block); vasoconstrictorInhibits monoamine oxidase at high doses
DurationShort (half-life about an hour); frequent redosingLong (many hours); fewer doses a day
RoutesSnorted, injected; freebase and crack smokedSwallowed, snorted, injected; "ice" smoked
Medical use todayRare (topical anaesthesia of nose and throat)ADHD and narcolepsy (amphetamine salts, methylphenidate)
ICD-10 codeF14 cocaineF15 other stimulants, including caffeine
Exam trap

Cocaine blocks reuptake; amphetamines increase release (by reversing the transporter). Both raise synaptic dopamine and noradrenaline, and both act on the mesolimbic reward pathway. Cocaine is a natural plant alkaloid and a local anaesthetic; amphetamine and methamphetamine are synthetic. Do not credit Freud with local anaesthesia: Carl Koller demonstrated cocaine anaesthesia of the eye in 1884, the year of Freud's "Über Coca". Under the NDPS Act cocaine is legally a "narcotic drug", but pharmacologically it is a stimulant.

Stimulant intoxication and its physical dangers

When injected or smoked, a stimulant produces an instant surge of well-being, confidence and euphoria. Users describe feeling clever, attractive, tireless and in control; they talk fast, laugh easily, make plans and feel no need to eat or sleep. With higher doses the pleasure acquires an edge: restlessness, irritability, anxiety, suspiciousness and hypervigilance, grinding of the teeth, and repetitive, pointless activity. DSM-5-TR's criteria for stimulant intoxication require recent use of an amphetamine-type substance, cocaine or another stimulant; clinically significant behavioural or psychological change (euphoria or blunted feeling, changes in sociability, hypervigilance, sensitivity to others, anxiety, tension or anger, stereotyped behaviour, impaired judgement); and at least two of nine physical signs. Those signs are tachycardia or bradycardia; pupillary dilation; raised or lowered blood pressure; sweating or chills; nausea or vomiting; weight loss; psychomotor agitation or retardation; muscular weakness, respiratory depression, chest pain or cardiac arrhythmias; and confusion, seizures, abnormal movements (dyskinesias, dystonias) or coma.

Why does a list of stimulant effects include slowing of the heart, low blood pressure and psychomotor retardation? Because the picture depends on the dose, the person's tolerance and how long the use has gone on. The ordinary stimulant effects of a fresh dose are the common ones. The depressant-looking effects (sadness, slow pulse, low blood pressure, slowed movement) appear mainly with chronic high-dose use, when the person is exhausted and the transmitter stores are depleted. The specifier "with perceptual disturbances" is added when hallucinations occur with intact reality testing, or when there are illusions, in the absence of delirium.

Stimulant intoxication can kill, and it kills in a different way from opioids. The surge of noradrenaline, together with cocaine's constriction of blood vessels, makes the heart work harder while narrowing the arteries that feed it, so heart attacks occur even in young people with normal coronary arteries; dangerous rhythm disturbances, strokes (both bleeding and blocked vessels) and tears in the wall of the aorta are other emergencies. Body temperature can climb dangerously (hyperthermia), especially in hot, crowded places, and severe toxicity brings convulsions, muscle breakdown, kidney failure and death. Combinations add their own dangers. Cocaine taken with alcohol produces a compound called cocaethylene in the liver, which is more toxic to the heart than either drug; cocaine injected with heroin (a "speedball") combines the cardiac risk of one with the respiratory risk of the other. Long-term methamphetamine users often lose weight, develop severe tooth decay and skin sores from picking, and show cognitive problems; Pinel notes that people who have used amphetamine or methamphetamine, but not cocaine, have a raised risk of later developing Parkinson's disease, although the evidence is correlational. There is no antidote like naloxone for stimulants. Emergency care is supportive: a calm, cool environment, benzodiazepines for agitation and seizures, cooling for hyperthermia and monitoring of the heart.

Exam trap

Pupils are dilated in stimulant intoxication (and in opioid withdrawal and hallucinogen intoxication); they are pinpoint in opioid intoxication. Stimulant intoxication kills mainly through the heart and brain (myocardial infarction, arrhythmia, stroke) and through hyperthermia and seizures, not through respiratory depression as with opioids. Cocaine plus alcohol forms cocaethylene. DSM-5-TR's list of intoxication signs includes bradycardia and low blood pressure as well as their opposites, because chronic high-dose use can produce depressant-like effects.

Stimulant psychosis and formication

In the early 1950s a puzzling group of patients began to appear in London hospitals. They were admitted with delusions of persecution and diagnosed as having paranoid schizophrenia, yet within days of admission their symptoms cleared and they went home. A young psychiatrist at the Maudsley Hospital, Philip Connell, suspected amphetamine, then freely available in tablets and inhalers. He developed a urine test to detect it and collected cases, and in 1958 he published the monograph Amphetamine Psychosis. Connell showed that amphetamine could produce a psychosis that was often indistinguishable from acute paranoid schizophrenia: persecutory delusions and ideas of reference, auditory and visual hallucinations, in a person who was usually fully alert and oriented (only a handful of his patients were disoriented). It occurred mainly in people taking large doses, and it usually settled within about a week of stopping. Doubters suggested that the drug had merely triggered schizophrenia in people already prone to it, so researchers tested the idea directly: in 1970 Burton Angrist and Samuel Gershon gave volunteers who had used amphetamine before, but had no history of psychosis, repeated doses of it under hospital supervision, and several developed paranoid psychoses. Stimulant psychosis became one of the main supports for the dopamine hypothesis of schizophrenia (Chapter 4, Section 4.8), and substance-induced psychotic disorder is described in Chapter 4, Section 4.5.6.

The psychosis usually builds through a binge. Suspiciousness comes first: the user checks the window, peers through the curtains, believes the neighbours or the police are watching. Ideas of reference follow (a passing car, a remark on television, a cough in the next room are taken as signals), then frank persecutory delusions, often with jealousy, and hallucinations. DSM-5-TR describes paranoid ideation, auditory hallucinations in a clear sensorium and tactile hallucinations, which the person usually recognises as drug effects; visual hallucinations are common as well, as Connell had found. The person may be violent in what he believes is self-defence. Many show stereotypy, repetitive purposeless behaviour, and the Swedish psychiatrist Gösta Rylander, studying people who injected stimulants in Stockholm, described in 1972 what users called "punding": hours spent compulsively taking apart and reassembling radios and clocks, sorting objects or grooming. Cocaine psychosis is usually brief, lasting hours to days, because the drug is short-acting; amphetamine and methamphetamine psychoses last longer. Most clear within days to a week once the person stops and sleeps, although DSM-5-TR notes that cocaine-related disturbances can persist for up to a month, and a minority of people go on to develop a lasting psychotic illness.

The tactile hallucinations deserve special attention because examiners ask about them. Formication (from the Latin formica, ant) is the false perception of insects crawling on or under the skin. Users of cocaine call them "cocaine bugs", and the French psychiatrist Valentin Magnan (1835 to 1916) described in 1889 how his cocaine-using patients felt foreign bodies, like fine sand or powder, moving beneath the skin, a symptom since called Magnan's sign. The sensation is so convincing that people scratch and dig at the skin with fingernails, needles or knives to get the "bugs" out, and arrive with excoriations and infected sores on their forearms and face. During intoxication many users recognise the sensations as drug effects, which is why DSM-5-TR's "perceptual disturbances" specifier refers to hallucinations with intact reality testing; when insight is lost, the sensations become part of a delusion of infestation. Formication is a sign (Chapter 2, Section 2.3) rather than a diagnosis, and it has a short list of usual causes: stimulant intoxication (cocaine and amphetamines), and withdrawal from alcohol (delirium tremens, Section 10.3) or from sedative-hypnotics (Section 10.7). It is also reported with nerve damage from diabetes or alcohol, around the menopause, and in delusional infestation (Ekbom's syndrome), where a person is fixedly convinced, without drugs, that parasites live in the skin.

Satish, a 29-year-old events manager in Mumbai, was brought to the emergency department by his flatmate at four in the morning. For three days and nights he had been taking mephedrone ("MD") and then methamphetamine at a series of parties, and he had not slept. He was pacing, sweating and talking rapidly, convinced that the neighbours had hidden cameras in the ceiling fan and were sending what they filmed to a gang who planned to kill him. He kept looking at the door and said he could hear men whispering his name in the corridor. Every few seconds he rubbed his forearms, which were scratched raw: tiny insects, he said, were crawling under his skin, and he had tried to cut one out with a razor blade. His pupils were widely dilated, his pulse was 128 a minute, his blood pressure high and his temperature 38.4 degrees Celsius. Yet he knew where he was, the date and the name of his flatmate, and he could count backwards without error. A urine test was positive for amphetamines. With a quiet room, intravenous fluids, a benzodiazepine for agitation and, for two days, a low dose of an antipsychotic, he slept for most of 36 hours. By the fourth day the voices and the insects had gone, and he was embarrassed about the cameras. His clear consciousness and intact orientation are what distinguish a stimulant psychosis from delirium (Section 10.8): the confusion, fluctuation and disorientation of delirium are absent, even though the hallucinations are vivid.

How do you tell a stimulant psychosis from a first episode of schizophrenia? The clues are the history of a binge in the days before, prominent visual and tactile hallucinations (in schizophrenia hallucinations are mainly auditory), physical signs of stimulant use, a positive urine test, relatively preserved emotional expression with little thought disorder or negative symptoms, and above all rapid resolution within days of abstinence. If psychotic symptoms persist well beyond a month of abstinence, an independent psychotic disorder becomes much more likely.

Exam trap

Formication (tactile hallucinations of insects crawling on or under the skin) is most characteristic of stimulant intoxication (cocaine bugs, Magnan's sign) and of alcohol or sedative withdrawal, classically delirium tremens. The tempting wrong options do not produce it. Major depressive disorder with psychotic features produces mood-congruent delusions (guilt, ruin, disease) and, less often, accusing or derogatory voices, not crawling insects. Specific phobia is intense fear of an object or situation such as insects or spiders: the fear is of a real or anticipated stimulus, and there is no false perception at all. Generalised anxiety disorder is chronic, uncontrollable worry with tension, restlessness and fatigue; the tingling or numbness some anxious people feel comes from overbreathing and is not experienced as insects. Amphetamine psychosis resembles paranoid schizophrenia (Connell 1958) but occurs in clear consciousness and usually resolves within days to a week.

The crash and stimulant withdrawal

What goes up must come down, and with stimulants the coming down has its own name: the crash. After a binge the stores of dopamine and noradrenaline are depleted, the receptors have adapted to a flood that has suddenly stopped, and the person is left exhausted, flat and miserable. DSM-5-TR's criteria for stimulant withdrawal require the stopping or reduction of prolonged stimulant use, followed within a few hours to several days by dysphoric mood plus at least two of five physiological changes: fatigue; vivid, unpleasant dreams; insomnia or hypersomnia; increased appetite; and psychomotor retardation or agitation. Notice the mirror image again. Intoxication brought euphoria, energy, sleeplessness, loss of appetite and agitation; withdrawal brings dysphoria, fatigue, long heavy sleep, hunger and slowing. The manual adds that the heart rate often falls (bradycardia is a reliable sign), and that anhedonia and craving are common although they are not among the criteria.

Physically, stimulant withdrawal is mild. There are no seizures, no delirium and no danger to life of the kind that alcohol or sedative withdrawal brings, and this is why the older literature called cocaine "psychologically" rather than "physically" addictive. The danger is psychological. The depressed mood can be intense enough to meet the criteria for a major depressive episode, it usually resolves within about a week, and DSM-5-TR singles out depressive symptoms with suicidal thoughts or behaviour as the most serious problem of the crash. A person crashing after a binge needs to be asked about suicide. Table 10.25 sets intoxication and withdrawal side by side.

The most influential description of what happens after a cocaine binge came from Frank Gawin and Herbert Kleber at Yale. In 1986 they reported detailed longitudinal interviews with 30 chronic cocaine users and described a three-phase sequence of abstinence, shown in Figure 10.12. The first phase, the crash, begins within hours of the end of a binge and lasts up to about four days: agitation and depression give way to exhaustion, very long sleep and ravenous hunger, and during this phase craving is surprisingly low; the person wants sleep, not cocaine. The second phase, withdrawal proper, lasts for weeks (up to about ten in their account): mood is flat, energy is low and nothing gives pleasure, and against this joyless background craving returns, sharpened by memories of the high and by every reminder of using. This is when relapse is most likely. The third phase, extinction, is indefinite: mood returns to normal, but people, places, money, music or a glimpse of white powder can still trigger sudden, intense craving months or years later. Later studies of cocaine-dependent patients kept on a hospital or research ward, away from such cues (William Weddington and colleagues at the National Institute on Drug Abuse in 1990, following 12 men for 28 days, and Sally Satel and colleagues at Yale in 1991, following 22 patients for three weeks), found that mood distress and craving were worst at admission and then improved gradually and steadily, without a distinct crash or second phase, which suggests that much of the craving Gawin and Kleber saw in outpatients was driven by cues in the environment, exactly as conditioning theory predicts (Section 10.2).

Table 10.25 Stimulant intoxication and stimulant withdrawal: another set of mirror images.
FeatureIntoxicationWithdrawal (the crash)
MoodEuphoria, confidence, grandiosity; later anxiety, irritability, paranoiaDysphoria, anhedonia, sometimes suicidal depression
Energy and activityIncreased; agitation, stereotypyFatigue; psychomotor retardation (or agitation)
SleepLittle or noneHypersomnia (or insomnia); vivid, unpleasant dreams
AppetiteLost; weight lossIncreased
Heart and pupilsTachycardia, raised blood pressure, dilated pupilsBradycardia
PerceptionPossible paranoid psychosis, formicationNot typical
Physical dangerHeart attack, arrhythmia, stroke, hyperthermia, seizuresLittle physical danger; the risk is suicide and relapse
Exam trap

A tired, hungry, oversleeping, depressed person a day or two after a binge is in stimulant withdrawal; the key words are dysphoria with fatigue, hypersomnia and increased appetite. Stimulant withdrawal does not cause seizures or delirium. Craving and anhedonia are common but are not DSM-5-TR withdrawal criteria. The most serious risk of the crash is suicidal depression. Gawin and Kleber's (1986) three phases are crash, withdrawal and extinction, in that order.

MDMA (ecstasy) and the synthetic cathinones

MDMA (3,4-methylenedioxymethamphetamine) is a ring-substituted amphetamine. It was first made by the German company Merck in 1912 as an intermediate in a chemical synthesis and then forgotten. In 1976 the American chemist Alexander Shulgin (1925 to 2014) made it again, noticed its unusual emotional effects and introduced it to a psychotherapist friend, and in the late 1970s and early 1980s some therapists in the United States used it to help patients talk about painful experiences. At the same time it spread into nightclubs and dance parties as "ecstasy", and in 1985 the United States banned it. It is usually taken as a tablet or as crystals ("Molly") at parties and festivals.

MDMA works mainly as a releaser of serotonin: like amphetamine it enters nerve terminals through a transporter and reverses it, but it acts most strongly on the serotonin transporter, releasing large amounts of serotonin, with smaller effects on noradrenaline and dopamine; it also raises the hormone oxytocin. The result is a stimulant high with a distinctive emotional colour. Users feel euphoric, energetic and above all close to other people: warm, open, trusting and empathic. For this reason MDMA is called an empathogen or entactogen, and Pinel describes it as both a stimulant and an empathogen. DSM-5-TR, however, does not file MDMA with the stimulants. It lists MDMA among the hallucinogens, with mescaline in the chemical group called the phenylalkylamines, and notes that its effects come from both its hallucinogenic and its stimulant properties. A person with problems from MDMA is therefore diagnosed with other hallucinogen use disorder or other hallucinogen intoxication, never with a stimulant-related disorder, and the manual adds that ecstasy users have a higher risk of developing a hallucinogen use disorder than users of other hallucinogens. Because DSM-5-TR has no hallucinogen withdrawal diagnosis, there is no MDMA withdrawal diagnosis either, although many regular users report malaise, poor appetite, low, anxious or irritable mood, poor concentration and disturbed sleep after stopping. ICD-11, by contrast, gives MDMA and related drugs a category of their own (Table 10.27). The physical effects are those of an amphetamine: a fast heart, raised blood pressure, jaw clenching and teeth grinding, sweating and loss of appetite.

The deaths that make the news come from a few specific dangers. The first is hyperthermia: MDMA raises body temperature, and hours of dancing in a hot, crowded venue can push it to levels that cause muscle breakdown, kidney failure, clotting failure and death. The second is hyponatraemia, a dangerous fall in blood sodium: MDMA makes the body release antidiuretic hormone and hold on to water, so a user who drinks large amounts of water to avoid overheating can dilute the blood sodium enough to make the brain swell, causing confusion, seizures and coma. The third is serotonin toxicity (serotonin syndrome), particularly when MDMA is combined with antidepressants, tramadol or other serotonergic drugs. Some users also describe a "midweek low", a few days of low mood and irritability after a weekend of use, attributed to depleted serotonin. Whether regular MDMA use permanently damages serotonin neurons has been argued for decades. High doses damage serotonergic axons in animals, and heavy users perform slightly worse on some memory tests, but Pinel notes that the effects in humans have often been small or hard to reproduce. The debate was not helped when an influential paper in Science in 2002, claiming that a typical recreational dose regimen caused severe damage to dopamine neurons in monkeys, was retracted in 2003 after the researchers discovered that the animals had been injected with methamphetamine from mislabelled bottles.

Interest in MDMA as a therapeutic drug has returned. Trials of MDMA-assisted psychotherapy for post-traumatic stress disorder reported promising results, and since July 2023 Australia has allowed specially authorised psychiatrists to prescribe it for PTSD. In August 2024, however, the United States Food and Drug Administration declined to approve it, asking for further studies because of concerns about the design and conduct of the trials. Psychedelic-assisted therapy more generally is discussed in Section 10.7.

The synthetic cathinones are relatives of cathinone, the stimulant in the leaves of the khat shrub (Catha edulis), which are chewed socially in Yemen and East Africa. Chemists have made dozens of synthetic versions, such as mephedrone, MDPV and alpha-PVP, sold as "legal highs" or, in North America, disguised as "bath salts". They act like amphetamines or cocaine and can cause severe agitation, paranoid psychosis, hyperthermia and death. In India mephedrone, known as "MD" or "meow meow", spread in Mumbai's party scene and was brought under the NDPS Act in February 2015; Satish's binge in Section 10.6.4 began with it. ICD-11 gives the synthetic cathinones a separate category; DSM-5-TR includes them among the stimulants.

Exam trap

MDMA (ecstasy) is a ring-substituted amphetamine that acts mainly by releasing serotonin; it is an empathogen (entactogen). In DSM-5-TR it sits under other hallucinogen-related disorders, not stimulant-related disorders. Its specific dangers are hyperthermia, hyponatraemia and serotonin toxicity. The synthetic cathinones ("bath salts", mephedrone) are stimulants related to khat. Do not confuse MDMA with ketamine or PCP, which are dissociative NMDA antagonists (Section 10.7).

Treating stimulant use disorder

DSM-5-TR's stimulant use disorder uses the same eleven criteria as the other substance use disorders (Section 10.1), with one familiar note: tolerance and withdrawal do not count when the stimulant is taken as prescribed, for example for ADHD or narcolepsy. The manual adds that stimulant use disorder can develop very fast, sometimes within a week of starting to use.

The disappointing fact about treatment is that, despite decades of trials, no medicine has yet been approved for treating cocaine or methamphetamine use disorder. Researchers have tried antidepressants to lift the crash, dopamine agonists to replace the missing dopamine, disulfiram, modafinil, topiramate and even vaccines that make antibodies against cocaine; none has proved reliably effective. The most encouraging trial so far was published in 2021 by Madhukar Trivedi and colleagues: in adults with moderate or severe methamphetamine use disorder, a combination of extended-release injectable naltrexone (every three weeks) and daily oral bupropion produced a response (at least three of four urine samples free of methamphetamine at the end of treatment) in 13.6 percent, compared with 2.5 percent on placebo. That is a real effect, but most patients did not respond. For now, the treatments with the strongest evidence are psychological, and the strongest of all came from a simple behavioural idea.

Key study: Higgins's vouchers for cocaine-free urine (1991 to 1994)

In the late 1980s cocaine dependence was considered almost untreatable in outpatients. Stephen Higgins and his colleagues at the University of Vermont reasoned that if drug use is behaviour maintained by its reinforcing consequences (Section 10.2), then a competing, immediate reinforcer for not using might change it. They combined the community reinforcement approach (which helps the person build a rewarding life without the drug: relationships, work, recreation) with contingency management: patients gave urine samples several times a week, and each cocaine-free sample earned a voucher that could be exchanged for goods in local shops, with the value rising for each consecutive clean sample and resetting after a positive one. After early reports in 1991 and 1993, they published a randomised trial in 1994 in which 40 cocaine-dependent adults received the community reinforcement treatment with or without the vouchers. In the voucher group 75 percent completed 24 weeks of treatment, against 40 percent without vouchers, and the average length of continuous cocaine abstinence confirmed by urine tests was 11.7 weeks against 6.0 weeks. Many later trials have confirmed that contingency management is one of the most effective treatments for stimulant use disorders. The criticisms are practical and moral: the effect fades when the incentives stop, the programme costs money, and many people dislike "paying people not to use drugs". The technique itself is described in Book 2, Section 4.5.7.

Other psychological treatments with good evidence are cognitive-behavioural therapy focused on coping with craving and high-risk situations, the community reinforcement approach on its own, and structured outpatient programmes that combine these with family involvement and drug testing; mutual-help groups such as Narcotics Anonymous help many people stay abstinent (Book 2, Sections 7.11.7 and 9.9). Acute problems are treated as they arise: the crash with rest, food and attention to suicide risk, stimulant psychosis with a calm environment, benzodiazepines and, if needed, a short course of an antipsychotic.

Stimulants are still uncommon in India compared with alcohol, cannabis and opioids. The 2019 national survey found current use of cocaine in 0.10 percent of Indians aged 10 to 75 and of amphetamine-type stimulants in 0.18 percent, among the lowest of all the substance categories. Use is concentrated in particular places and groups: cocaine among affluent party-goers in the big cities, mephedrone in Mumbai and other cities, and methamphetamine tablets (yaba, a mixture of methamphetamine and caffeine) and crystal methamphetamine entering the north-eastern states from Myanmar.

Exam trap

There is no approved medication for cocaine or methamphetamine use disorder; there is no stimulant equivalent of methadone or naloxone. The best-supported treatment is contingency management (vouchers or prizes for drug-free urine), developed for cocaine by Stephen Higgins in the early 1990s, usually combined with the community reinforcement approach. Disulfiram's established use is for alcohol (Section 10.4), not stimulants.

Tobacco and nicotine

Tobacco, Nicotiana tabacum, is a plant of the Americas. Spanish and Portuguese sailors carried it to Europe in the sixteenth century, and its active alkaloid is named after Jean Nicot, the French ambassador in Lisbon who sent tobacco to the French court in 1560 and promoted it as a medicine. Portuguese traders brought it to India around the end of the sixteenth century, and within a few generations it was being smoked in hookahs and chillums, chewed with betel and lime, and later rolled into the bidi, a little tobacco wrapped in a tendu leaf. Freud's story in Section 10.6.1 already shows its power. Pinel puts the point another way: can you think of any other psychoactive drug that people take almost continuously, even while walking along the street?

Nicotine acts on nicotinic acetylcholine receptors, ion channels that normally open in response to acetylcholine. The subtype most important for addiction, made of alpha4 and beta2 subunits, sits on dopamine neurons in the ventral tegmental area and on the nerve terminals that feed them; when nicotine opens these channels, the dopamine neurons fire and release dopamine in the nucleus accumbens, the same final common path that cocaine and heroin use (Section 10.2). Nicotine also releases noradrenaline, acetylcholine, serotonin and endorphins, which helps explain the smoker's paradox of feeling both more alert and more relaxed after a cigarette. Three features of the way nicotine is delivered make it extraordinarily addictive. Inhaled smoke carries nicotine from the lungs to the brain within about ten to twenty seconds, faster than an injection into an arm vein. Nicotine's half-life is short (about two hours), so smokers top up throughout the day. And each cigarette is about ten puffs, so a person who smokes twenty cigarettes a day gives the brain some two hundred separate doses, each paired with a cup of tea, the end of a meal, a work break, a phone call or a moment of stress, so that the whole fabric of daily life becomes a set of cues for smoking. Repeated exposure also changes the receptors themselves: they desensitise during the day (the first cigarette of the morning, after the receptors have recovered overnight, is usually the most satisfying) and they increase in number in smokers' brains.

A non-smoker's first few cigarettes usually cause nausea, dizziness, coughing, sweating and sometimes vomiting; within days tolerance removes these effects, and the smoker reports feeling relaxed, alert and less hungry. That fast tolerance is part of why DSM-5-TR has no diagnosis of tobacco intoxication: in regular users clinically significant intoxication is very rare (ICD-10 does code acute intoxication with tobacco, and ICD-11 keeps a code for nicotine intoxication, which is seen mainly in naive users or after unusually large doses).

DSM-5-TR's tobacco use disorder uses the standard eleven criteria for a substance use disorder, two or more within twelve months, with examples adapted to tobacco (smoking in bed as hazardous use; arguments about smoking as a social problem). Some criteria are rarely met: because tobacco is cheap, legal and does not intoxicate, few smokers spend much time getting it or recovering from it, and few fail in their work because of it. The criteria most often met are craving, unsuccessful efforts to cut down, use despite knowing the harm, tolerance and withdrawal. Markers of dependence include smoking within 30 minutes of waking, smoking daily, the number of cigarettes per day and waking at night to smoke; the Fagerström questionnaire built on such items belongs to the Assessment book. A person using nicotine replacement long term, who meets no other criteria, is in remission "on maintenance therapy".

Tobacco withdrawal is the syndrome that every smoker who has tried to quit knows. DSM-5-TR requires daily use for at least several weeks, then an abrupt stop or reduction, followed within 24 hours by at least four of seven signs: irritability, frustration or anger; anxiety; difficulty concentrating; increased appetite; restlessness; depressed mood; and insomnia. It usually begins within 24 hours, peaks after two or three days and lasts two or three weeks. The heart rate falls by 5 to 12 beats a minute in the first few days, and weight rises by an average of 2 to 3 kilograms over the first year. About half of daily smokers who stop for two days or more have four or more symptoms, most commonly anxiety, irritability and difficulty concentrating. Craving is not one of the criteria, but it is almost universal and it is the main reason people fail. Notice that tobacco withdrawal, unlike alcohol withdrawal, contains no tremor, seizures or hallucinations, and unlike opioid withdrawal no streaming eyes or diarrhoea: it is a syndrome of mood, concentration and appetite.

Thangam, a 52-year-old auto-rickshaw driver in Madurai, had smoked about twenty-five bidis a day since he was sixteen and also chewed tobacco with betel leaf after meals. When the doctor at the medical college told him that his breathlessness was chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, he threw away his bidis that evening. By the next afternoon he was snapping at passengers, could not sit still at the auto stand, kept losing track of fares, felt hungry all the time and lay awake most of the night; on the third day he felt low and tearful. On the fifth day, waiting at the tea stall where he had always smoked his mid-morning bidi, he borrowed one from another driver "just to calm down", and within a week he was back to twenty. His withdrawal had all the classic features (irritability, restlessness, poor concentration, increased appetite, insomnia, low mood), and his relapse shows how cues such as the tea stall drive craving long after the first days. On his second attempt he set a quit date with the hospital's tobacco cessation clinic, used a nicotine patch with nicotine gum for breakthrough cravings, and planned what he would do instead at the tea stall; at six months he was still not smoking.

Why does any of this matter so much? Because tobacco kills more people than all the other drugs in this chapter together. In 1950 Richard Doll (1912 to 2005) and Austin Bradford Hill (1897 to 1991) reported in the British Medical Journal that patients with lung cancer in London hospitals were far more likely than matched patients without it to be smokers, and in 1951 they began following up about 34,000 male British doctors. After 40 years of this study, now known as the British Doctors Study, Doll, Richard Peto and colleagues concluded in 1994 that about half of all regular cigarette smokers are eventually killed by their habit. After 50 years (2004), men born between 1900 and 1930 who kept smoking cigarettes had died on average about ten years younger than lifelong non-smokers, but stopping at age 50 had halved the excess risk and stopping at 30 had avoided almost all of it. The World Health Organization's current estimate is that tobacco kills more than seven million people a year, and that second-hand smoke alone causes more than 1.6 million premature deaths; about 1.2 billion people use it, most of them in low- and middle-income countries. Smoking causes cancers of the lung, mouth, throat, oesophagus, stomach, pancreas, kidney and bladder, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, heart attacks and strokes, and harms the fetus. Smokeless tobacco causes cancers of the mouth and oral precancerous conditions such as leukoplakia and oral submucous fibrosis. Pinel's most chilling illustration is Buerger's disease, a narrowing of the blood vessels of the legs seen mostly in male smokers, in which continued smoking leads to gangrene and amputation after amputation, and yet surgeons report patients smoking in their hospital beds after the second or third operation.

Indian context

The second Global Adult Tobacco Survey (GATS-2, 2016 to 2017) found that 28.6 percent of Indian adults aged 15 and over, about 26.7 crore people, used tobacco in some form, down from 34.6 percent in the first survey of 2009 to 2010. Use was 42.4 percent among men and 14.2 percent among women. India is unusual in that smokeless tobacco is used by more people (21.4 percent of adults) than smoked tobacco (10.7 percent). The smokeless products include khaini (tobacco rubbed with lime), gutka (tobacco with areca nut, lime and flavourings), zarda (flavoured tobacco chewed with betel), mawa, mishri and gul (roasted tobacco rubbed on the teeth and gums), and betel quid with tobacco; among smoked products, the bidi is more common than the cigarette. Women use tobacco mainly in smokeless forms. The main law is the Cigarettes and Other Tobacco Products Act (COTPA), 2003, which bans smoking in public places, tobacco advertising, sale to anyone under 18 and sale within 100 yards of an educational institution, and requires health warnings on packs (pictorial warnings now cover most of the pack). Most states have banned gutka under food safety regulations since 2012, and the Prohibition of Electronic Cigarettes Act, 2019, bans e-cigarettes. The National Tobacco Control Programme runs tobacco cessation centres and a national quitline.

Exam trap

"Which substance has the highest addictive liability: marijuana, alcohol, nicotine (tobacco) or cocaine?" The answer is nicotine. Addictive liability (the capture rate) means the proportion of people who try a drug who go on to become dependent, and in the large American surveys that proportion is higher for tobacco than for any other drug. In the National Comorbidity Survey (Anthony, Warner and Kessler, 1994) about a third of everyone who had ever smoked had become dependent, against nearly a quarter of heroin users, about one in six cocaine users, about one in seven drinkers and fewer than one in ten users of cannabis; the later NESARC survey put the lifetime figure for nicotine at about two thirds (Section 10.2.5 and Table 10.11 give the full figures). Cocaine is the tempting option because it is more intensely rewarding per dose and animals will work hard for it, but a smaller share of people who try it become dependent. Alcohol is used by far more people but captures a smaller proportion of them, and marijuana has the lowest liability of the four. Nicotine wins because of its speed of delivery, the hundreds of doses a day, its legality and cheapness, and because it does not intoxicate, so it fits into every part of life. DSM-5-TR has tobacco use disorder and tobacco withdrawal but no tobacco intoxication.

Helping people stop smoking

Most smokers want to stop, and most attempts fail: Pinel notes that only about 20 percent of attempts to stop smoking succeed for two years or more, and attempts made without help do worse. The behavioural side of treatment (the five As of brief advice, setting a quit date, planning for high-risk situations, coping with cravings and relapse prevention) is described in Book 2, Section 9.4.4. Here we look at the medicines, which roughly double the chance of success and work best combined with behavioural support. Table 10.26 summarises them.

Nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) supplies nicotine without the tar, carbon monoxide and other toxins of smoke, and in a slower, steadier form that relieves withdrawal and craving without the rapid spike that makes smoking so reinforcing. It comes as chewing gum (chewed until the taste comes, then "parked" against the cheek so the nicotine is absorbed through the lining of the mouth), skin patches worn for 16 or 24 hours, lozenges, inhalators and sprays. Combining a patch, for a steady background level, with a short-acting form for breakthrough cravings works better than either alone. Bupropion, an antidepressant that blocks the reuptake of dopamine and noradrenaline and also blocks nicotinic receptors, is started a week or two before the quit date; it reduces craving and the low mood of withdrawal, but it lowers the seizure threshold and must not be used by people with epilepsy, eating disorders or a risk of seizures from alcohol or sedative withdrawal.

Varenicline, introduced in 2006, is the most effective single medicine. It is a partial agonist at the alpha4 beta2 nicotinic receptor, and its logic is exactly that of buprenorphine in Figure 10.11. Because it partly activates the receptor, it releases enough dopamine to ease withdrawal and craving; because it binds tightly and occupies the receptor, a cigarette smoked on top of it gives much less reward. Its common side effects are nausea and vivid dreams. Early reports of depression, agitation and suicidal thoughts led to a boxed warning in the United States, but the large EAGLES trial (2016), which included smokers with psychiatric illnesses, found no significant increase in moderate or severe neuropsychiatric adverse events with varenicline compared with placebo or the nicotine patch, and in December 2016 the warning was removed. Varenicline was modelled on cytisine, a plant alkaloid from the seeds of the laburnum tree that has been sold cheaply in Eastern Europe as a smoking aid since the 1960s. Trials published in 2011 by Robert West and colleagues (against placebo) and in 2014 by Natalie Walker and colleagues (against NRT) showed that cytisine works, and because it is so inexpensive it is attractive for countries like India; the World Health Organization's 2024 guideline on tobacco cessation recommends varenicline, NRT, bupropion and cytisine. Electronic cigarettes, first produced by the Chinese pharmacist Hon Lik in 2003, help some smokers to quit but also draw young people into nicotine use; India banned them in 2019.

Table 10.26 Medicines that help people stop using tobacco.
MedicineHow it worksPractical pointsCautions
Nicotine replacement (gum, patch, lozenge, inhalator, spray)Replaces nicotine slowly and steadily without the toxins of smokePatch plus a short-acting form works bestSkin irritation (patch), hiccups and jaw ache (gum)
BupropionBlocks dopamine and noradrenaline reuptake; blocks nicotinic receptorsStart 1 to 2 weeks before the quit dateInsomnia, dry mouth; seizures, so avoid in epilepsy and eating disorders
VareniclinePartial agonist at the alpha4 beta2 nicotinic receptorMost effective single drugNausea, vivid dreams; neuropsychiatric fears not confirmed by EAGLES (2016)
CytisinePlant-derived partial agonist at the same receptorCheap; recommended by WHO (2024)Nausea, sleep disturbance
Clinical link

People with schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, depression and other substance use disorders smoke far more than the general population and die years earlier, much of the gap being due to tobacco. Two facts matter on a psychiatric ward. First, compounds in tobacco smoke (not the nicotine itself) speed up a liver enzyme that breaks down several psychiatric medicines, clozapine and olanzapine among them; when a patient stops smoking, for example on admission to a smoke-free ward, the blood levels of these drugs can rise sharply and cause toxicity, so doses may need to be reduced. Second, the irritability and restlessness of tobacco withdrawal can be mistaken for agitation, akathisia or alcohol withdrawal; DSM-5-TR notes that relief of the symptoms by nicotine confirms the diagnosis.

Exam trap

Varenicline is a partial agonist at the alpha4 beta2 nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (not an antagonist, and not a dopamine agonist); cytisine works the same way. Bupropion is an antidepressant that inhibits dopamine and noradrenaline reuptake and is contraindicated in epilepsy and eating disorders. Tobacco withdrawal requires four of seven signs within 24 hours; it peaks at 2 to 3 days and lasts 2 to 3 weeks. Doll and Hill linked smoking to lung cancer in 1950.

Caffeine

Caffeine is the most widely used psychoactive drug in the world. It is found in coffee, tea, cola drinks, energy drinks, chocolate, many cold and headache remedies and some slimming pills; a cup of brewed coffee typically contains roughly 100 milligrams, a cup of tea rather less than half that, and DSM-5-TR reports that American adults who use caffeine take about 280 milligrams a day on average. Because it is woven into daily rituals (the morning filter coffee, the office tea break), many people do not realise that they are physically dependent on it until they miss a dose.

Caffeine works by blocking adenosine receptors. Adenosine builds up in the brain during the hours of waking, and by acting on its receptors it inhibits the activity of many neurons and produces the growing pressure to sleep (Chapter 8). Caffeine is an adenosine receptor antagonist: it occupies the receptors without activating them, so the "brake" of adenosine is released and the person feels alert, with faster reaction times and less fatigue. Through receptors that interact with dopamine receptors in the striatum it also has mild rewarding effects. Its half-life is about 4 to 6 hours; oral contraceptives slow its elimination and smoking speeds it up, so a heavy coffee drinker who stops smoking may find the same coffee suddenly making him jittery.

DSM-5-TR recognises two caffeine diagnoses in its main text. Caffeine intoxication requires recent consumption of caffeine, typically a high dose well in excess of 250 milligrams, followed by five or more of twelve signs: restlessness, nervousness, excitement, insomnia, flushed face, diuresis (passing a lot of urine), gastrointestinal disturbance, muscle twitching, a rambling flow of thought and speech, tachycardia or cardiac arrhythmia, periods of inexhaustibility, and psychomotor agitation. The first seven can appear at quite modest doses in sensitive people such as children, the elderly and those not used to caffeine; the last five usually need more than a gram a day. Picture a postgraduate student who, in the week before an entrance examination, drinks six strong coffees and two energy drinks a day: he is wired and tremulous, his heart races, he cannot sleep, his stomach churns, he keeps running to the toilet and his thoughts tumble out faster than he can organise them. Caffeine intoxication can look like a panic attack, generalised anxiety, mania or amphetamine intoxication, and the timing of the symptoms in relation to caffeine is the clue. It usually clears within a day, but very large doses (5 to 10 grams, possible with caffeine tablets or powders) can be fatal.

Caffeine withdrawal requires prolonged daily use, then an abrupt stop or reduction, followed within 24 hours by three or more of five signs: headache; marked fatigue or drowsiness; dysphoric or depressed mood or irritability; difficulty concentrating; and flu-like symptoms (nausea, vomiting, or muscle pain or stiffness). Headache is the hallmark: diffuse, throbbing and worse on movement. Symptoms usually begin 12 to 24 hours after the last dose, peak after one or two days and last two to nine days, although headaches can recur for up to three weeks, and they vanish within an hour of a cup of coffee. Withdrawal can follow even modest daily intake (about 100 milligrams), and it is often misread as the flu or a migraine: the "weekend headache" of the person who drinks coffee at work but sleeps late on Sunday, or the headache on a fasting day or before an operation when nothing may be taken by mouth.

What DSM-5-TR does not have in its main text is a caffeine use disorder. Many caffeine users show tolerance and withdrawal, but the manual judged that there was not yet enough evidence that a full use disorder is clinically significant and common, so caffeine use disorder is listed in Section III as a condition for further study. Its proposed criteria deliberately set a higher bar than the other use disorders: the first three criteria (persistent desire or unsuccessful efforts to cut down, continued use despite knowing it is causing a physical or psychological problem, and withdrawal) must all be present, so that only cases of real clinical importance are labelled. ICD-11 similarly gives caffeine codes for harmful use, intoxication and withdrawal but no caffeine dependence. Caffeine can also induce an anxiety disorder or a sleep disorder (Chapter 6 and Chapter 8, Section 8.10). Table 10.27 brings together the diagnoses available for the drug classes of Sections 10.5 and 10.6; the full ten-class comparison is at the end of Section 10.7.

Table 10.27 The diagnoses available for opioids, stimulants, tobacco and caffeine, with MDMA, which DSM-5-TR counts as a hallucinogen.
ClassICD-10ICD-11DSM-5-TR use disorderDSM-5-TR intoxication and withdrawal

Exam trap

Caffeine is an adenosine receptor antagonist, not an adenosine agonist and not a dopamine reuptake blocker. DSM-5-TR has caffeine intoxication (more than 250 milligrams; five of twelve signs) and caffeine withdrawal (three of five signs within 24 hours; headache is the hallmark), but caffeine use disorder is only a Section III condition for further study. Contrast tobacco, which has a use disorder and withdrawal but no intoxication diagnosis. In ICD-10, caffeine is coded with the other stimulants under F15.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. The triad of opioid overdose is coma, pinpoint pupils and .
  3. Withdrawal from heroin usually begins within about hours of the last dose and peaks within 1 to 3 days.
  4. Buprenorphine is a agonist at the mu receptor, so its effect on breathing has a ceiling.
  5. Methadone maintenance was introduced by Vincent Dole and Marie in 1965.
  6. Cocaine blocks the reuptake of dopamine, whereas amphetamine increases its by reversing the transporter.
  7. The tactile hallucination of insects crawling on or under the skin is called .

  9. Varenicline is a partial agonist at the alpha4 beta2 receptor.
