Answers: (1) semi-synthetic, (2) respiratory depression, (3) 6 to 12, (4) partial, (5) Nyswander, (6) release, (7) formication, (8) Higgins, (9) nicotinic acetylcholine, (10) adenosine.

10.7 Cannabis, Hallucinogens, Inhalants and the Sedative-HypnoticsLower yield · not asked yet

The drugs you have met so far in this chapter each push the brain in one clear direction. Alcohol and the opioids slow it down; cocaine, the amphetamines, nicotine and caffeine speed it up. The four groups in this section refuse to line up so neatly. Cannabis is the most widely used illegal drug in the world, yet in much of India it is also a legal festival drink, sold in licensed shops and stirred into milk at Holi. The hallucinogens change what a person sees, hears and feels while leaving him wide awake and alert, which is exactly what delirium does not do. The inhalants are not drugs at all in the ordinary sense: they are glue, petrol, paint thinner and the correction fluid in a schoolchild's pencil box. And the sedative-hypnotics are medicines, written on prescription pads by doctors for anxiety and sleeplessness, which turn out to be among the most dangerous drugs a person can stop taking.

Two questions will run through the whole section. The first is what each drug does in the brain, and what intoxication with it actually looks like when the person is sitting in front of you. The second is what happens when a regular user stops, and whether that withdrawal can harm him. The answers to the second question cover the whole range, from "no recognised withdrawal at all" (the hallucinogens and the inhalants) to "withdrawal can kill" (the sedatives, like alcohol). At the end of the section, Table 10.31 sets all ten of the DSM-5-TR substance classes side by side, so that you can compare intoxication and withdrawal across the entire chapter at a glance.

Cannabis: one plant, many preparations

Cannabis is the hemp plant, Cannabis sativa (Pinel also lists Cannabis indica and Cannabis ruderalis as species). For thousands of years people have used it three ways at once: the fibres of its stem for rope and cloth, its seeds as food, and its leaves and flowers for their effects on the mind. Most of the psychoactive chemicals sit in a sticky resin that coats the flowering tops of the female plant, less in its leaves and almost none in its stalks and seeds. That single botanical fact explains the difference between the three preparations every Indian student has heard of, because each is made from a different part of the plant and so carries a different dose.

Bhang is made from the dried leaves (and small stems and seeds) of the plant, ground with water into a green paste and then drunk in milk, in the spiced drink thandai or in lassi, or rolled into small balls and eaten. It is the mildest form and the one with the deepest roots in Indian religious life: it is associated with the god Shiva and drunk openly at Holi and Mahashivratri. Ganja is the dried flowering tops of the unpollinated female plant, the part richest in resin; it is smoked, often in a clay pipe (chillum) or rolled into a cigarette, and it is what Western writers call marijuana. Charas is the resin itself, rubbed off the flowering tops by hand and pressed into dark sticks or balls; it is the Indian equivalent of hashish, and it is the strongest of the three. Hashish oil, a solvent extract of the resin, is stronger still. Table 10.28 puts them in order.

Table 10.28 The cannabis preparations, in rising order of potency. Percentages are the typical THC content that DSM-5-TR gives for plant material, hashish and hash oil; street samples vary widely.
PreparationPart of the plantHow it is usually takenTypical THC content
BhangDried leaves, small stems and seedsDrunk in milk, thandai or lassi; eaten as ballsLowest
Ganja (marijuana)Dried flowering tops of the female plantSmoked in a chillum or a "joint"About 10 to 15 percent (plant material)
Charas (hashish)Resin rubbed from the flowering topsSmoked, often mixed with tobaccoAbout 30 to 40 percent
Hashish oilSolvent extract of the resinSmoked, vaped or "dabbed"About 50 to 55 percent

Two modern developments have changed the picture. The first is potency. DSM-5-TR notes that the THC content of seized illegal cannabis has risen steadily over the past twenty years, and that legal products in some American states now reach about 20 percent in plant material and about 68 percent in concentrated extracts. A joint today is not the joint of the 1970s. The second is route. Smoked cannabis acts within minutes, so a user can stop when he feels the effect; cannabis eaten in food ("edibles") or drunk as bhang takes an hour or more to begin and lasts much longer, which is why first-time bhang drinkers at a Holi party so often take a second glass "because nothing is happening" and spend the evening terrified. Newer methods include vaping (heating the plant without burning it) and dabbing (heating a concentrated butane extract and inhaling it), both popular with young users.

Key study: the Indian Hemp Drugs Commission (1893 to 1894)

In 1893 members of the British House of Commons, worried by reports that ganja smoking was filling the lunatic asylums of Bengal, pressed the Government of India to act. The government appointed a seven-member commission, with W. Mackworth Young as its president, which began work on 3 July 1893 and was soon asked to cover the whole of India rather than Bengal alone. It travelled across the country and took evidence from almost 1,200 witnesses: doctors, officials, soldiers, cultivators, yogis and fakirs among them. It also visited the asylums to examine the cases blamed on hemp drugs.

The report, completed in 1894, ran to more than 3,000 pages. Its central conclusion was that the moderate use of hemp drugs was "practically attended by no evil results at all", with no appreciable physical, mental or moral injury, whereas excessive use was harmful: it tended to weaken the constitution and "indicates and intensifies mental instability". The asylum visits helped to undermine the belief, common at the time, that ganja simply caused insanity. The commission remains one of the largest and earliest official inquiries into any drug, and its distinction between moderate and excessive use anticipates the modern idea that the harm of a drug depends on the pattern of use, not just on the drug.

India's law still reflects this history. The Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances (NDPS) Act of 1985 makes ganja and charas illegal, but its definition of cannabis leaves out the leaves and seeds when they are not accompanied by the flowering tops, so bhang falls outside it and is regulated by state excise laws instead (Section 10.1 explains the Act). The numbers are large. In the national survey Magnitude of Substance Use in India (2019), about 2.8 percent of Indians aged 10 to 75, roughly 3.1 crore people, had used a cannabis product in the previous year: about 2 percent bhang and about 1.2 percent ganja or charas.

Exam trap

Learn the three Indian preparations by the part of the plant: bhang = leaves (weakest, drunk), ganja = flowering tops (smoked; the same thing as marijuana), charas = resin (the same thing as hashish, strongest of the three). A favourite distractor swaps ganja and charas. A second trap is the law: bhang is outside the NDPS Act's definition of cannabis, ganja and charas are inside it.

THC, the endocannabinoids and how cannabis acts

For most of the twentieth century nobody knew which of the plant's many chemicals produced the "high". The answer came from the Israeli chemist Raphael Mechoulam and his colleague Yehiel Gaoni at the Weizmann Institute. Working with hashish seized by the Israeli police, they isolated the main active compound, worked out its structure and partly synthesised it, and published the result in the Journal of the American Chemical Society in 1964. The compound is delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC). (Their paper called it delta-1-THC, under an older numbering system for the same molecule; you may see both names.) The plant contains more than 80 related chemicals, the cannabinoids, but THC is the one that produces intoxication and dependence.

Knowing the molecule raised a deeper question. Drugs act by fitting receptors, and receptors exist because the brain makes its own chemical to fit them. Why would the human brain carry a receptor for a chemical from a plant? The receptor was found first. In 1988 a cannabinoid receptor was identified in the rat brain, and in 1990 it was cloned; it is now called the CB1 receptor. A second receptor, CB2, found mainly on cells of the immune system, was cloned in 1993. Pinel points out that CB1 turned out to be one of the most common metabotropic receptors in the whole brain. Then, in 1992, Mechoulam's laboratory in Jerusalem, with William Devane and Lumír Hanuš, isolated from pig brain the brain's own chemical that binds the receptor. They named it anandamide, from the Sanskrit word ananda, bliss. A second such chemical, 2-arachidonoylglycerol (2-AG), was described in 1995. Together these are the endocannabinoids, and the receptors and enzymes that make and break them form the endocannabinoid system.

The endocannabinoids work in an unusual direction. Most neurotransmitters travel forward, from the neuron that releases them to the one that receives them. Endocannabinoids are made on demand by the receiving neuron and travel backwards across the synapse to CB1 receptors on the sending terminal, where they turn down the release of other transmitters, both excitatory glutamate and inhibitory GABA. They are a brake that the receiving cell applies to its own input. THC hijacks that brake everywhere at once. The map of CB1 receptors, made around 1990, reads like a list of cannabis's effects: they are dense in the hippocampus (hence the impaired short-term memory), the basal ganglia and cerebellum (hence clumsiness and slowed reactions), the cortex (hence distorted thinking and time sense) and the hypothalamus (hence the hunger), and they are sparse in the brainstem centres that control breathing, which is one reason a fatal overdose of cannabis alone is virtually unknown. Like other drugs of abuse, THC also increases dopamine release in the nucleus accumbens, indirectly, by quietening the GABA neurons that normally restrain the dopamine cells of the ventral tegmental area (Section 10.2 explains the reward circuit).

One other cannabinoid deserves a line. Cannabidiol (CBD) is the second most abundant cannabinoid in many plants. It does not produce a high, it does not act like THC at CB1, and it may blunt some of THC's anxiety-provoking and psychosis-like effects. Cannabis bred for high THC usually contains little CBD, and that imbalance is one suggested reason why modern high-potency cannabis seems more likely to cause psychotic symptoms.

Intoxication, use disorder and withdrawal

Pinel quotes a 1972 American government commission's description of a usual "social" dose: initial restlessness and hilarity, then a dreamy, carefree relaxation, a sense that space and time have expanded, sharper touch, taste, smell and hearing, hunger with a craving for sweets, and subtle changes in the way thoughts form. To an onlooker, the commission added, the person might look no different at all. Higher doses tell a different story: short-term memory fails, the user loses the thread of a sentence, multistep tasks fall apart, and a sense of unreality, sensory distortion and paranoia can set in. The first-time user, or the user who has eaten far more than he meant to, may have a frank panic attack, convinced that his heart is about to stop or that he is going mad.

DSM-5-TR's criteria for cannabis intoxication pull these observations into a rule. There must be recent use; problematic behavioural or psychological change during or shortly after it (impaired coordination, euphoria, anxiety, a sensation of slowed time, impaired judgement, social withdrawal); and at least two of four physical signs within two hours: conjunctival injection (red eyes), increased appetite, dry mouth and a fast heart rate. Notice what is not on the list. The pupils are not markedly changed, unlike opioid intoxication (pinpoint) or stimulant intoxication (dilated). Appetite goes up, whereas alcohol and sedatives usually reduce it, and DSM-5-TR uses exactly this difference, together with the nystagmus, ataxia and aggression that alcohol produces and cannabis usually does not, to tell the two intoxications apart. The specifier "with perceptual disturbances" is used when hallucinations occur with intact reality testing (the person knows the drug is responsible); if insight is lost, the diagnosis becomes a cannabis-induced psychotic disorder. Smoked cannabis acts within minutes and its effects last three to four hours; eaten cannabis takes a few hours to act and lasts longer. Because THC dissolves in fat, it is released slowly from body fat, so effects can linger or return for 12 to 24 hours and urine tests for its metabolite can stay positive for weeks in a heavy user.

Can a drug with such gentle effects cause dependence? For decades many people, users and some doctors among them, said no. The evidence now says yes, though less often than with most other drugs. Pinel, citing a 2016 review, gives the proportion of users who become addicted as about 9 percent, against about 21 percent for cocaine, 23 percent for alcohol and 68 percent for tobacco; these are estimates of a user's lifetime chance of ever becoming dependent, a different measure from the proportions already dependent at interview in the National Comorbidity Survey (Table 10.11), which is why tobacco's figure there is lower, about a third, while cannabis stays at about 9 percent in both (Section 10.2 explains these "capture rates" and why tobacco tops the list on either measure). DSM-5-TR gives a higher figure, that 20 to 30 percent of cannabis users have symptoms consistent with a use disorder, mainly because it counts recent users rather than everyone who has ever tried the drug. Cannabis use disorder uses the same eleven criteria as every other substance use disorder (Section 10.1). Early onset, before about age 15, is one of the strongest predictors of later cannabis use disorder and of other substance and mental disorders in young adulthood. Heritable factors account for between 30 and 80 percent of the variance in risk, depending on the study.

The decisive change came in 2013, when DSM-5 added cannabis withdrawal, which DSM-IV had not recognised. It follows the stopping of heavy and prolonged use, usually daily or almost daily for at least a few months, and requires three or more of seven features within about a week: irritability, anger or aggression; nervousness or anxiety; sleep difficulty, including insomnia and disturbing dreams; decreased appetite or weight loss; restlessness; depressed mood; and at least one physical symptom causing real discomfort (abdominal pain, shakiness, sweating, fever, chills or headache). Onset is usually 24 to 48 hours after the last use, the symptoms peak within two to five days and they settle within one to two weeks, although poor sleep can persist longer. Unlike withdrawal from alcohol, sedatives or opioids, the emotional and behavioural symptoms are more prominent than the physical ones, and the syndrome is not dangerous. It matters clinically because it keeps people using: sleep difficulty is the withdrawal symptom most often linked to relapse.

Push a case through these criteria. Chandan, 23, a final-year commerce student in Varanasi, began smoking ganja with friends at 16. By 22 he was smoking several chillums a day, starting soon after waking; evenings that began with "just one" often ended at dawn, and most of his day went in buying, smoking and sleeping off ganja. He had missed two semester examinations, given up the cricket he once played every evening and stopped visiting his friends from college, and his parents had found his stash twice; each time there was a bitter quarrel at home, and each time he promised to stop, and did not. He told the psychologist that he needed "almost double" what he used to smoke to feel anything. Before his final examinations he stopped completely. Two days later he could not sleep, his dreams were vivid and unpleasant, he had no appetite, he sweated at night and he shouted at his younger sister over nothing. On the fifth day he smoked again, "only to get some sleep". Count the criteria: larger amounts than intended, unsuccessful efforts to cut down, much time spent, failure in his role as a student, important activities given up, continued use despite family conflict, tolerance and withdrawal. That is eight of eleven, a severe cannabis use disorder, with cannabis withdrawal.

Two longer-term consequences complete the clinical picture. The first is the amotivational syndrome, a term introduced by William McGlothlin and Louis Jolyon West in 1968 for the apathy, passivity, loss of ambition and poor school or work performance seen in some heavy, long-term users. DSM-5-TR still uses the label, describing reduced goal-directed activity and lowered self-efficacy, and notes that chronic use can produce a lack of motivation that resembles persistent depressive disorder. Whether it is a separate syndrome is doubtful: the same picture could be continuous intoxication, an underlying depression, or a personality that was unmotivated before the drug arrived. The second is the cannabinoid hyperemesis syndrome, bouts of nausea and repeated vomiting in regular heavy users, which DSM-5-TR says is increasingly seen in emergency departments; many sufferers discover that long hot showers relieve it, and that odd habit is a clue to the diagnosis. Finally, the synthetic cannabinoids sold as "Spice" or "K2" (plant material sprayed with laboratory chemicals that act on CB1) are classed with cannabis in DSM-5-TR, but their potency is unpredictable and they can cause seizures, cardiac problems, psychosis and death.

Exam trap

Cannabis intoxication gives red eyes, hunger, dry mouth and a fast pulse, with no marked change in the pupils. Cannabis withdrawal exists in DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR (it was added in 2013), so an option saying that cannabis has no withdrawal syndrome is wrong; the classes with no withdrawal diagnosis are the hallucinogens (including phencyclidine) and the inhalants. THC was isolated by Mechoulam and Gaoni (1964); anandamide, the first endocannabinoid, was named from the Sanskrit for bliss (1992). The "amotivational syndrome" is the classic chronic cannabis picture, and its existence as a separate syndrome is disputed.

Cannabis, psychosis, the adolescent brain and medical cannabis

The most serious question about cannabis is whether it can cause a lasting psychotic illness. Three different things need to be kept apart. First, intoxication itself can bring paranoia and perceptual changes that fade as the drug wears off. Second, a heavy dose, especially of a potent preparation, can produce a cannabis-induced psychotic disorder, with delusions or hallucinations that outlast ordinary intoxication and need treatment in their own right. In India this was described long ago: in 1974 the psychiatrists Gurbakhsh Singh Chopra and J. W. Smith reported 200 in-patients who had developed serious psychiatric reactions after taking cannabis. Third, and most important, cannabis may raise the risk of schizophrenia itself in people who would not otherwise have developed it.

The evidence for that third claim began with a Swedish cohort. In 1987 Sven Andréasson and colleagues followed 45,570 young men conscripted into the Swedish army and found that those who had used cannabis more than fifty times by the time of conscription, at 18 to 20, were six times more likely than non-users to be diagnosed with schizophrenia over the next fifteen years. When the same cohort was re-analysed in 2002 by Stanley Zammit and colleagues, with 27 years of follow-up and better control for other drugs and social factors, the excess risk for the heaviest users shrank to about threefold but did not disappear. More recently Marta Di Forti and colleagues compared 901 people with a first episode of psychosis with 1,237 controls across 11 sites in Europe and Brazil (published in 2019). Daily use of high-potency cannabis carried nearly five times the odds of psychosis, and they estimated, assuming the link is causal, that about 30 percent of new cases in London and about 50 percent in Amsterdam, where potent cannabis is easy to buy, could have been prevented if high-potency cannabis had not been available. DSM-5-TR summarises the field by saying that cannabis use in critical periods is consistently associated with a threefold increase in the risk of psychosis. Chapter 4, Section 4.7 places these findings within the wider causes of schizophrenia.

Association, however, is not causation, and Pinel urges caution. People destined to develop schizophrenia might use cannabis to soothe early symptoms (self-medication), or a third factor, such as childhood abuse, which DSM-5-TR mentions, or shared genes, might raise the risk of both. The dose-response pattern, the sequence (use usually comes first) and the potency effect all favour a causal contribution, but the honest conclusion is that cannabis is one contributory cause among many, which matters most for young people with a family history of psychosis. The same caution applies to the adolescent brain. A study of the Dunedin birth cohort in New Zealand, published in 2012, found that people who used cannabis persistently from adolescence lost about eight IQ points between childhood and midlife; a critic, Ole Rogeberg, argued in 2013 that differences in social background could explain the result, the authors replied that they could not, and other cohorts have found that family background accounts for much of the gap. Pinel concludes that there is still no convincing direct evidence that cannabis causes brain damage, that the memory problems of heavy users largely fade within about 72 hours of stopping, and that hippocampal differences in users might have been present before use. What is not in dispute is that early, heavy use predicts worse outcomes, whatever the mechanism.

Finally, the plant is also a source of medicines. Synthetic THC has been used for decades to reduce nausea and vomiting during cancer chemotherapy and to stimulate appetite in people with AIDS; a mouth spray containing THC and CBD (Sativex) was introduced in several countries in 2010 for the muscle spasticity of multiple sclerosis; and a purified CBD medicine (Epidiolex) was approved in the United States in 2018 for two severe childhood epilepsies. DSM-5-TR adds that when cannabis or a cannabinoid is taken as medically indicated, tolerance and withdrawal may occur but should not be the main basis for diagnosing a use disorder, exactly the rule that applies to prescribed opioids and benzodiazepines.

Clinical link

A young person who presents with a first psychotic episode should always be asked, specifically and without judgement, about cannabis: how often, what form (bhang, ganja, charas or something stronger), and since what age. The answer shapes the diagnosis (intoxication, a cannabis-induced psychotic disorder or a primary psychotic illness), the advice (continued use after a first episode predicts relapse) and the family work. Urine tests stay positive for weeks in heavy users, so a positive test proves use, not intoxication at the time of the symptoms.

Exam trap

The Swedish conscript study (Andréasson and colleagues, 1987) found about a sixfold risk of schizophrenia in men who had used cannabis more than fifty times; better-controlled analyses and later studies put the risk at roughly threefold, highest with daily use of high-potency cannabis. These are associations: the papers may call cannabis "a risk factor" or "a contributory cause", never "the cause" of schizophrenia. A cannabis-induced psychotic disorder (symptoms tied to use, beyond ordinary intoxication) is a different diagnosis from schizophrenia.

The hallucinogens: LSD, psilocybin and mescaline

A hallucinogen is a drug whose main effect is to change perception, mood and thought while the user remains awake, alert and oriented. The name is a little misleading, because true hallucinations (perceptions with no outside object that the person takes to be real) are uncommon; what most users experience are intensified and distorted perceptions that they know are caused by the drug. Other names reflect other views of the same experience. "Psychotomimetic" (mimicking psychosis) was the 1950s research term, used when these drugs were studied as models of schizophrenia; "psychedelic" (mind-manifesting), coined by the British psychiatrist Humphry Osmond in the mid-1950s, reflects the hope that they could reveal the mind's hidden contents. DSM-5-TR groups the classic hallucinogens by chemistry: the phenylalkylamines (mescaline, and also MDMA, which DSM-5-TR places here), the indoleamines (psilocybin and its active product psilocin, from "magic mushrooms", and dimethyltryptamine or DMT), the ergolines (LSD and morning glory seeds) and a few plants such as Salvia divinorum and jimsonweed (a species of datura). Cannabis is deliberately excluded and has its own class, because its effects differ too much.

Key study: Albert Hofmann and "Bicycle Day" (1938 to 1943)

Albert Hofmann (1906 to 2008) was a chemist at the Sandoz pharmaceutical laboratories in Basel, Switzerland, working on compounds derived from ergot, a fungus that grows on rye. On 16 November 1938 he synthesised the twenty-fifth compound in a series of lysergic acid derivatives, lysergic acid diethylamide, LSD-25. Tests on animals showed nothing of interest, and it was shelved. Five years later, on 16 April 1943, he made it again and, while working, was overcome by restlessness, dizziness and a dreamlike state in which he saw an uninterrupted play of vivid images and colours; he suspected that a trace of the substance had been absorbed through his skin.

Three days later, on 19 April 1943, he tested the idea on himself, deliberately swallowing 250 micrograms, a dose he believed tiny but which proved to be several times a strong dose. As the effects mounted he asked his assistant to take him home by bicycle (wartime restrictions limited the use of cars), and on the ride the world around him became distorted and threatening. He feared he was going mad or dying; by the next day he had recovered completely, with a sense of renewal. Users later named the anniversary "Bicycle Day". The conclusion was startling: a substance active in millionths of a gram could transform perception, emotion and the sense of self. Sandoz supplied LSD to psychiatrists for research from 1947, and its potency helped convince a generation of researchers that mental states have a chemical basis. The criticism is equally clear: a single self-experiment, however vivid, cannot separate drug from expectation, a point the idea of "set and setting" would later make.

The other classic hallucinogens came from plants long used in religious ritual. Psilocybin, from mushrooms eaten in Mexican ceremonies, was isolated by Hofmann himself in 1958 from Psilocybe mexicana. Mescaline, from the peyote cactus of Mexico and the south-western United States (Lophophora williamsii), was isolated by the German chemist Arthur Heffter in 1897 and first synthesised in 1919; the novelist Aldous Huxley's account of taking it, The Doors of Perception (1954), introduced it to a wide public. Despite their different chemistry, these drugs act in the same way: they are agonists at the 5-HT2A receptor, a type of serotonin receptor dense in the cortex. The key evidence is that a drug blocking 5-HT2A receptors, ketanserin, abolished the psychological effects of psilocybin in volunteers in a 1998 study by Franz Vollenweider and colleagues. Their shared receptor also explains a neat example of cross-tolerance: tolerance to LSD develops within a few days of daily use, and a person tolerant to LSD is also tolerant to psilocybin and mescaline, but not to cannabis or amphetamine, which act on other systems.

What is a "trip" like? With LSD the effects begin within about an hour and last eight to twelve hours; psilocybin's are shorter, DMT's (when smoked) only minutes. Colours become intense, surfaces seem to breathe or ripple, geometric patterns appear with the eyes closed, and objects leave trails as they move. Senses can cross, so that a sound is "seen" as colour; this is synaesthesia. Time slows or stops; the boundary between self and world can seem to dissolve (depersonalisation and derealisation); emotions swing from euphoria to fear, and many users describe experiences they regard as mystical. DSM-5-TR captures this in its criteria for hallucinogen intoxication (formally, other hallucinogen intoxication): recent use; problematic psychological change such as marked anxiety or depression, ideas of reference, fear of "losing one's mind", paranoid ideas or impaired judgement; perceptual changes occurring in a state of full wakefulness and alertness, such as intensified perception, depersonalisation, derealisation, illusions, hallucinations or synaesthesias; and at least two of seven physical signs: pupillary dilation, tachycardia, sweating, palpitations, blurring of vision, tremors and incoordination. The phrase about full wakefulness is the clinical key. A person in hallucinogen intoxication is not clouded, not disoriented and usually knows that the drug is responsible; a person whose perceptions are disturbed because consciousness itself is clouded has a delirium (Section 10.8).

Whether a trip is pleasant or terrifying depends heavily on what the user brings to it and where he takes it. Timothy Leary (1920 to 1996), the Harvard psychologist whose psilocybin research ended with his departure from Harvard in 1963 and who became the best-known evangelist of LSD, popularised the phrase set and setting in 1961: "set" is the user's mindset (his expectations, mood and personality) and "setting" the physical and social surroundings. A frightened user in a chaotic setting is at risk of a bad trip, an acute panic with paranoid fears and the conviction that he will never come back to normal, during which accidents and dangerous behaviour can occur. The usual treatment is "talking down": a quiet room, a calm and reassuring companion who reminds him that the drug will wear off, and, if needed, a benzodiazepine.

Two facts about the aftermath are examined often. First, DSM-5-TR includes no hallucinogen withdrawal syndrome, because a clinically significant one has not been consistently documented in humans; hallucinogen use disorder therefore has only ten applicable criteria, the withdrawal criterion being dropped. Hallucinogen use disorder is rare (about 0.1 percent of Americans aged 12 and over in a year), and the classic hallucinogens produce little compulsive use. Second, some users later re-experience the perceptual effects when sober. DSM-5-TR calls this hallucinogen persisting perception disorder (HPPD), the formal name for flashbacks: after the drug has gone, the person again sees geometric patterns, false movement at the edges of vision, flashes or intensification of colour, trails behind moving objects, after-images, halos, or objects that look too large (macropsia) or too small (micropsia). Reality testing is intact; he knows these are drug effects. The disturbances may be brief and episodic or nearly continuous, may last weeks, months or years, can be triggered by cannabis, alcohol, darkness or exercise, and occur mainly after LSD; DSM-5-TR gives an initial estimate of about 4.2 percent of hallucinogen users. Persisting psychosis after hallucinogens is uncommon, and a large American survey cited in DSM-5-TR found no evidence that lifetime psychedelic use was an independent risk factor for mental health problems or suicidal behaviour.

That reassuring finding has fed a revival of research. After decades in which these drugs were banned and research stopped, controlled trials have tested psilocybin for treatment-resistant depression and MDMA-assisted psychotherapy for post-traumatic stress disorder. Australia, from 1 July 2023, allowed specially authorised psychiatrists to prescribe psilocybin for treatment-resistant depression and MDMA for post-traumatic stress disorder. In the United States, the Food and Drug Administration declined to approve MDMA-assisted therapy in August 2024, asking for further research; the developer was reported in August 2026 to have resubmitted its application without a new trial, and no decision has been announced. The developer of a synthetic psilocybin, COMP360, completed its application for treatment-resistant depression on 9 November 2026, and if it is approved it would be the first classic psychedelic medicine licensed there. Until then, these remain experimental treatments, given only with careful screening, preparation and supervision. Table 10.29 sums up the hallucinogens, together with the dissociative drugs described next.

Table 10.29 The hallucinogens and the dissociative drugs compared.
DrugSource and historyMain actionWhat to remember
LSDSynthesised by Hofmann (Sandoz) 1938; effects found 19435-HT2A agonistActive in micrograms; lasts 8 to 12 hours; main cause of HPPD
Psilocybin"Magic mushrooms"; isolated by Hofmann 19585-HT2A agonist (as psilocin)Shorter trip; trials in treatment-resistant depression
MescalinePeyote cactus; isolated by Heffter 18975-HT2A agonistHuxley's The Doors of Perception (1954)
MDMA (ecstasy)Amphetamine derivativeReleases mainly serotoninA hallucinogen in DSM-5-TR; taught with stimulants in Section 10.6
Phencyclidine (PCP)Anaesthetic of the 1950s; street drug from the 1960sNMDA receptor antagonistNystagmus, violence, analgesia, rigidity

Exam trap

LSD was synthesised in 1938 and its effects discovered in 1943, both by Albert Hofmann; examiners use either year, so read the stem ("synthesised" or "discovered"). The classic hallucinogens act on 5-HT2A (serotonin) receptors, not on dopamine or GABA. Their intoxication occurs in clear consciousness, with dilated pupils. There is no hallucinogen withdrawal in DSM-5-TR. "Flashbacks" are hallucinogen persisting perception disorder, with insight preserved. Cannabis is not a hallucinogen in DSM-5-TR, whereas MDMA is.

Phencyclidine and ketamine: the dissociative drugs

DSM-5-TR gives phencyclidine (PCP, "angel dust") and its relatives a separate category within the hallucinogen class, because they act quite differently. PCP was first made in 1926 and was developed in the 1950s as a surgical anaesthetic; it was abandoned for human use in the mid-1960s because so many patients woke from it agitated, deluded and irrational, and it appeared on the streets in the 1960s. Its gentler, shorter-acting relative ketamine, made in 1962, is still a widely used anaesthetic in human and veterinary medicine. Both are called dissociative anaesthetics: at low doses they produce a feeling of being separated from one's body and surroundings, and at high doses stupor and coma. Both block the NMDA receptor, the main receptor for the excitatory transmitter glutamate, by plugging its ion channel. Because PCP intoxication can reproduce both the positive and the negative symptoms of schizophrenia in healthy people, it is the basis of the glutamate hypothesis of schizophrenia (Chapter 4, Section 4.8).

Phencyclidine intoxication is unlike the dreamy LSD trip. DSM-5-TR requires problematic behavioural change, such as belligerence, assaultiveness, impulsiveness, unpredictability, agitation and impaired judgement, and, within one hour, two or more of eight signs: vertical or horizontal nystagmus (jerking movements of the eyes), raised blood pressure or heart rate, numbness or reduced response to pain, ataxia, slurred speech (dysarthria), muscle rigidity, seizures or coma, and hyperacusis (sounds painfully loud). Picture a young man brought to an emergency department by police, sweating, rigid, staring with eyes that flick from side to side, convinced that the staff are attacking him, and apparently feeling nothing from the cuts on his hands. The combination of violence, nystagmus and insensitivity to pain is the signature that separates PCP from other hallucinogens and from stimulants. Because PCP users can switch suddenly to violence, "talking down" is less useful than it is for LSD; the person is nursed in a quiet, low-stimulation room and given a benzodiazepine, and doctors watch for dangerously high temperature, muscle breakdown (rhabdomyolysis) and seizures. Its psychoactive effects last a few hours but it takes eight days or longer to leave the body, and in vulnerable people it can precipitate a psychotic episode resembling schizophrenia that lasts for weeks. As with the other hallucinogens, DSM-5-TR includes no PCP withdrawal, because withdrawal symptoms have not been clearly established in human users.

Ketamine has a double life. As a street drug ("Special K") it is snorted at parties for its dissociation, and a large dose produces the frightening detached state users call the "K-hole"; heavy, long-term use can badly damage the bladder. As a medicine it is the most important new idea in the drug treatment of depression in decades: a single low dose can lift severe depression within hours, and DSM-5-TR notes its usefulness in major depressive disorder. A nasal spray of esketamine (one of its two mirror-image forms) was approved in the United States in 2019 for treatment-resistant depression, given under supervision because of its dissociative effects and its potential for misuse (Chapter 5, Section 5.7 discusses its place among the treatments for depression).

Inhalants: the drugs in the household cupboard

Walk through any large Indian railway station late at night and you may see children, some no older than ten, holding a handkerchief or a small plastic bag to their faces. Inside is a cloth soaked in correction fluid ("whitener"), the rubber solution used to mend cycle tubes and shoes, or petrol. These are inhalants: volatile chemicals whose vapour is breathed in for its intoxicating effect. DSM-5-TR's inhalant class covers the volatile hydrocarbons, such as toluene, found in glues, fuels, paints, paint thinners and similar products; gases that are not hydrocarbons, such as nitrous oxide ("laughing gas") and the amyl and butyl nitrites ("poppers"), are classed as "other substances" instead. Users sniff vapour straight from the container, "huff" it from a soaked rag held over the mouth and nose, or "bag" it from a plastic bag held over the face.

Inhalants are cheap, legal, easy to hide and everywhere, which is why they are the drug of the very young and the very poor. In the Indian national survey of 2019 they were the only class of substance whose current use was higher among children and adolescents (1.17 percent) than among adults (0.58 percent), and DSM-5-TR notes that in low- and middle-income countries homeless children living on the streets have extensive inhalant problems, because of poverty, easy availability and the need to blunt hunger, cold and fear. Several Indian cities, Delhi among them, have restricted the sale of whiteners and thinners to minors. In America, calls to poison-control centres about deliberate inhalant misuse peak at age 14, and the disorder usually remits in early adulthood.

Pharmacologically, most solvents behave like a very fast-acting alcohol: like alcohol they enhance GABA-A receptor function and block NMDA receptors. Because the vapour goes from lungs to brain in seconds and wears off within minutes to an hour, users take repeated breaths over hours to stay intoxicated. DSM-5-TR's criteria for inhalant intoxication require problematic behavioural change (belligerence, assaultiveness, apathy, impaired judgement) and two or more of thirteen signs that read like severe drunkenness: dizziness, nystagmus, incoordination, slurred speech, unsteady gait, lethargy, depressed reflexes, psychomotor retardation, tremor, generalised muscle weakness, blurred or double vision, stupor or coma, and euphoria. Users also describe hallucinations, headache and nausea. Clues that a youngster is using include the smell of solvent on breath or clothes, paint or glue on the face or hands, a rash around the nose and mouth ("glue-sniffer's rash"), and repeated episodes of apparent intoxication with negative results on standard urine drug screens, which do not detect inhalants. As with the hallucinogens, DSM-5-TR includes no inhalant withdrawal diagnosis, although some heavy users do report withdrawal-like symptoms.

The real danger of inhalants is their toxicity. Acutely, any volatile hydrocarbon can cause sudden sniffing death: the solvent makes the heart muscle oversensitive to adrenaline, so that a fright or a burst of exertion (being chased by a policeman or a shopkeeper) triggers a fatal arrhythmia, even on a first occasion. People also die from suffocation inside a plastic bag, from inhaling vomit, from burns when fuel vapour ignites, and from accidents while intoxicated. Chronically, solvents dissolve the fatty myelin of the nervous system: long-term toluene users develop white-matter damage (leukoencephalopathy), cerebellar ataxia, brain atrophy and a dementia-like cognitive decline, and some solvents damage peripheral nerves, the liver, the kidneys and the bone marrow. DSM-5-TR adds that very frequent nitrous oxide users can develop spinal cord damage (subacute combined degeneration), nerve damage and psychosis; the gas inactivates vitamin B12, so the picture is that of B12 deficiency. Inhalant use disorder is strongly linked with conduct disorder, antisocial personality disorder, childhood maltreatment and the use of many other drugs.

Indian context

Inhalant use among street children in India is a problem of child welfare as much as of addiction. A child who sniffs whitener on a railway platform is usually also a child who has run away from violence at home, works for a few rupees a day and sleeps in the open. Treatment therefore means shelter, food, schooling and protection under child-welfare law as well as de-addiction care, and outreach workers from voluntary organisations, not hospitals, are often the first contact. The fact that inhalants are the one class used more by children than by adults in the national survey is a fact worth remembering for an interview.

Exam trap

Inhalant intoxication looks like alcohol intoxication (dizziness, nystagmus, slurred speech, unsteady gait, euphoria) but starts and ends within minutes. Standard urine drug screens miss inhalants. There is no inhalant withdrawal diagnosis in DSM-5-TR. Nitrous oxide and nitrites are "other substances", not inhalants, in DSM-5-TR. The acute killer is sudden sniffing death from cardiac arrhythmia.

Sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics: barbiturates, benzodiazepines and the Z-drugs

Three words describe what these drugs are prescribed for. A sedative calms, a hypnotic induces sleep and an anxiolytic relieves anxiety, and the same drug usually does all three, depending on the dose. DSM-5-TR puts them in one class, the sedative, hypnotic or anxiolytic substances, which includes the benzodiazepines, the benzodiazepine-like "Z-drugs" (zolpidem, zaleplon), the barbiturates, and older drugs such as methaqualone (barbiturate-like) and meprobamate (a carbamate). It notes two things about the class: like alcohol, these are brain depressants that produce intoxication and withdrawal very similar to those of alcohol, and they are available both on prescription and illegally. Buspirone, an anxiolytic that acts on serotonin receptors, is excluded because it is not associated with significant misuse.

The barbiturates. Barbituric acid was first synthesised by the German chemist Adolf von Baeyer in 1864. In 1902 and 1903 Emil Fischer and Joseph von Mering made one of its derivatives, barbital, and found that it put dogs to sleep, and from 1904 it was sold as the sleeping pill Veronal; phenobarbital (Luminal) followed in 1912. For half a century the barbiturates were the standard treatment for anxiety and insomnia, and they were also the standard means of accidental and deliberate overdose: Marilyn Monroe's death in 1962 was among the many caused by barbiturates taken with other drugs. Their great defect is a narrow margin between the dose that sedates and the dose that stops breathing, made worse by the fact that tolerance develops to the sedative effect much faster than to the lethal effect. Barbiturates also speed up the liver enzymes that break down many other drugs, and withdrawal from them is the most dangerous of all sedative withdrawals. Today phenobarbital survives mainly as an anti-epileptic drug, which is still widely used for epilepsy in India because it is cheap, and short-acting barbiturates such as thiopental (thiopentone) are used to induce anaesthesia.

The benzodiazepines. In the mid-1950s Leo Sternbach, a chemist at the Swiss company Hoffmann-La Roche, made a compound that sat untested on a shelf until, in 1957, routine animal screening revealed that it tamed animals without knocking them out. It was marketed in 1960 as chlordiazepoxide (Librium), and its simpler relative diazepam (Valium) followed in 1963. The benzodiazepines replaced the barbiturates almost completely and became, during the 1970s, among the most prescribed drugs in the world. They are used for anxiety, insomnia, alcohol withdrawal (Section 10.4), epileptic seizures and status epilepticus, muscle spasm, sedation before procedures and catatonia. Table 10.30 sorts the common ones by how long they act, which matters more than anything else for dependence and withdrawal.

Table 10.30 The sedative-hypnotics compared. Half-lives are approximate; long-acting drugs owe much of their duration to active breakdown products.
GroupExamplesDuration of actionPoints the papers ask
Short-acting benzodiazepinesMidazolam, triazolamHoursWithdrawal can begin within hours of the last dose
Intermediate-acting benzodiazepinesAlprazolam, lorazepam, oxazepam, temazepamAbout 10 hours or a little moreAlprazolam is among the most misused; lorazepam and oxazepam have no active metabolites, so they are preferred in liver disease and old age
Long-acting benzodiazepinesDiazepam, chlordiazepoxide, clonazepamA day or moreSmooth withdrawal; used for alcohol detoxification and for tapering other benzodiazepines
Z-drugsZolpidem, zopiclone, zaleplonShortAct at the benzodiazepine site; tolerance, dependence and complex sleep behaviours occur
BarbituratesPhenobarbital, thiopentalShort to very longNarrow safety margin; induce liver enzymes; most dangerous withdrawal

How they act. All the drugs in this class, and alcohol too, act on the same receptor: the GABA-A receptor, a channel in the neuron's membrane that opens when the inhibitory transmitter GABA binds to it, letting negatively charged chloride ions flow into the cell and making it less likely to fire. The drugs bind to their own sites on the receptor, separate from GABA's site, and make GABA work better; they are positive allosteric modulators, often loosely called GABA agonists. The difference between the families lies in how they do it, and it explains their safety (Figure 10.13). Benzodiazepines make the channel open more often when GABA is present, but they cannot open it by themselves; because their effect depends on the brain's own GABA, it has a ceiling, and an overdose of a benzodiazepine alone is rarely fatal. Barbiturates make the channel stay open longer, and at high doses they open it directly, even without GABA; there is no ceiling, and breathing can stop. Alcohol also enhances GABA-A function, and in addition blocks the NMDA glutamate receptor (Section 10.3).

The Z-drugs and GHB. Zolpidem, zopiclone (and its relative eszopiclone) and zaleplon were introduced as hypnotics that were chemically different from the benzodiazepines and, it was hoped, less habit-forming. In fact they bind the benzodiazepine site of the GABA-A receptor, and tolerance, dependence and withdrawal all occur. They can also cause complex sleep behaviours, such as sleepwalking, cooking or even driving while not fully awake, with no memory afterwards, which led the American regulator to add its strongest warning to their labels in 2019 (Chapter 8, Section 8.10 discusses drug-induced sleep disorders). Gamma-hydroxybutyrate (GHB), a depressant that acts mainly at GABA-B receptors, is used medically for narcolepsy and illicitly as a club drug and in drug-facilitated sexual assault; its steep dose-response curve makes overdose easy and its withdrawal can be as severe as alcohol's.

Intoxication and overdose. Sedative, hypnotic or anxiolytic intoxication is, in DSM-5-TR's words, a picture that may be identical to alcohol intoxication: problematic behavioural change (inappropriate sexual or aggressive behaviour, mood lability, impaired judgement) with one or more of slurred speech, incoordination, unsteady gait, nystagmus, impaired attention or memory, and stupor or coma. Without the smell of alcohol on the breath, only the history or a toxicology screen tells them apart. Memory impairment is prominent, usually an anterograde amnesia like an alcoholic blackout, which is one reason these drugs are used in drug-facilitated crime. In older people even prescribed doses cause confusion, falls and fractures, and DSM-5-TR warns that intoxication in an elderly person can resemble a progressive dementia. Overdose of a benzodiazepine alone is seldom fatal because of the ceiling effect; combined with alcohol or opioids it is a different matter, as Section 10.7.9 explains. Flumazenil, a competitive antagonist at the benzodiazepine site, reverses benzodiazepine sedation within minutes, but it is used cautiously, because its effect is shorter than that of most benzodiazepines (so sedation can return) and because in a dependent person it can precipitate sudden withdrawal and seizures.

Prescribed dependence. The benzodiazepines differ from almost every other drug in this chapter in one way: most dependent users never set out to get high. DSM-5-TR describes two routes into the disorder. The commoner begins with young people who use these drugs socially, often alongside alcohol, opioids or stimulants (to "come down" from cocaine, for example, or to "boost" methadone). The second begins in a doctor's surgery, with a prescription for anxiety or sleeplessness, and continues as the dose creeps up, the patient justifies each increase by the original symptoms, and he begins to collect prescriptions from several doctors. By the 1980s the scale of this problem was clear in Britain, where research by the psychopharmacologist Malcolm Lader and the clinician Heather Ashton documented withdrawal in long-term patients on ordinary doses, and in 1988 the British Committee on Safety of Medicines advised that benzodiazepines be prescribed for anxiety or insomnia only for two to four weeks. DSM-5-TR is careful about such patients: when a benzodiazepine is prescribed for a proper medical reason and taken as prescribed, tolerance and withdrawal do not count towards a use disorder, which is diagnosed only if the other criteria are also met.

Simran, 46, a school administrator in Chandigarh, was prescribed alprazolam 0.25 milligrams at night when she could not sleep after her husband's heart attack. Six years later she was taking 2 milligrams a day in four doses, obtained from two doctors and a chemist who did not ask for a prescription, and she felt anxious and shaky an hour or two before each dose was due. On a family trip to Shimla she ran out. By the next evening she was trembling, sweating and unable to sleep; ordinary sounds seemed painfully loud, the light hurt her eyes, she had a metallic taste in her mouth and she felt strangely unreal, "as if I was watching myself in a film". On the second day she had a generalised seizure in the hotel lobby. Notice the features of benzodiazepine withdrawal that go beyond the anxiety she was first treated for: perceptual hypersensitivity, depersonalisation and, most dangerous, a seizure. Her short-acting drug, taken several times a day, had given her a small withdrawal between every dose, and stopping it abruptly produced a severe one within a day.

Indian context

In the national survey of 2019, about 1.08 percent of Indians aged 10 to 75 (about 1.18 crore people) were current non-medical users of sedatives, that is, taking them without a prescription or not as prescribed, which is easy where chemists sell benzodiazepines over the counter. Barbiturates have a second, legal history in India: thiopental ("sodium pentothal") was used by investigating agencies for "narcoanalysis", in the belief that a drugged suspect would tell the truth. In Selvi v. State of Karnataka (2010) the Supreme Court held that narcoanalysis, polygraph and brain-mapping tests carried out without the person's consent violate the constitutional protection against self-incrimination and the right to personal liberty; statements made under the drug are unreliable as well as coerced.

Exam trap

Benzodiazepines increase the frequency of GABA-A channel opening and need GABA to work (hence a ceiling and a wide safety margin alone); barbiturates increase the duration of opening and at high doses open the channel directly (hence the narrow margin). Both are positive allosteric modulators, loosely "GABA agonists"; neither acts on serotonin, dopamine or opioid receptors. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines, naloxone reverses opioids; neither reverses the other. Buspirone is an anxiolytic but not a sedative-hypnotic of abuse in DSM-5-TR.

Cross-tolerance in practice, and the withdrawal that can kill

Section 10.1 defined cross-tolerance: tolerance to one drug that extends to other drugs, usually of the same class or acting on the same target. The sedatives are where the idea earns its keep. A man who drinks a bottle of whisky a day is tolerant not only to alcohol but also to the benzodiazepines and barbiturates, because all three act on the GABA-A receptor; anaesthetists know that heavy drinkers need more of many anaesthetic and sedative drugs. The hallucinogens give a second example (tolerance to LSD carries over to psilocybin and mescaline, all three acting at 5-HT2A), and the opioids a third (Section 10.5). Cross-tolerance is usually incomplete: the second drug is weakened, not abolished. Its partner, cross-dependence, is the ability of one drug to suppress the withdrawal syndrome of another. These two facts are the whole logic of substitution and tapering: replace a dangerous or short-acting drug with a long-acting drug of the same family, stabilise the patient, then reduce the long-acting drug slowly. That is why alcohol withdrawal is treated with chlordiazepoxide or diazepam (Section 10.4), why a person dependent on alprazolam is switched to diazepam before tapering, why phenobarbital can be used to withdraw a person from barbiturates, and why methadone can replace heroin (Section 10.5).

Cross-tolerance has a deadly side. Tolerance to the sedative and pleasurable effects of these drugs develops much faster than tolerance to their depressant effect on the brainstem, which controls breathing; DSM-5-TR spells this out. A tolerant user who keeps raising his dose to feel the effect is narrowing, without knowing it, the gap between the dose that pleases him and the dose that stops his breathing. Add a second depressant and the effects combine. With alcohol and barbiturates the combination acts on the same receptor; with opioids it acts through a different receptor (the mu opioid receptor) on the same breathing centres, so there is no cross-tolerance to protect the user and the effects simply add. DSM-5-TR notes that benzodiazepines, safe on their own, are particularly dangerous with opioids and alcohol, and in 2016 the American regulator placed its strongest warning on both opioids and benzodiazepines after deaths involving the two together nearly tripled between 2004 and 2011. Many deaths labelled "heroin overdose" turn out, on toxicology, to involve alcohol or benzodiazepines as well.

The withdrawal is the mirror image of the drug. Long exposure to a drug that enhances inhibition makes the brain adapt by reducing its GABA-A sensitivity and increasing its excitatory glutamate activity. Remove the drug suddenly and the brain is left with weak brakes and a strong accelerator. Sedative, hypnotic or anxiolytic withdrawal in DSM-5-TR follows the cessation of, or reduction in, prolonged use, and requires two or more of eight features within several hours to a few days: autonomic hyperactivity (sweating, a pulse over 100), hand tremor, insomnia, nausea or vomiting, transient visual, tactile or auditory hallucinations or illusions, psychomotor agitation, anxiety and grand mal seizures. This is almost word for word the list for alcohol withdrawal, and for the same reason. DSM-5-TR adds that a grand mal seizure may occur in perhaps 20 to 30 percent of people undergoing untreated withdrawal, and that withdrawal can progress to a life-threatening withdrawal delirium, with clouded consciousness and hallucinations, which is then diagnosed instead (Section 10.8). The risk rises with dose and duration: withdrawal has been reported after as little as 15 milligrams of diazepam a day for several months, is more likely to be clinically important at about 40 milligrams, and is more likely to bring seizures or delirium at doses around 100 milligrams. Lingering anxiety, poor sleep and low mood can persist at a lower level for months and are easily mistaken for a returning anxiety disorder.

The timing depends on the drug's half-life (Figure 10.14). After drugs whose action lasts about ten hours or less, such as lorazepam, oxazepam or temazepam, symptoms begin within six to eight hours as blood levels fall, peak on the second day and improve markedly by the fourth or fifth. After a long-acting drug such as diazepam, symptoms may not appear for more than a week, peak in the second week and ease in the third or fourth. A patient who stops diazepam on Monday and feels well on Friday has not escaped withdrawal; it has not started yet.

The practical rules follow directly. A person who has taken a sedative-hypnotic regularly for more than a few weeks should never stop it suddenly. The usual approach is to switch a short-acting drug to an equivalent dose of a long-acting one (usually diazepam), then reduce the dose gradually over weeks or, after years of use, months, more slowly towards the end, while helping the person manage the insomnia and anxiety that return (cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia is taught in Book 2, Section 9.4.3). Seizures and withdrawal delirium are medical emergencies treated with benzodiazepines themselves. Simran was admitted, switched to diazepam and tapered over five months; she later said that the hardest part was not the drug but learning to sleep without it.

Exam trap

Cross-tolerance means that tolerance to one drug extends to another, so exposure to the first reduces the effect of the second; the papers' wording, "exposure to one drug reduces or changes tolerance to other drugs", is a loose way of saying this, and it is the option to choose. It does not mean "no addiction to any drug". The classic group is alcohol, benzodiazepines and barbiturates, which share the GABA-A receptor; it is the basis of benzodiazepine detoxification. Do not confuse it with cross-dependence (one drug suppresses the other's withdrawal) or with sensitisation (reverse tolerance, an increasing effect). Of all the withdrawal syndromes, only those of alcohol and the sedative-hypnotics regularly bring seizures and a delirium that can kill; opioid withdrawal is miserable but rarely dangerous in a healthy adult.

The ten classes side by side

You have now met every class of substance in DSM-5-TR. Before the chapter turns from what drugs do to the brain to what happens when the brain itself fails, it is worth seeing them together, because the examiners' favourite questions are comparisons: which drug gives pinpoint pupils, which gives nystagmus, which has no withdrawal, which withdrawal can kill. Table 10.31 summarises what Sections 10.3 to 10.7 have taught, using DSM-5-TR's criteria. (Hallucinogens are one class with two parts, phencyclidine and the other hallucinogens, which is why the table has eleven rows for ten classes.) Three patterns are worth carrying away. First, withdrawal is usually the mirror image of intoxication: the sedating drugs leave an agitated, sleepless, trembling brain behind; the stimulants leave a slowed, sleepy, hungry, dysphoric one; opioid withdrawal reverses opioid intoxication sign by sign, from small pupils to large ones and from constipation to diarrhoea. Second, only the depressants acting on GABA-A, alcohol and the sedative-hypnotics, have a withdrawal that is regularly dangerous to life. Third, three classes have a gap in their diagnoses: caffeine has no use disorder in the main text (caffeine use disorder is a condition for further study), tobacco has no intoxication diagnosis, and the hallucinogens (including phencyclidine) and the inhalants have no withdrawal diagnosis.

Table 10.31 The ten DSM-5-TR substance classes compared. Intoxication and withdrawal signs follow the DSM-5-TR criteria; the drug sections of this chapter give the details.
ClassExamplesIntoxication: key signsWithdrawal: key signsDangerous withdrawal?
Alcohol (10.3)Arrack, toddy, country liquor, beer, spiritsDisinhibition; slurred speech, incoordination, unsteady gait, nystagmus, poor attention or memory, stuporTremor, sweating, fast pulse, insomnia, nausea, anxiety, agitation, transient hallucinations, seizures; delirium tremensYes: seizures and delirium tremens
