Sedatives, hypnotics, anxiolytics (10.7)Benzodiazepines, Z-drugs, barbituratesAs for alcohol, without the smell: slurred speech, incoordination, nystagmus, memory gaps, stuporAs for alcohol: autonomic overactivity, tremor, insomnia, anxiety, transient hallucinations, grand mal seizures, deliriumYes: seizures and withdrawal delirium
Stimulants (10.6)Amphetamine, methamphetamine, cocaine, cathinonesEuphoria, hypervigilance; dilated pupils, fast (sometimes slow) pulse, raised (sometimes lowered) blood pressure, sweating, agitation; chest pain, seizuresThe "crash": dysphoric mood with fatigue, vivid unpleasant dreams, too much or too little sleep, increased appetiteNot medically, but depression and suicidal risk
Tobacco (10.6)Cigarettes, bidis, gutka, khainiNo intoxication diagnosisIrritability, anxiety, poor concentration, increased appetite, restlessness, low mood, insomniaNo
Other or unknownAnabolic steroids, nitrous oxide, nitrites, betel nut, kavaDepends on the substanceDepends on the substanceDepends on the substance
Exam trap

The eyes are the quickest clue. Pinpoint pupils: opioid intoxication. Dilated pupils: stimulant or hallucinogen intoxication, and opioid withdrawal. Red eyes with normal pupils: cannabis. Nystagmus: alcohol, sedatives, inhalants and, most strikingly (vertical as well as horizontal), phencyclidine. Among the "gaps", remember caffeine: no use disorder; tobacco: no intoxication; hallucinogens, phencyclidine and inhalants: no withdrawal. Cannabis does have a withdrawal syndrome. Withdrawal that can kill belongs to alcohol and the sedative-hypnotics.

10.8 From Organic Brain Syndrome to Neurocognitive Disorder: The Cognitive Domains and DeliriumHigh yield · 8 Qs

So far this chapter has been about what people do to their brains with drugs. The rest of it is about what happens when the brain itself begins to fail, whether suddenly, because of an infection, an operation or a drug, or slowly, over years, because of a degenerative disease. These are the disorders psychiatry used to call "organic", and they are the one group in the whole classification whose cause can usually be found, measured and sometimes cured. This section lays the foundations for the rest of the chapter. It explains how the names changed, defines the six cognitive domains on which modern diagnosis rests, sorts out the old vocabulary of dementia, amnesia, aphasia, apraxia and agnosia, and then teaches the commonest and most dangerous of these disorders, the one a psychologist in a general hospital will see every week: delirium. Sections 10.9 to 10.11 then take up the dementias and the amnesic syndromes one by one.

From "organic" to "neurocognitive": the history of a name

For most of the twentieth century psychiatry divided mental disorders into two great families. Organic mental disorders were those with a demonstrable physical disease of the brain or body behind them: the dementias, delirium, the amnesic syndromes and the psychiatric pictures produced by brain injury, epilepsy, infection or hormones. Functional disorders were those in which no such disease could be found: schizophrenia, the mood disorders and the neuroses, disorders of how the mind "functioned" rather than of its machinery (Chapter 1 and Chapter 2, Section 2.1 trace the origins of the split). The division was useful at the bedside, because it reminded clinicians to look for a treatable physical cause, but it rested on a false assumption: that "functional" disorders have no biological basis. Twin studies, brain imaging and the discovery of drugs that act on specific transmitters made that assumption untenable, and the word "organic" came to imply, wrongly, that schizophrenia or depression were somehow not disorders of the brain.

The American manuals show the change step by step. DSM-I (1952) spoke of acute and chronic brain disorders, roughly the reversible and the irreversible; DSM-II (1968) of organic brain syndromes; DSM-III (1980) and DSM-III-R (1987) of organic mental syndromes and disorders, a large category that also held organic mood, anxiety, delusional and personality syndromes. DSM-IV (1994) dropped the word "organic" altogether. It kept delirium, dementia and the amnestic disorders together in a chapter called "Delirium, Dementia, and Amnestic and Other Cognitive Disorders", because impaired cognition is their main feature, and moved the organic mood, anxiety and psychotic syndromes into the chapters for mood, anxiety and psychosis, as disorders "due to a general medical condition". DSM-5 (2013) renamed the chapter neurocognitive disorders (NCDs). It kept delirium, but replaced "dementia" and "amnestic disorder" with two new syndromes of different severity, major neurocognitive disorder and mild neurocognitive disorder, each of which is then labelled by its cause. DSM-5-TR (2022) keeps this scheme and stresses two defining points: the primary problem is cognitive, and it is acquired, a decline from a level the person once reached, which separates NCDs from intellectual disability and the other neurodevelopmental disorders (Chapter 3). The word "dementia" is still allowed, for continuity, in the subtypes where it is customary.

ICD-10 (1992), which Indian hospitals still widely use, kept the older name for its block F00 to F09: organic, including symptomatic, mental disorders. "Organic" here means that the syndrome can be attributed to an independently diagnosable disease of the brain; "symptomatic" means that the brain is affected secondarily, by a disease elsewhere in the body, such as a fever, kidney failure or a hormone disorder. Table 10.32 lists the block. Two details are favourites with examiners. First, the amnesic syndrome and delirium caused by alcohol and other drugs are not coded here but in the substance block: Korsakoff's syndrome from alcohol is F10.6 and delirium tremens is F10.4 (Section 10.1), which is why F04 and F05 carry the words "not induced by alcohol and other psychoactive substances". Second, there is no F08.

Table 10.32 The ICD-10 block of organic, including symptomatic, mental disorders (F00 to F09).
CodeNameIncludes, or note
F00Dementia in Alzheimer's diseaseEarly onset (before 65), late onset, atypical or mixed (Section 10.9)
F01Vascular dementiaAcute onset, multi-infarct, subcortical, mixed (Section 10.10)
F02Dementia in other diseases classified elsewherePick's disease, Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease, Huntington's disease, Parkinson's disease, HIV disease
F03Unspecified dementiaIncludes "senile dementia" not otherwise specified
F04Organic amnesic syndrome, not induced by alcohol and other psychoactive substancesThe alcoholic form (Korsakoff's syndrome) is F10.6 (Section 10.11)
F05Delirium, not induced by alcohol and other psychoactive substancesF05.0 not superimposed on dementia; F05.1 superimposed on dementia; F05.8 other, including delirium of mixed origin
F06Other mental disorders due to brain damage and dysfunction and to physical diseaseOrganic hallucinosis, catatonic, delusional, mood, anxiety, dissociative and emotionally labile disorders; mild cognitive disorder (Section 10.11)
F07Personality and behavioural disorders due to brain disease, damage and dysfunctionOrganic personality disorder, postencephalitic syndrome, postconcussional syndrome
F09Unspecified organic or symptomatic mental disorderThere is no F08

ICD-11, in force since 2022, follows DSM-5 in spirit. Its Chapter 6 has a block of neurocognitive disorders containing delirium (6D70), mild neurocognitive disorder (6D71), amnestic disorder (6D72) and dementia (6D80 to 6D8Z, with separate codes for dementia due to Alzheimer disease, cerebrovascular disease, Lewy body disease, frontotemporal dementia, dementia due to psychoactive substances, and dementia due to diseases classified elsewhere). Notice one difference that DSM-5-TR itself points out: ICD-10, ICD-11 and the old DSM-IV require several cognitive deficits for a diagnosis of dementia, whereas DSM-5's major NCD can be diagnosed when only one domain is substantially impaired. A person with a pure, severe memory disorder therefore has an amnestic disorder in ICD-11 but a major NCD in DSM-5-TR.

Exam trap

DSM-5 replaced "dementia" and "amnestic disorder" with major and mild neurocognitive disorder and kept "delirium"; it did not abolish delirium or rename it. DSM-IV, not DSM-5, was the edition that dropped the word "organic". ICD-10's F00 to F09 are the "organic, including symptomatic" disorders; delirium is F05, but delirium tremens is F10.4 and alcoholic Korsakoff's syndrome F10.6. In DSM-5-TR one impaired domain is enough for major NCD, so a pure amnesia counts.

The six cognitive domains, and major versus mild neurocognitive disorder

"Dementia" once meant, in practice, memory loss plus something else. The trouble with that definition is that some brain diseases begin elsewhere. Frontotemporal degeneration usually starts with a change in personality and social conduct while memory is still good; some strokes destroy language and leave everything else intact; a man recovering from a head injury may remember perfectly well but be unable to plan a day. DSM-5 therefore built its definitions on six cognitive domains, broad families of mental ability that can be impaired independently and tested separately (Table 10.33). The least familiar domain, and the newest to diagnostic manuals, is social cognition, the ability to read other people's emotions and intentions, added because its loss is the earliest sign of the behavioural variant of frontotemporal dementia (Section 10.10). The tests themselves belong to the Assessment book; the table names typical bedside tasks only so that you can picture what each domain means.

Table 10.33 The six DSM-5-TR cognitive domains.
DomainWhat it coversWhat impairment looks like in daily lifeTypical bedside tasks
Complex attentionSustained, divided and selective attention; processing speedEasily distracted by the television or a second conversation; everything takes longer; cannot hold a phone number just givenDigit span, serial sevens, months of the year backwards; timed tasks
Executive functionPlanning, decision-making, working memory, using feedback, inhibiting habits, mental flexibilityAbandons complex projects; cannot resume a task after an interruption; needs others to plan or decideVerbal fluency, trail making, clock drawing, naming the ink colour of a colour word
Learning and memoryImmediate span, recent memory (free recall, cued recall, recognition), very long-term memory, implicit learningRepeats the same question in one conversation; loses track of bills paid; relies on listsRecall of three words or a name and address after a few minutes; story recall
LanguageExpression (naming, word finding, fluency, grammar) and comprehensionSays "that thing" instead of names; word-finding pauses; later, misunderstands what is saidNaming objects, fluency for animals or letters, repetition, following spoken commands
Perceptual-motorVisual perception, visuoconstruction, praxis (learned movements) and gnosis (recognition)Gets lost in familiar places; struggles with tools or driving; more confused at duskCopying intersecting pentagons or a cube; miming the use of a comb; recognising faces
Social cognitionRecognising emotions; theory of mind (inferring others' thoughts and intentions)Tactless or disinhibited behaviour; loss of empathy; ignores social cues and safetyNaming emotions in pictures of faces; stories asking why a character acts or feels as he does

With the domains in place, the two syndromes are simple to state. Major neurocognitive disorder requires evidence of significant decline from a previous level in one or more domains, based on two kinds of evidence together: concern on the part of the person, someone who knows him well or the clinician, and a substantial impairment on standardised neuropsychological testing or, failing that, another quantified clinical assessment. The deficits must interfere with independence in everyday activities: at a minimum, the person needs help with complex instrumental activities of daily living such as paying bills or managing medicines. They must not occur only during a delirium, and must not be better explained by another mental disorder such as major depression or schizophrenia. Mild neurocognitive disorder has the same structure, with a modest decline, a modest impairment on testing, and preserved independence, although the person now needs greater effort, compensatory strategies or accommodation (lists, reminders, a son who checks the bank statement) to manage. DSM-5-TR gives rough testing guides: in major NCD, performance typically falls two or more standard deviations below appropriate norms (the third percentile or below); in mild NCD, between one and two standard deviations below (between the third and sixteenth percentiles).

Each rule has a reason. Concern and objective testing are both required because each alone misleads: a retired professor may still score in the normal range while clearly declining from his own high baseline, whereas a test score alone may label as impaired an elderly woman who never went to school and was never good at the tasks (which is why culturally appropriate norms and Indian adaptations of the tests matter). The independence criterion is what separates major from mild, and it is a functional, not a test-score, judgement. The exclusion of delirium matters because delirium also impairs cognition, but temporarily; an NCD can only be diagnosed when the person's consciousness is clear. And the exclusion of depression and schizophrenia protects the patient whose poor concentration is part of a treatable mood disorder (depressive "pseudodementia", Section 10.11). Major NCD is then rated by severity: mild (difficulties with instrumental activities such as housework or managing money), moderate (difficulties with basic activities such as feeding and dressing) or severe (fully dependent), and specified as with or without behavioural disturbance (psychotic symptoms, mood disturbance, agitation, apathy). Finally, the clinician names the cause: Alzheimer's disease, frontotemporal degeneration, Lewy body disease, vascular disease, traumatic brain injury, a substance or medication, HIV infection, prion disease, Parkinson's disease, Huntington's disease, another medical condition, multiple causes or an unspecified cause. Sections 10.9 to 10.11 teach them. Mild NCD overlaps closely with the older research idea of mild cognitive impairment (Section 10.9).

Exam trap

The six domains are complex attention, executive function, learning and memory, language, perceptual-motor function and social cognition; "intelligence", "personality" and "orientation" are not on the list. Major and mild NCD differ in the size of the decline and, decisively, in whether it costs the person independence in everyday activities. Major NCD roughly corresponds to the old "dementia". Severity of major NCD (mild, moderate, severe) is a different thing from mild NCD.

Naming the deficit: dementia, amnesia, aphasia, apraxia and agnosia

Long before DSM-5's domains, neurology had a precise vocabulary for what goes wrong when the brain is damaged, and the examiners still test it as a matching exercise. Each word names a failure of one kind of function, and each tells you, roughly, where to look. Most of them begin with the Greek "a-", meaning "without".

Dementia (from the Latin for "out of one's mind") is an acquired, usually progressive, global decline of intellect: memory, but also abstraction (the ability to form concepts and grasp general principles, tested by asking what a proverb means or how an orange and a banana are alike), judgement, reasoning, language and personality, all in clear consciousness. When a question asks which disorder chiefly affects abstraction, the answer is dementia, because abstraction is the highest-level function and the one a global decline erodes. Amnesia is a selective disorder of memory with other functions relatively intact: anterograde amnesia for new learning, retrograde amnesia for what was learned before the onset (Chapter 2, Section 2.7.2 sets out the forms; Section 10.11 the amnesic syndromes).

Aphasia is an acquired disorder of language, the ability to produce and understand words and sentences, caused by brain damage. It is not a disorder of articulation: a person with slurred but grammatical speech has dysarthria, a motor problem. The classical types, named after the patients that founded the subject, are set out in Table 10.35. Paul Broca described in 1861 a man who could say little except "tan" but understood much of what was said to him; Carl Wernicke described in 1874 patients who spoke fluently but meaninglessly and could not understand speech. Three related "a-words" complete the symbolic functions. Alexia is an acquired loss of the ability to read, agraphia of the ability to write, and acalculia of the ability to calculate (a term coined by the Swedish neurologist Salomon Henschen); they differ from the developmental dyslexia and dyscalculia of Chapter 3 because they are lost, not never acquired. Acalculia and agraphia appear together with finger agnosia (inability to name or identify one's own fingers) and right-left confusion in Gerstmann syndrome, the classic sign of damage to the dominant parietal lobe.

Apraxia is the inability to carry out skilled, purposeful, learned movements, although the person has the strength, sensation and coordination to perform them and understands what is asked; its modern study began with the German neurologist Hugo Liepmann in 1900. Asked to show how he would comb his hair, the patient with ideomotor apraxia waves the hand vaguely or uses his fingers as the comb, although he may comb his hair perfectly well when he picks up a real comb in the morning. In ideational apraxia the plan of a sequence is lost: he puts the tea leaves in the cup before boiling the water, or tries to strike the matchbox with the candle. Constructional apraxia is difficulty drawing or assembling (copying a cube or a clock face), and dressing apraxia difficulty putting clothes on the right way round. Apraxia is what the papers mean by a disorder of "motor skills".

Agnosia is a failure of recognition despite intact sensation: the eyes, ears and skin work, but the brain cannot attach meaning to what they report. The word was coined by Sigmund Freud in his book on aphasia in 1891, and a year earlier the German neurologist Heinrich Lissauer had proposed that visual recognition could fail in two ways: in apperceptive agnosia the person cannot put the parts of what he sees together into a whole (he cannot copy a drawing), whereas in associative agnosia he can copy the drawing perfectly but still does not know what it is. Special forms include prosopagnosia, the inability to recognise familiar faces (a man who knows his wife only by her voice), named by Joachim Bodamer in 1947; auditory agnosia for sounds; tactile agnosia or astereognosis, inability to recognise an object such as a key placed in the hand with the eyes closed; and simultanagnosia, the inability to see more than one object of a scene at a time. Closely related is anosognosia, unawareness of one's own deficit, named by Joseph Babinski in 1914 for patients who denied that their paralysed left side was paralysed. The brain areas involved in each of these syndromes are taught in the Physiological book. Table 10.34 sets out the matching.

Table 10.34 The cognitive disorders and the function each chiefly affects.
DisorderFunction chiefly affectedWhat the patient cannot do
DementiaGlobal intellect, including abstraction and judgementReason, grasp concepts and proverbs, judge, remember; a decline across many functions
AmnesiaMemoryLearn new information (anterograde) or recall the past (retrograde)
AphasiaLanguageProduce or understand spoken language
Alexia and agraphiaReading and writingRead or write, previously learned
AcalculiaCalculationDo previously easy arithmetic
ApraxiaSkilled purposeful movement ("motor skills")Perform a learned gesture or sequence on request, with strength and understanding intact
AgnosiaRecognitionRecognise objects, faces, sounds or shapes despite intact senses
Table 10.35 The classical types of aphasia.
TypeFluencyComprehensionRepetitionWhat it sounds like
Broca's (expressive, motor)Non-fluentRelatively goodPoorSlow, effortful, telegraphic: "Wife... hospital... Tuesday"; the patient is aware and frustrated
Wernicke's (receptive, sensory)FluentPoorPoorEffortless but empty speech with wrong and invented words; often unaware of the errors
ConductionFluentGoodVery poorUnderstands and speaks fairly well but cannot repeat a phrase

Transcortical sensoryFluentPoorPreservedRepeats (even echoes) what is said without understanding it

Exam trap

The matching the papers use is dementia: abstraction; amnesia: memory; aphasia (with alexia and acalculia): language; apraxia: motor skills. Every wrong option simply shuffles these pairs, so learn them as fixed couples. Agnosia is recognition, not movement; apraxia is movement, not language; dysarthria is articulation, not language. And do not confuse Wernicke's aphasia (a language disorder, 1874) with Wernicke's encephalopathy (thiamine deficiency, 1881, Section 10.11): same Carl Wernicke, two different disorders.

Delirium: an old word for acute brain failure

Begin with a patient. Vimal, 72, a retired railway station master from Lucknow, had coronary bypass surgery on a Monday. He had diabetes, wore a hearing aid (left at home in the rush to hospital) and, his daughter admitted, had been "a little forgetful" for a year. On Tuesday, in the cardiac intensive care unit, he was quiet and drowsy, and the nurses thought he was simply resting. On Wednesday night he became a different man. He pulled at his drip and his chest drain, tried to climb over the bed rails, and picked repeatedly at the bedsheet as though gathering something from it. He was sure he was back at his old station and kept telling the nurses that the 6.15 to Kanpur was late and that the signals must be checked; at one point he pointed in fear at "the men on the ceiling". He could not say what day it was. Asked to name the months of the year backwards, he managed "December, November" and then drifted into a story about a train. By Thursday afternoon he was calm, apologetic and almost himself, then worse again after dark.

Vimal had delirium, and everything about him is typical: an elderly, already vulnerable brain; a major physical insult; an onset over a day or two; a picture that swings between drowsy and agitated and is worst at night; attention so poor that a simple sequence cannot be held; disorientation; misperceptions and frightening visual hallucinations; and even the habit of acting out his old occupation, which clinicians call an occupational delirium. It is also typical that his first, quiet day was missed.

The syndrome is one of the oldest in medicine. The Hippocratic writers described two forms of mental disturbance in fever: phrenitis, a wild, sleepless frenzy, and lethargus, a dull, drowsy inertia. These are recognisably the hyperactive and hypoactive deliria of today. The Roman writer Celsus, in the first century AD, introduced the word delirium for the mental disorder that could follow fever or head injury. It comes from the Latin de lira, "out of the furrow": the image is of a ploughman whose plough has left the straight line of the furrow, a mind that has gone off its track. Over the centuries the same syndrome acquired many other names (acute confusional state, acute brain syndrome, acute organic reaction, toxic psychosis, metabolic encephalopathy, "ICU psychosis", "post-operative psychosis"), which made it seem like many disorders when it was one. In the second half of the twentieth century the Canadian psychiatrist Zbigniew Lipowski did more than anyone to argue that all these were a single syndrome, best called delirium, and he is usually credited with dividing it by motor activity into hyperactive and hypoactive forms. He also gave it a memorable alternative description: acute brain failure, a counterpart to acute heart failure or acute kidney failure. The comparison is apt. Just as a failing heart shows itself in breathlessness whatever has damaged it, a brain in acute failure shows itself in delirium whatever the cause, and the cause is almost always outside psychiatry.

Key study: Engel and Romano, "a syndrome of cerebral insufficiency" (1959)

In the 1940s and 1950s the American physicians George L. Engel and John Romano asked whether the confusion of seriously ill medical patients was a psychological reaction to illness or a direct sign of a brain that was not working. They recorded the electroencephalogram (EEG) of delirious patients while they assessed their awareness and cognition, and repeated the recordings as the patients' conditions changed. They also observed what happened when blood glucose, oxygen levels or acid-base balance were altered, or certain drugs were given.

The result was consistent: delirium went with slowing of the background rhythm of the EEG, the degree of slowing tracked the degree of impaired awareness, and both reversed together when the underlying disturbance was corrected. In their 1959 paper they called delirium "a syndrome of cerebral insufficiency", a general failure of brain metabolism measurable by EEG slowing, whatever its cause. The paper became a classic of neuropsychiatry and was reprinted in full in 2004. Its limitation is that a slowed EEG can still fall within the normal range in mild delirium, so a single recording without a baseline for comparison may miss it.

Section 2.2.3 of Chapter 2 sets out the DSM-5-TR criteria for delirium and what clouding of consciousness feels like from the inside; here is the logic of them, in brief. The core is a disturbance of attention and awareness: the patient cannot direct, focus, sustain or shift attention, questions must be repeated, and he is pulled away by every passing distraction. Attention is chosen as the core rather than memory because it is what fails first and most consistently when consciousness is clouded, and because a brain that cannot attend cannot be tested on anything else. The disturbance develops over a short period, usually hours to a few days, represents a change from the person's usual state, and fluctuates during the day, often worsening in the evening and at night when the orienting cues of daylight, visitors and ward routine fall away, which nurses call sundowning. There is an additional cognitive disturbance: memory (especially recent memory), orientation (to time first, then place), language (rambling, incoherent speech and poor comprehension), visuospatial ability or perception. The picture must not be better explained by an existing NCD and must not occur in coma, because a patient too deeply unconscious to respond cannot be said to be inattentive. And there must be evidence of a physical cause: a medical condition, intoxication or withdrawal, a medication, a toxin, or several of these.

Around this core lies the rest of the picture. The sleep-wake cycle is disturbed in almost every case, sometimes completely reversed, so that the patient dozes by day and is awake and agitated at night. Perceptual disturbances range from misinterpretations (a coat on a hook becomes a man standing in the corner) and illusions to vivid hallucinations, which are typically visual and often of people or small animals; delusions, when present, are fleeting, poorly organised and usually persecutory, built on the misperceptions of the moment. Emotion is unstable, with fear, irritability, anger, tearfulness, euphoria or apathy and rapid shifts between them. Afterwards the patient usually remembers little or nothing of the episode, because information that is never attended to is never stored. ICD-10 (F05) describes the same syndrome as an "etiologically nonspecific organic cerebral syndrome" with concurrent disturbances of consciousness and attention, perception, thinking, memory, psychomotor behaviour, emotion and the sleep-wake schedule, transient and of fluctuating intensity, with most cases recovering within four weeks or less, although a fluctuating delirium lasting up to six months is not uncommon (for example, in chronic liver disease or cancer). DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to specify the course, acute (hours or days) or persistent (weeks or months), and the cause: substance intoxication delirium, substance withdrawal delirium (the commonest being alcohol withdrawal delirium, or delirium tremens, taught in Section 10.3), medication-induced delirium, delirium due to another medical condition, or delirium due to multiple causes. A delirium-like state that falls short of the full criteria can be recorded as subsyndromal delirium.

Exam trap

The cardinal feature of delirium is a disturbance of attention and awareness (clouded consciousness), not memory loss, hallucinations or agitation. Its onset is acute and its course fluctuating, typically worse at night (sundowning). "Acute confusional state" and "acute brain failure" are other names for delirium; delirium tremens is one cause-specific form of it, alcohol withdrawal delirium (Section 10.3). The word was introduced by Celsus, and the Hippocratic phrenitis and lethargus foreshadow the hyperactive and hypoactive types.

At the bedside: the three motor types, the motor signs and the EEG

DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to specify the level of psychomotor activity, and the distinction matters because it decides whether the delirium is noticed. In hyperactive delirium, which Vimal showed on Wednesday night, the patient is restless, agitated and emotionally labile, pulls out lines, tries to leave the bed or the ward and refuses care; this form is often linked to drug withdrawal and medication side effects. In hypoactive delirium, which he showed on Tuesday, the patient is slow, sluggish and lethargic, sometimes nearly stuporous, lies still and says little. DSM-5-TR notes that this quiet form may be the more frequent in older adults, that it is often unrecognised in emergency departments and hospitals, and that it is commoner in women, whereas men more often show agitation; it is regularly mistaken for depression or for "tiredness after the operation". In delirium of mixed level of activity the patient's activity is normal although attention and awareness are disturbed, or it swings rapidly between the two states.

Delirium is a disorder of the whole brain, and the body shows it. Several motor signs are characteristic, and any or all of them may occur.

  • Asterixis, the "flapping tremor": when the patient holds his arms out with the wrists bent back and fingers spread, the hands repeatedly drop forward and jerk back up, like a bird's wings. It is not a true tremor but a series of brief lapses of muscle tone (negative myoclonus). It was described in 1949 by the American neurologists Raymond Adams and Joseph Foley in patients with liver failure, and it is a sign of metabolic delirium: hepatic encephalopathy, kidney failure, carbon dioxide retention in lung disease, and drug toxicity.
  • Multifocal myoclonus: sudden, brief, shock-like jerks of different muscles in different parts of the body, again typical of metabolic and toxic causes such as kidney failure.
  • Tremor: often coarse and irregular, and most marked in withdrawal states such as delirium tremens.
  • Carphologia and floccillation: aimless picking at the bedclothes, as though plucking threads or bits of fluff from them, which is what Vimal was doing. The words come from the Greek karphos, a straw or twig, and the Latin floccus, a tuft of wool, and the sign has been regarded as an ominous one since ancient medicine.
  • Changes in tone and reflexes: increased tone or rigidity, brisk reflexes and sometimes an upgoing plantar response, all reflecting widespread disturbance of brain function rather than a single lesion.
  • Slurred speech (dysarthria) and clumsiness, and, very sensitively, disturbed handwriting: a delirious patient asked to write a sentence often produces untidy, misspelt, poorly organised writing even when his speech seems almost normal.

Autonomic signs (a fast pulse, sweating, flushing, fever, raised blood pressure) are prominent in withdrawal and in anticholinergic delirium. None of these signs is found in a functional psychosis such as schizophrenia, which is why a careful physical examination is part of every psychiatric assessment of an acutely confused patient.

The electroencephalogram confirms what Engel and Romano found. DSM-5-TR describes generalised, irregular slowing into the theta range as the usual finding in delirium, with fast activity occasionally seen instead, for example in some cases of alcohol withdrawal delirium (and the fast beta rhythms produced by benzodiazepines can also appear). The EEG is useful in three situations: to distinguish delirium from a functional psychosis or a severe depression, in which the EEG is normal; to detect non-convulsive seizures, a treatable cause of confusion that otherwise looks like delirium; and to follow recovery. Its limitation is the one Engel and Romano noted: without a baseline, mild slowing may still look "normal".

Exam trap

A question listing asterixis, multifocal myoclonus, carphologia and tone and reflex abnormalities as motor signs of delirium wants the answer "all of the above": every one can occur, and tremor and dysarthria can be added to the list. Carphologia (picking at the bedclothes) is not a tic, a compulsion or a catatonic sign. The hypoactive type is the one most often missed and mistaken for depression. The EEG in delirium shows diffuse slowing; fast activity is the exception (some alcohol withdrawal delirium). A normal EEG argues against delirium and for a functional disorder.

Why delirium happens: causes, vulnerability and the surgical ward

The single most important fact about delirium is that it always has a physical cause, and usually more than one. Early in the twentieth century the German psychiatrist Karl Bonhoeffer pointed out that the brain has only a limited repertoire of responses to physical illness, so that very different causes (a fever, a poison, kidney failure, a head injury) produce the same "exogenous" reaction. Delirium is the commonest of those reactions. The picture tells you that the brain is failing, not why; the why has to be found by history, examination and tests. Table 10.36 groups the common causes. A popular memory aid lists them as "I WATCH DEATH": infection, withdrawal, acute metabolic disturbance, trauma, central nervous system disease, hypoxia, deficiencies, endocrine disorders, acute vascular events, toxins or drugs, and heavy metals.

Table 10.36 The common causes of delirium, grouped.
GroupExamples
InfectionUrinary and chest infections in the elderly, septicaemia, malaria, typhoid, meningitis and encephalitis, HIV
Drugs: intoxication and side effectsAnticholinergic drugs (including some bladder, antiparkinsonian and older antidepressant drugs), opioids, benzodiazepines and other sedatives, steroids, lithium toxicity, digoxin; datura poisoning; several drugs at once
Drugs: withdrawalAlcohol (delirium tremens, Section 10.3), benzodiazepines, barbiturates and other sedatives (Section 10.7)
Metabolic and electrolyte disturbanceLow or high blood sugar, low sodium, high calcium, dehydration, kidney failure (uraemia), liver failure
Lack of oxygenHeart failure, pneumonia, chronic lung disease, anaemia, carbon monoxide poisoning
Brain disease and injuryHead injury, stroke, epileptic seizures and the post-ictal state, brain tumour, raised intracranial pressure
Endocrine and deficiency statesThyroid and adrenal disorders; thiamine deficiency (Wernicke's encephalopathy, Section 10.11); vitamin B12 deficiency
Surgery and intensive careThe post-operative state, especially after heart and hip surgery; sleep deprivation, immobility and sedation in intensive care
Simple, overlooked causesUrinary retention, severe constipation, uncontrolled pain, a missing hearing aid or spectacles

Why do so many causes produce the same picture? The answer is still incomplete, but three mechanisms stand out. The first is a shortage of acetylcholine, the transmitter on which attention and arousal depend. Drugs that block acetylcholine reliably produce delirium, the drug physostigmine, which boosts acetylcholine, can reverse such a delirium, and the cholinergic losses of Alzheimer's disease (Section 10.9) help explain why people with dementia become delirious so easily. (DSM-5-TR adds a note of caution: in a meta-analysis, anticholinergic drug use was not confirmed as a validated predictor of delirium, so it is one cause among many, not the whole story.) The second is a relative excess of dopamine, which is why dopamine-enhancing drugs can provoke delirium and dopamine-blocking antipsychotics can calm its agitation. The third is inflammation: infections and major surgery release inflammatory chemicals that cross into the brain and disturb its function. Whatever the route, the final common pathway is a widespread failure of the brain's metabolism, which is exactly what Engel and Romano's slowed EEG was recording.

Indian context

A classic Indian cause of anticholinergic delirium is datura (dhatura), whose seeds contain atropine and scopolamine (hyoscine). It is sometimes eaten by accident or in ritual preparations, and Indian forensic reports describe travellers on trains and buses who were offered sweets or "prasad" laced with datura and robbed while they were stupefied. The patient is agitated and hallucinating, with the textbook signs that medical students learn as "hot as a hare, dry as a bone, red as a beet, blind as a bat, mad as a hatter": fever, dry skin and mouth, flushing, widely dilated pupils with blurred vision, and delirium, often with a full bladder and a racing pulse.

Who becomes delirious depends on two things at once, a balance first set out by Sharon Inouye and Peter Charpentier in 1996. Predisposing factors make a brain vulnerable: old age, an existing dementia or mild NCD, poor vision or hearing, severe illness, functional dependence, depression, a past stroke and heavy drinking, with male sex an added risk in DSM-5-TR's list. Precipitating factors are the insults that tip it over: an infection, a new drug, an operation, dehydration, a urinary catheter, physical restraint, a sleepless night on a noisy ward. The two trade off against each other. A very vulnerable patient, an 88-year-old woman with Alzheimer's disease and cataracts, may become delirious after a single sleeping tablet or a mild urinary infection; a robust young man needs a large insult, such as severe burns or septicaemia. Children with high fevers also become delirious easily. Pre-existing brain disease is the strongest predisposing factor of all; as many as half of people with dementia have at least one episode of delirium.

The numbers in hospital are large, and they climb with the seriousness of the setting. DSM-5-TR gives a community prevalence of only 1 to 2 percent, but 8 to 17 percent among older people arriving in North American emergency departments, 18 to 35 percent at the time of admission to hospital, 11 to 51 percent of older people after an operation, up to 81 percent of patients in intensive care, and up to 88 percent of people with a terminal illness in their last days. Surgery on the heart is especially likely to cause it. A 2024 meta-analysis of 106 studies put the pooled incidence after open cardiac surgery at about 23 percent, with very wide variation between studies that depended partly on the patients and partly on which screening tool was used to look for delirium. The reasons are easy to see in Vimal's case: his age and his vulnerable brain, the heart-lung bypass machine (which can send tiny emboli to the brain, lower its blood flow and trigger inflammation), long anaesthesia, opioid painkillers, sleep deprivation in intensive care, and the vascular disease that made the operation necessary in the first place. Depression and anxiety are also common after heart surgery, but they develop more slowly, over weeks, and are not the acute syndrome of the first days after the operation; schizophrenia is not caused by surgery at all. The older term post-cardiotomy delirium recognises how familiar this picture is.

Exam trap

The commonest psychiatric syndrome after cardiac surgery, and after surgery in older people generally, is delirium, not depression, anxiety or schizophrenia. Depression after a heart attack or bypass is common and important (Book 2, Section 9.3 covers it under health psychology), but delirium is commoner, earlier and more dangerous. Delirium always has a physical cause; "psychological stress" alone is never the answer.

Course, outcome and management

Delirium is usually short. DSM-5-TR says that in hospital it lasts about a week, although some symptoms often persist after discharge, and most people recover fully, with or without treatment, especially if they are not elderly. But "usually reversible" does not mean "harmless". Delirium can progress to stupor, coma, seizures or death if its cause is not found and treated, and it is a marker of how ill the patient is: as many as 38 to 41 percent of hospitalised people with delirium die within a year, the risk being greatest in those with cancer and other serious illness. Older people who become delirious are more likely to lose function, to need institutional care and to develop dementia, and there is increasing evidence that an episode of delirium can be followed by long-term cognitive decline, especially in people who already had some impairment. Sometimes delirium unmasks a dementia that was already developing: six months after his operation Vimal's daughter noticed that his memory was clearly worse than before, and he was later found to have an early Alzheimer's disease.

Management rests on four principles.

  • Find and treat the cause. This is the only treatment that cures. It means a full history (including from the family), a review of every drug the patient takes, a physical examination and investigations for infection, metabolic disturbance and hypoxia. In alcohol withdrawal it means benzodiazepines and thiamine (Sections 10.4 and 10.11); in anticholinergic delirium, stopping the drug.
  • Support the brain while it recovers. Keep the patient oriented with a visible clock and calendar, a window, explanations repeated as often as needed and the same nurses where possible; restore spectacles and hearing aids; let a family member stay; keep the room lit and active by day and quiet and dim at night; get the patient out of bed and walking; ensure fluids and food; remove catheters and lines that are not essential; and avoid physical restraint, which increases agitation, injury and the length of the delirium.
  • Review and reduce drugs. Stop or reduce anticholinergic, sedative and other drugs that can cause delirium, and avoid starting new ones casually.
  • Use drugs for symptoms only when necessary. When agitation, fear or hallucinations put the patient or others at risk, a low dose of an antipsychotic such as haloperidol is used for the shortest possible time. It calms; it does not cure. In the large MIND-USA trial in intensive care, published in 2018 by Timothy Girard and colleagues, neither haloperidol nor ziprasidone shortened delirium or improved survival compared with placebo. Benzodiazepines are avoided, because they worsen confusion and prolong delirium, with one crucial exception: in delirium caused by alcohol or sedative withdrawal they are the treatment, because they replace the missing drug (Section 10.7.9).
Key study: preventing delirium, the Hospital Elder Life Program (1999)

If so much delirium is precipitated by the hospital itself, can it be prevented? Sharon Inouye and colleagues at Yale tested this with 852 patients aged 70 or over admitted to a general medical service. Patients on one unit received a multicomponent programme aimed at six risk factors: cognitive impairment (orientation and stimulating activities), sleep deprivation (a non-drug sleep routine of warm milk or herbal tea, relaxation music and back massage, and a quiet ward at night), immobility (early walking), poor vision (spectacles, large-print materials), poor hearing (hearing aids, removing ear wax) and dehydration. They were compared with matched patients receiving usual care.

Delirium developed in 9.9 percent of the intervention group against 15.0 percent of the usual-care group, a reduction of about a third (matched odds ratio 0.60), and the total number of days spent delirious also fell. The conclusion was that a substantial part of delirium in hospital is preventable with simple, non-drug measures delivered by staff and trained volunteers. The study's limitation is that the groups were matched rather than randomised, and the programme reduced new episodes rather than making established delirium go away faster. It became the basis of the Hospital Elder Life Program now used in many countries.

Screening is part of prevention. The Confusion Assessment Method (CAM), introduced by Inouye and colleagues in 1990, lets a trained nurse or doctor diagnose delirium at the bedside in a few minutes from four features: an acute onset and fluctuating course, and inattention, together with either disorganised thinking or an altered level of consciousness (the Assessment book describes it).

Clinical link

Psychologists in general hospitals are often the people who recognise delirium, because they are called to see "depression", "behavioural problems" or "a difficult patient". Three things make the psychologist's contribution distinctive. First, cognitive screening that tests attention (digit span, months backwards), not just memory. Second, work with the family, who are frightened by a parent who does not recognise them and often fear that the change is permanent; explaining that delirium is usually temporary, and involving them in reorientation, helps both patient and family. Third, follow-up: many intensive care survivors have vivid, frightening memories of their delirious hallucinations, which can feed anxiety and post-traumatic symptoms months later, and a patient who has been delirious needs his cognition checked again once he has recovered.

Exam trap

Delirium is usually reversible but it is not benign: it signals serious physical illness and carries a high mortality (up to about 40 percent within a year of a hospital diagnosis). The treatment is to find and treat the cause; antipsychotics only calm distress or danger, and benzodiazepines worsen most deliria, except delirium due to alcohol or sedative withdrawal, where they are the treatment of choice. Physical restraint makes delirium worse, not safer.

Delirium or dementia?

In an elderly patient who is confused, the commonest question is whether the confusion is delirium, dementia or both, and DSM-5-TR calls this the most common differential diagnostic problem in the whole area. The answer matters enormously: delirium is a medical emergency with a cause to be found, whereas dementia is a chronic condition to be planned for, and a delirium mistaken for "just his dementia getting worse" may be a pneumonia left untreated. The two differ most clearly in time and in consciousness (Figure 10.15). Delirium begins over hours or days and fluctuates through each day, with attention and awareness disturbed from the start; dementia begins insidiously, over months or years, progresses slowly and steadily, and leaves consciousness and attention clear until its late stages. Table 10.37 sets out the full comparison.

Table 10.37 Delirium and dementia compared.
FeatureDeliriumDementia (major NCD)
OnsetAcute, over hours to days; often dated to a day or an eventInsidious, over months to years; often impossible to date
CourseFluctuating through the day, worse at night; lucid intervalsSlowly progressive (stepwise in vascular dementia); fairly stable from day to day
DurationDays to weeks, occasionally monthsMonths to years
ConsciousnessClouded; reduced awareness of the surroundingsClear until the late stages
AttentionMarkedly impaired from the start: the core featureRelatively preserved early
OrientationImpaired early, especially for time; fluctuatesImpaired later, as the disease advances
MemoryPoor registration and recent memory; little remembered of the episodeRecent memory lost first (in Alzheimer's), remote memory later
Thinking and speechDisorganised, rambling, incoherentImpoverished, with word-finding difficulty
PerceptionMisperceptions and hallucinations common, mainly visualHallucinations can occur, especially later or in Lewy body disease
Sleep-wake cycleDisrupted, often reversedUsually normal early; disturbed later
Psychomotor activityHyperactive, hypoactive or mixed; motor signs such as asterixis and myoclonusUsually normal until late (except in subcortical dementias)
EEGDiffuse slowing (fast activity in some withdrawal deliria)Normal or mildly slow early
Physical causeAlways present: infection, drugs, metabolic, surgeryA degenerative or vascular brain disease
OutcomeUsually reversible if the cause is treatedUsually irreversible and progressive

Three refinements prevent overconfidence. First, the two often coexist. The single strongest risk factor for delirium is an existing dementia, and on an elderly medical ward a delirium superimposed on dementia (ICD-10's F05.1) is the commonest situation of all; the clue is a sudden, recent worsening on top of a slow decline, and DSM-5-TR advises that when both are present, the delirium is treated first. Second, hallucinations, delusions, disorientation and memory loss occur in both disorders, so none of them separates the two; neither does age, because both are commonest in the elderly. Third, some dementias imitate delirium. NCD with Lewy bodies (Section 10.10) has marked fluctuations in attention and alertness and vivid visual hallucinations, so a fluctuating course alone does not prove delirium. And a depression in an older person can look like dementia (pseudodementia, Section 10.11), which Table 10.37 does not cover.

The examiners return again and again to one question in different words: what is the most reliable way to tell delirium from dementia? The answer is always the same cluster: acute onset, a fluctuating course and a disturbance of attention and consciousness in delirium, against memory impairment in clear consciousness, with an insidious onset and a progressive course, in dementia.

Exam trap

The distractors in delirium-versus-dementia questions follow a few patterns, all wrong. "Hallucinations (or delusions) occur only in delirium" or "only in dementia": they occur in both. "Memory impairment only in delirium" or "memory loss is greater in delirium": memory is impaired in both, and progressive memory loss is the hallmark of dementia. "Onset gradual in delirium, acute in dementia": this is reversed. "Reversibility only in dementia" or "dementia is always reversible": it is delirium that is usually reversible. "Delirium occurs only in young patients" or "older age at onset": both are commonest in the elderly. "Confabulation" points to Korsakoff's syndrome (Section 10.11), and "aphasia and apraxia as early features" to Alzheimer's disease (Section 10.9). The right answer is acute onset, fluctuation and disturbed attention or consciousness in delirium, versus impaired memory with clear consciousness in dementia.

Scan Your Brain

Answers: (1) resin, (2) Mechoulam; anandamide, (3) Albert Hofmann, (4) 5-HT2A, (5) hallucinogen persisting perception disorder, (6) phencyclidine (PCP), (7) longer, (8) cross-tolerance, (9) neurocognitive, (10) carphologia (or floccillation); asterixis.

10.9 Alzheimer's Disease and Mild Neurocognitive DisorderMedium yield · 2 Qs

Section 10.8 gave you the frame for this half of the chapter. A neurocognitive disorder is an acquired decline from a person's own earlier level in one or more of six cognitive domains; it is major when the decline costs the person independence in everyday life and mild when it does not. Notice what that frame leaves out: it says nothing about why the decline happened. A stroke, a tumour, a vitamin deficiency, a virus, a blow to the head and a slow, silent pile-up of abnormal proteins can all produce the same picture of a person who forgets, gets lost and can no longer manage money. So once a clinician has decided that a neurocognitive disorder is present, the next question is always about cause, and DSM-5-TR builds that question into the diagnosis itself: you do not diagnose "major neurocognitive disorder" and stop, you diagnose "major neurocognitive disorder due to" something.

By far the commonest answer to that question is Alzheimer's disease. It is the prototype against which every other dementia is described ("more stepwise than Alzheimer's", "memory better preserved than in Alzheimer's"), so it has to be learned first and learned well. Its story begins with one woman in Frankfurt at the start of the twentieth century.

Auguste D. and the disease that took Alzheimer's name

In 1901 Alois Alzheimer (1864 to 1915) was a senior physician at the municipal asylum in Frankfurt am Main. He was a psychiatrist who was also a skilled microscopist: with his friend and lifelong collaborator Franz Nissl (1860 to 1919), whose stain for nerve cells is still used, he spent years studying slices of the brains of patients who had died on the wards, hoping to find the physical lesion behind each kind of madness. That ambition was the programme of German psychiatry at the time. The patient who made his name arrived in November of that year.

Key study: Auguste Deter (1901 to 1906)

Auguste Deter (1850 to 1906), the wife of a railway clerk, was admitted to the Frankfurt asylum on 25 November 1901, aged 51. Since the spring she had become irrationally jealous of her husband, then forgetful; she hid things, could no longer cook, lost her way in her own flat and sometimes screamed that someone was going to kill her. Alzheimer interviewed her the next day. Asked her name, she said "Auguste"; asked her husband's name, she again said "Auguste". When he asked her to write her name she began, lost the thread, and said what became the most quoted sentence in the history of dementia: "I have lost myself" (in German, roughly "I have, so to speak, lost myself").

Alzheimer moved to Kraepelin's clinic in Munich but kept track of her. When she died on 8 April 1906, of septicaemia from infected bedsores, her brain was sent to him. Using a new silver stain he found two abnormalities: clumps of a strange material scattered between the neurons (the plaques) and thick, tangled bundles of fibrils inside them (the tangles), along with marked shrinkage of the cortex. He presented the case at a meeting of south-west German psychiatrists in Tübingen in November 1906, where it drew little interest, and published it in 1907 as "On a peculiar disease of the cerebral cortex". Her case file was then lost for almost ninety years until psychiatrists at the Frankfurt university clinic, led by Konrad Maurer, found it in the mid-1990s (accounts give 1995 or 1996) and published it in The Lancet in 1997. Re-examination of her preserved brain sections confirmed plaques and tangles without significant vascular disease. A 2013 report that she carried a presenilin 1 mutation could not be replicated the following year.

Alzheimer did not claim to have found a new disease; he claimed to have shown that a dementia could be explained by visible changes in the cortex. The name came from his chief, Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926), who in the eighth edition of his textbook of psychiatry (1910) used the term "Alzheimer's disease" for this kind of case. Because Auguste had fallen ill in her early fifties, Kraepelin classed the condition as a presenile dementia, one beginning before old age. The far commoner mental decline of the very old was called senile dementia and was widely assumed to be a different thing, the result of hardening of the arteries or simply of wear and tear.

For most of the twentieth century, then, "Alzheimer's disease" meant a rare illness of the middle-aged. Two pieces of evidence changed that. In Newcastle in 1968, Gary Blessed, Bernard Tomlinson and Martin Roth tested elderly people in life and then counted plaques in their brains after death; the more plaques, the worse the dementia had been, and the brains of most elderly people with "senile dementia" showed exactly the changes Alzheimer had described. Then in 1976 the American neurologist Robert Katzman (1925 to 2008) argued in an editorial that presenile Alzheimer's disease and senile dementia were one and the same disease, divided only by an arbitrary birthday, and that, counted properly, it was one of the major killers of old people. That argument won. Today Alzheimer's disease is understood to be a single disease that can begin at any adult age, with a rare early-onset form and a common late-onset form, and the age of 65 survives only as a coding convenience (ICD-10 still separates F00.0, dementia in Alzheimer's disease with early onset, before 65, from F00.1, with late onset).

Two older words are worth pinning down because they still appear as options. "Senile dementia" is a description, not a diagnosis: it means only "dementia beginning in old age" and says nothing about cause, so it could be Alzheimer's, vascular or anything else. Cerebral atrophy is also a description: it means shrinkage of the brain, with wider sulci and larger ventricles, and it is seen on scans in Alzheimer's disease, in many other dementias, after chronic heavy drinking and, to a modest degree, in normal ageing. Neither term names a disease with its own mechanism, and that is why neither can be the best answer when a paper asks which disease is linked to a specific chemical deficit.

Exam trap

"Which condition is related to a deficiency of acetylcholine in the brain?" The answer is Alzheimer's disease, because its defining chemical lesion is the loss of the cholinergic neurons of the basal forebrain (Section 10.9.4). "Senile dementia" and "atrophy" are descriptive labels that cover many causes, and Korsakoff's syndrome is a disorder of thiamine deficiency, usually caused by chronic drinking, that damages the mammillary bodies and thalamus (Section 10.11); Alzheimer's disease is not caused by alcohol. Dates: Auguste admitted 1901; Alzheimer's lecture 1906 and paper 1907; Kraepelin's naming 1910. If an option says "first described in 1906" or "in 1907", both refer to the same case.

How common it is, and who gets it

The World Health Organization (WHO) estimates that 57 million people were living with dementia in 2021, with nearly 10 million new cases every year and more than 60 percent of all cases in low- and middle-income countries such as India. Dementia is now the fifth leading cause of death in the world, and in 2019 it cost economies an estimated 1.3 trillion US dollars, much of it in unpaid care by families. Alzheimer's disease is the commonest single cause: WHO puts its share at 60 to 70 percent of cases, and DSM-5-TR gives a range from about 60 percent to over 90 percent depending on the setting and the criteria used. The range is wide because many old people have more than one kind of pathology, so "pure" figures depend on how mixed cases are counted.

The strongest risk factor, by a wide margin, is age. Alzheimer's disease is rare before 60, and after 65 the prevalence of dementia roughly doubles with every five years of age; a well-known meta-analysis by Anthony Jorm and colleagues in 1987 put the doubling time at about five years. DSM-5-TR quotes American figures that make the point: dementia due to Alzheimer's disease is found in 11 percent of people aged 65 and over and in 32 percent of those aged 85 and over, and 81 percent of all people with the disease are 75 or older. Onset is usually between 70 and 89. Early-onset cases, beginning in the forties or fifties, are uncommon and are often, though not always, caused by single-gene mutations (Section 10.9.5). More women than men have the disease, partly because women live longer; whether women also have a higher age-specific risk is less certain, since European studies have found a higher incidence in women and most North American studies have not.

Indian context

The first large rural study, the Indo-US study in Ballabgarh, Haryana (Vijay Chandra, Mary Ganguli and colleagues, 1998), screened 5,126 people aged 55 and over and found strikingly low rates: dementia in 1.36 percent and Alzheimer's disease in 1.07 percent of those aged 65 and over, far below Western figures. Possible reasons include fewer people surviving into the highest-risk ages, different risk factors, and under-detection where forgetfulness in the old is taken as natural (in Hindi an old person who has become forgetful may be said to have "turned sixty", sathiya gaye). The nationwide Longitudinal Ageing Study in India's dementia sub-study (LASI-DAD; Jinkook Lee and colleagues, 2023) gave a much higher national estimate: 7.4 percent of Indians aged 60 and over, about 8.8 million people, with large differences between states. Almost all of this care is given at home by families, usually women, with little formal support.

The course: from forgetting to dependence

The two words that define the course of Alzheimer's disease are insidious and gradual. Insidious means that nobody can say when it began: families date the onset to a wedding at which the person repeated the same story three times, or to a bill that went unpaid, but on questioning the changes had been creeping in for a year or more. Gradual means that the decline is steady, without the sudden drops of a stroke and without extended plateaus; DSM-5-TR builds this into the criteria. The disease runs to death, which comes on average about ten years after diagnosis (DSM-5-TR), sometimes as long as twenty, most often from aspiration pneumonia in a person who can no longer swallow safely.

Pinel describes the illness in three phases. In the preclinical stage the brain is already accumulating plaques and tangles but the person has no symptoms at all; studies of families with dominant mutations suggest that this silent phase lasts a decade or two. In the prodromal stage there is mild cognitive impairment, noticeable to the person and to careful tests but not yet disabling (Section 10.9.8). Then comes the dementia stage, which clinicians usually divide into early (mild), middle (moderate) and late (severe), as in Table 10.38.

Push a case through the stages and the pattern becomes clear. Rajinder is a 68-year-old retired headmaster in Ludhiana who for forty years kept the school accounts in his head. His wife first noticed that he asked the same questions again and again ("Is Pinky coming on Sunday?" five times in an afternoon), that he forgot conversations held the day before, and that he had started leaving notes all over the house. He laughed it off as age. Over the next two years he began to hunt for words, calling a spoon "the thing for eating dal" (circumlocution), lost his way driving home from the gurdwara he had attended for decades, and could no longer work out the change at the market. He insisted that the servant was stealing the money he had in fact hidden himself. Dressing became a puzzle: he put his kurta on inside out and could not work out the buttons, although his arms and fingers were perfectly strong. His speech remained fluent and polite, his walking was normal and there was no tremor, and on no day was he suddenly worse. This is the IHBAS 2025 vignette in living form: progressive memory impairment, then language difficulty and visuospatial deficits, with insidious onset and gradual progression.

Look at the order in which things went. The first casualty is episodic memory for recent events, the memory of what happened to me yesterday, because the damage starts in the entorhinal cortex and hippocampus that lay down new memories. Old memories of school, marriage and the partition stories of his parents are kept far longer, a pattern described in 1881 by the French psychologist Théodule Ribot (1839 to 1916) and known as Ribot's law: in a progressive dementia the most recent memories are lost first and the oldest last. Semantic memory (knowledge of words and facts) fades later, and procedural memory (skills such as cycling or tying a turban) lasts longest of all. As the disease spreads into the temporal and parietal association cortex, Indian teaching summarises the middle stage as the "four As": amnesia, aphasia (word-finding difficulty and empty, roundabout speech at first, with comprehension failing later), apraxia (inability to carry out skilled purposeful movements such as dressing or using a comb, despite normal strength) and agnosia (failure to recognise objects or faces despite normal sight). Visuospatial and executive skills fail alongside them, and orientation goes first for time, then for place, and only very late for person. Section 10.8 defined each of these deficits and matched it to the function it affects.

Table 10.38 The stages of dementia due to Alzheimer's disease (durations are rough guides; the stages blend into one another).
StageCognitionBehaviour and moodEveryday function
Early (mild), about 2 to 4 yearsRecent episodic memory, repeating questions, misplacing things, word-finding pauses, getting lost in unfamiliar placesApathy, irritability, depression; social manners preserved, insight partial and fadingManaging money, medicines and travel becomes hard; basic self-care intact
Middle (moderate), several yearsAphasia, apraxia, agnosia, visuospatial and executive failure; disorientation in time and placeDelusions of theft, misidentification, agitation, wandering, sundowning, broken sleepNeeds help with dressing, bathing and cooking; cannot be left alone
Late (severe)Speech reduced to a few words or none; family not recognisedAgitation may give way to apathy and immobilityIncontinent, rigid, bedbound; swallowing fails; death often from aspiration pneumonia

Alzheimer's disease is not only a disease of thinking. DSM-5-TR stresses that neuropsychiatric symptoms are almost universal (in one American sample 98 percent of patients developed them within five years), that more than half of people who develop dementia begin with them, and that they are often more distressing to families than the memory loss itself. The umbrella term for them is behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD). In the mild stage they are mostly depression, irritability and apathy; in the moderate stage delusions (classically that misplaced belongings have been stolen), misidentifications (the reflection in the mirror is a stranger; the house is not really his house), agitation, aggression and wandering; and many patients become more confused and restless in the late afternoon and evening, the phenomenon called sundowning. Hallucinations occur but are less prominent than in Lewy body disease (Section 10.10.4). Late in the illness come gait disturbance, difficulty swallowing, incontinence, myoclonus and seizures. Strikingly, the motor system is spared until late: the primary motor and sensory cortex and the basal ganglia are among the last regions the disease reaches, which is why a person with moderate Alzheimer's disease can walk briskly out of the house and get lost. That preserved movement is one of the most useful clues separating Alzheimer's disease from the subcortical dementias of Section 10.10.10. DSM-5-TR also notes a moderate risk of suicide that persists even many years after the diagnosis, so mood and suicidal thinking need to be asked about throughout the illness.

Alongside the typical amnestic picture there are less common presentations that begin elsewhere: a visuospatial form (posterior cortical atrophy, in which a person cannot read, judge distances or find objects in plain view while memory is relatively spared), a language form (logopenic aphasia, with halting, word-searching speech) and a dysexecutive or behavioural form. They are worth knowing because they are often misdiagnosed, and because the logopenic form is easily confused with the language variants of frontotemporal disorder (Section 10.10.3). Figure 10.16 sets the typical course beside the stepwise course of vascular dementia, the contrast the papers ask about most often.

Exam trap

"A progressive neurodegenerative disorder with memory impairment, language difficulties and visuospatial deficits, insidious onset and gradual progression" is Alzheimer's disease. Each distractor has a different signature: Lewy body dementia fluctuates and brings well-formed visual hallucinations and parkinsonism; vascular dementia begins abruptly and worsens in steps with focal neurological signs; frontotemporal dementia starts younger with personality change or a language disorder while memory and visuospatial skills are relatively spared. The key words to match are "memory first" and "smooth decline". Memory loss with confabulation and nystagmus in a heavy drinker is not Alzheimer's disease but Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome (Section 10.11).

What goes wrong in the brain

Look at the brain of a person who has died of Alzheimer's disease and the first thing you notice is that it is shrunken: the gyri are thin, the sulci gape, the ventricles are enlarged to fill the lost space, and the hippocampus and the rest of the medial temporal lobe are worst affected, with the temporal and parietal association cortex close behind. The primary motor, sensory and visual areas look relatively normal, which fits the clinical picture you have just met. Under the microscope Pinel lists the three defining features: neurofibrillary tangles, threadlike tangles of tau protein inside the cytoplasm of neurons; amyloid plaques, clumps outside the neurons made of degenerating nerve terminals around a core of a protein called beta-amyloid, which is present in healthy brains only in small amounts; and loss of neurons. Tau is a protein that normally stabilises the microtubules, the internal scaffolding and transport tracks of the axon. In Alzheimer's disease it becomes hyperphosphorylated (it carries too many phosphate groups), lets go of the microtubules and aggregates into the paired filaments that make up the tangles, and the neuron's transport system collapses.

Which of these lesions matters most for symptoms? Plaques, it turns out, correlate only loosely with how demented a person was in life; tangles correlate better, and the loss of synapses correlates best of all, as Robert Terry and colleagues showed in 1991. The spread of the tangles also explains the order of the symptoms. In 1991 Heiko Braak and Eva Braak, examining 83 brains from people with and without dementia, showed that tangles appear in a predictable sequence that can be divided into six Braak stages: in stages I and II they are confined to the transentorhinal and entorhinal cortex, the gateway to the hippocampus, and the person has no symptoms; in stages III and IV they fill the limbic system, including the hippocampus, and memory begins to fail; in stages V and VI they flood the association cortex of the neocortex and the person is demented. Memory goes first because the entorhinal cortex and hippocampus are hit first.

The cholinergic hypothesis. The first chemical theory of the disease came from a simple measurement. In 1976 Peter Davies and A. J. F. Maloney, in Edinburgh, reported in The Lancet a selective loss of the enzyme that makes acetylcholine (choline acetyltransferase) in the cortex and hippocampus of patients with Alzheimer's disease; other neurotransmitter systems were much less affected. Where were the missing cholinergic neurons? The cortex gets most of its acetylcholine from a small group of large neurons in the basal forebrain, just above the hypothalamus, called the nucleus basalis of Meynert after the Viennese anatomist Theodor Meynert (1833 to 1892). In 1981 and 1982 Peter Whitehouse and colleagues at Johns Hopkins showed that these neurons are severely depleted in Alzheimer's disease. Meanwhile drugs that block acetylcholine receptors, such as scopolamine, had been shown to impair memory in healthy young volunteers in a way that resembled ageing. In 1982 Raymond Bartus and colleagues pulled these findings together in Science as the cholinergic hypothesis: the memory loss of Alzheimer's disease (and some of the memory loss of ageing) is caused by the failure of the basal forebrain cholinergic system. That hypothesis led directly to the first drugs for the disease, the cholinesterase inhibitors (Section 10.9.7). Pinel's verdict is the modern one: acetylcholine depletion from basal forebrain damage contributes to Alzheimer's amnesia, but it is clearly not the only factor, because the damage is diffuse and reaches the medial temporal lobes and the prefrontal cortex as well. That is also why the drugs built on it help only modestly.

Exam trap

Match each transmitter to the disorder in which its loss is the textbook lesion. Acetylcholine: Alzheimer's disease (nucleus basalis of Meynert). Dopamine: Parkinson's disease (substantia nigra to striatum); dopamine excess in the mesolimbic pathway is the classical hypothesis of positive psychotic symptoms (Chapter 4, Section 4.8). GABA: Huntington's disease destroys the GABA-releasing neurons of the striatum; low GABA function is also linked to anxiety and epilepsy. Serotonin and noradrenaline: the monoamine hypothesis of depression (Chapter 5). Glutamate is the excitotoxic villain targeted by memantine, not a transmitter that is deficient.

The amyloid cascade hypothesis. The second and now dominant theory begins with the plaque. In 1984 George Glenner and Caine Wong isolated beta-amyloid and worked out its sequence. It turned out to be a fragment of a much larger membrane protein, the amyloid precursor protein (APP), whose gene lies on chromosome 21. APP is normally cut by enzymes called secretases. When it is cut first by beta-secretase and then by gamma-secretase (an enzyme complex whose working core is a protein called presenilin), the product is beta-amyloid, and one form of it, 42 amino acids long, is especially sticky: it clumps into small soluble aggregates called oligomers and eventually into plaques. In 1991 Alison Goate, working in John Hardy's group in London, found the first mutation in the APP gene in a family with early-onset Alzheimer's disease. In 1992 Hardy and Gerald Higgins set out the amyloid cascade hypothesis in a short paper in Science: the accumulation of beta-amyloid is the first event, and everything else (tau tangles, inflammation, synapse loss, neuron death and dementia) follows from it (Figure 10.17).

The case for the hypothesis is strong. Every mutation known to cause early-onset familial Alzheimer's disease alters the production or stickiness of beta-amyloid. People with Down syndrome, who carry a third copy of chromosome 21 and hence of the APP gene, make extra beta-amyloid and almost all develop plaques and tangles by the age of 40. And in 2012 a rare variant of the APP gene that reduces beta-amyloid production was found in Iceland to protect against the disease, a natural experiment pointing in the same direction. The case against is also strong, and Pinel spells it out. Many elderly people carry heavy loads of plaques without any dementia (the "high-plaque normals"). Plaque load tracks symptoms poorly. And in the decade before the 2020s one anti-amyloid drug after another cleared plaques or lowered amyloid in animals and then failed in patients. The modern position is a softened cascade: beta-amyloid (probably the soluble oligomers rather than the plaques) is an early and necessary trigger, but tau is the agent that does the damage, and inflammation by the brain's immune cells, the microglia, and vascular disease shape how fast it happens. Pinel also describes the newer pathogenic spread hypothesis: misfolded tau or beta-amyloid "seeds" the misfolding of normal copies of the protein, which then spread from neuron to neuron along connected pathways, much as prions do (Section 10.10.7). That idea fits the orderly Braak sequence remarkably well.

Exam trap

Plaques are outside neurons and made of beta-amyloid; tangles are inside neurons and made of hyperphosphorylated tau. Do not swap them. The pathology starts in the entorhinal cortex and hippocampus (medial temporal lobe), not in the frontal lobe (frontotemporal disorder), the substantia nigra (Parkinson's) or the caudate (Huntington's). Lewy bodies (alpha-synuclein) and Pick bodies (tau) belong to other diseases (Section 10.10).

Genes, risk and reserve

Alzheimer's disease comes in two genetic forms. The rare early-onset familial Alzheimer's disease is inherited as an autosomal dominant trait: a child of an affected parent has a one-in-two chance of carrying the mutation, and a carrier will almost certainly develop the disease, often in the forties or fifties. Three genes are responsible, and they map neatly onto the cascade in Figure 10.17: APP on chromosome 21 (the first found, in 1991), presenilin 1 (PSEN1) on chromosome 14 (1995, the commonest of the three) and presenilin 2 (PSEN2) on chromosome 1 (also 1995). DSM-5-TR summarises them as the rare Mendelian mutations on chromosomes 1, 14 and 21. Familial early-onset disease is rare, and Pinel notes that these mutations contribute little (about 1 percent) to the common late-onset form; they matter far more for what they have taught about mechanism than for the number of patients.

The common late-onset form is not inherited in that simple way, but it is still strongly genetic: DSM-5-TR puts the genetic share of the risk at 60 to 80 percent, spread across more than 45 risk genes of small effect and one of large effect. That one is the gene for apolipoprotein E (APOE), a cholesterol-carrying protein, on chromosome 19. The gene comes in three common forms (alleles), e2, e3 and e4. In 1993 a group at Duke University led by Allen Roses showed that the e4 allele is overrepresented in late-onset Alzheimer's disease. Carrying one copy raises the risk roughly threefold, and carrying two copies raises it much more (estimates range from about eight to fifteen times, depending on the study and the population), while also bringing onset forward by several years. The e2 allele is mildly protective. APOE e4 appears to bind beta-amyloid and slow its clearance from the brain, encouraging plaques. But e4 is a risk factor, not a cause: many carriers, even some with two copies, live to great age without dementia, and many patients carry no e4 at all. DSM-5-TR is therefore clear that APOE testing is not a diagnostic test; and it notes that the strength of the e4 effect differs between ethnic groups.

Down syndrome is the third genetic route. Because people with Down syndrome have three copies of chromosome 21, they have three copies of the APP gene; Pinel reports that by the age of 40 almost all have numerous plaques and tangles, and by 60 about two-thirds have developed dementia. Chapter 3, Section 3.3.2 teaches Down syndrome itself. Table 10.39 brings the genes together.

Table 10.39 The genetics of Alzheimer's disease.
Gene or conditionChromosomeType of effectTypical onset
Amyloid precursor protein (APP)21Autosomal dominant mutation (rare)Forties to fifties
Presenilin 1 (PSEN1)14Autosomal dominant; commonest familial geneCan be as early as the thirties
Presenilin 2 (PSEN2)1Autosomal dominant (rare)Forties to sixties
