Apolipoprotein E e4 (APOE)19Risk factor: raises risk and lowers age at onset; neither necessary nor sufficientLate onset, earlier in carriers
Down syndrome (trisomy 21)21 (extra copy)Extra APP gene dosePlaques by 40; dementia in about two-thirds by 60

Beyond genes, the risk of dementia is shaped by a whole life. The Lancet Commission on dementia prevention, an international panel led by Gill Livingston, has tried three times to estimate how much dementia could in principle be prevented. In 2017 it named nine modifiable risk factors accounting for about 35 percent of dementia; in 2020 it named twelve, accounting for about 40 percent; and in its 2024 report it named fourteen, accounting for nearly half. Grouped by stage of life, the fourteen are: in early life, less education; in midlife, hearing loss, high LDL cholesterol, depression, traumatic brain injury, physical inactivity, diabetes, smoking, high blood pressure, obesity and excessive alcohol; and in later life, social isolation, air pollution and untreated loss of vision (the two factors added in 2024 were high LDL cholesterol and vision loss). These figures concern dementia of all causes, not Alzheimer's disease alone, and they assume that associations found in observational studies are causal, which is the main criticism of them. Still, their message is a hopeful one: a large share of dementia may be postponed by things a society can change.

Why should schooling protect a brain against plaques? The best answer is the idea of cognitive reserve, developed especially by Yaakov Stern: education, mentally demanding work and a rich mental life build extra neural capacity and more flexible ways of using it, so that more damage must accumulate before symptoms show. Barlow and Durand describe the telling consequence. Better-educated people develop symptoms later, but once symptoms appear they decline faster, which suggests that education does not stop the disease but buys a period of normal functioning while the pathology quietly advances. In India a clinic-based study from Hyderabad (Suvarna Alladi and colleagues, 2013) found that people who spoke two languages developed dementia about four and a half years later than those who spoke one, another possible source of reserve.

Key study: the Nun Study (Snowdon and colleagues, 1996)

The epidemiologist David Snowdon studied elderly American nuns of the School Sisters of Notre Dame, women who had lived remarkably similar lives (no smoking, similar diet, housing and work), which removes many of the confounds that plague studies of ordinary populations. The sisters agreed to yearly cognitive testing and to donate their brains. Snowdon's team found the short autobiographies that 93 of them had written as young women joining the order, at an average age of 22, and scored each for idea density, the number of distinct ideas packed into every ten words. About 58 years later, the sisters whose youthful writing had been low in idea density scored worse on cognitive tests; and among the 14 who had died and been examined at autopsy, Alzheimer's disease was found in all of those with low idea density and in none of those with high idea density. The criticism is the size of the autopsy sample, and whether low idea density was a cause (less reserve) or a very early sign of a brain already vulnerable. The study also found sisters whose brains were full of plaques and tangles but who had shown little dementia in life, which is the reserve idea in its purest form.

Exam trap

APOE is on chromosome 19; APP is on 21; presenilin 1 on 14; presenilin 2 on 1. APOE e4 is a susceptibility gene for late-onset disease, not a cause of early-onset familial disease and not a diagnostic test. Early Alzheimer's in Down syndrome is explained by the extra APP gene on the extra chromosome 21. Huntington's disease (chromosome 4) is the textbook single-gene dementia, not Alzheimer's.

Making the diagnosis

For most of the twentieth century a diagnosis of Alzheimer's disease in life was a matter of probability, confirmed only by the microscope after death. It is still made mainly at the bedside, by establishing three things. First, that there is a neurocognitive disorder: a history of decline taken from someone who knows the person well (patients with Alzheimer's disease often underestimate their problems), a mental state examination, and cognitive testing. The brief screening tests are taught in the Assessment book: the Mini-Mental State Examination (Marshal Folstein, Susan Folstein and Paul McHugh, 1975), a 30-point test of orientation, registration, attention, recall, language and copying; the Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA; Ziad Nasreddine and colleagues, 2005), more sensitive to mild impairment; and Indian adaptations for people with little schooling, such as the Hindi Mental State Examination. Second, that the pattern fits Alzheimer's disease: insidious onset, memory first, steady progression. Third, that nothing else explains it better, which is why every patient needs a physical and neurological examination, blood tests for the treatable mimics (thyroid function, vitamin B12, syphilis serology, HIV where relevant, glucose, electrolytes, liver and kidney function) and usually a brain scan (Section 10.10.9).

DSM-5-TR turns this reasoning into criteria, and it is worth understanding why each rule is there. The general criteria for major or mild neurocognitive disorder must be met (Section 10.8). The onset must be insidious and the progression gradual, in at least one cognitive domain for mild disorder and at least two for major disorder; this excludes the sudden onset of stroke and the acute onset of delirium. The clinician must then grade the certainty. Probable Alzheimer's disease is diagnosed if there is evidence of a causative gene mutation (from genetic testing or family history), or, for major disorder, if all three of the following are present: clear decline in learning and memory and in at least one other domain; a steadily progressive decline without extended plateaus; and no evidence of a mixed cause, such as cerebrovascular disease or another neurodegenerative disease. Otherwise the diagnosis is possible Alzheimer's disease. For mild neurocognitive disorder the bar is higher: without a known mutation only "possible" is allowed, because at the mild stage it is not yet certain that the deficits will progress. Finally the disturbance must not be better explained by cerebrovascular disease, another neurodegenerative disease, a substance, or another mental or medical disorder. A specifier records whether behavioural disturbance is present. ICD-10 codes the same condition as F00 (with F00.0 early onset, F00.1 late onset and F00.2 atypical or mixed), and ICD-11 as 6D80, dementia due to Alzheimer disease.

Technology is steadily turning "probable" into something closer to certain. Structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) shows hippocampal and temporoparietal atrophy and, just as important, rules out tumours, subdural haematomas, strokes and hydrocephalus. Positron emission tomography (PET) with a glucose tracer shows reduced metabolism in the temporal and parietal lobes. Amyloid PET and tau PET make the plaques and tangles visible in a living person, and the cerebrospinal fluid of a patient with Alzheimer's disease shows low beta-amyloid 42 (because it is trapped in plaques) and raised total tau and phosphorylated tau (released from dying neurons). DSM-5-TR predicted that blood tests would follow, and they have: plasma measures of phosphorylated tau now detect Alzheimer pathology with useful accuracy, and in May 2025 the United States Food and Drug Administration (FDA) cleared the first blood test to help diagnose it. These biomarkers have pushed some researchers to define Alzheimer's disease biologically, by the presence of amyloid and tau whether or not there are symptoms, a move that remains controversial because many people with positive biomarkers never become demented.

The differential diagnosis of an older person with failing memory runs through the rest of this chapter: delirium, which is acute and fluctuating with disturbed attention (Section 10.8); depression presenting as dementia, the "pseudodementia" of Section 10.11; the other neurocognitive disorders of Section 10.10; the medical causes of Section 10.11; and, at the mildest end, normal ageing (Section 10.9.8). Mixed cases are common in the very old, and when Alzheimer's disease and vascular disease both contribute DSM-5-TR asks for both diagnoses (or for neurocognitive disorder due to multiple etiologies).

Exam trap

The definitive diagnosis of Alzheimer's disease has traditionally been neuropathological (plaques and tangles at autopsy or biopsy); clinical criteria give "probable" or "possible". A normal CT scan does not exclude Alzheimer's disease, and a scan showing atrophy does not prove it. The MMSE and MoCA screen for impairment; they do not diagnose its cause.

Treatment: what the drugs can and cannot do

There is no cure for Alzheimer's disease, and every treatment available today either eases symptoms for a while or slows the decline a little. The first class of drugs grew straight out of the cholinergic hypothesis. If the cholinergic neurons are dying, the acetylcholine that the survivors release can be made to last longer by blocking the enzyme that breaks it down in the synapse, acetylcholinesterase. The cholinesterase inhibitors do exactly that. The first, tacrine, was approved in the United States in 1993 but caused liver damage and fell out of use. It was followed by donepezil (1996), rivastigmine (which also blocks a second enzyme, butyrylcholinesterase, and is available as a skin patch) and galantamine (originally derived from the snowdrop plant). They are used in mild to moderate disease (donepezil in severe disease as well) and, as you will see, in Lewy body dementia and Parkinson's disease dementia. Their benefit is real but modest: on average a small improvement or a temporary halt on cognitive scales, small gains in daily function and behaviour, and then the decline resumes, because the drugs do nothing to the disease process. Their side effects are those of extra acetylcholine: nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, loss of appetite and weight, slowing of the heart and fainting, muscle cramps and vivid dreams.

The second class acts on glutamate. Excessive stimulation of the NMDA type of glutamate receptor is thought to damage neurons (excitotoxicity), and memantine, a low-affinity NMDA receptor antagonist, dampens that over-activity without blocking the normal signals needed for learning. It is used in moderate to severe Alzheimer's disease, alone or together with a cholinesterase inhibitor, and is generally well tolerated (dizziness, headache, constipation, sometimes confusion). Like the cholinesterase inhibitors, it treats symptoms only.

The third class is new, and it is the first that attacks the disease itself: monoclonal antibodies that bind beta-amyloid and help the brain's immune cells clear it. The story has had three acts. Aducanumab received accelerated approval from the FDA in June 2021, on the strength of its ability to remove amyloid rather than clear evidence of clinical benefit; the decision was fiercely criticised, other regulators refused it, and its maker discontinued it in 2024. Lecanemab followed (FDA approval in 2023): in its main trial, in about 1,800 people with mild cognitive impairment or mild dementia due to Alzheimer's disease, it slowed decline on a dementia rating scale by about 27 percent over 18 months, roughly half a point on an 18-point scale. Donanemab was approved by the FDA in 2024, after a trial in which decline was about 35 percent slower over about a year and a half. The price of these benefits is a side effect called amyloid-related imaging abnormalities (ARIA): swelling of the brain (oedema) and tiny bleeds (microhaemorrhages) seen on MRI. They are usually silent but occasionally cause confusion, seizures or death, and they are most frequent in people with two copies of APOE e4; in the donanemab trial ARIA was seen in 36.8 percent of treated patients against 14.9 percent on placebo. So the drugs are given only in the early stages, only to people whose amyloid has been confirmed by PET or cerebrospinal fluid, by repeated intravenous infusion, with scheduled MRI scans.

As of late 2026, as far as can be verified, regulators remain divided. In the European Union lecanemab was authorised in April 2025 and donanemab in September 2025, both restricted to people with one or no copy of APOE e4 because of the ARIA risk. In the United Kingdom the medicines regulator approved both in 2024, but the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence ruled in 2025 that their modest benefit did not justify their cost for routine National Health Service use; the manufacturers' appeals succeeded on several procedural points in early 2026, and the decision was sent back to NICE's committee to reconsider. In India the Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) approved donanemab in November 2025 and it was launched in May 2026, and lecanemab was launched in India in 2026 after CDSCO authorisation. Against this, a Cochrane systematic review published in April 2026, pooling 17 trials with more than 20,000 participants, judged the effect of anti-amyloid antibodies on cognition and dementia severity to be trivial and confirmed the raised risk of ARIA; its critics, including the manufacturers, objected that it pooled failed drugs with the two that succeeded. For the examination, remember four things: they remove amyloid; they slow early disease modestly; ARIA is their main danger, especially in APOE e4 homozygotes; and they have no place in moderate or severe dementia. Table 10.40 summarises the drugs.

Table 10.40 Drugs for Alzheimer's disease.
DrugHow it worksStage usedMain problems
Donepezil, rivastigmine, galantamineCholinesterase inhibitors: raise acetylcholine at surviving synapsesMild to moderate (donepezil also severe); also Lewy body and Parkinson's dementiaNausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, weight loss, slow pulse, fainting, vivid dreams
MemantineLow-affinity NMDA glutamate receptor antagonist: reduces excitotoxicityModerate to severe; can be combinedDizziness, headache, constipation, confusion
Lecanemab, donanemabMonoclonal antibodies that clear beta-amyloidMild cognitive impairment or mild dementia with confirmed amyloidARIA (brain swelling, microbleeds), infusions, MRI monitoring, cost; benefit modest
AducanumabAnti-amyloid antibodyAccelerated approval 2021Benefit unproven; discontinued 2024

Much of the work of treatment is not with the memory but with the behaviour. When a person with dementia becomes agitated, aggressive or sleepless, the first step is to look for a cause: pain, constipation, a urinary infection, a new drug, delirium, hunger, boredom or a frightening environment. Non-drug approaches come first (a predictable routine, daylight and activity, simple calm communication, and training for carers), and they are taught in Book 2, Section 10.7.4 on agitation and wandering. Depression is treated with an antidepressant such as sertraline or citalopram; drugs with anticholinergic effects (older tricyclics, many antihistamines and bladder drugs) and benzodiazepines worsen confusion and falls and are avoided. Antipsychotics help severe agitation or distressing psychosis a little, but they increase the risk of stroke and death in people with dementia, a danger the FDA has warned about since 2005, so they are used only when there is real risk, at low dose and for as short a time as possible; in 2023 the FDA approved brexpiprazole specifically for agitation in Alzheimer's dementia. Psychological and social approaches to the person (cognitive stimulation, reality orientation, reminiscence and validation therapy) are in Book 2, Section 10.7.3, and support for the family caregiver, whose own health suffers badly, is in Book 2, Section 10.7.2.

Clinical link

Diagnosis early in the disease is also the moment for planning while the person can still decide: who will manage money and property, whether driving should stop, and what treatment the person would or would not want later. In India the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 allows an adult to make an advance directive about mental health care and to appoint a nominated representative; capacity must be judged for each decision, and a person with mild dementia often retains it for many of them. The family is nearly always the care system, so a clinical psychologist's work with a patient with Alzheimer's disease is usually also work with the spouse, son or daughter-in-law who provides the care.

Exam trap

Cholinesterase inhibitors and memantine are symptomatic treatments; they do not stop the disease. Memantine is an NMDA antagonist, not a cholinergic drug. Only the anti-amyloid antibodies remove the pathology, and only in early disease. Antipsychotics in dementia carry a raised risk of stroke and death, and in Lewy body dementia they can be catastrophic (Section 10.10.4).

Normal ageing, mild cognitive impairment and mild neurocognitive disorder

Every older person forgets names, and every forgetful older person who reads about Alzheimer's disease wonders whether it has begun. Where does normal ageing end? Normal ageing slows the speed of processing and makes retrieval less efficient: names come to mind later, the reason for entering a room is momentarily lost, and new learning takes more repetitions. But the information is not lost. Cues and recognition bring it back, new things can still be learned, and everyday life goes on unimpaired. In 1962 the Canadian psychiatrist V. A. Kral drew the distinction that clinicians still use, between benign senescent forgetfulness, in which a person forgets details of an experience but remembers the experience itself and knows that he is forgetting, and a "malignant" forgetfulness in which whole recent events vanish, which often led on to dementia.

In 1999 Ronald Petersen and colleagues at the Mayo Clinic gave the in-between state a name and a definition that stuck. They compared 76 people with what they called mild cognitive impairment (MCI) with 234 healthy older people and 106 patients with mild Alzheimer's disease. People with MCI complained of poor memory (ideally confirmed by someone who knew them), had memory test scores clearly below what was expected for their age and education, but had otherwise normal general cognition and essentially normal daily activities, and were not demented. Followed over time, they declined faster than the healthy controls but more slowly than the patients with Alzheimer's disease: they were, in effect, in between. In Petersen's clinic samples about 10 to 15 percent of people with MCI progressed to dementia each year, against about 1 to 2 percent of healthy older people. Petersen later divided MCI into amnestic MCI, in which memory is the main problem and which usually leads to Alzheimer's disease, and non-amnestic MCI, affecting language, attention or executive or visuospatial function, which more often leads to frontotemporal, Lewy body or vascular disease. Not everyone with MCI gets worse: a sizeable share, especially in community rather than clinic samples, stays stable or returns to normal, often because the impairment was caused by depression, medication, poor sleep or a medical illness. That is the reason MCI is never treated as a diagnosis of early dementia.

DSM-5 absorbed this concept into mild neurocognitive disorder: a modest decline from a previous level in one or more cognitive domains, shown by concern from the person, an informant or the clinician and by test performance typically between one and two standard deviations below the norm (Section 10.8), with independence in everyday activities preserved, although the person may need more effort, compensatory strategies or accommodation. It is broader than MCI, since it covers mild decline of any cause (mild neurocognitive disorder due to Alzheimer's disease, to vascular disease, to traumatic brain injury and so on), and it is the stage at which DSM-5-TR allows only "possible" Alzheimer's disease without a known mutation. ICD-10 had a rough equivalent in F06.7, mild cognitive disorder, and ICD-11 lists mild neurocognitive disorder alongside dementia. A still earlier state, subjective cognitive decline, describes people who feel their memory is worse but perform normally on tests; their risk of later decline is slightly raised, but many are simply anxious or depressed. Table 10.41 sets the three states side by side.

Table 10.41 Normal ageing, mild cognitive impairment and dementia due to Alzheimer's disease.
FeatureNormal ageingMild cognitive impairment (mild NCD)Dementia (major NCD)
MemorySlower recall of names and details; cues and recognition helpClearly below age norms, usually recent memoryRecent events lost entirely; cues help little
Other domainsSlower processing onlyNormal or mildly affectedTwo or more domains impaired
AwarenessAware and often worriedUsually awareInsight reduced or lost
Daily lifeIndependentIndependent, with more effort or aidsNeeds help; independence lost
CourseStableAbout 10 to 15 percent a year progress (clinic samples); some revertProgressive

What should be done for someone with MCI? The honest answer is: look for and treat anything reversible, treat vascular risk factors and hearing loss, encourage exercise and mental and social activity, review medicines, and follow the person up. Drugs have disappointed here. In a large trial in 2005 Petersen's group found that neither donepezil nor vitamin E reduced the rate of progression from amnestic MCI to Alzheimer's disease over three years, although donepezil had a brief early effect. The anti-amyloid antibodies are now licensed for MCI that is shown by biomarkers to be due to Alzheimer's disease, with the limits described in Section 10.9.7.

Exam trap

Mild cognitive impairment (Petersen, 1999) and DSM-5's mild neurocognitive disorder are not dementia: the defining difference is that independence in daily activities is preserved. The usual quoted conversion rate is 10 to 15 percent per year (against 1 to 2 percent in healthy older people), so most people with MCI have not become demented after a year, and some return to normal. Benign senescent forgetfulness (Kral, 1962) is normal ageing, not MCI.

10.10 The Other Neurocognitive DisordersMedium yield · 3 Qs

Alzheimer's disease accounts for perhaps half to two-thirds of dementia; the rest is a crowd of other causes, and the papers love to make you tell them apart. DSM-5-TR lists the named causes after "major or mild neurocognitive disorder due to": Alzheimer's disease, frontotemporal degeneration, Lewy body disease, vascular disease, traumatic brain injury, substance or medication use, HIV infection, prion disease, Parkinson's disease, Huntington's disease, another medical condition, multiple etiologies, and unspecified. You will not remember them as a list. You will remember them as patterns, by asking five questions of every patient. How did it start, suddenly or creeping? How has it moved since, steadily, in steps, up and down from day to day, or frighteningly fast? What failed first: memory, behaviour, language or attention? Are there signs in the body, such as a tremor, a jerk, a weak arm or a stiff walk? And how old was the person when it began? Each disorder in this section has its own answers to those five questions, and Table 10.45 at the end lines them up. First, though, one word that the papers use as a trap.

What makes a disease neurodegenerative

A neurodegenerative disease is one in which neurons progressively lose their structure and function and die, usually in a characteristic set of brain regions and usually because a particular protein misfolds and accumulates. The person does not recover between bad patches; the disease moves only one way. The diseases are now often grouped by the protein that accumulates: beta-amyloid and tau in Alzheimer's disease; tau or a protein called TDP-43 in frontotemporal degeneration; alpha-synuclein in Parkinson's disease, Lewy body dementia and multiple system atrophy (together called the synucleinopathies); mutant huntingtin in Huntington's disease; the prion protein in Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease; and, in most cases, TDP-43 in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. The pathogenic spread hypothesis that you met in Section 10.9.4 proposes that in all of them a misfolded protein seeds the misfolding of its normal neighbours and spreads along connected pathways.

Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), described by the French neurologist Jean-Martin Charcot (1825 to 1893) in 1869 and known in America as Lou Gehrig's disease after the baseball player it killed, is the main motor neuron disease: the upper and lower motor neurons degenerate, producing weakness, wasting, twitching of muscles (fasciculations) and stiffness, until the muscles of breathing fail, usually within a few years. Its sufferers were long thought to keep their minds intact, and many do, but a substantial minority develop behavioural and cognitive change, and some develop full frontotemporal dementia; the two diseases share pathology (TDP-43) and a common gene, as Section 10.10.3 explains.

Now look at two diseases that are not neurodegenerative. Epilepsy, Pinel explains, is diagnosed in people whose seizures are repeatedly generated by their own chronic brain dysfunction; about 0.8 percent of people are diagnosed with it at some point. Its defining feature is not the death of neurons but paroxysms: sudden bursts of excessive, synchronous firing (seen as spikes on the electroencephalogram) that begin and end, with the brain often working normally in between. Many cases arise from faults at inhibitory synapses. A very prolonged seizure can damage the brain, through the hypoxia that accompanies it, and epilepsy can complicate neurodegenerative diseases, but epilepsy itself is a disorder of excitability, not of progressive neuronal loss. Its psychiatric syndromes are taught in Section 10.11. Multiple sclerosis (MS) is a progressive disease that attacks the myelin of axons in the central nervous system. It is usually regarded as an autoimmune disorder in which the immune system attacks myelin as if it were foreign; it begins typically in early adulthood, is commoner in women and in people who grew up in cold climates, and in most patients runs a relapsing and remitting course before becoming steadily progressive. Visual disturbance, weakness, numbness, tremor and ataxia are its common signs, and some patients develop cognitive slowing and emotional change. Pinel notes that neurons are damaged in MS even without demyelination, so it has a degenerative component; but it is classified as an inflammatory demyelinating disease, not as a neurodegenerative one. A stroke, similarly, is a vascular injury, sudden rather than progressive, which leads us to the second commonest cause of dementia.

Exam trap

"Which is NOT a neurodegenerative disorder: Parkinson's, Alzheimer's, epilepsy, Huntington's?" The answer is epilepsy, a paroxysmal disorder of neuronal firing. Neurodegenerative: Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, Huntington's, frontotemporal degeneration, Lewy body disease, ALS, progressive supranuclear palsy, prion disease. Multiple sclerosis is an inflammatory demyelinating (autoimmune) disease and is the next-best "not neurodegenerative" answer. Neither MS nor schizophrenia is the disorder of severe memory loss and confusion caused by chronic drinking: that is Korsakoff's syndrome (Section 10.11).

Vascular neurocognitive disorder

For the first half of the twentieth century the usual explanation for the confusion of old age was "hardening of the arteries": narrowed vessels were supposed to starve the whole brain slowly of blood, producing arteriosclerotic dementia. In 1974 the neurologist Vladimir Hachinski, with Niels Lassen and John Marshall, argued in The Lancet that this picture was wrong. Measurements of cerebral blood flow showed that blood flow falls in dementia because brain tissue has died, not the other way round; what destroys the brain is not a slow starvation but the cumulative damage of many strokes, large and small. They called the result multi-infarct dementia, and their idea explains why vascular dementia so often behaves differently from Alzheimer's disease. A year later Hachinski's group published a bedside checklist, the Hachinski Ischaemic Score, that weights the features of a vascular cause: abrupt onset, stepwise deterioration, a fluctuating course, nocturnal confusion, relative preservation of personality, depression, bodily complaints, emotional incontinence, a history of high blood pressure, a history of strokes, signs of atherosclerosis elsewhere, and focal neurological symptoms and signs. A total of 7 or more suggests a vascular cause and 4 or less a degenerative one (the scale itself belongs to the Assessment book). Today the broader term vascular neurocognitive disorder covers every way in which disease of the blood vessels can damage cognition.

Here is what that looks like. Shyam is a 71-year-old cloth merchant in Kanpur with high blood pressure that he treats only when he remembers, diabetes and a forty-year bidi habit. Two years ago his right hand went weak for a day and recovered; nobody thought much of it. Eight months ago, after what his son calls "a turn", he became muddled and slow, and he has never quite returned to his old self. Since then there has been another sudden worsening, after which he began to walk with small shuffling steps and to leak urine. His family describe good days and bad days. He is slow to answer, has lost interest in the shop and weeps at sentimental television scenes and then laughs a moment later, but he knows that his memory is not what it was and is upset about it. His memory is patchy rather than uniformly poor: he recalls the cricket scores perfectly while forgetting that his daughter visited. On examination his speech is slightly slurred, the reflexes on his right side are brisk and his right plantar response is extensor. An MRI scan shows several small deep infarcts (lacunes) and widespread damage to the white matter.

Every feature in that history points to a vascular cause. The onset was abrupt, linked in time to a vascular event. The course went down in steps, with plateaus and even partial recovery in between (the dashed line of Figure 10.16). The deficits were patchy, sometimes called "islands" of preserved function, because they depend on where the infarcts happened to fall. Personality and insight were relatively preserved, unlike in Alzheimer's or frontotemporal disease. And there were focal neurological signs. Emotional lability of the kind Shyam shows (sudden crying or laughing out of proportion to feeling, sometimes called pseudobulbar affect or emotional incontinence) is typical of damage to the pathways between the cortex and the brainstem. Depression and apathy are very common; late-onset depression with slowing and poor executive function in an older person with small vessel disease has been called "vascular depression" (Chapter 5).

DSM-5-TR's criteria for major or mild vascular neurocognitive disorder capture this reasoning. The criteria for a neurocognitive disorder must be met. The clinical features must be consistent with a vascular cause, shown either by an onset of cognitive deficits linked in time to one or more cerebrovascular events, or by a decline that is most prominent in complex attention (including processing speed) and frontal-executive function, the signature of small vessel disease. There must be evidence of cerebrovascular disease, from history, examination or neuroimaging, sufficient to account for the deficits. And the picture must not be better explained by another brain or systemic disease. The diagnosis is probable if imaging shows significant tissue damage from cerebrovascular disease, or the syndrome is clearly linked in time to documented strokes, or there is both clinical and genetic evidence; otherwise it is possible. As a rough guide, DSM-5-TR says that a single stroke or extensive white matter disease is usually enough for mild vascular neurocognitive disorder, whereas major disorder generally needs two or more strokes, a strategically placed stroke, or white matter disease combined with one or more lacunes.

The clinical forms differ with the size and site of the damage. Multi-infarct (cortical) vascular dementia follows repeated strokes in the territories of larger arteries and produces the classic stepwise course with aphasia, neglect or other cortical signs depending on the territories hit. A strategic infarct is a single stroke in a critical place, such as the thalamus, the angular gyrus or the basal forebrain, which can produce dementia on its own. Post-stroke neurocognitive disorder appears immediately after a stroke; DSM-5-TR notes that 20 to 30 percent of people are diagnosed with dementia within three months of a stroke. And subcortical ischaemic vascular disease, or small vessel disease, follows the narrowing of the tiny penetrating arteries that supply the white matter, basal ganglia and thalamus, usually in people with long-standing high blood pressure or diabetes. Its extreme form was described in 1894 by the Swiss-born psychiatrist Otto Binswanger (1852 to 1929), professor at Jena in Germany, as a slowly progressive dementia with severe damage to the white matter beneath the cortex; it is known as Binswanger's disease or subcortical arteriosclerotic encephalopathy. Small vessel disease produces a slower, more gradual decline than multi-infarct dementia, with slowed thinking, apathy, executive failure, a shuffling gait and urinary problems, and it can look very much like Alzheimer's disease or like normal pressure hydrocephalus (Section 10.10.9). A rare hereditary form, cerebral autosomal dominant arteriopathy with subcortical infarcts and leukoencephalopathy (CADASIL), caused by mutations in the NOTCH3 gene on chromosome 19, brings migraine, strokes and dementia to people in their forties and fifties. Leakage from vessels whose walls are infiltrated by amyloid (cerebral amyloid angiopathy) causes haemorrhages and is another vascular route to dementia.

Vascular disease is the second commonest cause of neurocognitive disorder after Alzheimer's disease. Its prevalence rises steeply after 65 and its risk factors are simply those of stroke: high blood pressure, diabetes, smoking, obesity, high cholesterol, atrial fibrillation and other sources of emboli. DSM-5-TR notes higher rates among African Americans, Mexican Americans and South Asian Americans than among non-Latinx White Americans, possibly because of their higher rates of diabetes and cardiovascular disease, which makes vascular cognitive impairment a large and largely preventable problem in India. In the very old, pure vascular dementia is less common than mixed dementia, in which Alzheimer pathology and vascular damage add together; when both contribute, both diagnoses are made. Treatment is above all prevention of the next stroke: control of blood pressure and diabetes, stopping smoking, antiplatelet drugs or anticoagulation where indicated, statins, exercise, and rehabilitation of the deficits already present. Cholinesterase inhibitors give at best a small benefit.

Exam trap

Vascular dementia: abrupt onset, stepwise course, patchy deficits, focal neurological signs, relatively preserved personality and insight, vascular risk factors. Alzheimer's: insidious onset and smooth decline, no focal signs early. Multi-infarct dementia was named by Hachinski and colleagues (1974); Binswanger's disease (1894) is the subcortical, small vessel form. Vascular dementia does not explain confabulation and nystagmus in a heavy drinker; that is Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome (Section 10.11).

Frontotemporal neurocognitive disorder

In 1892 Arnold Pick (1851 to 1924), professor of psychiatry in Prague, described a patient with behavioural change and a progressive loss of language whose brain showed shrinkage that was confined, asymmetrically, to the left temporal lobe. The point of his report was that a degenerative dementia need not be global: atrophy limited to one lobe could produce symptoms limited to that lobe's functions. Pick reported further cases over the next years, some with marked frontal atrophy and disinhibited behaviour. In 1911 Alzheimer described what Pick had not seen, the microscopic signature of many of these brains: dense, round, silver-staining inclusions inside the neurons, now called Pick bodies and known to be made of tau, together with swollen, "ballooned" neurons. For decades the whole syndrome was called Pick's disease. Today the clinical syndrome is called frontotemporal dementia (DSM-5-TR: frontotemporal neurocognitive disorder), the underlying pathology frontotemporal lobar degeneration, and "Pick's disease" is reserved for the subtype with Pick bodies. ICD-10 coded the condition as F02.0, dementia in Pick's disease; ICD-11 has a separate category, 6D83, frontotemporal dementia.

Frontotemporal dementia is a disease of the middle-aged. DSM-5-TR says that it most commonly presents in the fifties (the range runs from the twenties to the eighties), that it is a common cause of dementia beginning before 65, and that only 20 to 25 percent of cases begin after 65. It accounts for about 5 percent of all dementia in unselected autopsy series. It runs faster than Alzheimer's disease, with a median survival of 6 to 11 years from the first symptoms. It is strongly familial: about 40 percent of patients have a family history of early-onset dementia and about 10 percent show autosomal dominant inheritance. The main genes are MAPT, which codes for tau, and GRN (progranulin), both on chromosome 17, and C9orf72 on chromosome 9, an expanded repeat in which, discovered in 2011, is the commonest genetic cause both of frontotemporal dementia and of ALS. That shared gene, and the shared protein TDP-43, explain why some patients have both diseases. Under the microscope about 40 percent of cases show tau inclusions (Pick's disease among them), about half show TDP-43, and a small remainder show another protein, FUS.

The commonest presentation, in about 60 percent of cases, is the behavioural variant. Balwinder is a 54-year-old bank officer in Amritsar whose wife brought him to the psychiatric outpatient department. Over eighteen months the careful, rather formal man she married had become tactless and crude. He made sexual remarks to women customers, told jokes at a funeral and helped himself to food from other people's plates at a wedding. He spent a large part of their savings on lottery tickets. He now eats whole boxes of jalebis at a sitting and has put on ten kilograms. When his mother was admitted to hospital he showed no concern and asked when dinner would be ready. He insists on walking the same route to the gurdwara at exactly the same time each day and has filled a cupboard with old newspapers. He was suspended from work and is entirely unbothered by it. A doctor had diagnosed mania, but he had never been elated, he slept normally and lithium did nothing. Strikingly, he still remembers appointments, finds his way about the city without difficulty and scores 27 out of 30 on the Mini-Mental State Examination. An MRI scan shows atrophy of both frontal lobes and of the front of the temporal lobes.

DSM-5-TR's criteria explain every part of that story. The disorder must have an insidious onset and gradual progression. For the behavioural variant there must be three or more of five behavioural symptoms: disinhibition (socially inappropriate words and acts, impulsivity, loss of manners); apathy or inertia; loss of sympathy or empathy (coldness towards family that is quite unlike the person); perseverative, stereotyped or compulsive and ritualistic behaviour (repeated routes, hoarding, counting, fixed routines); and hyperorality and dietary changes (craving sweets, overeating, putting non-food objects in the mouth). There must also be a prominent decline in social cognition or executive ability. And, crucially, learning and memory and perceptual-motor function must be relatively spared: the frontal and anterior temporal lobes are damaged, but the hippocampus and the parietal lobes are not, at least at first. Insight is usually lost early, which is why the patient rarely comes on his own, and the first referral is often to a psychiatrist, because the picture can be mistaken for mania, depression, schizophrenia, a late-onset personality disorder or an ordinary midlife crisis. The diagnosis is probable if a causative mutation is found or if imaging shows disproportionate frontal or temporal involvement; otherwise it is possible.

The other presentation is the language variant, a form of primary progressive aphasia, the syndrome that the neurologist Marsel Mesulam described in 1982 as a slowly progressive loss of language without, for a long time, a general dementia. In the semantic variant, linked to atrophy of the anterior temporal lobes (usually more on the left), the person loses the meanings of words and eventually of objects: speech remains fluent and grammatical but grows empty, nouns are replaced by "thing", and the patient may ask "What is a tiger?" on being told about one. In the nonfluent or agrammatic variant, linked to atrophy of the left posterior frontal lobe and insula, speech becomes slow, effortful and halting, with grammatical errors and distorted sounds, while the understanding of single words is preserved. A third form, logopenic progressive aphasia, with word-finding pauses and difficulty repeating sentences, is usually caused by Alzheimer pathology rather than frontotemporal degeneration (Section 10.9.3). Frontotemporal degeneration also overlaps with two parkinsonian tau diseases, progressive supranuclear palsy and corticobasal degeneration (Section 10.10.5), and with motor neuron disease.

There is no disease-modifying treatment. Cholinesterase inhibitors do not help, because the cholinergic system is relatively intact, and they can make behaviour worse. Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors and trazodone can reduce disinhibition, compulsions and overeating; antipsychotics are used sparingly. Most of the work is with the family: explaining that the coldness and the rudeness are symptoms, protecting money and property early (as Balwinder's lottery spending shows), managing access to food, and supporting carers who are often still bringing up children and paying a mortgage.

Clinical link

A marked change of personality or conduct beginning for the first time in a person in his fifties, without a past psychiatric history, should make you think of a brain disease, frontotemporal dementia above all, before you settle on a primary psychiatric diagnosis. The clues are loss of insight, loss of empathy, new compulsions and a sweet tooth, with memory and orientation still good. Ordinary bedside cognitive screening tests can be almost normal; tests of executive function and social cognition, a careful history from the family, and frontal and temporal atrophy on a scan make the diagnosis.

Exam trap

Frontotemporal dementia: younger onset (often the fifties), personality and behaviour change or language loss first, memory and visuospatial skills relatively spared, insight lost early. Alzheimer's: older onset, memory first, social graces long preserved. Pick's disease (Arnold Pick, 1892; Pick bodies described by Alzheimer, 1911) is a frontotemporal tauopathy, not an alcohol-related or amnesic disorder, so it is wrong whenever the stem describes a heavy drinker with memory loss and confabulation. Logopenic aphasia points to Alzheimer pathology, not frontotemporal degeneration.

Neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies

In 1912 the young German neurologist Friedrich Heinrich Lewy (1885 to 1950), working in Alzheimer's laboratory in Munich, described round inclusions inside neurons deep in the brains of patients who had died with Parkinson's disease; a few years later they were named Lewy bodies after him, and they are now known to be clumps of the protein alpha-synuclein. For half a century they were regarded as a sign of Parkinson's disease. Then in 1976 the Japanese psychiatrist Kenji Kosaka described a patient with dementia whose Lewy bodies were scattered through the cerebral cortex, and in the following years he and others showed that this was not a rarity. In the 1990s an international consortium led by Ian McKeith of Newcastle agreed consensus criteria (first published in 1996, revised in 2005 and most recently in 2017) for what they named dementia with Lewy bodies, the condition DSM-5-TR calls major or mild neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies. When the American actor Robin Williams died in 2014, the autopsy found diffuse Lewy body disease, and his widow's account of his last confused, frightened year did much to make the disease known.

Paramjit is a 74-year-old retired nurse in Chandigarh. For a year she has seen children sitting quietly on the end of her bed and a man in a turban standing beside the almirah; she can describe their clothes in detail, they never speak, and on some days she knows they are not real. Her cognition swings dramatically: on a good morning she does the newspaper crossword, but on bad days she stares into space for long periods, is drowsy at noon and talks in a muddled way, so that her family twice took her to hospital fearing a stroke, and both times no cause was found. Her husband says that for years before any of this she shouted, kicked and punched in her sleep as if fighting someone. She now walks slowly with small steps, her face has become expressionless, and she has had several falls and two faints on standing up. When a doctor prescribed haloperidol for the hallucinations she became rigid, could not swallow and was admitted to hospital. Her memory for recent events is surprisingly good on her good days, but she cannot copy a drawing of two overlapping pentagons.

Paramjit's story contains the whole of DSM-5-TR's criteria. The disorder has an insidious onset and gradual progression, and its diagnosis rests on core and suggestive features. The three core features are: fluctuating cognition with pronounced variations in attention and alertness (so marked that it can resemble a delirium, as in her two hospital visits); recurrent visual hallucinations that are well formed and detailed (people, children and animals are typical, often silent, and the patient may have some insight into them); and spontaneous features of parkinsonism (slowness, rigidity, a shuffling gait; tremor is less prominent than in Parkinson's disease) beginning after the cognitive decline. The two suggestive features are REM sleep behaviour disorder, the acting out of dreams because the normal paralysis of REM sleep is lost (Chapter 8, Section 8.10.4), which can precede the dementia by many years; and severe neuroleptic sensitivity. A probable diagnosis needs two core features, or one suggestive feature with at least one core feature; a possible diagnosis needs one core feature, or one or more suggestive features. McKeith's 2017 revision differs slightly, and you should know both: it lists four core features (fluctuation, visual hallucinations, REM sleep behaviour disorder and parkinsonism), moves severe sensitivity to antipsychotics into a list of supportive features, and adds indicative biomarkers: reduced uptake of a dopamine transporter tracer in the basal ganglia on a SPECT or PET scan, reduced uptake on a cardiac scan (MIBG myocardial scintigraphy) showing loss of the heart's sympathetic nerves, and sleep-laboratory confirmation of REM sleep without the normal muscle paralysis.

The cognitive profile differs from Alzheimer's disease in a way that helps at the bedside. Attention, executive function and visuoperceptual ability (copying figures, judging shapes and distances) are affected early and severely, while memory and learning are relatively better preserved early on, and the hippocampi look relatively normal on an MRI scan. Associated features listed by DSM-5-TR include repeated falls, faints and brief episodes of unresponsiveness, autonomic failure (a fall in blood pressure on standing, constipation, urinary incontinence), excessive daytime sleepiness, reduced sense of smell (hyposmia), hallucinations in other senses, delusions, depression, anxiety and apathy. The disorder usually begins in the mid-seventies, is perhaps commoner in men, and progresses to death in an average of about 5.5 to 7.7 years after cognitive decline begins. In community studies it accounts for about 4 percent of dementia and in clinic studies about 7.5 percent, although Lewy bodies are found at autopsy in 20 to 35 percent of dementia cases, often alongside Alzheimer pathology.

Neuroleptic sensitivity deserves its own paragraph because it kills. In 1992 McKeith and colleagues reported in the British Medical Journal that patients with this form of dementia who were given antipsychotic drugs often reacted catastrophically: sudden severe parkinsonism, rigidity, immobility, worsening confusion, falls and inability to swallow, sometimes resembling neuroleptic malignant syndrome, with a marked rise in mortality. DSM-5-TR says that up to 50 percent of patients have severe sensitivity, so antipsychotics, especially older high-potency drugs such as haloperidol, must be avoided or used with extreme caution. The good news is that Lewy body dementia has an even greater cholinergic deficit than Alzheimer's disease, and cholinesterase inhibitors (rivastigmine, donepezil) often help more than they do in Alzheimer's, improving attention, fluctuations and even hallucinations. Levodopa can ease the parkinsonism but may worsen the hallucinations, so the doses are a balancing act; melatonin or clonazepam help the REM sleep behaviour disorder; and falls and low blood pressure need practical management.

Exam trap

Lewy body dementia is most characteristically associated with recurrent, well-formed visual hallucinations, not auditory ones (prominent auditory hallucinations suggest schizophrenia or a mood disorder). Aphasia belongs to Alzheimer's disease and the language variants of frontotemporal dementia. Loss of smell does occur, but only as a supportive, non-specific feature shared with Parkinson's and Alzheimer's disease. The other two hallmarks are fluctuating attention and alertness and parkinsonism, plus REM sleep behaviour disorder and severe sensitivity to antipsychotics.

Parkinson's disease and its neurocognitive disorder

In 1817 the London surgeon and apothecary James Parkinson (1755 to 1824) published An Essay on the Shaking Palsy, describing six people with "involuntary tremulous motion" and a stooped, hurrying gait, with "the senses and intellects being uninjured". The last phrase turned out to be wrong for many patients, which is why the disease belongs in this section. Pinel describes Parkinson's disease as a movement disorder of middle and old age affecting 1 to 2 percent of people over 65, slightly more often men. Its four motor signs are a tremor that is most marked at rest and disappears during voluntary movement and sleep (the "pill-rolling" tremor of the fingers), muscular rigidity, slowness and difficulty initiating movement (bradykinesia), and, later, unstable posture, together with a mask-like face. Sleep disturbance, loss of the sense of smell and depression can precede the motor signs by up to a decade. The core lesion is the degeneration of the dopamine-releasing neurons of the substantia nigra that project along the nigrostriatal pathway to the striatum, and the surviving neurons contain Lewy bodies of alpha-synuclein. Levodopa, the precursor from which the brain makes dopamine, relieves the symptoms for some years before its benefits fade and its side effects (involuntary movements) grow; dopamine agonists and, when drugs fail, deep brain stimulation of the subthalamic nucleus are the next steps. The motor system and the drugs belong to the Physiological book; here the interest is in the mind.

Parkinson's disease is full of psychiatry. Depression, anxiety and apathy are common and often precede the diagnosis. Psychosis, usually visual hallucinations or a sense that someone is present, appears as the disease advances, often provoked by the dopaminergic drugs themselves, and must be treated without the antipsychotics that block dopamine (quetiapine or clozapine at low dose, or pimavanserin, a drug acting on serotonin receptors). And dopamine agonists such as pramipexole and ropinirole cause impulse control disorders in a substantial minority of patients: pathological gambling, compulsive shopping, hypersexuality and binge eating appear in previously careful people and vanish when the drug is reduced. That observation is one of the strongest pieces of human evidence for the role of dopamine in the reward circuit (Section 10.2) and in gambling disorder (Section 10.1).

Cognitive decline is common. Pinel reports that most patients who live more than ten years after diagnosis develop dementia, and DSM-5-TR that as many as 80 percent eventually do; among those not demented, a quarter have mild neurocognitive disorder. The profile is that of a subcortical dementia (Section 10.10.10): slowed thinking, poor attention and executive function, visuospatial difficulty and trouble retrieving memories, with language relatively intact. DSM-5-TR's criteria for neurocognitive disorder due to Parkinson's disease require that the disturbance occurs in the setting of established Parkinson's disease and has an insidious onset and gradual progression; it is probable when there is no evidence of a mixed cause and the Parkinson's disease clearly came first, and possible when only one of those holds. Rivastigmine and donepezil help, and antipsychotic sensitivity is a danger here too.

Since Parkinson's disease dementia and Lewy body dementia share the same protein, the same Lewy bodies and many of the same symptoms, what separates them? Only the order of events, captured in the one-year rule of the Lewy body consensus criteria (Figure 10.18). If parkinsonism has been present for at least a year before dementia develops, the diagnosis is neurocognitive disorder due to Parkinson's disease. If dementia comes first, or at the same time, or within a year of the onset of parkinsonism, or without parkinsonism at all, the diagnosis is Lewy body dementia. DSM-5-TR notes that the Parkinson's disease experts allow cognitive decline to precede the motor diagnosis, so within that twelve-month overlap the clinician may choose either; outside it, both sets of experts agree. The rule is a convention, not a law of nature: many neurologists regard the two as points on a single spectrum of Lewy body disease. ICD-11 builds the same rule into its definition of dementia due to Lewy body disease.

Three other degenerative parkinsonian disorders bring cognitive change and are worth one line each. Progressive supranuclear palsy (a tauopathy) causes a palsy of vertical gaze, a stiff upright posture, early backward falls, slowed thinking and executive dysfunction; it was through this disease that the idea of a "subcortical dementia" became established (Section 10.10.10). Corticobasal degeneration (also a tauopathy) causes asymmetrical rigidity, apraxia of one limb and the "alien limb" that seems to act on its own. Multiple system atrophy (a synucleinopathy) causes parkinsonism with early, severe autonomic failure, and cognition is relatively spared. Finally, remember that antipsychotic drugs themselves cause parkinsonism, which must not be mistaken for a degenerative disease.

Exam trap

Parkinson's disease is the loss of dopamine neurons in the substantia nigra (Lewy bodies of alpha-synuclein), not an acetylcholine disease, although its dementia adds a cholinergic deficit. One-year rule: parkinsonism at least a year before dementia means Parkinson's disease dementia; dementia before, with or within a year of parkinsonism means Lewy body dementia. Parkinson's disease is not caused by alcohol and does not produce the profound amnesia of Korsakoff's syndrome. James Parkinson (1817) wrote that the intellect was "uninjured"; he was wrong for most long-surviving patients.

Huntington's disease

In 1872 a young general practitioner from East Hampton on Long Island, George Huntington (1850 to 1916), still in his early twenties, published a short paper called "On Chorea". His father and grandfather had been doctors in the same district and had watched the same families for decades, and Huntington described a "hereditary chorea" with three marked peculiarities: its hereditary nature, a tendency to insanity and suicide, and its appearance as a grave disease only in adult life. He also noticed that it never skipped a generation: if a child of an affected parent escaped it, that child's descendants were free of it too. In those few lines he described an autosomal dominant disease decades before anyone knew what a gene was. The disease was long called Huntington's chorea, from the Greek word for dance, after its writhing, dance-like movements; it is now called Huntington's disease because the movements are only one part of it.

Pinel calls it a progressive motor disorder that, unlike Parkinson's disease, is rare, has a simple genetic basis and is always associated with severe dementia. The first sign is often fidgetiness. As it advances, rapid, complex, jerky movements of whole limbs take over; the person cannot keep the tongue out or keep a steady grip (the grip squeezes and relaxes, "milkmaid's grip"), speech becomes slurred, swallowing fails and the gait becomes lurching and unstable. Late in the course chorea may give way to rigidity and slowness. But DSM-5-TR stresses that the psychiatric and cognitive changes often come first, by a decade or more: irritability, anxiety, depressed mood, apathy, disinhibition, impulsivity, obsessive-compulsive symptoms and, more rarely, psychosis. The cognitive decline begins with executive functions (speed, organisation, planning), with memory retrieval affected more than storage, and it follows the subcortical pattern of Section 10.10.10. The risk of suicide is markedly raised, both before and after the movements begin; in a large European cohort the commonest causes of death were pneumonia (19.5 percent), other infections (6.9 percent) and suicide (6.6 percent). Symptoms most often begin between 35 and 45, and death follows about 10 to 20 years after diagnosis. Scans show atrophy of the caudate nucleus and putamen, so that the frontal horns of the lateral ventricles balloon outwards. The American folk singer Woody Guthrie, who died of the disease in 1967, is its most famous victim.

The genetics are as simple as Huntington guessed. The disease is caused by a single dominant mutation in the gene for a protein called huntingtin (HTT), on the short arm of chromosome 4. The mutation is a trinucleotide repeat expansion: a stretch of the gene in which the three DNA letters C-A-G are repeated, coding for a run of the amino acid glutamine. Most people have fewer than 27 repeats. Repeats of 27 to 35 do not cause disease but are unstable and can expand when passed on; 36 to 39 repeats cause the disease in some carriers, usually late in life; and DSM-5-TR states that 40 or more repeats are invariably associated with the disease. The longer the repeat, the earlier the onset. Because the repeat tends to lengthen as it passes down the generations, especially through the father's sperm, the disease tends to begin earlier in each generation, a phenomenon called anticipation; the juvenile form, beginning before 20 and often inherited from the father, has very long repeats and brings rigidity, slowness, dystonia and seizures rather than chorea. The expanded huntingtin protein is, in Pinel's word, "stickier" than normal, clumps inside cells and is toxic, killing first the GABA-releasing medium spiny neurons of the striatum and later neurons throughout the brain. Each child of an affected parent has a 50 percent chance of inheriting the mutation; and because symptoms do not begin until about 40, after the peak reproductive years, the gene has stayed in the population.

Key study: Nancy Wexler and the villages of Lake Maracaibo (1979 to 1993)

In 1968 the mother of Nancy Wexler, a young American psychologist, was diagnosed with Huntington's disease, the illness that also took three of her mother's brothers and her grandfather; Nancy and her sister each faced a one-in-two risk. Their father founded a foundation to fund research, and from 1979 Wexler led expeditions to villages on the shores of Lake Maracaibo in Venezuela, where an extraordinary number of people were affected: an extended family of thousands, descended from one ancestor, in which the disease was everywhere. Year after year the team recorded the family tree and collected blood samples. Using DNA from this pedigree and an American family, James Gusella and colleagues found in 1983 a genetic marker close to the gene, on chromosome 4, one of the first disease genes to be located in this way. A further ten years of collaborative work by the Huntington's Disease Collaborative Research Group identified the gene itself and its expanded CAG repeat in 1993. The study is a model of how a single, carefully documented family can reveal a gene; its criticism is mainly ethical, about what researchers owed the poor villagers whose blood made the discovery possible. In 2020 Wexler disclosed that she herself had the disease.

The discovery of the gene created one of the hardest decisions in medicine. A simple blood test can now tell a healthy 25-year-old whether he will develop an incurable disease in middle age. Pinel puts the question to the reader: would you want to know? International guidelines for predictive genetic testing require counselling before and after the test, a period to reflect, the right of the at-risk person not to know, no testing of children for an adult-onset disease, and care when a test on one person will reveal the status of another (a positive result in a young adult whose at-risk parent has never been tested reveals that the parent, too, carries the gene). Only a minority of people at risk choose to be tested. The advantages are real: those who test negative are freed of fear, those who test positive can plan, and couples can use prenatal or preimplantation testing. There is still no treatment that slows the disease. The chorea can be reduced with drugs that deplete dopamine stores, such as tetrabenazine (which can worsen depression), or with antipsychotics; depression, irritability and obsessional symptoms are treated in the usual ways; and physiotherapy, speech and swallowing therapy and support for the family carry the rest.

The papers test Huntington's disease against three other genetic conditions taught in Chapter 3, Section 3.3. Table 10.42 sets them side by side, and one confusion is worth naming: the gene for phenylketonuria lies on chromosome 12, and notes that put Huntington's disease on chromosome 12 have mixed the two up.

Table 10.42 Huntington's disease and the genetic conditions it is confused with.
DisorderGenetic faultInheritanceChromosomeCore picture
Huntington's diseaseCAG repeat expansion in HTTAutosomal dominant, with anticipation4Chorea, psychiatric change, subcortical dementia; onset usually 35 to 45
Down syndromeExtra copy of chromosome 21 (trisomy)Usually not inherited; risk rises with maternal age21Intellectual disability, typical features, early Alzheimer's disease
PhenylketonuriaMutations in the phenylalanine hydroxylase geneAutosomal recessive12Intellectual disability if untreated; prevented by a low-phenylalanine diet
Fragile X syndromeCGG repeat expansion in FMR1X-linkedXCommonest inherited cause of intellectual disability; autistic features
Exam trap

"An autosomal dominant disorder caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion on chromosome 4" is Huntington's disease. Down syndrome is a chromosomal trisomy (21), phenylketonuria is autosomal recessive (chromosome 12), and fragile X is an X-linked CGG repeat. Both Huntington's and fragile X show anticipation, so "repeat expansion" alone does not decide; the repeat (CAG versus CGG), the chromosome (4 versus X) and the inheritance (dominant versus X-linked) do. Huntington's disease destroys the caudate and its GABA neurons; it is not an alcohol-related amnesia.

Prion disease

Some dementias move so fast that families can watch the decline week by week. The fastest are the prion diseases, which are unlike any other disease in medicine because they are transmissible yet contain no genetic material at all. Their agent is a prion, a name coined in 1982 by the American neurologist Stanley Prusiner for a "proteinaceous infectious particle": an abnormally folded form of a normal protein found on nerve cell membranes, the prion protein, encoded by the PRNP gene. The misfolded form converts normal prion protein into copies of itself, which clump, kill neurons and leave the brain full of tiny holes, like a sponge; hence the older name, the spongiform encephalopathies. Prions resist ordinary sterilisation, which is why some cases have been passed on by surgery. Prusiner, whose idea was ridiculed for years, received the Nobel Prize in 1997.

The commonest human prion disease is Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD), named after the German neurologists Hans Gerhard Creutzfeldt (1885 to 1964) and Alfons Maria Jakob (1884 to 1931), who described cases in 1920 and 1921. DSM-5-TR describes the forms. Sporadic CJD, the great majority, arises spontaneously at a rate of about one or two cases per million people per year, with a peak age of about 67. Genetic forms, up to 15 percent of cases, are caused by autosomal dominant mutations of PRNP and include familial CJD, Gerstmann-Sträussler-Scheinker syndrome and fatal familial insomnia (a disease of the thalamus that destroys sleep). Acquired forms have followed corneal transplants, grafts of dura mater, contaminated neurosurgical instruments and injections of growth hormone and gonadotropin extracted from human pituitary glands. The typical picture begins with weeks of vague fatigue, anxiety, poor sleep or appetite and poor concentration; then comes a rapidly progressive dementia, often reaching the major level within a few months, with myoclonus (sudden shock-like jerks, often provoked by a startle), ataxia, visual disturbance and other abnormal movements, ending in a mute, immobile state and death, usually within a year. The diagnostic clues are a characteristic electroencephalogram showing periodic sharp, often triphasic, synchronous discharges at about 0.5 to 2 per second; bright signals in the cortex and deep grey matter on diffusion-weighted MRI, the most sensitive test; raised 14-3-3 protein and tau in the cerebrospinal fluid; and a highly specific test called real-time quaking-induced conversion (RT-QuIC), which amplifies tiny amounts of the abnormal prion. Confirmation still requires brain tissue. There is no treatment.

In the mid-1990s a new form appeared in the United Kingdom. Variant CJD struck young people, often in their teens and twenties, began with psychiatric symptoms (low mood, withdrawal, anxiety) and painful sensations before the dementia and the jerks, and ran a longer course. It was traced to eating beef from cattle with bovine spongiform encephalopathy, "mad cow disease", which had spread through British herds fed on the processed remains of other animals; a few cases were later transmitted by blood transfusion. The prion had crossed from one species to another.

Key study: Gajdusek and kuru (1957 to 1976)

In 1957 the American physician Daniel Carleton Gajdusek (1923 to 2008) and the local medical officer Vincent Zigas described kuru, a fatal disease of trembling, staggering and dementia among the Fore people of the eastern highlands of Papua New Guinea; in the Fore language the word means to shiver or tremble. It killed mainly women and children. Anthropologists working with the Fore pointed to the explanation: at mortuary feasts, as a mark of respect, women and young children ate the bodies of dead relatives, including the brain. In 1966 Gajdusek and his colleagues transmitted kuru to chimpanzees by injecting brain tissue from patients, proving that a degenerative brain disease could be infectious, with an incubation period of years. When the feasts stopped, in the late 1950s, kuru slowly disappeared, although cases continued to appear for decades in people exposed as children, showing how long the incubation could be. Gajdusek shared the 1976 Nobel Prize. He believed the agent was a "slow virus"; Prusiner's prion theory later explained what it actually was.

Exam trap

Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease: rapidly progressive dementia (weeks to months) with myoclonus and periodic sharp-wave complexes on the EEG; death usually within a year. "Mad cow disease" (bovine spongiform encephalopathy) caused variant CJD in young people, not the sporadic form. Kuru: the Fore of Papua New Guinea, Gajdusek (Nobel 1976); prions: Prusiner (Nobel 1997). CJD is not the answer for an alcohol-related amnesia with confabulation.

HIV infection, brain injury and substances

HIV. The human immunodeficiency virus enters the brain early in infection, carried by infected white blood cells, and infects the brain's macrophages and microglia rather than neurons; the damage comes from the inflammation and toxic products that follow. Before effective treatment, many people with AIDS developed a severe dementia, described in 1986 by Bradford Navia, Richard Price and colleagues as the AIDS dementia complex. Since antiretroviral therapy became widely available, severe dementia has become uncommon and milder impairment common. A consensus meeting at Frascati in 2007 divided HIV-associated neurocognitive disorders into three levels: asymptomatic neurocognitive impairment (abnormal test scores without functional impairment), mild neurocognitive disorder, and HIV-associated dementia. DSM-5-TR reports that a third to over half of people with HIV show some neurocognitive disturbance, but that most of this is asymptomatic; of those with a disturbance, about a quarter meet criteria for mild disorder and typically fewer than 5 percent for major disorder. The pattern is typically subcortical: slowed processing, poor executive function and demanding attention, difficulty learning new information with better recall of what has been learned, apathy, and motor slowing and clumsiness, while aphasia is uncommon. DSM-5-TR's criteria require documented HIV infection and exclusion of other causes, especially the opportunistic brain infections (toxoplasmosis, cryptococcal meningitis, progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy) and lymphoma, which must be treated in their own right. Antiretroviral therapy is the treatment.

Traumatic brain injury. A blow to the head, or any force that moves the brain violently inside the skull, can damage it diffusely by stretching and shearing axons and focally by bruising (contusion) and bleeding; Pinel describes the closed-head injury in which the brain strikes the skull on the side opposite the blow. In India the commonest causes are road traffic accidents, especially on two-wheelers without helmets, and falls, which matter most in the elderly. DSM-5-TR's criteria for neurocognitive disorder due to traumatic brain injury require evidence of a traumatic brain injury with one or more of loss of consciousness, post-traumatic amnesia, disorientation and confusion, or neurological signs; and the neurocognitive disorder must appear immediately after the injury, or immediately after consciousness returns, and persist beyond the acute period. That timing rule separates it from a dementia that merely happens to follow an old injury. The severity of the injury is graded from the duration of unconsciousness, the duration of post-traumatic amnesia and the Glasgow Coma Scale score: roughly, mild injury means unconsciousness of 30 minutes or less, post-traumatic amnesia of a day or less and a score of 13 to 15; severe injury means unconsciousness of a day or more, post-traumatic amnesia of a week or more and a score of 3 to 8. The usual deficits are in complex attention, processing speed, memory, executive function and social cognition, often with irritability, apathy or disinhibited personality change (the frontal lobe syndromes of Section 10.11). Recovery after mild injury is usually complete within 3 to 12 months; after moderate and severe injury it continues for months or years but is often incomplete. The memory gaps around the injury, retrograde for the moments before it and anterograde for the period of confusion after it, are described in Book 1, Section 7.10.

Many people have persistent symptoms after a mild injury: headache, dizziness, fatigue, irritability, poor concentration and memory, insomnia and intolerance of noise and alcohol. ICD-10 called this postconcussional syndrome (F07.2). DSM-5-TR is cautious about it: when symptoms persist after a mild injury it asks the clinician to consider depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, anxiety, substance use, poor sleep and pessimistic expectations of recovery, and to diagnose a neurocognitive disorder only if the problems persist after those have been treated. Repeated injuries are a different matter. In 1928 the American pathologist Harrison Martland described boxers who had become "punch drunk": slowed, unsteady, tremulous and forgetful after years of blows, a condition later called dementia pugilistica. In 2005 the pathologist Bennet Omalu described the same kind of damage in the brain of a retired American football player, and the condition is now called chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE): a tau disease, with tangles clustered around small blood vessels at the depths of the cortical folds, causing mood change, impulsivity, aggression, suicidality, memory loss and parkinsonism. Pinel tells the case of the footballer Junior Seau, who shot himself in 2012 and whose brain met the criteria. CTE can be confirmed only after death, and figures such as its presence in 110 of 111 donated brains of professional American football players (Jesse Mez and colleagues, 2017) come from brains donated because families were worried, so they exaggerate its frequency.

Substances and medicines. DSM-5-TR's substance or medication-induced neurocognitive disorder is a decline that persists beyond the usual duration of intoxication and acute withdrawal, caused by a substance capable of producing it. Alcohol is by far the commonest culprit, producing both alcohol-related dementia and the amnesia of Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome, which Section 10.11 teaches. Sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics, inhalants (Section 10.7) and some other drugs can also do it, and in the elderly a heavy load of anticholinergic medicines can produce an apparent dementia that lifts when the drugs are stopped. Some improvement with sustained abstinence is the rule.

Exam trap

HIV-associated neurocognitive disorder follows a subcortical pattern (slowing, apathy, poor retrieval, motor clumsiness, little aphasia). For neurocognitive disorder due to traumatic brain injury, DSM-5-TR requires the disorder to appear immediately after the injury (or after consciousness returns) and to persist. "Punch drunk" (Martland, 1928), dementia pugilistica and chronic traumatic encephalopathy (Omalu, 2005) describe the damage of repeated head blows, a tau disease confirmed only at autopsy.

The dementias that can be reversed

Every dementia work-up is, first of all, a search for a cause that can be treated, and the reason is not that such causes are common but that missing one is a disaster. In a 2003 review of studies of people being investigated for dementia, the geriatrician A. Mark Clarfield found that about 9 percent had a potentially reversible cause, but that only about 0.6 percent actually recovered fully or partly after treatment. Those few patients are the point of the blood tests and the scan.

The dementia that most rewards recognition is normal pressure hydrocephalus (NPH), described in 1965 by the Colombian neurosurgeon Salomón Hakim and the American neurologist Raymond Adams. The ventricles of the brain enlarge, although the pressure of the cerebrospinal fluid measured at lumbar puncture is normal, and they stretch the fibres that run around them. The result is a triad that students remember as "wet, wobbly and wacky": a disturbance of gait, usually first (a broad-based, shuffling walk in which the feet seem stuck to the floor, a "magnetic" gait); urinary urgency and incontinence; and a subcortical dementia of slowness, apathy and inattention. It may follow a subarachnoid haemorrhage, meningitis or head injury, but in older people it usually has no known cause. Imaging shows ventricles enlarged out of proportion to any atrophy; removing some cerebrospinal fluid by lumbar puncture (a tap test) temporarily improves the walking; and a shunt that drains fluid from the ventricles into the abdomen can bring lasting improvement, best for the gait and least for the dementia, and best when the condition is treated early. Table 10.43 lists the other treatable causes. Most of the medical ones are explained in Section 10.11, and the table points there.

Table 10.43 Potentially reversible causes of dementia.
CauseCluesTreatmentWhere taught
Depression (pseudodementia)Fairly abrupt onset, low mood, complains of poor memory, "don't know" answersAntidepressants, ECTSection 10.11
HypothyroidismSlowing, weight gain, cold intolerance, dry skin, hoarse voiceThyroxineSection 10.11
Vitamin B12 deficiencyAnaemia with large red cells, neuropathy, spinal cord signsB12 injectionsSection 10.11
Thiamine deficiency (Wernicke-Korsakoff)Heavy drinking, ataxia, eye signs, confabulationUrgent parenteral thiamineSection 10.11
Neurosyphilis (general paresis)Grandiosity, abnormal pupils, positive serologyPenicillinSection 10.11
HIV infectionRisk history, subcortical slowingAntiretroviral therapySection 10.10.8
Chronic subdural haematomaA fall (often forgotten), headache, fluctuating drowsiness; elderly, drinkers, anticoagulantsSurgical drainageThis section
Brain tumourHeadache, seizures, focal signs; frontal tumours change personalitySurgery, radiotherapySection 10.11
Normal pressure hydrocephalusGait disturbance, incontinence, dementiaShuntThis section
Drugs and toxinsAnticholinergics, benzodiazepines, many medicines at once, heavy metalsStop or reduce the drugThis section
Wilson's diseaseYoung person, liver disease, tremor, psychiatric change, copper rings in the corneaCopper-removing drugsThis section
Autoimmune encephalitisSubacute confusion, psychosis, seizuresImmunotherapySection 10.11

Two entries deserve a sentence. A chronic subdural haematoma is a slowly enlarging collection of blood between the brain and its covering, usually after a minor fall that the patient may have forgotten; it is commonest in the elderly, in heavy drinkers and in people taking anticoagulants, it causes headache, drowsiness and confusion that fluctuate (and so can mimic both dementia and delirium), and it is cured by draining it.
