Wilson's disease, described by Samuel Alexander Kinnier Wilson (1878 to 1937) in 1912, is an autosomal recessive disorder of copper metabolism in which copper accumulates in the liver and the basal ganglia; it presents in adolescents and young adults with liver disease, tremor, rigidity, dysarthria and psychiatric symptoms (mood change, irritability, psychosis) that are easily mistaken for a primary mental illness, and the brownish ring of copper in the cornea (the Kayser-Fleischer ring) gives it away. Treated early with drugs that remove copper, it can be halted.

Exam trap

Normal pressure hydrocephalus: gait disturbance, urinary incontinence and dementia (Hakim and Adams, 1965), with enlarged ventricles and normal cerebrospinal fluid pressure; treated with a shunt. Do not confuse its triad with Wernicke's (confusion, ataxia, eye signs) or with the parkinsonian signs of Lewy body disease. "Reversible" does not mean "common": truly reversed dementia is rare, which is exactly why it must be searched for.

Cortical and subcortical dementia, and the whole picture

By now you will have noticed that the dementias fall into two families. Alzheimer's disease and frontotemporal dementia damage the cerebral cortex and its "instruments": language, skilled movement, recognition and the storage of new memories. Huntington's disease, Parkinson's disease, small vessel vascular disease, HIV and normal pressure hydrocephalus damage the structures beneath the cortex (the basal ganglia, the thalamus, the brainstem nuclei and the white matter connections) and slow the whole machine down instead. In 1974 Martin Albert, Robert Feldman and Anne Willis described patients with progressive supranuclear palsy whose dementia consisted of forgetfulness, slowed thinking, apathy or depression and an impaired ability to use what they knew, without aphasia, apraxia or agnosia, and called it a subcortical dementia. In 1975 Paul McHugh and Marshal Folstein described the same pattern in Huntington's disease, and in the 1980s Jeffrey Cummings and others set it against the cortical dementia of Alzheimer's disease and Pick's disease. The distinction rests on a simple idea. The cortex holds the instruments; the subcortical circuits, linking the basal ganglia and thalamus with the frontal lobes, supply speed, motivation and the ability to retrieve and use what is stored. Damage the instruments and the person cannot speak, use objects or learn; damage the circuits and the person can still do all those things, but slowly, without initiative, and without being able to pull out on demand what he knows.

The memory difference is the most useful single test. In a cortical dementia new information is never properly stored, so recall is poor, recognition is also poor, and giving cues does not help. In a subcortical dementia information is stored but cannot be retrieved efficiently, so free recall is poor but recognition is much better and cues help. Table 10.44 sets out the contrast.

Table 10.44 Cortical versus subcortical dementia (a contrast of typical patterns; real patients often show some of both).
FeatureCortical dementiaSubcortical dementia
ExamplesAlzheimer's disease, frontotemporal dementiaHuntington's, Parkinson's, progressive supranuclear palsy, HIV, small vessel vascular disease, normal pressure hydrocephalus, Wilson's disease
LanguageAphasia (anomia, poor comprehension)No aphasia; slurred speech (dysarthria), reduced output
MemoryStorage fails: recall and recognition both poor; cues do not helpRetrieval fails: recall poor, recognition better; cues help
Praxis and recognitionApraxia, agnosia, acalculiaLargely spared
Speed of thoughtNormal until lateSlowed (bradyphrenia)
Executive functionImpaired laterImpaired early and prominently
Mood and personalityUnconcerned or mildly depressedApathy, depression, irritability prominent
MovementNormal until lateEarly abnormalities: posture, gait, tremor, chorea, rigidity

The distinction has critics. As early as 1986 Peter Whitehouse pointed out that Alzheimer's disease destroys subcortical nuclei too (the nucleus basalis of Meynert, which you met in Section 10.9.4, is one of them), that the "subcortical" diseases eventually reach the cortex, and that the two patterns overlap so much that they describe tendencies rather than separate classes. Lewy body dementia, with its cortical visuospatial deficits and its subcortical slowing and parkinsonism, is the obvious mixed case. Most clinicians therefore now speak of a predominantly cortical or a predominantly frontal-subcortical pattern. The contrast nonetheless remains a powerful bedside tool and a favourite of examiners. Section 10.9.1 explained the older descriptive terms "senile dementia" (dementia beginning in old age, of any cause) and "cerebral atrophy" (shrinkage of the brain, seen in many conditions); neither is a disease in its own right. Table 10.45 brings the whole section together.

Table 10.45 The subtypes of major and mild neurocognitive disorder at a glance.
SubtypeOnset and courseEarly signsPathology or causeKey fact for the papers

Exam trap

Cortical dementia (Alzheimer's, frontotemporal) means aphasia, apraxia, agnosia and amnesia with normal movement; subcortical dementia (Huntington's, Parkinson's, progressive supranuclear palsy, HIV, small vessel disease, normal pressure hydrocephalus) means slowing, apathy, depression, retrieval failure and early movement disorder without aphasia. Albert and colleagues popularised the term "subcortical dementia" in 1974 for progressive supranuclear palsy (the phrase itself had been used earlier, in 1932, by von Stockert for a patient with encephalitis lethargica). If a stem gives you a dementia with chorea, think Huntington's; with a rest tremor first, Parkinson's; with visual hallucinations and fluctuations, Lewy bodies; with jerks and a rapid decline, prion disease; with personality change in the fifties, frontotemporal; with steps and focal signs, vascular; with smooth memory loss in old age, Alzheimer's.

10.11 Wernicke-Korsakoff Syndrome, Pseudodementia and Mental Disorders Due to Medical IllnessHigh yield · 10 Qs

Every section of this chapter so far has been about one of two things: what drugs do to the brain, or what happens when the brain itself wears out. This last section sits where those two meet, and then widens the view. It begins with a vitamin. Thiamine costs almost nothing, yet a brain starved of it for a few weeks can lose, within days, the machinery that turns experience into lasting memory; Jimmie G., whose story opened this chapter, is the most famous example. The section then turns to two kinds of impostor. The first is depression in an older person, which can look so like dementia that it was given a name of its own, pseudodementia. The second is physical illness: a tumour that pours out cortisol, a sluggish thyroid gland, a spirochaete, an antibody against the brain's own receptors, any of which can produce depression, anxiety, mania, psychosis or a change of personality that looks exactly like a "functional" psychiatric disorder. One lesson ties these together, and every clinician has to learn it early: before you decide that a mind is ill, make sure that the body is not.

Thiamine and the starving brain

Why should a vitamin matter to memory? Ask most people what heavy drinking does to the brain and they will tell you a story about poison. The most dramatic damage that alcohol does to memory is, surprisingly, mostly a story about food. Thiamine, or vitamin B1, is a water-soluble vitamin found in whole grains, pulses, nuts, yeast and meat. The body cannot make it and keeps only a small reserve, enough for about two to three weeks. Inside the cell it is converted into thiamine pyrophosphate (also called thiamine diphosphate), a helper molecule, or coenzyme, for three enzymes at the centre of energy metabolism. Two of them work in the main line of energy production: pyruvate dehydrogenase carries the products of glucose breakdown into the citric acid (Krebs) cycle, where most of a cell's energy is made, and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase drives one of the steps of that cycle itself. The third, transketolase, runs a side pathway that supplies the raw materials for nucleic acids and helps cells defend themselves against oxidative damage.

Now picture a neuron. It burns almost nothing but glucose, and it cannot store fuel. Take thiamine away and the neuron becomes a car with a full tank and a broken fuel pump: the glucose is there, but it cannot be burned. Energy production fails, lactic acid builds up, cells swell, and the first to die are the small, busy clusters of neurons packed around the fluid-filled spaces in the middle of the brain, the third ventricle, the cerebral aqueduct of the midbrain and the floor of the fourth ventricle. Hold on to that geography; every symptom in the next few pages follows from it.

The disease of thiamine lack was known long before the vitamin. In the 1890s Christiaan Eijkman (1858 to 1930), a Dutch army doctor working in Java, noticed that chickens fed on polished white rice developed a paralysis very like the human disease beriberi, and that rice with its bran left on prevented it. The protective factor in the bran turned out to be thiamine, and Eijkman shared the Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine in 1929. Beriberi is thiamine deficiency of the peripheral nerves and the heart; the brain disease came to be recognised as its cerebral form. When H. E. de Wardener and B. Lennox reported 52 cases of the brain disease that they had seen in a prisoner-of-war hospital in Singapore, among men held by the Japanese on a diet made up largely of polished rice, they called their 1947 paper "Cerebral beriberi".

Why are heavy drinkers in particular so exposed? There are four reasons, and they add up. First, a person who drinks heavily takes a large part of his daily calories as alcohol, which contains no vitamins at all, and he often eats little else. Second, alcohol interferes with the absorption of thiamine from the gut, so even the thiamine he does eat is poorly taken up. Third, a fatty or cirrhotic liver stores less of it and converts less of it into its active form. Fourth, drinkers often vomit, have diarrhoea or go through bouts of near starvation. John P. J. Pinel uses this disorder to illustrate what he calls converging operations. Because the syndrome is common in heavy drinkers, it was first thought to be a direct toxic effect of alcohol. That interpretation was overturned by evidence of different kinds (case studies of patients, comparisons of heavy drinkers with abstainers, and controlled experiments on animals) that converged on the same answer: the syndrome turned up in malnourished people who drank little or no alcohol, and rats made thiamine-deficient developed memory deficits and patterns of brain damage like those of heavy drinkers. Alcohol is not wholly innocent, though: it speeds up the damage in thiamine-deficient rats, so it probably adds some direct toxicity of its own.

That still leaves a puzzle. Most heavy drinkers never develop the syndrome, so why do some? In 1977 John P. Blass and Gary E. Gibson grew skin cells from four patients with the syndrome and found that their transketolase gripped thiamine pyrophosphate far less tightly than the enzyme in healthy people's cells (it needed roughly twelve times as much of the coenzyme to work at half speed), and that the abnormality persisted when the cells were grown with plenty of thiamine and no alcohol. The fault was inborn. A person with such an enzyme would be perfectly well on a normal diet but unusually vulnerable when drinking and poor eating cut his thiamine supply. DSM-5-TR lists this genetic transketolase abnormality, together with poor nutrition, as a risk factor.

Heavy drinking is the commonest route, but not the only one. Carl Wernicke's own first series, as you will see in a moment, included a young woman whose deficiency came from persistent vomiting, not from drink. Thiamine deficiency also follows the severe vomiting of pregnancy (hyperemesis gravidarum), weight-loss (bariatric) surgery on the stomach, starvation and anorexia nervosa (Chapter 8), prolonged vomiting from any cause, cancer and its chemotherapy, kidney dialysis, AIDS and long periods of intravenous feeding without added vitamins. In 2003 an outbreak struck infants in Israel who had been fed a soy-based formula that lacked thiamine. Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome is therefore best understood as a nutritional disease to which heavy drinkers are especially exposed, not simply as a poisoning by alcohol.

Exam trap

Korsakoff's syndrome and Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome are caused by deficiency of thiamine, vitamin B1, classically in chronic alcoholism (MET Mock 5; Biopsych and Testing paper). Know what each distractor causes. Vitamin B12 (cobalamin) deficiency causes megaloblastic anaemia, damage to the spinal cord (subacute combined degeneration) and nerves, depression and a reversible dementia (Section 10.11.9). Vitamin D deficiency causes rickets in children and osteomalacia (soft bones) in adults. Folate deficiency causes megaloblastic anaemia, neural tube defects in the unborn child and low mood. Niacin (vitamin B3) deficiency causes pellagra, with dermatitis, diarrhoea and dementia. Hypoxia (lack of oxygen) damages the hippocampus and can cause amnesia, but it is not the cause of Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome. Lead poisoning causes encephalopathy and lasting cognitive loss in children, and anaemia, abdominal colic and wrist drop in adults. B1 is thiamine; B12 is cobalamin. Do not swap them.

Wernicke's encephalopathy: an emergency hiding in plain sight

In 1881 Carl Wernicke (1848 to 1905), the German neurologist whose name you already know from the receptive aphasia he had described seven years earlier, published part of his textbook of brain diseases, the Lehrbuch der Gehirnkrankheiten. In it he described three patients: two men with alcoholism, aged 33 and 36, and a woman of 20 who had swallowed sulphuric acid and then developed persistent vomiting. All three became confused and drowsy, could not move their eyes properly and walked unsteadily. All three died, and at autopsy Wernicke found tiny pinpoint haemorrhages in the grey matter around the third and fourth ventricles and the aqueduct. He believed he was looking at an inflammation and called it polioencephalitis haemorrhagica superior ("polio" is Greek for grey, the same root as in poliomyelitis). Its real cause, thiamine deficiency, was not established until the 1930s and 1940s. The disorder is now called Wernicke's encephalopathy: an acute disorder of the brain caused by thiamine deficiency and marked by confusion, abnormal eye movements and an unsteady gait.

Those three signs became the textbook triad, and each is worth seeing clearly. The first is a change in mental state, usually a global confusional state. The patient is apathetic, drowsy and inattentive; he cannot follow a conversation, does not know where he is or what day it is, and without treatment may sink into stupor and coma. The second is ophthalmoplegia, weakness or paralysis of the muscles that move the eyes. Most often the lateral rectus muscles of both eyes are weak, so the eyes cannot turn outwards (DSM-5-TR describes it as a lateral gaze paralysis), and the patient may complain of double vision. It is almost always accompanied by nystagmus, an involuntary rhythmic jerking of the eyes, usually from side to side when the patient looks sideways. The third is ataxia, unsteadiness of stance and gait: the patient stands with his feet wide apart, sways and lurches, and cannot walk heel to toe, while his arms may be relatively spared. Many patients also have numb, weak legs and absent ankle reflexes from the peripheral neuropathy that so often accompanies heavy drinking.

Key study: Harper's autopsies (1983 and 1986)

How often is Wernicke's encephalopathy missed? The Australian neuropathologist Clive Harper answered the question from the other end, by examining brains after death. In a nine-year series of autopsies in Western Australia he found the characteristic lesions in 2.8 percent of all the brains examined, yet only 20 percent of his 131 cases had been diagnosed during life, and 83 percent of the lesions were old, chronic ones. In 1986 Harper, M. Giles and R. Finlay-Jones went back to the hospital notes of 131 cases proved at autopsy: only 16 percent had had the full triad recorded, and 19 percent had had no documented sign at all. The conclusion changed practice. If you wait for all three signs, you will miss most cases. Harper suggested that many patients reach the chronic stage through repeated small episodes of deficiency that nobody notices.

Clinicians now use looser rules. In 1997 D. Caine, Harper and their colleagues in Sydney proposed that a heavy drinker should be diagnosed with Wernicke's encephalopathy if he shows any two of four signs: a history of dietary deficiency, eye signs, signs of cerebellar dysfunction (unsteadiness, poor coordination), and either an altered mental state or mild memory impairment. The European Federation of Neurological Societies adopted these criteria in its guideline of 2010, and added a blunt piece of advice: suspect the disorder in any condition that can lead to thiamine deficiency, whether or not the patient drinks.

Each sign maps onto a site of damage, as Figure 10.19 shows. The lesions are symmetrical and lie close to the midline, in the tissue that lines the third and fourth ventricles and the aqueduct. The eye signs come from damage to the brainstem nuclei that control eye movements (the oculomotor, trochlear and abducens nuclei) and to the vestibular nuclei, around the floor of the fourth ventricle. The ataxia comes from damage to the upper part of the cerebellar vermis, the midline strip of the cerebellum that keeps the trunk balanced, together with the vestibular damage. The confusion, and later the memory loss, come from damage in the diencephalon: to the mammillary bodies, two small rounded swellings on the underside of the hypothalamus that are affected in almost every case, and to the medial thalamus, especially the dorsomedial nucleus of the thalamus (also called the mediodorsal nucleus). The grey matter around the aqueduct, the periaqueductal grey, is often affected too. In the acute stage these regions are swollen and speckled with small haemorrhages; in the chronic stage the mammillary bodies shrink. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) can show symmetrical changes in exactly these places and supports the diagnosis, but a normal scan does not rule it out.

Wernicke's encephalopathy is a medical emergency, and it is one of the few emergencies in neuropsychiatry with a cheap, safe and effective treatment: thiamine, given by injection (intravenously or intramuscularly), in high doses, at once. The 2010 European guideline recommends 200 mg three times a day, preferably intravenously, given before any carbohydrate and continued until the signs stop improving. Tablets are not enough, because a drinker's gut absorbs thiamine poorly and the brain needs it now. The response can be dramatic. The eye-movement palsies often begin to improve within hours, so quickly that the improvement itself helps confirm the diagnosis; the ataxia and the confusion recover more slowly, over days and weeks. Left untreated, the patient may slide into stupor, coma and death, and many survivors, in some series most of them, are left with Korsakoff's syndrome.

You will often read that glucose must never be given before thiamine. The reasoning is sound. Thiamine is needed to burn glucose, so a large glucose load (an intravenous drip of dextrose, for example) can use up the last of a patient's thiamine and tip a borderline deficiency into Wernicke's encephalopathy. The evidence, however, is thinner than the slogan suggests. When E. Schabelman and D. Kuo reviewed the literature in 2012, they found 19 relevant papers, none of them better than case reports or series. Their advice reflects current practice: a patient with low blood sugar must never be left without glucose while someone looks for thiamine, because hypoglycaemia itself damages the brain; give thiamine at the same time or straight afterwards, and never keep an at-risk patient on glucose drips for long without it.

Clinical link

This is why thiamine is part of every alcohol detoxification regimen (Section 10.4), and why many units give it by injection to every malnourished drinker on admission instead of waiting for signs. In a 2001 report, set out as guidelines for emergency departments in 2002, the Royal College of Physicians of London advised that drinkers who may be poorly nourished should be given B vitamins by injection at the outset in emergency departments, especially when drunkenness hides the clinical signs. The same vigilance applies outside psychiatry: to the woman with hyperemesis gravidarum, to the patient after bariatric surgery (the European guideline advises checking thiamine for at least six months afterwards), and to anyone being refed after starvation. A few rupees' worth of vitamin given in time can prevent a lifetime of amnesia.

Exam trap

Wernicke's triad is confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia (with nystagmus). Amnesia, confabulation and apathy belong to Korsakoff's syndrome. Wernicke's encephalopathy is acute and largely reversible with thiamine; Korsakoff's syndrome is chronic and largely irreversible. The full triad is present in only a minority (16 percent in Harper's autopsy series), so "all three signs are needed for the diagnosis" is false. Finally, do not confuse Wernicke's encephalopathy (1881, thiamine deficiency) with Wernicke's aphasia (1874, a fluent receptive aphasia from damage to the left superior temporal gyrus): the same man described both.

Korsakoff's syndrome: a memory that cannot hold

Six years after Wernicke's description, a Moscow psychiatrist began to describe the other half of the story. Sergei Sergeievich Korsakoff (1854 to 1900) took his doctorate in 1887 with a thesis on alcoholic paralysis, and in a series of papers from 1887 to about 1891, in Russian, French and German, he described a peculiar mental disorder that accompanied polyneuritis (disease of many peripheral nerves at once). He saw it in about thirty heavy drinkers and in fourteen patients whose illness had nothing to do with alcohol, and he called it polyneuritic psychosis, or cerebropathia psychica toxaemica. What struck him was the memory. His patients, he wrote, promptly forgot what had just happened, yet remembered the events of the years before their illness. He noticed abnormal eye movements in some of them but did not stress them; the link with Wernicke's cases was made by others. The eponym is usually credited to the German psychiatrist Friedrich Jolly, who in 1897 proposed that the disorder be named after Korsakoff (sources differ on his exact wording). Today Korsakoff's syndrome (also called Korsakoff's psychosis, or Korsakoff's amnesic syndrome) means a chronic disorder of memory, caused by thiamine deficiency and seen most often in heavy drinkers, in which the ability to learn new information is severely impaired while alertness, immediate memory and general intelligence are relatively preserved. Notice the old word "psychosis": in the nineteenth century it meant any serious mental disorder, not hallucinations and delusions.

Babu, a 47-year-old lorry driver from Salem, had drunk arrack every evening for twenty years and had eaten almost nothing for a month when his wife brought him to the district hospital, confused, staggering and seeing double. The duty doctor found nystagmus and weakness of outward gaze in both eyes and gave intravenous thiamine at once; within two days his eyes moved normally, though his walking improved much more slowly. By the third week the fog had lifted, and the ward team could see what was left. Babu was alert, polite and chatty. He could repeat seven digits forwards without a mistake, and he still played cards as shrewdly as ever. But he could not recall the doctor's name five minutes after hearing it for the fourth time, greeted the same nurse each morning as if they had never met, and when asked what he had done that morning said that he had driven a load of turmeric to Erode and stopped for a meal on the way. He had not left the ward in three weeks. He thought the year was 2016 and that his married daughter was still at school. Asked about his memory, he said it was perfectly good.

Babu shows almost every feature of the syndrome. The core is anterograde amnesia, the inability to form new lasting memories of events and facts after the illness begins. DSM-5-TR describes the alcohol-induced amnestic-confabulatory (Korsakoff's) type as an impairment of recent memory out of proportion to any other cognitive problem, with severe difficulty learning new information and rapid forgetting. Information gets in (Babu can repeat seven digits), but it does not stay: a few minutes later, once his attention has moved on, it has gone. Patients ask the same question over and over, cannot learn the way to the toilet in a new ward, and do not recognise people they see every day.

The second feature is retrograde amnesia, the loss of memories formed before the illness. In Korsakoff's syndrome it is patchy and often extensive, sometimes reaching back decades, and it has a temporal gradient: the most recent years are lost most completely, while memories of childhood and youth are relatively spared. In 1979 Marilyn S. Albert, Nelson Butters and J. Levin tested eleven patients with alcoholic Korsakoff's syndrome using photographs of famous faces from different decades, questions about famous people and events, and a multiple-choice questionnaire. The patients had a marked retrograde amnesia with a steep gradient, and because the researchers had included items of both passing and lasting fame, they could show that the gradient was not simply an artefact of older items being easier. The pattern had been predicted a century before. In 1881 the French psychologist Théodule Ribot (1839 to 1916) argued that memory dissolves in the reverse of the order in which it was built, the newest and least consolidated memories going first and the oldest last; this is Ribot's law. Pinel adds that anterograde amnesia dominates in the early stages, and that the retrograde loss can deepen as the disorder progresses, eventually stretching back into childhood.

The third feature is the one that makes Korsakoff's syndrome unforgettable to anyone who has seen it. Confabulation is the production of false memories, often fluent and plausible, to fill the gaps in memory, without any intention to deceive (Chapter 2, Section 2.7.5 describes it as a sign). Babu's trip to Erode is an example. In 1987 the British neuropsychiatrist Michael D. Kopelman distinguished two types. Provoked confabulation appears in answer to a question that probes memory ("What did you do this morning?"). It is common, and Kopelman argued that it may be a normal response to a faulty memory, because healthy people make similar errors when asked to recall a story after a very long delay. Spontaneous confabulation pours out unprompted, can be fantastic (the patient describes his meeting with a film star last week) and is sometimes acted upon. Kopelman suggested that it appears when frontal-lobe dysfunction is added to an organic amnesia, so that the patient can no longer check and reject the false memories his brain throws up. Confabulation tends to be most florid in the early weeks of the illness and to fade as the syndrome becomes chronic.

Several other features sit around these three. Patients are disoriented, especially in time: they lose track of the date and of how long they have been in hospital. They are apathetic, with little initiative or spontaneous interest, and seem emotionally bland. They usually lack insight, denying that anything is wrong with their memory, as Babu did. Many also show the effects of frontal damage: poor planning, difficulty shifting from one way of thinking to another, and an unusual vulnerability to interference, so that material learned earlier intrudes into what they are trying to learn now. In its advanced stages, Pinel notes, the disorder brings sensory and motor problems, extreme confusion and personality change, and a risk of death from liver, gut or heart disease.

Just as important, for diagnosis and for the exam, is what Korsakoff's syndrome leaves alone. Consciousness is clear: unlike the patient with Wernicke's encephalopathy or delirium, the Korsakoff patient is alert and attentive. Immediate memory, the span of what can be held in mind for a few seconds (tested with the digit span), is normal or nearly so. General intelligence, as measured by tests of knowledge and reasoning acquired long ago, is largely preserved, and so are language and old skills: Babu still plays cards, and other patients have kept up chess, carpentry or music. Most remarkably, some kinds of new learning survive. Patients can learn motor skills such as mirror drawing, they show priming (a word seen once is completed more readily later, although they do not remember having seen it), and they can be conditioned. The first and most famous demonstration came from Geneva.

Key study: Claparède's pin (1911)

The Swiss psychologist Édouard Claparède (1873 to 1940) studied a woman with Korsakoff's syndrome at the Bel-Air asylum in Geneva. She was 47 when he first tested her in 1906, and her illness had begun around 1900. She could name the capitals of Europe and do mental arithmetic, but after five years in the asylum she did not know where she was, did not recognise the doctors she saw every day or the nurse who had looked after her for six months, and forgot a story read to her within three minutes. To see whether an emotional experience would last longer, Claparède hid a pin between his fingers and pricked her hand as he shook it. A few minutes later she had forgotten the prick. Yet when he reached for her hand again, she pulled it back without knowing why. Pressed for a reason, she asked, "Is there perhaps a pin hidden in your hand?", and explained, "Sometimes pins are hidden in people's hands." She never recognised the idea as a memory. Claparède published the case in 1911, and it remains one of the first demonstrations of what is now called implicit memory: learning that shows itself in behaviour without conscious recollection (Book 1, Section 7.5 sets out the explicit and implicit memory systems).

Exam trap

In Korsakoff's syndrome the amnesia is mainly anterograde, with a temporally graded retrograde amnesia that can be profound (older memories are kept better than recent ones). Consciousness is clear, immediate memory (digit span) and general intelligence are relatively preserved, and implicit and procedural memory survive. Confabulation is not lying: there is no intent to deceive, which separates it from malingering. "Korsakoff's psychosis" does not mean that the patient hallucinates; "psychosis" is the old general word for a serious mental illness. So when a paper asks which illness caused by long-term alcohol use "often involves profound retrograde amnesia" (RML 2024), the answer is Korsakoff's psychosis.

One disease in two stages, and where the damage lies

For almost a century Wernicke's encephalopathy and Korsakoff's syndrome were described in different books, one as a neurological emergency and the other as a psychiatric curiosity. The study that joined them for good came from the American neurologists Maurice Victor, Raymond D. Adams and George H. Collins, who in 1971 published a monograph based on 245 patients, 82 of whom had been examined after death. They followed patients from the acute illness onwards and found that the two disorders were stages of one disease. Most drinkers who survived Wernicke's encephalopathy were left, as their confusion cleared, with Korsakoff's amnesia, and the brains of patients with Korsakoff's syndrome showed the same lesions as those with Wernicke's encephalopathy, only older. The combined term Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome therefore names a single disorder of thiamine deficiency with an acute phase (Wernicke's encephalopathy: confusion, ataxia and eye signs) and a chronic phase (Korsakoff's syndrome: amnesia and confabulation in clear consciousness). Figure 10.20 shows how the one turns into the other in a treated patient such as Babu.

Not every patient passes visibly through the first stage. Some arrive at Korsakoff's syndrome with no recorded episode of Wernicke's encephalopathy, and Harper's finding that most lesions at autopsy were old ones suggests why: repeated small episodes of deficiency, each too mild to be noticed, can add up to the chronic disorder. That is one more reason to give thiamine to every at-risk drinker instead of waiting for signs.

Which lesion causes the amnesia? Compare Korsakoff's syndrome with the amnesia of Henry Molaison (H.M.), whose medial temporal lobes, including most of both hippocampi, were removed by surgery in 1953 (Book 1, Section 7.10). Both men lost the ability to form new explicit memories, but their damage lay in different places. Korsakoff's amnesia is a diencephalic amnesia, caused by damage to the medial diencephalon (the medial thalamus, and the hypothalamus with its mammillary bodies) rather than to the medial temporal lobe. Pinel points out that the damage in Korsakoff's syndrome is diffuse, reaching the neocortex, the cerebellum and other structures (some studies include the hippocampus, although neuron counts described below found it intact), so that no single lesion can be blamed with certainty; but the mediodorsal (dorsomedial) nuclei of the thalamus are damaged in almost every patient, and the mammillary bodies nearly as often. Converging evidence comes from rare patients with damage confined to the same region. The best known is N.A., a radar technician in the United States Air Force whose roommate, playing with a fencing foil behind his chair, accidentally stabbed him up the right nostril; the foil passed through the thin bone at the base of the skull into his brain. N.A. was left with a lasting anterograde amnesia, first described by Hans-Lukas Teuber, Brenda Milner and H. G. Vaughan in 1968, and an MRI scan taken in the late 1980s showed extensive medial diencephalic damage, including the mediodorsal nuclei and the mammillary bodies.

More recent work has sharpened the picture. In 2000 A. Harding, G. Halliday and their colleagues in Sydney counted neurons in the brains of drinkers who had had Wernicke's encephalopathy, with and without Korsakoff's amnesia. Neuron loss in the mammillary bodies and the mediodorsal thalamus was substantial in both groups, the amnesic and the non-amnesic alike; only loss in the anterior nuclei of the thalamus consistently separated the patients with Korsakoff's syndrome, and the hippocampus was unaffected. Why should the anterior thalamus matter so much? Because it is a relay in a loop that links the hippocampus to the rest of the memory system. In 1937 James Papez (1883 to 1958) traced a circuit that runs from the hippocampus through the fornix to the mammillary bodies, from there through the mammillothalamic tract to the anterior nucleus of the thalamus, then to the cingulate cortex and back to the hippocampus. Papez thought that the circuit served emotion; it is now known to matter more for memory (Book 1, Chapter 11). The Papez circuit explains why lesions in quite different places, the hippocampus in H.M., the mammillary bodies and anterior thalamus in Korsakoff's syndrome, the fornix in rare surgical cases, all produce amnesia: break the loop anywhere and new memories are not laid down. The mediodorsal nucleus belongs to a second loop, with the prefrontal cortex, and its damage probably adds the frontal features of the syndrome: the poor organisation of memory, the vulnerability to interference and perhaps the failure to check confabulations.

What becomes of patients like Babu? Thiamine and abstinence stop the damage from getting worse, but the amnesia itself recovers only partly, and slowly. In the long follow-up by Victor and his colleagues, partial recovery was the rule: only about a fifth to a quarter of patients recovered fully, roughly a quarter did not improve at all, and improvement, when it came, crept on over many months. Pinel puts it soberly: thiamine limits further damage and often brings slight improvement, but the brain dysfunction already acquired is mostly irreversible. Care therefore has two aims. The first is to prevent further damage, with complete abstinence (helped by the treatments in Section 10.4), a proper diet and continued vitamins. The second is to help the person live with the memory that remains. Because implicit and procedural memory are relatively spared, patients learn best by doing the same thing in the same way, many times over, in an unchanging environment, with diaries, labelled cupboards, phone alarms and a fixed daily routine. Trial-and-error learning is avoided, because a patient who cannot remember his mistakes will learn them implicitly and repeat them; errorless learning, in which the correct response is supplied before the patient has a chance to guess wrongly, works better (Book 2, Section 9.9.3). Many patients cannot live independently again, and in India that care usually falls on the family.

The manuals file the syndrome in different places, and the papers occasionally ask where. ICD-10 codes the Korsakoff stage in drinkers as F10.6, the alcohol-induced amnesic syndrome (one of the clinical patterns of Section 10.1), and codes Wernicke's encephalopathy itself among the vitamin deficiencies, as E51.2, a form of thiamine deficiency; Korsakoff's syndrome without alcohol goes under F04, the organic amnesic syndrome. DSM-5 abolished the separate category of amnestic disorder, so DSM-5-TR calls the condition alcohol-induced major neurocognitive disorder, amnestic-confabulatory type, adding "persistent" when it lasts; a man like Babu, with severe alcohol use disorder, would be coded F10.26. ICD-11 goes furthest towards the biology. It codes Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome under vitamin B1 (thiamine) deficiency (5B5A.1, with Wernicke encephalopathy as 5B5A.10 and Korsakoff syndrome as 5B5A.11), in its chapter on endocrine, nutritional or metabolic diseases, and explicitly excludes both from 6D72.10, amnestic disorder due to use of alcohol, a category reserved for memory impairment caused by alcohol itself.

Exam trap

Anterograde amnesia with confabulation after bilateral damage to the mammillary bodies and dorsomedial thalamus is Korsakoff's syndrome (MET Mock 3). Each distractor has its own lesion. Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease is a prion disease with spongiform change in the cortex and deep grey matter, and a dementia that progresses over months with myoclonus. Pick's disease is a frontotemporal degeneration: shrunken frontal and temporal lobes, Pick bodies, and early change of personality or language with memory relatively spared. Huntington's chorea is an inherited degeneration of the caudate nucleus with chorea, depression and a subcortical dementia (Section 10.10 teaches all three). And the hippocampus is H.M.'s lesion, not Korsakoff's: the alcoholic amnesia is diencephalic.

Telling the alcoholic amnesia from its look-alikes

The papers test this topic mostly with one vignette in several costumes, so it is worth pushing it through slowly, as a worked example. A man with a long history of heavy drinking presents with severe memory deficits, confabulation and nystagmus. Which diagnosis fits (Real PYQ 2024; NIMHANS Mock 1)? Take each option in turn and ask what it would predict.

  1. Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome. Every finding points here. Nystagmus is a Wernicke sign, from damage around the floor of the fourth ventricle; severe memory loss with confabulation is the Korsakoff stage; chronic drinking is the setting in which thiamine deficiency develops. An eye sign together with an amnesia in a drinker is the signature of the whole syndrome, its acute and chronic phases overlapping. This is the answer.
  2. Alzheimer's disease. Memory loss, yes, but it comes on insidiously over years, usually after 65, and spreads to language (aphasia), skilled movement (apraxia) and visuospatial ability. There are no eye-movement signs, confabulation is not its hallmark, and drinking does not cause it (Section 10.9).
  3. Vascular dementia. The course is sudden or stepwise, following strokes, with patchy deficits, focal neurological signs (weakness of one side, a lost half of the visual field, slurred speech) and vascular risk factors such as hypertension and diabetes (Section 10.10). A brainstem stroke can cause nystagmus, but it would not explain an amnesia with confabulation in a heavy drinker.
  4. Frontotemporal dementia. The early picture is a change of personality (disinhibition, apathy, loss of empathy, compulsive or tactless behaviour) or a progressive loss of language, typically beginning between about 45 and 65, with memory relatively spared at first. Nystagmus has no place in it (Section 10.10).

The other questions on the topic change the distractors but not the logic: when chronic drinking and a disorder of memory appear together in the stem, the answer is Korsakoff's syndrome. Asked which illness caused by long-term alcohol use often involves profound retrograde amnesia (RML 2024), you choose Korsakoff's psychosis over Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease and Huntington's disease. Parkinson's disease is a disorder of movement, caused by the loss of the dopamine neurons of the substantia nigra, with tremor at rest, rigidity and slowness of movement; dementia, if it comes, comes late. Huntington's disease is an inherited, autosomal dominant degeneration of the caudate nucleus with chorea, depression and a subcortical dementia. Neither has anything to do with drinking. Asked which amnesic disorder chronic alcoholism may cause (RML 2021), you choose Korsakoff's syndrome over Alzheimer's, Pick's and Parkinson's diseases; Pick's disease, the classic frontotemporal dementia, spares memory early. Asked which neuropsychological disorder is marked by severe memory impairment and confusion and often caused by chronic alcohol abuse (IHBAS 2024), you choose Korsakoff's syndrome over schizophrenia, Huntington's disease and multiple sclerosis. Schizophrenia is a psychotic disorder of hallucinations, delusions, disorganisation and negative symptoms (Chapter 4); it brings real cognitive deficits, but it is not an amnesic syndrome and alcohol does not cause it. Multiple sclerosis is an autoimmune disease in which patches of the myelin sheath in the brain and spinal cord are inflamed and destroyed, usually beginning in young adults with episodes of blurred vision, numbness or weakness that come and go; depression and slowed thinking are common in it, but it is not caused by alcohol. (Strictly, the "confusion" in that stem belongs to the Wernicke stage and the memory loss to the Korsakoff stage, but the papers use "Korsakoff's syndrome" for the whole.)

In the clinic the look-alikes are wider than the exam's options, and Table 10.46 lines them up. Two come straight from Section 10.3. Delirium tremens, the most severe stage of alcohol withdrawal, also brings confusion and memory failure in a drinker, but it begins two or three days after the last drink, with clouded consciousness, tremor, sweating, fever, a racing pulse and vivid hallucinations, and it is over within days; Korsakoff's syndrome is quiet, alert and lasting. An alcoholic blackout is amnesia only for a period of intoxication, and memory works normally once the person is sober. Two others come from elsewhere: transient global amnesia, described below, and dissociative amnesia (Chapter 7, Section 7.5), in which a person loses autobiographical memory, sometimes even his sense of who he is, after psychological trauma or stress, while his ability to learn new information is intact. That is almost the reverse of the Korsakoff pattern.

Table 10.46 Korsakoff's syndrome and the conditions it is confused with.
ConditionOnset and courseMemory pictureThe separating clue
Korsakoff's syndromeAfter Wernicke's encephalopathy, or creeping in; chronicSevere anterograde, graded retrograde; confabulation; digit span normalClear consciousness; drinking or malnutrition; diencephalic damage
Wernicke's encephalopathyAcute, over daysGlobal confusion, poor attentionEye signs and ataxia; responds to thiamine
Delirium tremensAbout 48 to 72 hours after the last drink; lasts daysDisorganised by clouded consciousnessTremor, fever, sweating, vivid visual and tactile hallucinations (Section 10.3)
Alcoholic blackoutDuring heavy intoxicationGap for the drinking episode onlyNormal memory when sober
Alcohol-related dementiaGradual, after years of drinkingMemory loss within a broader decline, executive functions includedGlobal decline; partly reversible with abstinence
Alzheimer's diseaseInsidious, progressive over yearsRecent memory first; then aphasia, apraxia, agnosiaOlder age; no link with drinking; no eye signs (Section 10.9)
Transient global amnesiaSudden; clears within hoursDense anterograde, patchy retrograde; repeated questionsIdentity kept; middle-aged or older; usually a single attack
Dissociative amnesiaAfter psychological stressPersonal past lost, sometimes identity; new learning intactPsychological trigger; no brain lesion (Chapter 7, Section 7.5)

Not all the cognitive damage of heavy drinking is Korsakoff's. Many long-term drinkers develop a slower, broader decline, called alcohol-related dementia when it is severe, in which memory, attention, planning and problem solving all suffer and there is no dense, isolated amnesia; DSM-5-TR names it alcohol-induced major neurocognitive disorder, nonamnestic-confabulatory type. Brain scans of people with chronic alcohol use disorder frequently show cortical thinning, loss of white matter and enlarged sulci and ventricles, and Pinel notes that the commonest finding, even in heavy drinkers without Korsakoff's syndrome, is a general loss of cortical white and grey matter. British clinicians use the umbrella term alcohol-related brain damage for the whole range, because most patients have a mixture: some thiamine damage, some direct toxicity, some head injury, some liver disease, some vascular disease. The hopeful finding is that much of this damage is reversible. In 1978 P. L. Carlen and his colleagues scanned eight chronic drinkers repeatedly with computed tomography; the four who stayed abstinent and improved in function showed partial reversal of their cerebral atrophy, while those who kept drinking did not. DSM-5-TR adds that about 30 to 40 percent of people with alcohol use disorder have a mild neurocognitive disorder in the first two months of abstinence, that substantial or even complete recovery is common in those who achieve stable abstinence before the age of 50, and that the impairment is most likely to become permanent in those who go on drinking past 50. Whether alcohol on its own, without thiamine deficiency, can cause a true dementia is still argued; that stopping drinking is the treatment is not.

Korsakoff's syndrome is the commonest example of a wider group. An amnestic syndrome (or amnesic syndrome) is a disorder in which memory is impaired out of proportion to every other cognitive function, in clear consciousness. ICD-10 defines its organic amnesic syndrome (F04, or F10.6 when alcohol is the cause) by three requirements: a prominent impairment of recent memory, with both anterograde and retrograde amnesia; a history or evidence of injury to or disease of the brain, especially one affecting the diencephalon or the medial temporal lobes on both sides; and the absence of any defect in immediate recall (the digit span), of any disturbance of attention or consciousness, and of global intellectual impairment. Confabulation, lack of insight and apathy often accompany it but are not required. Each exclusion has a purpose: a defect of attention or consciousness would make the diagnosis delirium (Section 10.8), and a global intellectual decline would make it dementia. ICD-11 keeps the category as amnestic disorder (6D72). DSM-5, as Section 10.8 explained, folded it into major or mild neurocognitive disorder, because a major neurocognitive disorder can now be diagnosed when only one cognitive domain is significantly impaired.

The causes of an amnestic syndrome fall into two families, matching the two parts of the memory circuit. Damage to the medial temporal lobes and hippocampi on both sides can come from surgery, as in H.M.; from herpes simplex encephalitis, a viral infection with a liking for the temporal lobes, which destroyed the memory of the musician Clive Wearing in 1985 (his story opens Book 1, Chapter 7); and from lack of oxygen. Pinel tells the case of R.B., who at 52 suffered a period of ischaemia when the pump circulating his blood broke down during heart bypass surgery. He was left amnesic, and after his death in 1983 the damage was found to be largely confined to one strip of the hippocampus, the CA1 field, whose neurons are exceptionally sensitive to the loss of oxygen. Damage to the diencephalon comes from thiamine deficiency, from strokes of the thalamus, from tumours in the region of the third ventricle and from penetrating injuries like N.A.'s. Head injury can damage both, and adds a period of post-traumatic amnesia after the blow.

One amnesia is frightening to watch but harmless. In transient global amnesia, a syndrome named by C. Miller Fisher and the same Raymond Adams, an otherwise healthy middle-aged or older adult suddenly becomes unable to lay down new memories. He knows who he is and can still drive or cook, but he asks the same questions again and again ("What are we doing here? What day is it?"), because each answer vanishes within minutes, and he has a patchy retrograde amnesia for the preceding days or weeks. The attack typically lasts four to six hours and then clears, leaving only a gap for the episode itself, and it seldom recurs. Attacks are often set off by physical exertion, a plunge into cold water, sexual intercourse or emotional stress. Pinel describes it as perhaps the strongest evidence that damage to the hippocampus alone can cause amnesia: small abnormalities have been found on MRI in the CA1 field, appearing some hours after the attack begins and usually gone ten days later, a time course that suggests a brief ischaemia.

Exam trap

When the stem combines chronic drinking with a disorder of memory, choose Korsakoff's syndrome, or Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome when an eye sign or ataxia is mentioned. Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, Huntington's, Pick's and Creutzfeldt-Jakob diseases, vascular and frontotemporal dementia, schizophrenia and multiple sclerosis are not caused by drinking. Within the alcohol family, separate Korsakoff's syndrome (clear consciousness, lasting) from delirium tremens (clouded consciousness, autonomic storm, days) and from a blackout (amnesia only for the drinking episode). And separate organic amnesia (new learning lost, identity kept) from dissociative amnesia (personal past and sometimes identity lost, new learning intact).

Pseudodementia: when depression looks like dementia

Mani, a 68-year-old retired postmaster from Thanjavur, came to the memory clinic on his own initiative, his son trailing behind him. "My brain is finished, doctor," he said. "I forget everything. I think I am getting Alzheimer's." The trouble had begun about two months earlier, fairly suddenly, after a heart attack had left him frightened and housebound. He had stopped reading the newspaper, which he said he could no longer follow, slept badly and woke at four every morning, had lost five kilograms, and felt that he had become a burden on his son. On a bedside cognitive test he scored poorly, but the pattern was odd. To many questions he simply said, "I don't know, I can't," and gave up; when the psychologist encouraged him and gave him time, he got several of them right. He remembered recent events about as well, or as badly, as distant ones, and he described in detail the hospital stay he insisted he had forgotten. He had had a severe depression at 52. Eight weeks after he started an antidepressant his mood had lifted, he was reading again, and his test score was back in the normal range.

Mani shows a picture that has puzzled and misled clinicians for more than a century: an older person whose depression looks like dementia. Pseudodementia is cognitive impairment that resembles dementia but is caused by a psychiatric disorder, most often depression, and that improves when the psychiatric disorder is treated. The phenomenon was known in the late nineteenth century as "melancholic dementia". The word itself (in German, Pseudodemenz) is often credited to Carl Wernicke, but it never appears in his published writings; the earliest printed use that historians have traced is by his student Georg Stertz in 1910, who applied it to a psychogenic state after trauma. Its modern meaning comes from the psychiatrist Leslie Gordon Kiloh, whose paper "Pseudo-dementia" appeared in Acta Psychiatrica Scandinavica in 1961. Kiloh had noticed patients who had been labelled demented and then recovered when their psychiatric illness was treated, and he meant the word to describe a clinical presentation, not a diagnosis: "pseudodementia" tells you what the patient looks like, and you still have to find out what he has. Others disliked the prefix, because the cognitive impairment is perfectly real. Marshal F. Folstein and Paul R. McHugh preferred the dementia syndrome of depression (1978), and modern writers also speak of depression-related cognitive impairment.

Why should depression damage thinking at all? Because depression is not only a disorder of mood. It slows thought and movement (psychomotor retardation), narrows attention, drains the effort that learning demands and weakens the executive functions that organise memory. A depressed person encodes new information shallowly, his attention turned inwards on his own misery, and he retrieves poorly whenever retrieval takes effort. That is why the memory of a depressed patient often improves sharply when he is given cues or a recognition format (choosing the right answer from a list), whereas in Alzheimer's disease the information was never properly stored, so cues and recognition help much less. DSM-5-TR describes the overlap directly: in older people with depression, memory difficulties may be the chief complaint and may be mistaken for early dementia ("pseudodementia"), and when the depressive episode is successfully treated the memory problems often fully abate. In 1979 the American psychiatrist Charles E. Wells listed the clinical features that help separate pseudodementia from dementia. His list, refined by later writers, is the basis of Table 10.47, and it repays careful reading, because nearly every row turns on a single idea: the depressed patient emphasises his failures, while the patient with dementia hides them.

Table 10.47 Depressive pseudodementia and dementia compared (after Wells, 1979, and later reviews).
FeatureDepressive pseudodementiaDementia (Alzheimer type)
OnsetFairly abrupt; can often be datedInsidious and vague
Duration before help is soughtShort, weeks to monthsLong, often years
Past historyPrevious depression commonUsually no past depression
Which comes firstLow mood, then cognitive complaintsCognitive decline, then any mood change
Who complainsThe patient, often bitterly and in detailThe family; the patient minimises or hides the loss
Effort and answersLittle effort; "I don't know"Tries hard; near-miss answers, confabulation or covering up
Mood and biologyDepressed, guilty, hopeless; early waking, weight lossMood often shallow, labile or normal early
Pattern of memory lossRecent and remote memory about equally poor; patchy gaps; cues helpRecent memory worst; consistent pattern; cues help little
Performance across testsVaries from task to task and day to dayConsistently poor on tasks of similar difficulty
Worse at nightUncommonCommon ("sundowning")
Brain imaging and EEGNormal for ageOften atrophy; EEG may be slowed
Treatment of depressionCognition improves with moodLittle gain in cognition

For a long time the story ended there: treat the depression and the "dementia" disappears. Then the patients were followed for longer, and an awkward truth emerged.

Key study: when pseudodementia turns into dementia (1989 to 2019)

In Canada, V. A. Kral and O. B. Emery treated 44 older patients (mean age 76.5) for depressive pseudodementia; when the depression cleared, their cognition returned to its previous level. They then retested the patients every six months for 4 to 18 years, 8 years on average. By the end, 39 of the 44 (89 percent) had developed a dementia of the Alzheimer type. In 1993 George S. Alexopoulos and his colleagues in New York followed 57 older inpatients with major depression for almost three years on average: irreversible dementia developed in 43 percent of those whose depression had come with a "reversible dementia", against 12 percent of those with depression alone, a risk nearly five times higher. In 2019 Michael Connors, L. Quinto and Henry Brodaty pooled 18 follow-up studies covering 284 patients with pseudodementia: a third (33 percent) went on to irreversible dementia, about half (53 percent) no longer met criteria for dementia, and the rest were lost to follow-up. The lesson is double. Pseudodementia is real and often treatable, but in older people it can also be the first sign of a dementing illness, so the patient who recovers must still be followed up.

DSM-5-TR makes the same point in its account of major depression: in some individuals, particularly elderly persons, a major depressive episode may be the first presentation of an irreversible dementia. Depression in late life is now seen as both a risk factor for dementia and, in some people, its earliest symptom, which is why the old question "depression or dementia?" so often turns out to have the answer "both". Depression is also by far the commonest cause of pseudodementia, but not the only one. In the pooled sample of Connors and his colleagues, 238 of the 284 patients were depressed, while 18 had conversion disorder, 14 a psychosis and 11 bipolar disorder. A rarer and more theatrical form is the Ganser syndrome, described by Sigbert Ganser in 1898 among prisoners awaiting trial, in which the person gives approximate answers: asked how many legs a cow has, he says three; asked the colour of snow, he says black. The answers show that he knows the right answer well enough to miss it. The syndrome is now classed with the dissociative disorders (Chapter 7).

Clinical link

Never accept a diagnosis of dementia in an older person until depression has been looked for and, if it is present, treated. The cost of missing it is high: a person written off with an untreatable label when he had a treatable illness, and a depression left to deepen, with all its risk of suicide. The bedside clues are those in Table 10.47; the tests used to measure cognition and late-life depression are covered in the Assessment book, and the treatment of late-life depression, with antidepressants and, when it is severe, electroconvulsive therapy, in Chapter 5. When the patient improves, book the follow-up anyway.

Exam trap

The common cause of pseudodementia is depression (RML 2024). Each distractor fails for its own reason. Schizophrenia brings real cognitive deficits and, in old age, a chronic impoverished picture, but it is not the common cause of a reversible, dementia-like state. Mania can make a patient so distractible that he seems disoriented, and a few such cases are reported, but they are rare. Hypochondriasis (now illness anxiety disorder or somatic symptom disorder, Chapter 7) may bring constant worry about memory, but tested memory is normal: worry without impairment is the opposite of pseudodementia. And remember the two classic contrasts: the depressed patient complains and says "I don't know"; the patient with dementia conceals and gives near misses.

When the body makes the mind ill

Pseudodementia is a psychiatric illness disguised as a brain disease. The rest of this section deals with the opposite disguise: a bodily disease that presents as a psychiatric illness. The brain is an organ of the body, bathed in its hormones, fed by its blood and exposed to its infections and antibodies, so almost any disease of the body can disturb mood, thinking and behaviour. Older psychiatry called such conditions organic, because they had a known physical cause, and set them against the functional disorders, which had none; Section 10.8 explained why DSM-IV dropped the word "organic" and DSM-5 kept it out. The idea survives in the terms secondary mental syndrome and mental disorder due to another medical condition: a psychiatric syndrome judged, on the evidence, to be the direct physiological consequence of a medical illness. The stakes are high. DSM-5-TR estimates that about 60 percent of older people who develop a psychosis for the first time have a medical cause for it.

All three manuals provide for these syndromes. ICD-10 places them in F06, other mental disorders due to brain damage and dysfunction and to physical disease (among them organic hallucinosis, F06.0; organic catatonic disorder, F06.1; organic delusional disorder, F06.2; organic mood disorders, F06.3; and organic anxiety disorder, F06.4), and in F07, personality and behavioural disorders due to brain disease, damage and dysfunction (organic personality disorder, F07.0; postencephalitic syndrome, F07.1; and postconcussional syndrome, F07.2). ICD-11 groups them as secondary mental or behavioural syndromes associated with disorders or diseases classified elsewhere (6E60 to 6E6Z), which include secondary psychotic, mood, anxiety and neurocognitive syndromes and secondary personality change, always coded alongside the medical disease. DSM-5-TR does not gather them into one chapter. Instead, each chapter carries its own version: psychotic disorder, catatonic disorder, bipolar and related disorder, depressive disorder, anxiety disorder and obsessive-compulsive and related disorder due to another medical condition, personality change due to another medical condition, and neurocognitive disorder due to another medical condition.

The DSM-5-TR diagnoses share one template, and each rule in it exists to prevent a particular mistake. First, the psychiatric syndrome must be prominent: depressed mood, anxiety or panic, hallucinations or delusions, a change of personality. Second, there must be evidence from the history, the physical examination or laboratory findings that the syndrome is the direct pathophysiological consequence of a medical condition; suspicion is not enough, and the disease has to be found. Third, the syndrome must not be better explained by another mental disorder. The commonest trap here is adjustment disorder: a woman who becomes miserable and anxious after being told she has cancer is reacting psychologically to the meaning of her illness, and that is not a disorder "due to" the cancer. DSM-5-TR adds that anxiety due to a medical condition often has a prominent physical component, such as breathlessness, while the person with an adjustment disorder is especially distressed about what the illness means. Fourth, the syndrome must not occur only during a delirium, which would simply be diagnosed as delirium (Section 10.8). Fifth, it must cause clinically significant distress or impairment. When the cause is a drug rather than a disease (a steroid tablet, interferon injections, a street drug), the diagnosis becomes a substance/medication-induced disorder instead.

How do you decide that a disease is the cause and not a coincidence? DSM-5-TR admits that there are no infallible rules, but it offers three considerations, set out in Figure 10.21. The first is biological plausibility: a medical condition must be present that is known to be able to produce the syndrome through a physiological mechanism (a severe generalised infection, lupus, porphyria, temporal lobe epilepsy, an overactive thyroid). The second is temporality: the psychiatric syndrome begins, worsens and improves along with the medical disease, and DSM-5-TR calls this the strongest evidence of all. The third is typicality, or rather atypicality: features that would be unusual in the primary psychiatric disorder, such as a first episode at an unusual age, no family history, or hallucinations that are visual, tactile or olfactory instead of auditory. DSM-5-TR notes, for example, that olfactory hallucinations suggest temporal lobe epilepsy.

In practice every new psychiatric presentation deserves a physical examination and a few basic investigations, and some deserve many. The warning signs that should make you look harder are a first episode after middle age with no previous history; a sudden onset; any disturbance of consciousness, attention or orientation; visual, tactile or olfactory hallucinations; seizures, headache, abnormal movements or other neurological signs; physical changes such as weight loss, fever or an altered appearance; and a poor response to treatment that usually works. The investigations follow from the suspicion: a blood count, blood sugar, electrolytes and calcium, liver and kidney function, thyroid function, vitamin B12 and folate, tests for syphilis and HIV, and, where indicated, brain imaging, an electroencephalogram (EEG) or examination of the cerebrospinal fluid. Ordering them is the doctor's job, but a clinical psychologist who notices the warning signs and asks "has he been examined?" can save a patient years of the wrong treatment.

Exam trap

A mental disorder "due to another medical condition" needs evidence of a direct physiological cause (history, examination or laboratory findings). A psychological reaction to being ill is an adjustment disorder; a syndrome caused by a drug or medication is substance/medication-induced; a syndrome that occurs only during a delirium is coded as delirium. "Organic" is the ICD-10 word (F06, F07); "secondary" is the ICD-11 word (6E6); "due to another medical condition" is the DSM-5-TR phrase.

Hormones and the mind: the endocrine disorders

Gurpreet, a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Ludhiana, had been treated for depression for a year with two different antidepressants and was no better. She slept badly, cried easily, snapped at her pupils, could not concentrate on the lessons she prepared, and said she felt "like a stranger in my own body". That phrase made her new psychiatrist look at her body. She had put on twelve kilograms, almost all of it on her face and trunk, while her arms and legs had grown thin; her face had become round and flushed; there were broad purple stretch marks on her abdomen; she bruised easily; and her blood pressure and blood sugar, normal two years earlier, were now high. Her cortisol levels were raised, and an MRI scan showed a small tumour of the pituitary gland. Six months after it was removed, her mood had lifted without any change in her antidepressant.

Cushing's syndrome is the result of prolonged exposure to too much cortisol, the main glucocorticoid hormone of the adrenal cortex. Its commonest cause by far is treatment with corticosteroid drugs; its commonest natural cause is Cushing's disease, a small pituitary tumour that secretes adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) and drives the adrenal glands to overproduce cortisol, as in Gurpreet. The American neurosurgeon Harvey Cushing (1869 to 1939) described the syndrome in 1932 from twelve cases drawn from his own practice and the published literature, and noted "emotional disturbances" among its features from the start. The psychiatric picture is dominated by depression. Major depression affects more than half of patients with active disease (about 50 to 81 percent across the studies reviewed by Rosario Pivonello and colleagues in 2015), and it is typically irritable and anxious, with insomnia, poor concentration and emotional lability. Anxiety and panic are common, and about two thirds of patients have cognitive impairment, especially of memory and concentration, which fits the shrinkage of the hippocampus, a structure dense with cortisol receptors, seen on their scans. Mania or hypomania occurs in a minority (reported rates vary widely), and psychosis is uncommon. The depression is often an early sign of the disease, and it tends to lift when the cortisol excess is corrected, by surgery or by drugs that block cortisol production such as metyrapone and ketoconazole, more reliably than with antidepressants, although some symptoms and some loss of brain volume can persist after cure.

Corticosteroid drugs such as prednisolone and dexamethasone produce a slightly different picture, because the dose is often high and starts suddenly. In the first days of treatment the commonest effects are euphoria, insomnia and restlessness, and some patients become hypomanic or manic; with long-term use depression becomes more common, and stopping steroids abruptly can bring fatigue and low mood. The risk climbs steeply with the dose. In a classic 1972 survey of 676 patients by the Boston Collaborative Drug Surveillance Program, psychiatric reactions occurred in 1.3 percent of those taking 40 mg of prednisone a day or less, 4.6 percent of those taking 41 to 80 mg, and 18.4 percent of those taking more than 80 mg. Strictly these are substance/medication-induced disorders, a distinction that matters for coding but not for the patient.

The opposite disorder, too little cortisol, was described by the London physician Thomas Addison (1793 to 1860) in 1855. In Addison's disease the adrenal cortex is destroyed, most often by an autoimmune process and, where tuberculosis is common, as in India, still often by tuberculosis. It comes on slowly, and its early symptoms are easy to mistake for depression or even anorexia nervosa: fatigue, weakness, apathy, low mood, irritability, poor appetite and weight loss. The clues are physical: low blood pressure with dizziness on standing, a craving for salt, and darkening of the skin, especially in the creases of the palms, in scars and inside the mouth. Untreated, an infection or injury can tip the patient into an adrenal crisis, with collapse and delirium.

The thyroid gland sets the pace of the body's metabolism, and the mind keeps time with it. In hypothyroidism, an underactive thyroid, everything slows: thinking, speech and movement grow sluggish, memory and concentration fail, and the patient feels tired, cold, constipated and low. The skin is dry, the hair coarse and thin, the voice hoarse, the face puffy, and weight goes up. In older people the result can look exactly like dementia, and hypothyroidism is one of the reversible causes that must be excluded before dementia is diagnosed (Section 10.10). Depression is the commonest psychiatric picture, and DSM-5-TR names hypothyroidism, together with Cushing's syndrome, as the endocrine conditions most closely associated with depression. In severe, untreated cases a psychosis can develop, with paranoid delusions, hallucinations and confusion. In 1949 the London physician Richard Asher (1912 to 1969), who two years later named Munchausen syndrome, described fourteen such patients in the British Medical Journal under the title "Myxoedematous madness", and the phrase myxoedema madness has stuck (myxoedema is the old name for severe hypothyroidism, from the swollen, waterlogged look of the skin). His message was that a treatable cause was hiding behind the madness. Lithium, the mood stabiliser, can itself cause hypothyroidism (Chapter 5), which is why thyroid function is checked in people who take it.

In hyperthyroidism (thyrotoxicosis, most often from Graves' disease) the pace quickens. The patient is anxious, restless, irritable and unable to sit still, sleeps poorly, has a fine tremor and a racing or irregular heart, sweats, cannot bear the heat, and loses weight in spite of a large appetite. The picture is easily mistaken for generalised anxiety disorder or panic disorder, and occasionally for mania; the clues are the weight loss with a good appetite, the heat intolerance and, in Graves' disease, staring, protruding eyes. In older people, hyperthyroidism can instead produce an "apathetic" picture of fatigue, withdrawal and low mood that passes for depression.

Three more endocrine disorders complete Table 10.48. In hyperparathyroidism an overactive parathyroid gland raises the calcium in the blood, and hypercalcaemia causes fatigue, depression, poor concentration and, when severe, psychosis or delirium, along with kidney stones, bone pain and abdominal pain, summed up by generations of medical students as "stones, bones, groans and psychic moans". A phaeochromocytoma is a rare tumour, usually of the adrenal medulla, that releases adrenaline and noradrenaline in bursts; each burst brings a pounding headache, sweating, palpitations, tremor, pallor and a sense of dread with a sharp rise in blood pressure, so like a panic attack that DSM-5-TR uses anxiety disorder due to this tumour as its coding example. And hypoglycaemia, from an insulin-secreting tumour of the pancreas or, far more often, from too much insulin in a person with diabetes, causes episodes of anxiety, sweating, tremor, hunger, confusion and strange behaviour that end within minutes of taking sugar.

Table 10.48 Psychiatric syndromes of endocrine and metabolic disease.
ConditionPsychiatric pictureKey clue
Cushing's syndrome (too much cortisol)Depression (commonest), anxiety, irritability, cognitive impairment; mania or psychosis less oftenCentral obesity, round face, purple striae, bruising, high blood pressure and sugar; raised cortisol
Corticosteroid treatmentEarly euphoria, insomnia, hypomania or mania; depression with long use or withdrawal; psychosisDose-related; taking prednisolone or dexamethasone
Addison's disease (too little cortisol)Fatigue, apathy, depression, irritabilityWeight loss, low blood pressure, salt craving, darkened skin
HypothyroidismDepression, slowing, poor memory, reversible dementia; "myxoedema madness"Cold intolerance, weight gain, dry skin, hoarse voice; raised TSH
HyperthyroidismAnxiety, restlessness, irritability, insomnia; mimics GAD or panic; occasionally maniaWeight loss with a good appetite, heat intolerance, tremor, fast pulse, eye signs
HyperparathyroidismFatigue, depression, poor concentration; psychosis or delirium if severeHigh calcium; kidney stones, bone pain
PhaeochromocytomaAttacks like panic attacksEpisodic headache, sweating and palpitations with surges of blood pressure
HypoglycaemiaEpisodes of anxiety, confusion, odd behaviourSweating, hunger, tremor; relieved by sugar; insulin use or insulinoma
Indian context

Hypothyroidism is common in India. In 2013 A. G. Unnikrishnan and colleagues tested 5,360 adults in eight cities (Bangalore, Chennai, Delhi, Goa, Mumbai, Hyderabad, Ahmedabad and Kolkata) and found hypothyroidism in 10.95 percent, about one adult in ten; for nearly a third of them (3.47 percent of the whole sample) it had never been detected. It was three times as common in women as in men (15.86 versus 5.02 percent) and commoner in older adults and in inland cities. Anyone assessing low mood, fatigue or memory complaints in an Indian clinic, especially in a woman or an older adult, should make sure the thyroid has been tested.

Exam trap

Cushing's syndrome most commonly produces depression, with anxiety and cognitive impairment, and occasionally psychosis (MET Mock 2). "Mania only" is wrong: mania occurs, more often with steroid drugs than with the natural disease, but depression is far commoner. Catatonia (stupor, mutism, posturing, negativism; Chapter 4) can be caused by medical illness, classically by encephalitis such as anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis, but it is not the typical picture of Cushing's. A selective loss of smell points elsewhere: to head injury, to a tumour under the frontal lobes, and to the early stages of Parkinson's and Alzheimer's diseases. Do not swap Cushing's (too much cortisol) with Addison's (too little: apathy, fatigue and low mood with low blood pressure and darkened skin).

Deficiencies, infections and immune attacks on the brain

Vitamin B12 (cobalamin) comes only from foods of animal origin, which matters in a country where many people are lifelong vegetarians. Deficiency, from a vegetarian diet, from pernicious anaemia (an autoimmune failure to absorb the vitamin), from stomach surgery or from long-term use of drugs such as metformin, damages the blood, the spinal cord, the peripheral nerves and the brain. The blood shows a megaloblastic anaemia, with large, immature red cells. The spinal cord suffers subacute combined degeneration, in which the tracts that carry position and vibration sense and the motor tracts degenerate together, so the patient becomes numb, unsteady in the dark and weak. The mind suffers fatigue, irritability, depression, slowed thinking, a reversible dementia and occasionally a psychosis with paranoid delusions and hallucinations, which A. D. Smith called "megaloblastic madness" in 1960. Two clinical points matter most. The neuropsychiatric damage can come before the anaemia, or without it, so a normal blood count does not rule out B12 deficiency; and the damage becomes permanent if treatment with B12 injections is delayed. Folate deficiency, from a poor diet, heavy drinking or drugs such as phenytoin and methotrexate, also causes a megaloblastic anaemia and is associated with depression; in pregnancy it causes neural tube defects in the baby.

Pellagra, the deficiency of niacin (vitamin B3), is remembered by its three Ds: dermatitis (a rough, darkened rash on sun-exposed skin, often in a band around the neck), diarrhoea and dementia, with a fourth D, death, if it is not treated. Early on the mental changes are depression, anxiety, irritability and insomnia; later come confusion, delirium and dementia. Pellagra was epidemic in the maize-eating American South until Joseph Goldberger (1874 to 1929) of the United States Public Health Service showed, from 1914 onwards, that it was caused by diet and not by infection. In India it was found among people who lived on jowar (sorghum), and in 1960 Coluthur Gopalan and S. G. Srikantia, working in Hyderabad, proposed that an excess of the amino acid leucine in jowar was partly to blame. Today it is seen mainly in heavy drinkers, in people with malabsorption, and in patients taking the anti-tuberculosis drug isoniazid. DSM-5-TR lists deficiencies of thiamine and niacin among the nutritional causes of neurocognitive disorder due to another medical condition.

Of all the medical causes of mental illness, one has the most remarkable history. In the nineteenth century the asylums of Europe and America were crowded with patients suffering from general paresis of the insane (general paralysis, or GPI). It began, often in middle age, with a change of personality and poor judgement, sometimes with wildly grandiose delusions (the patient announced that he was a millionaire or an emperor), although a simple, progressive dementia and depression were in fact the commoner pictures. It went on to paralysis and death within a few years. A classic sign was the Argyll Robertson pupil: small, irregular pupils that constrict when the eyes focus on a near object but not when a light is shone into them.
