The cause, syphilis, was long suspected and long disputed.

Key study: from Bayle to the Nobel Prize (1822 to 1927)

In 1822 the French physician Antoine-Laurent Bayle (1799 to 1858) described general paralysis as a distinct disease with a characteristic inflammation of the membranes covering the brain. Ninety years later, in 1913, Hideyo Noguchi (1876 to 1928) and J. W. Moore, working at the Rockefeller Institute and a New York state hospital, demonstrated the syphilis spirochaete, Treponema pallidum, in the brains of patients who had died of general paralysis. For the first time a major mental illness had a proven physical cause: a germ. Treatment came from an odd observation, that patients sometimes improved after a fever. In 1917 the Viennese psychiatrist Julius Wagner-Jauregg (1857 to 1940) deliberately infected patients with malaria, letting the fever attack the heat-sensitive spirochaete before curing the malaria with quinine, and many improved. This malaria therapy won him the Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine in 1927, the first awarded to a psychiatrist. Penicillin, from the 1940s, made it obsolete.

Neurosyphilis has not vanished. It can still present as depression, mania, psychosis or dementia, which is why a blood test for syphilis remains part of the investigation of a new dementia or a late-onset psychosis. The human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) also enters the brain early. Section 10.10 describes HIV-associated neurocognitive disorder; in addition, depression is the commonest psychiatric problem in people living with HIV, mania can appear late in the illness, opportunistic infections of the brain can cause delirium and psychosis, and some antiretroviral drugs, efavirenz in particular, cause vivid dreams, insomnia and low mood. Herpes simplex encephalitis attacks the temporal lobes, as it did in Clive Wearing, leaving a dense amnesia and sometimes a change of personality, and other infections common in India, tuberculous meningitis, Japanese encephalitis and cerebral malaria among them, can leave lasting cognitive and behavioural change (ICD-10's postencephalitic syndrome, F07.1). Wilson's disease, an inherited disorder of copper metabolism described by Samuel Alexander Kinnier Wilson in 1912, deserves a line of its own: it can present in adolescence or early adulthood with a change of personality, mood disorder or psychosis, sometimes before the tremor and liver disease are obvious, and the golden-brown Kayser-Fleischer ring at the edge of the cornea gives it away.

The immune system can also turn on the brain. Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is an autoimmune disease, commoner in women and usually beginning in young adulthood, in which antibodies attack the skin, joints, kidneys, blood and brain. When it involves the nervous system (neuropsychiatric lupus) it can cause headache, seizures, strokes, cognitive impairment, depression, anxiety, delirium and psychosis. Two things complicate the picture: the high-dose steroids that control lupus can themselves cause mania, depression or psychosis, and a painful, disfiguring chronic illness brings its own psychological burden. The clues are the butterfly rash across the cheeks, joint pains, mouth ulcers, kidney disease and a positive antinuclear antibody test.

The newest member of this family was discovered in 2007, and it has changed how psychiatrists think about a first psychosis in a young person. Josep Dalmau and his colleagues in Philadelphia described twelve women, aged 14 to 44, who developed prominent psychiatric symptoms, then memory loss, seizures, abnormal involuntary movements of the face and limbs (dyskinesias), instability of pulse, blood pressure and breathing, and a falling level of consciousness that often needed a ventilator. All had antibodies in the blood and cerebrospinal fluid against the NMDA (N-methyl-D-aspartate) glutamate receptor, and eleven had a teratoma of the ovary, a tumour that contained nerve tissue carrying the same receptor: the immune response against the tumour had turned against the brain. After the tumour was removed and immunotherapy given, eight of the nine patients so treated improved or recovered fully. Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis is now known to be one of the commonest autoimmune encephalitides. Most patients are young women; many are first seen by a psychiatrist, with agitation, paranoia, hallucinations, mood change or catatonia, before the neurological signs appear; and the illness responds to treatment. A young person with a first psychosis who also has seizures, abnormal movements, catatonia, autonomic instability or an unusually severe reaction to antipsychotic drugs should be tested for it. The American journalist Susannah Cahalan told her own story of the illness in the memoir Brain on Fire. Table 10.49 gathers these conditions, with the neurological ones of Section 10.11.10.

Table 10.49 Psychiatric syndromes of deficiency, infection, immune and neurological disease.
ConditionPsychiatric pictureKey clue
Thiamine (B1) deficiencyWernicke's confusion; Korsakoff's amnesia and confabulationEye signs, ataxia; drinking, vomiting, malnutrition
Vitamin B12 deficiencyDepression, irritability, slowed thinking, reversible dementia, psychosisVegetarian diet or pernicious anaemia; numb, unsteady legs; anaemia may be absent
Folate deficiencyDepression, cognitive impairmentPoor diet, alcohol, phenytoin; megaloblastic anaemia
Pellagra (niacin, B3)Depression and anxiety, then delirium and dementiaRash on sun-exposed skin, diarrhoea; drinkers, isoniazid
Neurosyphilis (general paresis)Personality change, grandiosity, depression, dementiaArgyll Robertson pupils; positive syphilis tests
HIV infectionDepression, late mania, psychosis, neurocognitive disorderRisk history; HIV test; opportunistic infections
Herpes simplex encephalitisAcute confusion, then dense amnesia and behavioural changeFever, seizures, temporal lobe changes on MRI
Systemic lupus erythematosusCognitive impairment, depression, psychosis, deliriumYoung woman; rash, joint pains, kidney disease; steroids confound
Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitisAgitation, psychosis, catatonia, then amnesiaYoung woman; seizures, dyskinesias, autonomic instability; ovarian teratoma
Wilson's diseasePersonality change, mood disorder or psychosis in a young personTremor, liver disease, Kayser-Fleischer rings
Multiple sclerosisDepression, cognitive slowing, euphoria in advanced diseaseRelapsing neurological episodes; white matter plaques on MRI
Temporal lobe epilepsyIctal fear, déjà vu, smell hallucinations; postictal and interictal psychosisBrief stereotyped attacks; EEG
Frontal tumour or injuryDisinhibition or apathy, poor judgement, personality changeHeadache, seizures, focal signs, loss of smell; imaging
Exam trap

Keep the pairings straight. B12 deficiency damages cord and mind and can occur without anaemia; niacin deficiency is pellagra, with its three Ds; thiamine deficiency is Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome. General paresis is neurosyphilis: Bayle described it in 1822, Noguchi and Moore found the spirochaete in the brain in 1913, and Wagner-Jauregg won the 1927 Nobel Prize for malaria fever therapy, not for insulin coma (Manfred Sakel, 1933) or electroconvulsive therapy (Ugo Cerletti and Lucio Bini, 1938). Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis (Dalmau, 2007) is the young woman with a first psychosis followed by seizures and abnormal movements, often with an ovarian teratoma.

Epilepsy, brain injury and the organic personality

Epilepsy, a tendency to recurrent unprovoked seizures, is not itself a mental disorder (nor, as Section 10.10 stressed, a neurodegenerative one), but its ties to psychiatry are old and close. Its psychiatric symptoms are described by their timing in relation to the seizures. Ictal symptoms are part of the seizure itself. A focal seizure beginning in the temporal lobe may open with a rising feeling in the stomach, sudden fear, a dreamy state, déjà vu (the feeling that a new experience has happened before) or jamais vu, depersonalisation, or a brief and often unpleasant hallucination of smell or taste, such as burning rubber, before awareness clouds and automatisms such as lip-smacking and fumbling begin. Because such attacks are brief, stereotyped and come out of the blue, they can be mistaken for panic attacks or dissociation. Postictal psychosis is the commonest of the epileptic psychoses, seen in 2 to 7.8 percent of people with epilepsy according to DSM-5-TR. Typically, after a cluster of seizures and a clear interval of hours to a few days, the patient develops delusions, hallucinations and often a disturbance of mood, which last days to weeks. Interictal psychosis occurs between seizures and is not tied to them in time. In 1963 Eliot Slater, A. W. Beard and E. Glithero described these "schizophrenia-like psychoses of epilepsy", which usually arise after many years of temporal lobe epilepsy and resemble paranoid schizophrenia, although emotional warmth tends to be better preserved. Depression, anxiety and suicide are all commoner in people with epilepsy than in the general population, and some antiepileptic drugs add mood and behavioural side effects of their own.

A more controversial idea is that of an interictal personality. In 1975 Stephen G. Waxman and Norman Geschwind (1926 to 1984) described a distinctive pattern of behaviour between seizures in many people with temporal lobe epilepsy: altered sexual behaviour (usually reduced interest), intense religiosity, and a tendency to write and draw extensively, sometimes compulsively (hypergraphia). Later accounts added "viscosity", a sticky, long-winded, over-detailed way of talking that makes conversations hard to end. The Geschwind syndrome (the interictal behaviour syndrome) is often quoted, but controlled studies disagree about how specific it is, and it should never be used to stereotype people with epilepsy.

A brain tumour can present to a psychiatrist long before it presents to a neurologist, especially when it grows slowly in a "silent" region. Tumours of the frontal lobes (a meningioma growing beneath them is the classic example) cause apathy, disinhibition, poor judgement and a change of personality that can pass for depression, mania or simple bad behaviour, and such a tumour may also destroy the sense of smell. Temporal lobe tumours cause seizures, psychosis and mood change, and tumours near the third ventricle cause amnesia. The warning signs are a new headache that is worse in the morning, vomiting, seizures, focal neurological signs, and a psychiatric illness that does not fit its usual pattern. Head injury brings post-traumatic amnesia; the postconcussional syndrome (ICD-10 F07.2), in which headache, dizziness, fatigue, irritability, poor concentration and memory, insomnia and a reduced tolerance of stress follow a head injury; and, when the frontal and temporal lobes are bruised, a lasting change of personality (Section 10.10 covers traumatic brain injury as a neurocognitive disorder). Stroke brings depression to many survivors; DSM-5-TR notes that depression beginning within days of a stroke is most strongly linked to left frontal lesions, and that the depressive episodes after stroke last 9 to 11 months on average.

When brain disease changes a person's character, families say the same thing in every language: "He is not the same man." DSM-5-TR uses almost those words, noting that the person is often described by others as "not himself". ICD-10 calls the condition organic personality disorder (F07.0): a significant change in a person's habitual pattern of behaviour, especially in the expression of emotions, needs and impulses, caused by disease, damage or dysfunction of the brain. Its features include a reduced ability to persevere with goal-directed activity; emotional lability, shallow and unwarranted cheerfulness, or irritability with brief outbursts of anger; apathy; the expression of needs and impulses without regard for consequences or social convention (crude jokes, sexual remarks, overeating, neglect of hygiene); suspiciousness; and changes in the rate and flow of speech. ICD-10 includes the frontal lobe syndrome under this heading. DSM-5-TR's equivalent is personality change due to another medical condition, also coded F07.0: a persistent personality disturbance that is a change from the person's previous pattern (in children, a marked deviation from normal development lasting at least a year), with evidence that a medical condition caused it. It is specified by its predominant feature as the labile, disinhibited, aggressive, apathetic, paranoid, other, combined or unspecified type. The diagnosis is not given when the change is a psychological reaction (a man who becomes dependent on his wife after a head injury because he needs her help) or when it occurs only during a delirium. And unlike the personality disorders of Chapter 11, which are lifelong patterns visible by early adulthood, a personality change due to a medical condition has a definite onset after an illness or injury; ICD-11 calls it secondary personality change.

Frontal lobe damage is the classic cause, and it comes in three broad forms, because the prefrontal cortex is not one region but several, each joined in a loop with the basal ganglia and thalamus. Jeffrey L. Cummings, describing these frontal-subcortical circuits in 1993, matched three of them to three behavioural syndromes (Table 10.50). Damage to the orbitofrontal cortex, on the underside of the frontal lobes above the eyes, produces the disinhibited syndrome: the patient is impulsive, tactless, socially inappropriate and emotionally shallow, with a facetious, punning humour that German neurologists called Witzelsucht (joking addiction) and a silly euphoria once called moria. Damage to the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex produces the dysexecutive syndrome: the patient cannot plan, organise, shift strategy or think abstractly, and keeps repeating the same response (perseveration), although his memory and intelligence scores may be normal. Damage to the medial frontal cortex and anterior cingulate produces the apathetic syndrome: the patient lacks drive and spontaneity, speaks and moves little and shows no interest in anything (abulia, a loss of will), and in the extreme lies awake and silent, following people with his eyes, a state called akinetic mutism. The frontal lobe syndrome is the general name for these pictures, which in practice often overlap.

Table 10.50 The three frontal lobe syndromes.
SyndromeRegion damagedWhat you see
DisinhibitedOrbitofrontal cortexImpulsivity, tactlessness, sexual and social disinhibition, shallow euphoria, Witzelsucht, poor judgement
DysexecutiveDorsolateral prefrontal cortexPoor planning and organisation, inflexibility, perseveration, concrete thinking
ApatheticMedial frontal cortex and anterior cingulateApathy, abulia, little speech or movement; akinetic mutism at the extreme

The most famous patient in the history of neuropsychology belongs here. On 13 September 1848 Phineas Gage (1823 to 1860), a railway construction foreman in Vermont, was tamping explosive powder into a hole in rock when it went off and drove the iron rod, more than a metre long, up through his left cheek and out of the top of his head, destroying much of his left frontal lobe. He survived. His physician, John Martyn Harlow (1819 to 1907), reported in 1868 that the efficient, capable foreman had become fitful, irreverent, profane, impatient of advice and unable to carry through his plans, so that his friends said he was "no longer Gage". The details have been argued over (the historian Malcolm Macmillan showed that many later accounts exaggerated the change, and that Gage later worked for years as a stagecoach driver in Chile), but the case remains the first clear evidence that the frontal lobes govern personality and social conduct. Book 1 and the Physiological book tell it in full; for this chapter, Gage is simply the first well-documented personality change due to another medical condition.

Exam trap

Organic personality disorder (ICD-10 F07.0) and personality change due to another medical condition (DSM-5-TR) are a change from a previous personality caused by brain disease, not a lifelong personality disorder (Chapter 11). Orbitofrontal damage disinhibits (Witzelsucht, moria); dorsolateral damage disorganises (the dysexecutive syndrome); medial frontal damage demotivates (apathy, abulia, akinetic mutism). Postictal psychosis is the commonest epileptic psychosis; olfactory hallucinations point to temporal lobe epilepsy; and epilepsy itself is neither a mental disorder nor neurodegenerative.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  3. Harper's autopsy series found the full triad recorded in only percent of cases.
  4. Filling gaps in memory with false memories, without any intent to deceive, is called .
  5. The two diencephalic structures most consistently damaged in Korsakoff's syndrome are the mammillary bodies and the nucleus of the thalamus.
  6. The principle that the newest memories are lost first and the oldest last is 's law.
  7. Claparède's hidden pin showed that a Korsakoff patient could retain memory without conscious recollection.
  8. The term pseudodementia was given its modern meaning by in 1961, and its commonest cause is .

Answers: (1) thiamine, B1, (2) ophthalmoplegia, (3) 16, (4) confabulation, (5) dorsomedial (mediodorsal), (6) Ribot, (7) implicit, (8) Leslie Kiloh, depression, (9) myxoedematous (myxoedema) madness, (10) general paresis of the insane (neurosyphilis).

The case of Jimmie G., revisited

Read Jimmie's story again with Section 10.11 open beside it. Everything Sacks found fits Korsakoff's syndrome: a chronic disorder of memory, caused by thiamine deficiency and seen most often in heavy drinkers, in which the ability to learn new information is severely impaired while alertness, immediate memory and general intelligence are relatively preserved. The core was anterograde amnesia. Information got in but did not stay: the watch, the tie and the glasses under the cover were gone after a minute's chat, the doctor who had shown him the mirror was a stranger two minutes later, and the diary entries in his own handwriting were unrecognisable. Behind it lay a retrograde amnesia with the temporal gradient that Albert, Butters and Levin measured in 1979 and that Ribot's law predicts: his schooldays, his mathematics and his submarine were vivid, but the twenty years of navy service that followed, up to 1965, were gone, so that in 1975 he was sure the year was 1945 and that he was nineteen. What the syndrome leaves alone, he kept. He was alert, warm and attentive, he did excellently on intelligence testing and he could become keenly involved in a game. That is why the word "demented" on his transfer note was wrong: Korsakoff's syndrome is an amnestic syndrome, memory impaired out of proportion to every other cognitive function in clear consciousness, not the global intellectual decline of dementia or the clouded attention of delirium (Sections 10.8 and 10.11.5).

The chapter also explains how a clever, competent man came to this. Heavy drinking starves the brain of thiamine in four ways at once (alcohol brings calories without vitamins, cuts absorption from the gut, weakens the liver's store and comes with vomiting and bouts of near starvation), and the neurons that die first lie around the third and fourth ventricles, in the mammillary bodies and the dorsomedial thalamus, so that his was a diencephalic amnesia, not the hippocampal amnesia of Henry Molaison. His sudden illness around Christmas 1970, when he became deliriously excited and confused, fits the course that Victor, Adams and Collins described in 1971, in which the confusion of the acute stage clears to reveal the amnesia it had hidden, although, as Harper's autopsies suggest, many patients reach the chronic stage through repeated small episodes of deficiency that nobody notices. Sacks's nine years of observation, in which Jimmie's memory did not change at all, match Pinel's verdict that thiamine limits further damage but that the dysfunction already acquired is mostly irreversible. Luria's reply anticipated the second aim of care in Section 10.11.4, helping the person to live with the memory that remains. In the garden, where Jimmie took over some of the work and almost never lost his way, he showed the kind of learning that Korsakoff's syndrome spares, the implicit and procedural learning that Claparède's pin first revealed and that grows best by doing the same thing in the same place many times over; in chapel, wholly held and absorbed, he showed what Luria meant. A man does not consist of memory alone. But the cheapest lesson of the case is the one in the Clinical link of Section 10.11.2: a few rupees' worth of thiamine, given in time, can prevent a lifetime of amnesia.

Chapter summary

10.1 Drugs, Dependence and Addiction: The Vocabulary and the Manuals. A psychoactive substance alters mood, cognition, perception or behaviour by acting on the brain; use, misuse, hazardous use and harmful use are different things. Intoxication, withdrawal (the mirror image of the drug's effect) and tolerance (metabolic or functional) are the building blocks. Cross-tolerance among alcohol, barbiturates and benzodiazepines is the basis of benzodiazepine detoxification, cross-dependence lets one drug suppress another's withdrawal, sensitisation is reverse tolerance, and physical dependence is not addiction. Edwards and Gross described the alcohol dependence syndrome in 1976. ICD-10's F10 to F19 block runs from .0 acute intoxication to .7 residual and late-onset psychotic disorder, with no "drug abuse". DSM-5 merged abuse and dependence into substance use disorder (2 or more of 11 criteria; mild, moderate, severe; craving added, legal problems dropped). ICD-11 adds the episode of harmful use and places gambling and gaming disorder among the addictive behaviours. In India, alcohol leads, then cannabis and opioids.

10.2 Why People Become Addicted: Reward, Learning, Genes and Personality. Rush and Trotter called habitual drunkenness a disease, Huss coined "alcoholism" (1849), and Jellinek's The Disease Concept of Alcoholism (1960) named five species, of which gamma (loss of control) and delta (inability to abstain) were diseases. Olds and Milner found intracranial self-stimulation (1954), and Di Chiara and Imperato (1988) showed that abused drugs raise dopamine in the nucleus accumbens, the target of the mesolimbic pathway from the ventral tegmental area. Robinson and Berridge separated wanting from liking. Positive and negative reinforcement, Wikler's conditioned withdrawal, Siegel's conditioned tolerance and Solomon and Corbit's opponent process explain much of relapse. Marlatt's balanced-placebo design exposed the power of expectancy, and Kandel's stages (1975) made marijuana the classic gateway. Tobacco captures the largest share of its users. Cloninger described type I and type II alcoholism, heritability is about half, and Khantzian's self-medication hypothesis and Freud's oral fixation complete the picture.

10.3 Alcohol: The Drug, Intoxication, Withdrawal and the Damage Done. The liver turns ethanol into acetaldehyde (alcohol dehydrogenase) and then acetate (aldehyde dehydrogenase) at a roughly constant rate of about one drink an hour. Alcohol is a GABA-A positive modulator, functionally an agonist, and an NMDA antagonist; Steele and Josephs's alcohol myopia explains its varied effects on behaviour. Tolerance is mostly functional, and repeated withdrawals kindle worse ones (Ballenger and Post, 1978). Isbell's "rum fits" (1955) proved that stopping causes withdrawal: tremor from 6 to 24 hours, seizures and alcoholic hallucinosis (mainly auditory, in clear consciousness) at 12 to 48 hours, and delirium tremens, named by Sutton in 1813, at 48 to 72 hours, with clouded consciousness, visual and tactile hallucinations, formication and autonomic overactivity. Heavy drinking also brings depression, morbid jealousy (the Othello syndrome), suicide and damage to almost every organ. The CAGE and AUDIT detect problem drinking, and alcohol is the substance Indians use most.

10.4 Treating Alcohol Use Disorder: Detoxification, Drugs and Staying Sober. Vaillant's natural history (1983) showed a variable course, frequent natural recovery and few relapses after five years of abstinence. Detoxification uses long-acting benzodiazepines (chlordiazepoxide, diazepam), relying on cross-tolerance, or lorazepam and oxazepam in liver disease and old age, with thiamine before glucose; Saitz (1994) showed that symptom-triggered dosing with the CIWA-Ar uses less drug, and lorazepam, not phenytoin, prevents repeat withdrawal seizures. Disulfiram, discovered by Hald and Jacobsen (1948), inhibits aldehyde dehydrogenase so that acetaldehyde accumulates and drinking brings flushing, nausea and palpitations. Naltrexone, an opioid antagonist, blunts the reward of drinking, and acamprosate modulates glutamate, though it showed no effect in COMBINE (2006). Motivational interviewing, relapse prevention, the community reinforcement approach and Alcoholics Anonymous (founded 1935) work about equally well, as Project MATCH found, and the Sobells' controlled-drinking study was challenged by Pendery and colleagues in 1982.

10.5 Opioids: From Opium to Heroin, and Their Treatment. Opium is the dried latex of Papaver somniferum. Sydenham popularised laudanum, De Quincey wrote the first great account of addiction (1821), Sertürner isolated morphine (about 1804), and heroin was first made by Alder Wright (1874) and sold by Bayer from 1898. Morphine, codeine, thebaine, papaverine and noscapine are natural alkaloids; heroin (diacetylmorphine) is semi-synthetic, made from morphine; methadone, pethidine, fentanyl and tramadol are synthetic. Pert and Snyder found the opiate receptor (1973), and the mu receptor carries euphoria, analgesia, respiratory depression and miosis. Overdose is coma, pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression, reversed by naloxone. Withdrawal, the mirror image, brings yawning, lacrimation, rhinorrhoea, dilated pupils, piloerection, diarrhoea and aches, but is far less dangerous than alcohol or barbiturate withdrawal. Robins's Vietnam study showed how strongly environment controls use. Treatment combines detoxification (clonidine), Dole and Nyswander's methadone maintenance (1965), the partial agonist buprenorphine and the antagonist naltrexone.

10.6 Stimulants, Tobacco and Caffeine. Niemann named cocaine in 1860; Freud praised it in "Über Coca" (1884) and Koller made it a local anaesthetic, but the drug Freud could not give up was tobacco. Cocaine blocks the reuptake of dopamine and noradrenaline and amphetamines force their release, and smoked crack and freebase reach the brain fastest. Intoxication brings euphoria and dilated pupils; Connell (1958) showed that amphetamine psychosis can be indistinguishable from paranoid schizophrenia, and formication ("cocaine bugs", Magnan's sign) is its hallmark. Gawin and Kleber (1986) described crash, withdrawal and extinction. MDMA releases serotonin and counts in DSM-5-TR as a hallucinogen. No medicine is approved for stimulant use disorder, and Higgins's vouchers work best. Nicotine acts on nicotinic acetylcholine receptors, Doll and Hill linked smoking to lung cancer, and varenicline, a partial agonist, is the most effective single medicine for stopping. Caffeine blocks adenosine receptors; DSM-5-TR has no caffeine use disorder.

10.7 Cannabis, Hallucinogens, Inhalants and the Sedative-Hypnotics. Cannabis comes as bhang (leaves), ganja (flowering tops, marijuana) and charas (resin, hashish), and the Indian Hemp Drugs Commission (1893 to 1894) found moderate use practically harmless. Mechoulam and Gaoni identified THC (1964), and anandamide followed in 1992. Intoxication brings red eyes, hunger and a fast pulse; DSM-5 added cannabis withdrawal in 2013; McGlothlin and West named the amotivational syndrome (1968); and Andréasson's Swedish conscripts (1987) began the evidence linking heavy use to psychosis. Hofmann synthesised LSD in 1938 and found its effects in 1943; the classic hallucinogens act at 5-HT2A receptors, depend on set and setting and can leave hallucinogen persisting perception disorder. Phencyclidine and ketamine block the NMDA receptor, and inhalants are the drug of the very young and poor. Benzodiazepines increase the frequency and barbiturates the duration of GABA-A channel opening; cross-tolerance makes mixtures with alcohol dangerous, and sedative withdrawal can kill.

10.8 From Organic Brain Syndrome to Neurocognitive Disorder: The Cognitive Domains and Delirium. The old split between organic and functional disorders ended when DSM-IV dropped "organic", and DSM-5 replaced dementia and amnestic disorder with major and mild neurocognitive disorder, keeping delirium; ICD-10 keeps F00 to F09. DSM-5-TR assesses six domains: complex attention, executive function, learning and memory, language, perceptual-motor function and social cognition. Classical deficits pair with functions: dementia with abstraction, amnesia with memory, aphasia, alexia and acalculia with language, apraxia with skilled movement, agnosia with recognition. Delirium, Lipowski's acute brain failure, is an acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention and awareness, worse at night, hyperactive, hypoactive or mixed, with asterixis, multifocal myoclonus, carphologia and diffuse EEG slowing (Engel and Romano, 1959). It always has a physical cause, arises from predisposing and precipitating factors (Inouye), is the commonest psychiatric syndrome after cardiac surgery and differs from dementia by its acute onset, fluctuation and clouded attention.

10.9 Alzheimer's Disease and Mild Neurocognitive Disorder. Alzheimer described the plaques and tangles of Auguste Deter, admitted in 1901 at 51, in 1906; Kraepelin named the disease in 1910, and Katzman (1976) argued that presenile and senile forms are one disease. The course is insidious and gradual: episodic memory first, then language, praxis and visuospatial skill. Beta-amyloid plaques outside neurons and tau tangles inside them spread through the Braak stages from the entorhinal cortex, and the loss of the cholinergic neurons of the nucleus basalis of Meynert grounds the cholinergic hypothesis, while Hardy and Higgins proposed the amyloid cascade (1992). APP, presenilin 1 and 2 cause early-onset familial disease, Down syndrome brings it early, and APOE e4 raises late-onset risk; cognitive reserve (the Nun Study) delays symptoms. Cholinesterase inhibitors and memantine ease symptoms, anti-amyloid antibodies slow early disease modestly, and Petersen's mild cognitive impairment (1999) became mild neurocognitive disorder.

10.10 The Other Neurocognitive Disorders. Neurodegenerative diseases kill neurons progressively as misfolded proteins accumulate; epilepsy is not one, and multiple sclerosis is demyelinating. Vascular neurocognitive disorder, the second commonest, has an abrupt, stepwise course with focal signs (Hachinski's multi-infarct dementia; Binswanger's disease). Frontotemporal disorder (Pick, 1892) begins younger with personality change, disinhibition or progressive aphasia and spares memory early. Lewy body disorder brings fluctuating cognition, well-formed visual hallucinations, parkinsonism, REM sleep behaviour disorder and dangerous neuroleptic sensitivity, and the one-year rule separates it from Parkinson's disease dementia. Huntington's disease (1872) is an autosomal dominant CAG repeat expansion on chromosome 4 with chorea, caudate atrophy and subcortical dementia; Wexler's Lake Maracaibo families led to the gene (1993). Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease is a rapid prion dementia with myoclonus (Prusiner; Gajdusek's kuru). HIV, brain injury, substances and reversible causes such as normal pressure hydrocephalus complete the list, and the dementias divide into cortical and subcortical.

10.11 Wernicke-Korsakoff Syndrome, Pseudodementia and Mental Disorders Due to Medical Illness. Thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency, to which heavy drinkers are especially exposed, damages the neurons around the third and fourth ventricles. Wernicke's encephalopathy (1881) is an acute emergency of confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia with nystagmus, complete in only 16 percent of Harper's autopsy cases and treated with injected thiamine. Korsakoff's syndrome is a chronic amnesia in clear consciousness: severe anterograde and temporally graded retrograde amnesia, confabulation and apathy, with digit span, intelligence and implicit memory (Claparède's pin) spared. Victor, Adams and Collins (1971) showed the two are stages of one disease, with lesions of the mammillary bodies and dorsomedial thalamus. Kiloh (1961) gave pseudodementia its modern meaning: depression mimicking dementia, separated by Wells's features, though it can herald true dementia. Cushing's syndrome mainly causes depression and hypothyroidism "myxoedema madness"; B12 deficiency, neurosyphilis, lupus, anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis, epilepsy and frontal damage (Phineas Gage) complete the medical causes.

From the past papers

  1. The term psychoactive refers to: (RML 2021)

    (a) A drug-induced hallucination (b) A particularly active psychopath (c) A drug that alters mood, cognition and/or behaviour (d) The psychology of physical activity

    Answer: (c) A drug that alters mood, cognition and/or behaviour. A psychoactive substance is any substance that alters mood, cognition, perception, consciousness or behaviour by acting on the central nervous system, which in practice means it must reach the brain (Section 10.1.1). A hallucination is only one possible effect of some such drugs, "active psychopath" is a play on the personality construct of Chapter 11, and the psychology of physical activity is exercise and sport psychology.

  2. Cross tolerance is: (Amity 2023)

    (a) No addiction to any drug (b) Exposure to one drug reduces/changes tolerance to other drugs (c) Both a and b (d) None of the above

    Answer: (b) Exposure to one drug reduces/changes tolerance to other drugs. Cross-tolerance is tolerance to one drug that carries over to other drugs acting by the same mechanism, as among alcohol, the barbiturates and the benzodiazepines at the GABA-A receptor, which is why chlordiazepoxide can cover alcohol withdrawal (Sections 10.1.2 and 10.7.9). The keyed wording is loose (strictly, exposure to one drug produces tolerance to the other), but it is the only option that describes cross-tolerance; "no addiction to any drug" is meaningless, so (c) fails as well.

  3. Substance use disorder has the following patterns EXCEPT: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Acute intoxication and withdrawal (b) Dependence syndrome (c) Harmful use (d) Drug abuse

    Answer: (d) Drug abuse. ICD-10's F1 block codes acute intoxication (.0), harmful use (.1), dependence syndrome (.2), withdrawal state (.3) and withdrawal state with delirium (.4), so the first three options are all genuine patterns (Section 10.1.4). "Abuse" was the DSM-III and DSM-IV category, which DSM-5 merged with dependence into substance use disorder; ICD-10's nearest term is harmful use.

  4. In the "Gateway Theory" of substance use, which drug is most commonly cited as the primary "gateway" that leads to the use of harder substances like heroin or cocaine? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Alcohol (b) Nicotine (c) Marijuana (d) Ecstasy

    Answer: (c) Marijuana. In Denise Kandel's sequence (1975), beer or wine came first, then cigarettes or spirits, then marijuana, then other illicit drugs, so marijuana is the stage just before heroin and cocaine and the drug most often cited as the gateway (Section 10.2.4). Alcohol and nicotine come first in the sequence, and the Kandels later argued for nicotine as a biological gateway, but neither is the classic answer; ecstasy is a later club drug, not a gateway at all.

  5. Which of the following substances is generally considered to have the highest addictive liability and potential for dependence based on epidemiological and neuropharmacological data? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Marijuana (b) Alcohol (c) Nicotine (Tobacco) (d) Cocaine

    Answer: (c) Nicotine (Tobacco). Addictive liability is the proportion of users who become dependent: about a third of everyone who had ever smoked in the National Comorbidity Survey (Anthony, Warner and Kessler, 1994), and about two thirds over a lifetime in NESARC (Lopez-Quintero and colleagues, 2011), more than for any other drug (Section 10.2.5). Cocaine is the tempting option because it is more rewarding per dose and captures its users faster, but a smaller share of them; alcohol captures about one in seven to one in four of its users and marijuana about one in ten.

  6. Sigmund Freud stated that alcohol-dependent individuals had fixation in: (RML 2022)

    (a) Anal stage (b) Oral stage (c) Genital stage (d) Latency stage

    Answer: (b) Oral stage. Freud linked alcohol dependence, smoking and overeating to fixation at the oral stage, from birth to about 18 months, when pleasure and dependency centre on the mouth (Section 10.2.7). Anal fixation gives the orderly, stingy, obstinate character; latency is a period of dormant drives at which Freud placed no fixations; and the genital stage is mature adult sexuality, the goal of development rather than a fixation point.

  7. For which of the following neurotransmitter is Alcohol an agonist? (RML 2022)

    (a) Serotonin (b) Acetylcholine (c) GABA (d) Endorphins

    Answer: (c) GABA. Alcohol enhances inhibition at the GABA-A receptor (strictly, it is a positive allosteric modulator, like the benzodiazepines and barbiturates), and its second core action is to block the NMDA glutamate receptor (Section 10.3.2). It does release endorphins and dopamine and enhances one serotonin receptor, but it is not described as a serotonin, acetylcholine or endorphin agonist; nicotine is the acetylcholine (nicotinic) agonist.

  8. Which of the following substances is NOT a natural alkaloid compound? (Amity 2023)

    (a) Morphine (b) Heroin (c) Codeine (d) Papaverine

    Answer: (b) Heroin. Heroin (diacetylmorphine) does not occur in the poppy: it is semi-synthetic, made by acetylating morphine, and was first made by C. R. Alder Wright in 1874 (Sections 10.5.1 and 10.5.2). Morphine and codeine are natural opium alkaloids, and papaverine, the option students wrongly reject, is a genuine natural alkaloid that simply is not an opioid analgesic. The twin question in the same paper, "which is NOT a natural alkaloid of opium?", has the same answer.

  9. Which cognitive disorder is correctly matched with its primary function affected: Dementia, Amnesia, Aphasia, Apraxia? (RML 2025)

    (a) Dementia - Abstraction; Amnesia - Memory; Aphasia - Language; Apraxia - Motor skills (b) Dementia - Memory; Amnesia - Language; Aphasia - Motor skills; Apraxia - Abstraction (c) Dementia - Motor skills; Amnesia - Memory; Aphasia - Abstraction; Apraxia - Language (d) Dementia - Language; Amnesia - Motor skills; Aphasia - Memory; Apraxia - Abstraction

    Answer: (a) Dementia - Abstraction; Amnesia - Memory; Aphasia - Language; Apraxia - Motor skills. Dementia is a global decline of intellect that includes abstraction and judgement, amnesia is a disorder of memory, aphasia (with alexia and acalculia) is a disorder of language and symbolic function, and apraxia is the loss of skilled, purposeful movement (Section 10.8.3, Table 10.34). Every wrong option merely shuffles these fixed pairs, and the matching question in the same paper (abstraction, memory, language and motor skills against dementia, amnesia, aphasia and apraxia) uses the same pairs.

  10. In Delirium, the motor symptoms may include: i) Asterixis, ii) Multifocal myoclonus, iii) Carphologia, iv) Tone and reflex abnormalities. (Amity 2023)

    (a) Only ii & iv are correct (b) Only i & iii are correct (c) Only iv is correct (d) All are correct

    Answer: (d) All are correct. Delirium is a disorder of the whole brain, and its motor signs include asterixis (the flapping tremor of metabolic delirium), multifocal myoclonus, tremor, carphologia (picking at the bedclothes) and changes in tone and reflexes (Section 10.8.5). Any option that leaves one of them out is wrong.

  11. Which of the following psychiatric illness is most common after cardiac surgery? (RML 2024)

    (a) Schizophrenia (b) Delirium (c) Anxiety (d) Depression

    Answer: (b) Delirium. Delirium is the commonest psychiatric syndrome after cardiac surgery, with a pooled incidence of about 23 percent after open heart surgery, because age, bypass, long anaesthesia, opioids and sleepless nights in intensive care all strike a vulnerable brain at once (Section 10.8.6). Depression and anxiety are also common after heart surgery, but they develop more slowly, over weeks, and schizophrenia is not caused by surgery at all.

  12. A key of distinguishing feature of dementia versus delirium is: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Delusions in dementia but not delirium (b) Onset gradual in delirium versus acute in dementia (c) Hallucinations in dementia but not delirium (d) Impaired memory in dementia versus fluctuating consciousness in delirium

    Answer: (d) Impaired memory in dementia versus fluctuating consciousness in delirium. The cardinal distinction is a fluctuating disturbance of consciousness and attention in delirium against impaired memory with clear consciousness in dementia (Section 10.8.8, Table 10.37). Delusions and hallucinations occur in both, and the onset is acute in delirium and gradual in dementia, the reverse of option (b).

  13. A progressive neurodegenerative disorder characterized by memory impairment, language difficulties, and visuospatial deficits, with an insidious onset and gradual progression, is: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Lewy Body Dementia (b) Vascular Dementia (c) Alzheimer's Disease (d) Frontotemporal Dementia

    Answer: (c) Alzheimer's Disease. Memory first, then language (aphasia) and visuospatial deficits, with an insidious onset and a smooth, gradual decline, is the signature of Alzheimer's disease (Section 10.9.3). Lewy body dementia fluctuates and brings visual hallucinations and parkinsonism, vascular dementia begins abruptly and worsens in steps with focal signs, and frontotemporal dementia starts younger with personality change or language loss while memory and visuospatial skills are relatively spared.

  14. Which of the following condition is related to the deficiency of acetylcholine in the brain? (RML 2022)

    (a) Senile dementia (b) Atrophy (c) Alzheimer's disease (d) Korsakoff's syndrome

    Answer: (c) Alzheimer's disease. The defining chemical lesion of Alzheimer's disease is the loss of the cholinergic neurons of the nucleus basalis of Meynert, the basis of the cholinergic hypothesis (Bartus, 1982) and of the cholinesterase inhibitors (Sections 10.9.1 and 10.9.4). "Senile dementia" and "atrophy" are descriptions that cover many causes, and Korsakoff's syndrome is a disorder of thiamine deficiency that damages the mammillary bodies and thalamus.

  15. Which of the following neurological conditions is NOT classified as a neurodegenerative disorder? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Parkinson's disease (b) Alzheimer's disease (c) Epilepsy (d) Huntington's disease

    Answer: (c) Epilepsy. In a neurodegenerative disease neurons progressively lose their structure and function and die, usually as a particular protein misfolds and accumulates, and Parkinson's, Alzheimer's and Huntington's diseases are all of this kind (Section 10.10.1). Epilepsy is a paroxysmal disorder of neuronal firing, recurrent seizures generated by chronic brain dysfunction, not a progressive loss of neurons.

  16. Lewy body dementia is most characteristically associated with: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Aphasia (b) Auditory hallucinations (c) Visual hallucinations (d) Olfactory loss

    Answer: (c) Visual hallucinations. Recurrent, well-formed visual hallucinations are a core feature of neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies, alongside fluctuating cognition and parkinsonism (Section 10.10.4). Prominent auditory hallucinations suggest schizophrenia or a mood disorder, aphasia belongs to Alzheimer's disease and the language variants of frontotemporal dementia, and loss of smell is only a supportive, non-specific feature shared with Parkinson's and Alzheimer's diseases.

  17. A patient presents with severe memory deficits, confabulation, and nystagmus, with a history of chronic alcohol use. This clinical picture is classic for: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Alzheimer's Disease (b) Wernicke-Korsakoff Syndrome (c) Vascular Dementia (d) Frontotemporal Dementia

    Answer: (b) Wernicke-Korsakoff Syndrome. Nystagmus is a Wernicke sign, severe memory loss with confabulation is the Korsakoff stage, and chronic drinking is the setting of thiamine deficiency, so the whole picture is Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome (Section 10.11.5). Alzheimer's disease has no eye signs and is not caused by drinking, vascular dementia is sudden or stepwise with focal signs, and frontotemporal dementia begins with personality or language change.

  18. Which neuropsychological disorder is characterised by severe memory impairment and confusion, often caused by chronic alcohol abuse? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Schizophrenia (b) Korsakoff's syndrome (c) Huntington's disease (d) Multiple sclerosis

    Answer: (b) Korsakoff's syndrome. When chronic drinking and a disorder of memory appear together in the stem, the answer is Korsakoff's syndrome (strictly, the confusion belongs to the Wernicke stage, but the papers use the name for the whole; Section 10.11.5). Schizophrenia is a psychotic disorder, not an amnesic syndrome, Huntington's disease is an inherited degeneration of the caudate nucleus, and multiple sclerosis is an autoimmune demyelinating disease; none is caused by alcohol.

  19. Chronic alcoholism may result in which amnesic disorder? (RML 2021)

    (a) Korsakoff's syndrome (b) Alzheimer's disease (c) Pick's disease (d) Parkinson's disease

    Answer: (a) Korsakoff's syndrome. Korsakoff's syndrome, a chronic amnesia caused by thiamine deficiency in heavy drinkers, is the amnesic syndrome of chronic alcoholism, coded F10.6 in ICD-10 (Sections 10.11.3 to 10.11.5). Alzheimer's disease is not caused by alcohol, Pick's disease, the classic frontotemporal dementia, spares memory early, and Parkinson's disease is a disorder of movement caused by loss of the dopamine neurons of the substantia nigra.

  20. Which of the following illnesses caused by a long term abuse of alcohol often involves profound retrograde amnesia? (RML 2024)

    (a) Alzheimer's disease (b) Parkinson's disease (c) Huntington's disease (d) Korsakoff's psychosis

    Answer: (d) Korsakoff's psychosis. Besides its anterograde amnesia, Korsakoff's syndrome brings a patchy and often extensive retrograde amnesia, sometimes reaching back decades, with a temporal gradient in which recent years are lost most completely (Albert, Butters and Levin, 1979); "psychosis" here is the nineteenth-century word for any serious mental disorder (Section 10.11.3). Alzheimer's, Parkinson's and Huntington's diseases are not caused by drinking.

  21. Which of the following disorder is a common cause of pseudodementia? (RML 2024)

    (a) Schizophrenia (b) Mania (c) Depression (d) Hypochondriasis

    Answer: (c) Depression. Pseudodementia, given its modern meaning by Leslie Kiloh in 1961, is cognitive impairment that resembles dementia but is caused by a psychiatric disorder, most often depression, and improves when that disorder is treated (Section 10.11.6). Schizophrenia brings real cognitive deficits but is not the common cause of a reversible, dementia-like state, mania only rarely mimics dementia, and in hypochondriasis the worry about memory comes with normal tested memory.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Korsakoff's syndrome (Korsakoff's psychosis; Sergei Korsakoff, from 1887) is the amnesic disorder of chronic alcoholism: severe anterograde amnesia, a temporally graded and often profound retrograde amnesia, confabulation and apathy, in clear consciousness with digit span and general intelligence preserved; ICD-10 codes it F10.6. asked 3 times
  2. Delirium versus dementia: delirium has an acute onset (hours to days), a fluctuating course and a disturbance of attention and awareness (clouded consciousness), and is usually reversible; dementia has an insidious onset, a progressive course and impaired memory with clear consciousness. Hallucinations and delusions occur in both. asked 4 times
  3. Dementia chiefly affects abstraction (a global decline), amnesia memory, aphasia (with alexia and acalculia) language, and apraxia skilled purposeful movement (motor skills); agnosia is a failure of recognition. asked 2 times
  4. Heroin (diacetylmorphine) is not a natural alkaloid of opium: it is semi-synthetic, made by acetylating morphine (C. R. Alder Wright, 1874). Morphine, codeine, thebaine, papaverine and noscapine are natural; methadone, pethidine, fentanyl and tramadol are synthetic. asked 2 times
  5. Severe memory loss, confabulation and nystagmus in a chronic drinker is Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome: Wernicke's encephalopathy (Carl Wernicke, 1881) is the acute stage of confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia with nystagmus, and Korsakoff's syndrome the chronic amnesic stage (Victor, Adams and Collins, 1971, showed they are one disease). asked 2 times
  6. Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome is caused by deficiency of thiamine (vitamin B1), classically in chronic alcoholism; not vitamin B12 (cobalamin), vitamin D, folate, hypoxia or lead. It is treated at once with high-dose thiamine by injection. asked 2 times
  7. A psychoactive substance is a drug that alters mood, cognition, perception or behaviour by acting on the central nervous system. asked once
  8. Cross-tolerance: tolerance to one drug extends to other drugs acting by the same mechanism, as among alcohol, barbiturates and benzodiazepines at the GABA-A receptor; it is the basis of benzodiazepine detoxification for alcohol withdrawal. asked once
  9. The ICD-10 patterns of F10 to F19 are .0 acute intoxication, .1 harmful use, .2 dependence syndrome, .3 withdrawal state, .4 withdrawal state with delirium, .5 psychotic disorder, .6 amnesic syndrome and .7 residual and late-onset psychotic disorder; "drug abuse" is not one of them (it was a DSM-III and DSM-IV category). asked once
  10. In the gateway hypothesis, built on Denise Kandel's stages (1975: beer or wine, cigarettes or spirits, marijuana, other illicit drugs), marijuana is the drug most commonly cited as the gateway to heroin and cocaine, although alcohol and tobacco come first. asked once
  11. Nicotine (tobacco) has the highest addictive liability: about a third of all who had ever smoked had become dependent in the National Comorbidity Survey (Anthony, Warner and Kessler, 1994), and about two thirds over a lifetime in NESARC (Lopez-Quintero and colleagues, 2011). asked once
  12. Sigmund Freud held that alcohol-dependent people are fixated at the oral stage (birth to about 18 months). asked once
  13. Alcohol is a GABA-A agonist (strictly a positive allosteric modulator, like the benzodiazepines and barbiturates) and an NMDA glutamate antagonist; it is not a serotonin, acetylcholine or endorphin agonist. asked once
  14. The motor signs of delirium include asterixis (flapping tremor), multifocal myoclonus, tremor, carphologia (picking at the bedclothes) and abnormalities of tone and reflexes: all can occur. asked once
  15. Delirium is the commonest psychiatric syndrome after cardiac surgery (a pooled incidence of about 23 percent after open heart surgery). asked once
  16. Alzheimer's disease has an insidious onset and gradual progression: episodic memory loss first, then language difficulty (aphasia), apraxia and visuospatial deficits, with amyloid plaques and tau tangles in the brain. asked once
  17. Acetylcholine deficiency is the chemical lesion of Alzheimer's disease: loss of the cholinergic neurons of the nucleus basalis of Meynert (the cholinergic hypothesis), hence the cholinesterase inhibitors. asked once
  18. Epilepsy is not a neurodegenerative disorder (it is a paroxysmal disorder of neuronal firing); Alzheimer's, Parkinson's and Huntington's diseases, frontotemporal degeneration and ALS are. asked once
  19. Neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies: recurrent, well-formed visual hallucinations, fluctuating cognition, parkinsonism, REM sleep behaviour disorder and severe sensitivity to antipsychotics. asked once
  20. The common cause of pseudodementia is depression (Leslie Kiloh, 1961): fairly abrupt onset, "I don't know" answers, the patient complains of poor memory, and cognition improves when the depression is treated. asked once
  21. Korsakoff's amnesia is diencephalic: bilateral damage to the mammillary bodies and the dorsomedial (mediodorsal) nucleus of the thalamus, not H.M.'s hippocampus. asked once
  22. Formication, tactile hallucinations of insects crawling on or under the skin, is most characteristic of stimulant intoxication ("cocaine bugs", Magnan's sign) and of alcohol or sedative withdrawal (delirium tremens). asked once
  23. ICD-11 places gaming disorder (6C51), with gambling disorder (6C50), under disorders due to substance use or addictive behaviours; in DSM-5-TR gambling disorder is the only behavioural addiction and internet gaming disorder is a condition for further study. asked once
  24. Huntington's disease (George Huntington, 1872) is autosomal dominant, caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion on chromosome 4, with chorea, caudate atrophy and a subcortical dementia. asked once
  25. Cushing's syndrome mainly produces depression, with anxiety and cognitive impairment and occasionally psychosis or mania. asked once
  26. The circuit most consistently changed in substance use disorders is the mesolimbic dopamine system: the ventral tegmental area projecting to the nucleus accumbens (ventral striatum). asked once
  27. Disulfiram inhibits aldehyde dehydrogenase, so acetaldehyde builds up and drinking causes flushing, nausea and palpitations; naltrexone blocks opioid receptors and acamprosate modulates glutamate. asked once
